But that we do so make a picture is proved by the fact that upon being confronted by the real features of the person in question we always experience a certain amount of surprise, even when we have not been conscious of a different conception of him.
Be that as it may, however, there was no question in John Lenox’s mind as to the identity of the person who at last came briskly into the back office and interrupted his meditations. Rather under the middle height, he was broad-shouldered and deep-chested, with a clean-shaven, red face, with﻿—not a mole﻿—but a slight protuberance the size of half a large pea on the line from the nostril to the corner of the mouth; bald over the crown and to a line a couple of inches above the ear, below that thick and somewhat bushy hair of yellowish red, showing a mingling of gray; small but very blue eyes; a thick nose, of no classifiable shape, and a large mouth with the lips so pressed together as to produce a slightly downward and yet rather humorous curve at the corners. He was dressed in a sack coat of dark “pepper-and-salt,” with waistcoat and trousers to match. A somewhat old-fashioned standing collar, flaring away from the throat, was encircled by a red cravat, tied in a bow under his chin. A diamond stud of perhaps two carats showed in the triangle of spotless shirt front, and on his head was a cloth cap with ear lappets. He accosted our friend with, “I reckon you must be Mr. Lenox. How are you? I’m glad to see you,” tugging off a thick buckskin glove, and putting out a plump but muscular hand.
John thanked him as they shook hands, and “hoped he was well.”
“Wa’al,” said Mr. Harum, “I’m improvin’ slowly. I’ve got so ’st I c’n set up long enough to have my bed made. Come last night, I s’pose? Anybody to the deepo to bring ye over? This time o’ year once ’n a while the’ don’t nobody go over for passengers.”
John said that he had had no trouble.

===Chinese===

左边望得见一小截大街，街上榆树枫树光秃的枝桠，受西北风鼓动，朝街对面的对手挑战地挥舞。
凡是我们想过一想的陌生人，我们必定都在心里画过一幅像。有的淡到自己当时不觉；有的鲜活到一见本人、一见照片才散。可我们确实画了——证据是：一当本人真实的眉目摆到面前，我们总要吃几分惊，哪怕原先并不自觉有过另一副构想。
且不管它，总之，末了快步走进后屋、打断他思绪的那位是谁，约翰·伦诺克斯心里没有疑问。个头略低于中等，肩宽胸厚，脸刮得干净，红彤彤的——不是痣，是从鼻翼到嘴角那道线上半粒大豌豆大的一颗小凸起；头顶秃到耳朵上方两寸的一条线，线以下是一头厚而蓬的黄红色头发，间着灰；一双小小的、极蓝的眼睛；一只厚鼻子，说不上属于哪一类；一张大嘴，两片嘴唇抿得紧紧，嘴角压出一点下垂、却又相当幽默的弯。他穿一件深色「胡椒盐」的便装上衣，马甲裤子一色。一条略嫌老式的立领，从脖子上支开，围一条红领巾，在下巴底下打一个结。雪白的衬衫前胸三角里闪着一颗约莫两克拉的钻石扣；头上戴一顶布帽，带帽耳。他迎着我们的朋友开口：「我猜您准是伦诺克斯先生。可好？见到您真高兴，」一面拽下一只厚鹿皮手套，伸出一只肥厚而有力气的手。
握手时约翰道了谢，「望他安好」。
「这个嘛，」哈勒姆先生说，「慢慢见好。如今能坐起来，坐到人家给我铺好床了。昨晚到的，我猜？车站有人接您过来没有？这个时节，间或没人过去接客人。」
约翰说没费什么事。

===Original===

A man by the name of Robinson had brought him and his luggage.
“E-up!” said David with a nod, backing up to the fire which was burning in the grate of the Franklin stove, “ ‘Dug’ Robinson. ’D he do the p’lite thing in the matter of questions an’ gen’ral conversation?” he asked with a grin. John laughed in reply to this question.
“Where’d you put up?” asked David, John said that he passed the night at the Eagle Hotel. Mr. Harum had seen Dick Larrabee that morning and heard what he had to say of our friend’s reception, but he liked to get his information from original sources.
“Make ye putty comf’table?” he asked, turning to eject a mouthful into the fire.
“I got along pretty well under the circumstances,” said John.
Mr. Harum did not press the inquiry. “How’d you leave the gen’ral?” he inquired.
“He seemed to be well,” replied John, “and he wished to be kindly remembered to you.”
“Fine man, the gen’ral,” declared David, well pleased. “Fine man all ’round. Word’s as good as his bond. Yes, sir, when the gen’ral gives his warrant, I don’t care whether I see the critter or not. Know him much?”
“He and my father were old friends, and I have known him a good many years,” replied John, adding, “he has been very kind and friendly to me.”
“Set down, set down,” said Mr. Harum, pointing to a chair. Seating himself, he took off his cap and dropped it with his gloves on the floor. “How long you ben here in the office?” he asked.
“Perhaps half an hour,” was the reply.
“I meant to have ben here when you come,” said the banker, “but I got hendered about a matter of a hoss I’m looking at. I guess I’ll shut that door,” making a move toward the one into the front office.
“Allow me,” said John, getting up and closing it.
“May’s well shut the other one while you’re about it. Thank you,” as John resumed his seat. “I hain’t got nothin’ very private, but I’m ’fraid of distractin’ Timson’s mind. Did he int’duce himself?”
“Yes,” said John, “we introduced ourselves and had a few minutes conversation.”
“Gin ye his hull hist’ry an’ a few relations throwed in?”
“There was hardly time for that,” said John, smiling.
“Rubbed a little furn’ture polish into my char’cter an’ repitation?” insinuated Mr. Harum.
“Most of our talk was on the subject of his duties and responsibilities,” was John’s reply. (“Don’t cal’late to let on any more’n he cal’lates to,” thought David to himself.)

===Chinese===

一个姓罗宾逊的把他和行李一道送了来。
「哦，」大卫点头说，退到富兰克林炉膛里正烧着的火前，「『达格』·罗宾逊。问长问短、寒暄客套那一套，他给你办得周全没有？」他咧嘴一笑。约翰笑着回敬了这一问。
「落脚在哪儿？」大卫问。约翰说在鹰旅店过的夜。哈勒姆先生早晨见过迪克·拉腊比，我们这位朋友被接待的经过已经听他说了；可他喜欢从源头取货。
「住得还舒适？」他问，偏头朝炉火里吐了一口。
「就那个情形说，过得去，」约翰说。
哈勒姆先生没有追问。「将军身子可好？」他打听。
「看着好，」约翰答，「他还托我向您问好。」
「好人，将军，」大卫宣布，满心欢喜。「里里外外一个好人。一句话就是一张债券。是，先生；将军一开保票，那头牲口我见都没见过也放心。同他熟？」
「他同我父亲是老朋友，我认得他好多年了，」约翰答，又添一句，「他待我一直又好又亲。」
「坐，坐，」哈勒姆先生指着椅子说。他自己坐下，摘了帽子，连手套一起撂在地板上。「在办公室待了多久了？」他问。
「约莫半个钟头，」回答是。
「本想您来时我在这儿，」银行家说，「叫一匹我在相的马绊住了。那扇门我去关上，」朝通前间的那扇动了动。
「我来，」约翰起身关了。
「顺手那扇也关上。多谢，」约翰回座时他说。「我没什么背着人的事，就怕搅了蒂姆森的心神。他自我介绍没有？」
「介绍了，」约翰说，「我们互相介绍了，还谈了几分钟。」
「把他全部的家史、外加几位亲戚都奉送了？」
「那倒来不及，」约翰微笑说。
「没往我的品性和名头上擦几把家具油？」哈勒姆先生探着问。
「我们的谈话多半在他的职责和担子上，」约翰回答。（「不想露的，同我一样不多露，」大卫心里想。）

===Original===

“Allowed he run the hull shebang, didn’t he?”
“He seemed to have a pretty large idea of what was required of one in his place,” admitted the witness.
“Kind o’ friendly, was he?” asked David.
“Well,” said John, “after we had talked for a while I said to him that I was glad to think that he could have no unpleasant feeling toward me, seeing that he had given up the place of his own preference, and he assured me that he had none.”
David turned and looked at John for an instant, with a twinkle in his eye. The younger man returned the look and smiled slightly. David laughed outright.
“I guess you’ve seen folks before,” he remarked.
“I have never met anyone exactly like Mr. Timson, I think,” said our friend with a slight laugh.
“Fortunitly them kind is rare,” observed Mr. Harum dryly, rising and going to his desk, from a drawer of which he produced a couple of cigars, one of which he proffered to John, who, for the first time in his life, during the next half hour regretted that he was a smoker. David sat for two or three minutes puffing diligently, and then took the weed out of his mouth and looked contemplatively at it.
“How do you like that cigar?” he inquired.
“It burns very nicely,” said the victim. Mr. Harum emitted a cough which was like a chuckle, or a chuckle which was like a cough, and relapsed into silence again. Presently he turned his head, looked curiously at the young man for a moment, and then turned his glance again to the fire.
“I’ve ben wonderin’ some,” he said, “pertic’lerly since I see you, how ’twas ’t you wanted to come up here to Homeville. Gen’l Wolsey gin his warrant, an’ so I reckon you hadn’t ben gettin’ into no scrape nor nothin’,” and again he looked sharply at the young man at his side.
“Did the general say nothing of my affairs?” the latter asked.
“No,” replied David, “all ’t he said was in a gen’ral way that he’d knowed you an’ your folks a good while, an’ he thought you’d be jest the feller I was lookin’ fer. Mebbe he reckoned that if you wanted your story told, you’d ruther tell it yourself.”

===Chinese===

「他觉得自己撑着一整个摊子，是吧？」
「他对他那个位分上该干什么，主意相当大，」证人承认。
「挺友好？」大卫问。
「嗯，」约翰说，「谈了一阵之后我对他说：他是自己愿意走的，想必对我不会有什么不痛快；他向我担保，一点没有。」
大卫扭头看了约翰一瞬，眼里一点火光。年轻人迎着他的看，浅浅一笑。大卫放声笑了。
「看来你以前见过人，」他说。
「蒂姆森先生这样的人物，我确实从没见过，」我们这位朋友轻笑说。
「幸亏那一类稀少，」哈勒姆先生干干地说，起身走到写字台前，从抽屉里摸出两支雪茄，递一支给约翰——此后半个钟头里，约翰生平头一回收自己是个烟民。大卫坐了两三分钟，勤勤恳恳喷着，随后把烟从嘴里拔出来，若有所思地端详。
「这雪茄您觉得怎么样？」他问。
「烧得很顺，」牺牲品说。哈勒姆先生发出一声咳嗽，像一声低笑；或说一声低笑，像一声咳嗽；重新沉默。过了一会儿他转过头，好奇地看了年轻人一眼，又把目光挪回炉火。
「我有些纳闷，」他说，「打见着你，就越发纳闷：你怎么想起上霍姆维尔来。沃尔西将军开了保票，我料你也没闯过什么祸、欠过什么债，」他又锐利地看了身旁的年轻人一眼。
「将军没提我的事吗？」后者问。
「没有，」大卫答，「他只笼统说了两句：他同你、同你家熟识多年，说你正是我要寻的那号人。兴许他想：你的故事你要愿意讲，你自个儿讲最好。」

## XIV / 十四
===Original===

Whatever might have been John’s repugnance to making a confidant of the man whom he had known but for half an hour, he acknowledged to himself that the other’s curiosity was not only natural but proper. He could not but know that in appearance and manner he was in marked contrast with those whom the man had so far seen. He divined the fact that his coming from a great city to settle down in a village town would furnish matter for surprise and conjecture, and felt that it would be to his advantage with the man who was to be his employer that he should be perfectly and obviously frank upon all matters of his own which might be properly mentioned. He had an instinctive feeling that Harum combined acuteness and suspiciousness to a very large degree, and he had also a feeling that the old man’s confidence, once gained, would not be easily shaken. So he told his hearer so much of his history as he thought pertinent, and David listened without interruption or comment, save an occasional “E-um’m.”
“And here I am,” John remarked in conclusion.
“Here you *be*, fer a fact,” said David. “Wa’al, the’s worse places ’n Homeville﻿—after you git used to it,” he added in qualification. “I ben back here a matter o’ thirteen or fourteen year now, an’ am gettin’ to feel my way ’round putty well; but not havin’ ben in these parts fer putty nigh thirty year, I found it ruther lonesome to start with, an’ I guess if it hadn’t ’a’ ben fer Polly I wouldn’t ’a’ stood it. But up to the time I come back she hadn’t never ben ten mile away f’m here in her hull life, an’ I couldn’t budge her. But then,” he remarked, “while Homeville aint a metrop’lis, it’s some a diff’rent place f’m what it used to be﻿—in some *ways*. Polly’s my sister,” he added by way of explanation.
“Well,” said John, with rather a rueful laugh, “if it has taken you all that time to get used to it the outlook for me is not very encouraging, I’m afraid.”
“Wa’al,” remarked Mr. Harum, “I’m apt to speak in par’bles sometimes. I guess you’ll git along after a spell, though it mayn’t set fust rate on your stomech till you git used to the diet. Say,” he said after a moment, “if you’d had a couple o’ thousan’ more, do you think you’d ’a’ stuck to the law bus’nis?”
“I’m sure I don’t know,” replied John, “but I am inclined to think not.

===Chinese===

把心事掏给一个才认得半个钟头的人，约翰无论有多不情愿，他心里也承认：对方的好奇不但自然，而且正当。他不会不知道，自己的仪表做派，同此人平素所见的人判然有别。他料得到，自己从大城跑来小镇落户，本身就是一桩够人惊讶、够人揣测的事；也觉得，对这位即将当自己东家的人，凡是可以讲的私事，讲得透亮、亮得显眼，只有好处。他本能地觉得，哈勒姆这个人，精明和多疑都到了相当的浓度；可他同样觉得，老人家的信任一旦赢得，轻易不会动摇。于是他把自认为要紧的身世，讲了多多少少给对方听；大卫听着，不打断、不插嘴，只偶尔一声「嗯——哼」。

「我就这么来了。」约翰收尾道。

「你*是*来了，这不假。」大卫说。「这个嘛，比霍姆维尔糟的地方多着呢——习惯了以后。」他补了个限定。「俺回来也有个十三四年了，路数摸得差不多顺了；可先前离开这一带快三十年，头里还真嫌冷清，俺寻思要不是波莉，俺未必熬得住。只是俺回来之前，她这辈子就没出过这儿十里地，俺撬不动她。不过，」他说，「霍姆维尔虽算不得大码头，比起从前也换了些样了——有些*方面*。波莉是俺妹子。」他补了句解说。

「呢，」约翰笑得有点苦，「你要花那么些年才习惯，我的前景怕是不太乐观。」

「这个嘛，」哈勒姆先生说，「俺说话有时候爱打个比方。俺瞅过一阵子你就能对付，只是这口粮，胃没适应之前怕是不大受用。哎，」他停了停，「你当年手头要是再多个两千，你说你会死磕法律那一行不？」

「这我真说不好，」约翰答，「可我倾向于不会。

===Original===

General Wolsey told me that if I were very anxious to go on with it he would help me, but after what I told him he advised me to write to you.”
“He did, did he?”
“Yes,” said John, “and after what I had gone through I was not altogether sorry to come away.”
“Wa’al,” said Mr. Harum thoughtfully, “if I was to lose what little I’ve got, an’ had to give up livin’ in the way I was used to, an’ couldn’t even keep a hoss, I c’n allow ’t I might be willin’ fer a change of scene to make a fresh start in. Yes, sir, I guess I would. Wa’al,” looking at his watch, “I’ve got to go now, an’ I’ll see ye later, mebbe. You feel like takin’ holt today?”
“Oh, yes,” said John with alacrity.
“All right,” said Mr. Harum. “You tell Timson what you want, an’ make him show you everythin’. He understands, an’ I’ve paid him for’t. He’s agreed to stay any time in reason ’t you want him, but I guess,” he added with a laugh, “ ’t you c’n pump him dry ’n a day or two. It haint rained wisdom an’ knowlidge in his part o’ the country fer a consid’able spell.”
David stood for a moment drawing on his gloves, and then, looking at John with his characteristic chuckle, continued:
“Allowed he’d ben drawin’ the hull load, did he? Wa’al, sir, the truth on’t is ’t he never come to a hill yet, ’f ’t wa’n’t more ’n a foot high, but what I had to git out an’ push; nor never struck a turn in the road but what I had to take him by the head an’ lead him into it.” With which Mr. Harum put on his overcoat and cap and departed.
---
Mr. Timson was leaning over the counter in animated controversy with a man on the outside who had evidently asserted or quoted (the quotation is the usual weapon: it has a double barb and can be wielded with comparative safety) something of a wounding effect.
“No, sir,” exclaimed Chet, with a sounding slap on the counter, “no, sir! The’ ain’t one word o’ truth in’t. I said myself, ‘I won’t stan’ it,’ I says, ‘not f’m you ner nobody else,’ I says, ‘an’ what’s more,’ says I﻿—” The expression in the face of Mr. Timson’s tormentor caused that gentleman to break off and look around. The man on the outside grinned, stared at John a moment, and went out, and Timson turned and said, as John came forward, “Hello! The old man picked ye to pieces all he wanted to?”

===Chinese===

「沃尔西将军说，我要是死心塌地想接着干，他帮我；可听我一说情形，他劝我写信给您。」

「他这么说的，嗯？」

「是的，」约翰说，「再说经过了那些事，能抽身出来，我也不是全然不情愿。」

「这个嘛，」哈勒姆先生寻思着说，「俺要是把这点家当折了，惯常的日子过不成，连匹马都养不起，俺得承认，换个地方重打锣鼓，俺兴许也乐意。是的，先生，俺估摸会。好了，」他看看表，「俺得走了，晚些兴许再来看你。今儿就想上手？」

「哦，想。」约翰一口应承。

「好。」哈勒姆先生说。「你要什么同蒂姆森讲，叫他样样拿给你看。他心里有数，这份钱俺也付过了。你合理要用他多久，他都答应留下；不过俺瞅，」他笑着添一句，「一两天的工夫，你就能把他榨干。他那地界，智慧同学问好久没下过雨了。」

大卫站着戴手套，戴好了，带着他标志性的咯咯一笑，接着说：

「他当自己一直拉的是整车，是吧？这个嘛，先生，实情是：坡只要高过一尺，俺就得下车推；路上只要拐个弯，俺就得牵住他脑袋把他领进去。」说完，哈勒姆先生穿上大衣、戴上帽子，走了。

---

蒂姆森先生伏在柜台上，同外头一个人吵得正酣；那外头的分明是抛出了、或者引了句什么伤人的话（引语是惯用的兵器：倒钩成对，抡起来又比较安全）。

「不，先生，」切特一巴掌拍在柜台上，山响，「不，先生！那里头一个字真的都没有。俺亲口说的：『这个俺不受，』俺说，『你也不行，谁也不行，』俺说，『再说了，』俺说——」折磨蒂姆森先生的那张脸上的表情，让这位先生话头一断、回头看去。外头那人咧嘴一笑，盯着约翰瞅了一阵，出去了；蒂姆森转过身，正赶上约翰上前，说：「哟！老头子把你盘问透了没有？」

===Original===

“We are through for the day, I fancy,” said our friend, smiling, “and if you are ready to begin my lessons I am ready to take them. Mr. Harum told me that you would be good enough to show me what was necessary.”
“All right,” said Mr. Timson readily enough, and so John began his first day’s work in David’s office. He was surprised and encouraged to find how much his experience in Rush & Company’s office stood him in hand, and managed to acquire in a comparatively short time a pretty fair comprehension of the system which prevailed in “Harum’s bank,” notwithstanding the incessant divagations of his instructor.
It was decided between Timson and our friend that on the following day the latter should undertake the office work under supervision, and the next morning John was engaged upon the preliminaries of the day’s business when his employer came in and seated himself at his desk in the back room. After a few minutes, in which he was busy with his letters, he appeared in the doorway of the front room. He did not speak, for John saw him, and, responding to a backward toss of the head, followed him into the “parlor,” and at an intimation of the same silent character shut the doors. Mr. Harum sat down at his desk, and John stood awaiting his pleasure.
“How ’d ye make out yestidy?” he asked. “Git anythin’ out of old tongue-tied?” pointing with his thumb toward the front room.
“Oh, yes,” said John, smiling, as he recalled the unceasing flow of words which had enveloped Timson’s explanations.
“How much longer do you think you’ll have to have him ’round?” asked Mr. Harum.
“Well,” said John, “of course your customers are strangers to me, but so far as the routine of the office is concerned I think I can manage after today. But I shall have to appeal to you rather often for a while until I get thoroughly acquainted with my work.”
“Good fer you,” said David. “You’ve took holt a good sight quicker ’n I thought ye would, an’ I’ll spend more or less time ’round here fer a while, or be where you c’n reach me. It’s like this,” he continued; “Chet’s a helpless kind of critter, fer all his braggin’ an’ talk, an’ I ben feelin’ kind o’ wambly about turnin’ him loose﻿—though the Lord knows,” he said with feeling, “ ’t I’ve had bother enough with him to kill a tree.

===Chinese===

「今儿他那儿就算完了，」我们这位朋友笑着说，「你要是开得了课，我就领教。哈勒姆先生说，承你的情，把要紧的都指点给我。」

「行。」蒂姆森先生答应得倒爽快；约翰就这样在大卫的行里上了头一天的工。在拉什公司写字间攒下的那点底子，居然这么顶用，他又惊又受鼓舞；虽说授课人东拉西扯没个停，他还是没用多久，就把「哈勒姆银行」里通行的这一套摸了个大概。

蒂姆森同我们这位朋友议定：第二天起，由后者在监督下接手柜上的事。第二天早晨，约翰正忙着当天营业的开头几件，东家进来了，在里间自己的书桌前坐下。他忙了几分钟信件，随后出现在前屋门口。他没出声——约翰看见他了；脑袋往后一扬，约翰跟着他进了「客厅」，又照着同样无言的示意，把两扇门都关上。哈勒姆先生在桌前坐下，约翰站着候他示下。

「昨儿个咋样？」他问。「从老舌头打结的那儿抠出点东西没？」大拇指朝前屋一指。

「抠出了，」约翰笑着答——想起裹着蒂姆森讲解的那股滔滔不绝。

「你看还得留他几天？」哈勒姆先生问。

「呢，」约翰说，「您的主顾我自然还两眼一抹黑；可柜上的日常这一套，过了今儿我想就能应付。只是头一阵子，等我把活计吃透之前，恐怕还得常常请教您。」

「有你的。」大卫说。「你上手比俺想的快得多；这一阵俺会多多少少在这儿待着，要不就在你够得着的地方。是这样，」他接着说，「切特这家伙，别看他吹胡子瞪眼，其实是个没主见的物件，放他出去俺心里一直有点发虚——不过老天知道，」他动情地说，「俺为他操的心，砍倒一棵树都够了。

===Original===

But anyway I wrote to some folks I know up to Syrchester to git something fer him to do, an’ I got a letter to send him along, an’ mebbe they’d give him a show. See?”
“Yes, sir,” said John, “and if you are willing to take the chances of my mistakes I will undertake to get on without him.”
“All right,” said the banker, “we’ll call it a heat﻿—and, say, don’t let on what I’ve told you. I want to see how long it’ll take to git all over the village that he didn’t ask no odds o’ nobody. Hadn’t ben out o’ a job three days ’fore the’ was a lot o’ chances, an’ all ’t he had to do was to take his pick out o’ the lot on ’em.”
“Really?” said John.
“Yes, sir,” said David. “Some folks is gaited that way. Amusin’, ain’t it?﻿—Hullo, Dick! Wa’al?”
“Willis’ll give two hunderd fer the sorr’l colt,” said the incomer, whom John recognized as one of the loungers in the Eagle bar the night of his arrival.
“E-um’m!” said David. “Was he speakin’ of any pertic’ler colt, or sorril colts in gen’ral? I hain’t got the only one the’ is, I s’pose.”
Dick merely laughed. “Because,” continued the owner of the “sorril colt,” “if Steve Willis wants to lay in sorril colts at two hunderd a piece, I ain’t goin’ to gainsay him, but you tell him that two-forty-nine ninety-nine won’t buy the one in my barn.” Dick laughed again.
John made a move in the direction of the front room.
“Hold on a minute,” said David. “Shake hands with Mr. Larrabee.”
“Seen ye before,” said Dick, as they shook hands. “I was in the barroom when you come in the other night,” and then he laughed as at the recollection of something very amusing.
John flushed a little and said, a bit stiffly, “I remember you were kind enough to help about my luggage.”
“Excuse me,” said Dick, conscious of the other’s manner. “I wa’n’t laughin’ at you, that is, not in pertic’ler. I couldn’t see your face when Ame offered ye pie an’ doughnuts instid of beefsteak an’ fixins. I c’d only guess at that; but Ame’s face was enough fer me,” and Dick went off into another cachinnation.
David’s face indicated some annoyance. “Oh, shet up,” he exclaimed. “You’d keep that yawp o’ your’n goin’, I believe, if it was the judgment day.”

===Chinese===

「反正俺给锡拉切斯特几个认得的人写了信，给他寻个事由，回信也有了，让他捎着去，兴许人家给他个机会。明白？」

「明白，先生。」约翰说，「您若不怕我出错担着风险，我愿意试着不靠他顶下来。」

「好，」银行家说，「就这么赛一回合——还有，俺同你说的这些，别露出去。俺倒要看看，『他从没求过谁一星半点』这话传遍全村得几天。失业不出三天，机会就来了一大把，他只消从里头挑一个。」

「真的？」约翰说。

「真的，先生。」大卫说。「有人天生那个步法。有趣不？——哟，迪克！咋样？」

「威利斯出两百块，要那匹沙毛小马。」进来的这位说；约翰认出来了，正是他抵达那晚雄鹰酒柜边的一个闲人。

「嗯——哼！」大卫说。「他讲的是哪一匹特定的马，还是泛指天下的沙毛马？俺养的想来还不是天底下独一份。」

迪克光笑。「因为，」那匹「沙毛小马」的主人接着说，「史蒂夫·威利斯要是肯按两百一匹的价囤沙毛马，俺不拦着；可你告诉他，二百四十九块九毛九，买不走俺马房里那匹。」迪克又笑。

约翰朝前屋挪了一步。

「慢着。」大卫说。「同拉腊比先生握个手。」

「见过你。」迪克边握手边说。「那晚你进门，俺就在酒柜边上。」随后他笑起来，像想起了什么极好笑的事。

约翰微微一红，有点僵地说：「记得承你的情，帮我搬了行李。」

「见谅，」迪克说，觉察了对方的态度。「俺不是笑你——就是说，不是单笑你。艾姆端上馅饼炸面圈、替下牛排全套的时候，你那张脸俺没瞧见，只能猜；可艾姆那张脸，就够俺受用了。」迪克又爆出一阵大笑。

大卫脸上透出些不耐。「哦，闭嘴。」他喝道。「俺看哪，就算审判日到了，你那张破锣也照样敲个不停。」

===Original===

“Wa’al,” said Dick with a grin, “I expect the’ might be some fun to be got out o’ *that*, if a feller wa’n’t worryin’ too much about his own skin; an’ as fur’s I’m concerned﻿—” Dick’s further views on the subject of that momentous occasion were left unexplained. A significant look in David’s face caused the speaker to break off and turn toward the door, through which came two men, the foremost a hulking, shambling fellow, with an expression of repellent sullenness. He came forward to within about ten feet of David’s desk, while his companion halted near the door. David eyed him in silence.
“I got this here notice this mornin’,” said the man, “sayin’ ’t my note ’d be due to-morrer, an’ ’d have to be paid.”
“Wa’al,” said David, with his arm over the back of his chair and his left hand resting on his desk, “that’s so, ain’t it?”
“Mebbe so,” was the fellow’s reply, “fur ’s the comin’ due ’s concerned, but the payin’ part ’s another matter.”
“Was you cal’latin’ to have it renewed?” asked David, leaning a little forward.
“No,” said the man coolly, “I don’t know ’s I want to renew it fer any pertic’ler time, an’ I guess it c’n run along fer a while jest as ’tis.” John looked at Dick Larrabee. He was watching David’s face with an expression of the utmost enjoyment. David twisted his chair a little more to the right and out from the desk.
“You think it c’n run along, do ye?” he asked suavely. “I’m glad to have your views on the subject. Wa’al, I guess it kin, too, until *to-morro’* at four o’clock, an’ after that you c’n settle with lawyer Johnson or the sheriff.” The man uttered a disdainful laugh.
“I guess it’ll puzzle ye some to c’lect it,” he said. Mr. Harum’s bushy red eyebrows met above his nose.
“Look here, Bill Montaig,” he said, “I know more ’bout this matter ’n you think for. I know ’t you ben makin’ your brags that you’d fix me in this deal. You allowed that you’d set up usury in the fust place, an’ if that didn’t work I’d find you was execution proof anyways. That’s so, ain’t it?”
“That’s about the size on’t,” said Montaig, putting his feet a little farther apart. David had risen from his chair.
“You didn’t talk that way,” proceeded the latter, “when you come whinin’ ’round here to git that money in the fust place, an’ as I reckon some o’ the facts in the case has slipped out o’ your mind since that time, I guess I’d better jog your mem’ry a little.”

===Chinese===

「这个嘛，」迪克咧嘴一笑，「俺琢磨那场合也能淘换出点乐子，只要人不那么惦记自己那张皮；至于俺本人嘛——」关于那重大时刻的进一步见解，迪克没来得及发挥。大卫脸上一个有分量的眼色，叫他把话咬断、转向门口；门口进来两个人，前头那个五大三粗、走路拖沓，一脸招人嫌的阴沉。他走到离大卫书桌约莫十英尺处站住，同伴则守在门边。大卫默不作声地打量他。

「今儿早上俺收到这张通知，」那人说，「说俺的期票明儿到期，得付。」

「这个嘛，」大卫说，胳膊搭在椅背上，左手按着桌面，「是这么回事，不是吗？」

「兴许是，」那家伙答，「到期是到期；付钱那一节，另说。」

「你打算续期？」大卫微微前倾，问。

「不，」那人凉凉地说，「续到哪个日子俺看都免了；俺瞅它就照现在这样再跑一阵子得啦。」约翰看了看迪克·拉腊比。他正一脸极大的受用，盯着大卫的脸看。大卫把椅子又往右拧了半圈，脱出书桌。

「你瞅它还能接着跑，是吧？」他和和气气地问。「你在这个题目上的高见，俺领教了。这个嘛，俺看也能——跑到*明天*下午四点；往后，你同约翰逊律师或者县治安官结账去。」那人寲笑一声。

「俺瞅你讨账，够你费点脑子。」他说。哈勒姆先生两丛浓密的红眉毛在鼻梁上撞了头。

「听着，比尔·蒙泰格（Bill Montaig），」他说，「这桩事，俺知道的比你以为的多。俺知道你四处吹牛，说这回要摆俺一道。你打的算盘：先告俺放高利贷；那一手不成，俺横竖也执行不着你一根毛。是这么回事，不是吗？」

「差不多就这个谱。」蒙泰格说，两脚又叉开些。大卫已经离了座。

「当初你哭哭啼啼上这儿来求那笔钱的时候，可不是这个口气，」他接着说，「俺估摸这案子里有几样事实，打那以后从你脑子里滑出去了；俺看，还是俺来给你提个醒。」

===Original===

It was plain from the expression of Mr. Montaig’s countenance that his confidence in the strength of his position was not quite so assured as at first, but he maintained his attitude as well as in him lay.
“In the fust place,” David began his assault, “*I* didn’t *lend* ye the money. I borr’ed it for ye on my endorsement, an’ charged ye fer doin’ it, as I told ye at the time; an’ another thing that you appear to forgit is that you signed a paper statin’ that you was wuth, in good and available pusson’ls, free an’ clear, over five hunderd dollars, an’ that the statement was made to me with the view of havin’ me endorse your note fer one-fifty. Rec’lect that?” David smiled grimly at the look of disconcert which, in spite of himself, appeared in Bill’s face.
“I don’t remember signin’ no paper,” he said doggedly.
“Jest as like as not,” remarked Mr. Harum. “What *you* was thinkin’ of about that time was gittin’ that *money*.”
“I’d like to see that paper,” said Bill, with a pretence of incredulity.
“You’ll see it when the time comes,” asserted David, with an emphatic nod. He squared himself, planting his feet apart, and, thrusting his hands deep in his coat pockets, faced the discomfited yokel.
“Do you think, Bill Montaig,” he said, with measureless contempt, “that I didn’t know who I was dealin’ with? that I didn’t know what a low-lived, roost-robbin’ skunk you was? an’ didn’t know how to protect myself agin such an’muls as you be? Wa’al, I did, an’ don’t you stop thinkin’ ’bout it﻿—an’,” he added, shaking his finger at the object of his scorn, “*you’ll pay that note* or I’ll put ye where the dogs won’t bite ye,” and with that he turned on his heel and resumed his seat. Bill stood for a minute with a scowl of rage and defeat in his lowering face.
“Got any further bus’nis with me?” inquired Mr. Harum. “Anythin’ more ’t I c’n oblige ye about?” There was no answer.
“I asked you,” said David, raising his voice and rising to his feet, “if you had any further bus’nis with me.”
“I dunno’s I have,” was the sullen response.
“All right,” said David. “That bein’ the case, an’ as I’ve got somethin’ to do beside wastin’ my time on such wuthless pups as you be, I’ll thank you to git out. There’s the door,” he added, pointing to it.
“He, he, he, he, ho, ho, ha, h-o-o-o-o-o!” came from the throat of Dick Larrabee.

===Chinese===

看蒙泰格先生的脸色就知道，他对自己阵脚的信心已不如开场那么足；可他还是竭力把架子端着。

「头一件，」大卫开攻，「那笔钱不是*俺*借给你的。是俺拿自己的背书替你转借来的，转手钱是另算的——当时就同你讲了。再一件、你像是忘了的：你签过一张字据，写明你的动产家当，实打实、可变现、无抵押，值五百块开外；那张字据，就是冲着要俺替你那一百五十的期票背书才写的。记得吗？」比尔脸上不由自主透出的慌乱，让大卫狩笑。

「俺不记得签过什么字据。」他死犟着说。

「八成是。」哈勒姆先生说。「那阵子你满脑子想的，是到手那笔*钱*。」

「俺倒想瞧瞧那张纸。」比尔说，装出一副不信。

「到日子自会叫你看。」大卫断然一点头。他站定，两脚分开，双手深深插进大衣口袋，正对着这个败了阵的土棍。

「你以为，比尔·蒙泰格，」他说，轻蔑得没有底，「俺不知道自己在同什么东西打交道？不知道你这个下三滥、掏鸡窝的臭鼬是什么货色？不知道怎么防你这路畜生？俺全知道，你给俺想着点——还有，」他朝他鄙夷的对象点了点手指，「*那张票子你付定了*；不付，俺把你搁到狗都咬不着的地方去。」说罢他脚跟一转，归了座。比尔立了一分钟，阴沉的脸上一副又怒又败的凶相。

「同俺还有别的买卖？」哈勒姆先生问。「还有什么要俺效劳的？」没有回答。

「俺问你，」大卫提高嗓门、站起身，「同俺还有没有别的买卖。」

「没啥了。」回答阴沉沉的。

「好。」大卫说。「既然如此，俺的时间除了浪费在你这路不值钱的狗崽子上，还有别的用处——就劳驾，滚吧。门在那儿。」他一指。

「嘿、嘿、嘿、嘿、嚯、嚯、哈、嚯————！」这一串从迪克·拉腊比的喉咙里滚出来。

===Original===

This was too much for the exasperated Bill, and he erred (to put it mildly) in raising his arm and advancing a step toward his creditor. He was not swift enough to take the second, however, for David, with amazing quickness, sprang upon him, and twisting him around, rushed him out of the door, down the passage, and out of the front door, which was obligingly held open by an outgoing client, who took in the situation and gave precedence to Mr. Montaig. His companion, who so far had taken no part, made a motion to interfere, but John, who stood nearest to him, caught him by the collar and jerked him back, with the suggestion that it would be better to let the two have it out by themselves. David came back rather breathless and very red in the face, but evidently in exceeding good humor.
“Scat my —﻿—﻿—!” he exclaimed. “Hain’t had such a good tussle I dunno when.”
“Bill’s considered ruther an awk’ard customer,” remarked Dick. “I guess he hain’t had no such handlin’ fer quite a while.”
“Sho!” exclaimed Mr. Harum. “The’ ain’t nothin’ to him but wind an’ meanness. Who was that feller with him?”
“Name ’s Smith, I believe,” replied Dick. “Guess Bill brought him along fer a witness, an’ I reckon he seen all he wanted to. I’ll bet *his* neck’s achin’ some,” added Mr. Larrabee with a laugh.
“How’s that?” asked David.
“Well, he made a move to tackle you as you was escortin’ Bill out, an’ Mr. Lenox there caught him in the collar an’ gin him a jerk that’d ’a’ landed him on his back,” said Dick, “if,” turning to John, “you hadn’t helt holt of him. You putty nigh broke his neck. He went off﻿—he, he, he, he, ho!﻿—wrigglin’ it to make sure.”
“I used more force than was necessary, I’m afraid,” said Billy Williams’s pupil, “but there wasn’t much time to calculate.”
“Much obliged,” said David with a nod.
“Not at all,” protested John, laughing. “I have enjoyed a great deal this morning.”
“It *has* ben ruther pleasant,” remarked David with a chuckle, “but you mustn’t cal’late on havin’ such fun ev’ry mornin’.”
John went into the business office, leaving the banker and Dick.
“Say,” said the latter when they were alone, “that young man o’ your’n ’s quite a feller. He took care o’ that big Smith chap with one hand; an’ say, *you* c’n git round on your pins ’bout ’s lively ’s they make ’em, I guess. I swan!”

===Chinese===

这一下对恼羞成怒的比尔就太过分了，于是他犯了个错（往轻里说）——抬起胳膊，朝债主逼近一步。第二步他却没来得及迈——大卫以惊人的迅捷扑了上去，把他拧了个转，一路轰出房门、穿过过道、送出大门；大门恰有一位出门的主顾好心拉着——人家看明白了局面，礼让蒙泰格先生先行。他那位至今没参与的同伴，做了个要插手的手势，被站得最近的约翰一把攋住领子拽了回来，附赠一句建议：二位的事，还是让他们自己了断的好。大卫回来了，微微带喘，脸红红的，兴致却显然高到了顶。

「去他的———！」他喊。「这么痛快的摔跤，多久没闹过了，俺都记不清。」

「比尔公认是个难缠的主。」迪克说。「这般伺候，他怕是好久没消受过。」

「嗐！」哈勒姆先生喊。「他浑身上下就是一股子气加一肚子坏。跟他来那位是谁？」

「姓史密斯，俺记得。」迪克答。「比尔带他来当见证的；俺估摸他想看的都看全了。俺打赌*他*那脖子这会儿正疼着呢。」拉腊比先生笑着添一句。

「怎么讲？」大卫问。

「呢，你押着比尔往外走的时候，他伸手要招呼你，伦诺克斯先生在那儿一把逮住他领子，那一拽的力道，本来能叫他仰面朝天，」迪克说着转向约翰，「亏得你没撒手。你差点拧断他脖子。他走的时候——嘿、嘿、嘿、嚯！——一路转着脖子，验验还在不在。」

「只怕我手重了些，」比利·威廉姆斯的弟子说，「只是当时没工夫细算。」

「多谢。」大卫点点头说。

「哪里的话。」约翰笑着推辞，「今天上午我痛快得很。」

「确实*相当*愉快。」大卫咯咯笑道，「可别指望天天上午都有这样的乐子。」

约翰进了营业室，把银行家和迪克留在后屋。

「我说，」只剩他俩时，迪克开口，「你那年轻人真有俩下子。大个子史密斯那一下，他一只手就料理了；还有，*你老*这两条腿，也利索得数一数二了，俺看。俺的天！」

===Original===

he exclaimed, slapping his thigh and shaking with laughter, “the hull thing head-an’-shouldered any show I seen lately.” And then for a while they fell to talking of the “sorril colt” and other things.

===Chinese===

他一拍大腿，笑得直颤，喊道：「整场戏，最近俺看的哪一出都望尘莫及。」随后两人聊了一阵「沙毛小马」和别的。

## XIX / 十九
===Original===

Mrs. Cullom was sitting at one corner of the fire, and David drew a chair opposite to her.
“Feelin’ all right now? whisky hain’t made ye liable to no disorderly conduct, has it?” he asked with a laugh.
“Yes, thank you,” was the reply, “the warm things are real comfortin’, ’n’ I guess I hain’t had licker enough to make me want to throw things. You got a kind streak in ye, Dave Harum, if you did send me this here note﻿—but I s’pose ye know your own bus’nis,” she added with a sigh of resignation. “I ben fearin’ fer a good while ’t I couldn’t hold on t’ that prop’ty, an’ I don’t know but what you might’s well git it as ’Zeke Swinney, though I ben hopin’ ’gainst hope that Charley ’d be able to do more ’n he has.”
“Let’s see the note,” said David curtly. “H’m, humph, ‘regret to say that I have been instructed by Mr. Harum’﻿—wa’al, h’m’m, cal’lated to clear his own skirts anyway﻿—h’m’m﻿—‘must be closed up without further delay’ (John’s eye caught the little white stocking which still lay on his desk)﻿—wa’al, yes, that’s about what I told Mr. Lenox to say fur’s the bus’nis part’s concerned﻿—I might ’a’ done my own regrettin’ if I’d wrote the note myself.” (John said something to himself.) “ ’Tain’t the pleasantest thing in the world fer ye, I allow, but then you see, bus’nis is bus’nis.”
John heard David clear his throat, and there was a hiss in the open fire. Mrs. Cullom was silent, and David resumed:
“You see, Mis’ Cullom, it’s like this. I ben thinkin’ of this matter fer a good while. That place ain’t ben no real good to ye sence the first year you signed that morgidge. You hain’t scurcely more’n made ends meet, let alone the int’rist, an’ it’s ben simply a question o’ time, an’ who’d git the prop’ty in the long run fer some years. I reckoned, same as you did, that Charley ’d mebbe come to the front﻿—but he hain’t done it, an’ ’tain’t likely he ever will. Charley’s a likely ’nough boy some ways, but he hain’t got much ‘git there’ in his makeup, not more’n enough fer one anyhow, I reckon. That’s about the size on’t, ain’t it?”
Mrs. Cullom murmured a feeble admission that she was “ ’fraid it was.”
“Wa’al,” resumed Mr. Harum, “I see how things was goin’, an’ I see that unless I played euchre, ’Zeke Swinney ’d git that prop’ty, an’ whether I wanted it myself or not, I didn’t cal’late he sh’d git it anyway. He put a spoke in my wheel once, an’ I hain’t forgot it.

===Chinese===

卡洛姆（Cullom）太太坐在炉火的一角，大卫在她对面拉过一把椅子。
「这会儿缓过来了？威士忌没叫您闹出什么失体统吧？」他笑着问。
「好了，谢谢，」回答是，「热乎东西真受用；我猜我那点酒还到不了摔东西的份上。你心眼里有一副好心肠，戴夫·哈勒姆，虽说你打发来的是这张条子——不过你自己的生意你做主，」她认命地叹口气，添了一句。「我怕了好一阵子，怕那产业保不住；既然保不住，落在你手里也不比落在齐克·斯温尼（Zeke Swinney）手里差——虽说我也一直死马当活马盼着，盼查利能多顶点事。」
「条子我看看，」大卫简短地说。「嗯，哼，「很遗憾地奉告，哈勒姆先生吩咐我」——嗯，这个嘛，横竖先把自己撇清——嗯——「必须即刻了结，不得再延」（约翰的眼睛瞟见桌上还躺着那只白袜子）——嗯，是，生意那一面，我嘱咐伦诺克斯先生的大概就是这些——这便条要是我自己写，遗憾也归我自己遗憾。」（约翰心里回了一句什么。）「对您说，这不是天下顶好受的事，我认账；可是您瞧，生意到底是生意。」
约翰听见大卫清了清嗓子，敞炉里嗞的一响。卡洛姆太太不响；大卫接着说：
「您瞧，卡洛姆太太，事情是这样。这件事我想了有日子了。打您头一年签了那张抵押起，那块地就没真给过您什么好处。您勉强糊口，利息更谈不上；这几年，无非是早晚的事——产业到头来落谁手里的事。我原先同您一样盘算，想着查利兴许能出头——他没出，看这样子也出不了。查利这孩子有几样不赖，可他骨子里那股『闯劲』不多——我估摸，供一个人用都不大够。事情就这么大，是不是？」
卡洛姆太太有气无力地咕哝了一句，承认「怕是这样」。
「这个嘛，」哈勒姆先生接下去，「我看清了路数：我要不打一手尤克，那产业就归齐克·斯温尼了；我自己要不要它是一回事，反正不能叫他得。他坏过我一回道，我没忘。

===Original===

But that hain’t neither here nor there. Wa’al,” after a short pause, “you know I helped ye pull the thing along on the chance, as ye may say, that you an’ your son ’d somehow make a go on’t.”
“You ben very kind, so fur,” said the widow faintly.
“Don’t ye say that, don’t ye say that,” protested David. “ ’T wa’n’t no kindness. It was jest bus’nis: I wa’n’t takin’ no chances, an’ I s’pose I might let the thing run a spell longer if I c’d see any use in’t. But the’ ain’t, an’ so I ast ye to come up this mornin’ so ’t we c’d settle the thing up without no fuss, nor trouble, nor lawyer’s fees, nor nothin’. I’ve got the papers all drawed, an’ John﻿—Mr. Lenox﻿—here to take the acknowlidgments. You hain’t no objection to windin’ the thing up this mornin’, have ye?”
“I s’pose I’ll have to do whatever you say,” replied the poor woman in a tone of hopeless discouragement, “an’ I might as well be killed to once, as to die by inch pieces.”
“All right then,” said David cheerfully, ignoring her lethal suggestion, “but before we git down to bus’nis an’ signin’ papers, an’ in order to set myself in as fair a light ’s I can in the matter, I want to tell ye a little story.”
“I hain’t no objection ’s I know of,” acquiesced the widow graciously.
“All right,” said David, “I won’t preach more ’n about up to the sixthly﻿—How’d you feel if I was to light up a cigar? I hain’t much of a hand at a yarn, an’ if I git stuck, I c’n puff a spell. Thank ye. Wa’al, Mis’ Cullom, you used to know somethin’ about my folks. I was raised on Buxton Hill. The’ was nine on us, an’ I was the youngest o’ the lot. My father farmed a piece of about forty to fifty acres, an’ had a small shop where he done odd times small jobs of tinkerin’ fer the neighbors when the’ was anythin’ to do. My mother was his second, an’ I was the only child of that marriage. He married agin when I was about two year old, an’ how I ever got raised ’s more ’n I c’n tell ye. My sister Polly was ’sponsible more ’n anyone, I guess, an’ the only one o’ the whole lot that ever gin me a decent word. Small farmin’ ain’t cal’lated to fetch out the best traits of human nature﻿—an’ keep ’em out﻿—an’ it seems to me sometimes that when the old man wa’n’t cuffin’ my ears he was lickin’ me with a rawhide or a strap. Fur ’s that was concerned, all his boys used to ketch it putty reg’lar till they got too big.

===Chinese===

可那不相干。这个嘛，」略停一停，「您知道，我一直帮您把这件事往前拖，指望着——这么说吧——您同您儿子兴许能闯出个名堂。」
「你一直很照应，到如今，」寡妇轻轻说。
「快别这样说，快别，」大卫拦住。「那算不得照应，就是生意：我冒的险不大；要能看出个用处来，兴许还由它再拖一阵。看不出，所以我今儿请您上来，好把这事了结了——不吵不闹，不劳神，不出律师费，什么都不出。文书我全备好了，约翰——伦诺克斯先生——就在这儿，代收签字画押。今儿上午把这事收个尾，您没意见吧？」
「我想，你说怎么办就怎么办吧，」可怜的女人回答，声调里一片绝望，「长痛不如短痛。」
「那好，」大卫高高兴兴地说，不理会她那句要命的话，「不过，在办正事、签文书之前——为着把自己在这件事里的面目摆得尽量周正——我要给您讲个小故事。」
「我没意见，」寡妇温顺地应承。
「好，」大卫说，「讲道我不讲过第六条去——我点上支雪茄您介不介意？我不算会讲故事的人，卡了壳还能喷两口。多谢。这个嘛，卡洛姆太太，我家里的情形您从前知道一些。我是在巴克斯顿山（Buxton Hill）上长大的。家里九个，我顶小。我父亲种着四五十英亩一块地，还开着间小铺子，闲时替邻居们做点补锅锔碗的小活，有活的时候。我母亲是他的续弦，那一头就生了我一个。我两岁上下他又续了弦，我怎么活下来的，我说不清。我姐姐波莉担待得最多，我猜——满家老小，也只有她给过我一句人话。小田庄这行当，养不出人性里最好的几样——倒养得出最坏的几样——有时我想，老头子不拧我耳朵，就是拿生皮条抽我。那一层不提，他那些儿子个个挨打，一直挨到个头大得打不动。

===Original===

One on ’em up an’ licked him one night, an’ lit out next day. I s’pose the old man’s disposition was sp’iled by what some feller said farmin’ was, ‘workin’ all day, an’ doin’ chores all night,’ an’ larrupin’ me an’ all the rest on us was about all the enjoyment he got. My brothers an’ sisters﻿—‘ceptin’ of Polly﻿—was putty nigh as bad in respect of cuffs an’ suchlike; an’ my step-marm was, on the hull, the wust of all. She hadn’t no childern o’ her own, an’ it appeared ’s if I was jest pizen to her. ’T wa’n’t so much slappin’ an’ cuffin’ with her as ’twas tongue. She c’d say things that ’d jest raise a blister like pizen ivy. I s’pose I *was* about as ord’nary, no-account-lookin’, redheaded, freckled little cuss as you ever see, an’ slinkin’ in my manners. The air of our home circle wa’n’t cal’lated to raise heroes in.
“I got three four years’ schoolin’, an’ made out to read an’ write an’ cipher up to long division ’fore I got through, but after I got to be six year old, school or no school, I had to work reg’lar at anything I had strength fer, an’ more too. Chores before school an’ after school, an’ a two-mile walk to git there. As fur ’s clo’es was concerned, any old thing that ’d hang together was good enough fer me; but by the time the older boys had outgrowed their duds, an’ they was passed on to me, the’ wa’n’t much left on ’em. A pair of old cowhide boots that leaked in more snow an’ water ’n they kept out, an’ a couple pairs of woolen socks that was putty much all darns, was expected to see me through the winter, an’ I went barefoot f’m the time the snow was off the ground till it flew agin in the fall. The’ wa’n’t but two seasons o’ the year with me﻿—them of chilblains an’ stun-bruises.”
The speaker paused and stared for a moment into the comfortable glow of the fire, and then discovering to his apparent surprise that his cigar had gone out, lighted it from a coal picked out with the tongs.
“Farmin’ ’s a hard life,” remarked Mrs. Cullom with an air of being expected to make some contribution to the conversation.
“An’ yit, as it seems to me as I look back on’t,” David resumed pensively, “the wust on’t was that nobody ever gin me a kind word, ’cept Polly.

===Chinese===

有一个晚上把他撂倒，第二天跑了。我猜老头的性子也叫人毁了——照某位伙计的说法，种地就是『白天干活，夜里打杂』；抽我、抽我们一伙，差不多算他全部的消遣。我的哥哥姐姐——除了波莉——抡巴掌那一门也个个不弱；后娘又是全伙里顶狠的。她自己没孩子，我于她简直是一剂毒。她还不大动手，全凭一条舌头。她说出来的话，能起燎泡，同毒葛一样。我估摸我*也*真是个再平常不过、没出息、红头发、满脸雀斑的小猴崽子，举止缩头缩脑。我们那个家里的空气，本就养不出英雄。
「我上了三四年学，末了读写都会，算术学到长除法；可打六岁起，管它上不上学，凡是我力气够得着的活计，我全得按点上，还得添头。上学前、放学后的杂活，上学两英里地走着去。衣裳嘛，凡是挂得住的破烂给我穿都算好；等哥哥们穿剩传到我，上头剩不下什么了。一双老牛皮靴，进雪进水比挡的多；两双毛袜，差不多全是补丁——就指望这两样过冬；开春雪一化，到秋头雪再飘，我一路光脚。我一年只有两季——冻疮一季，石头碰伤一季。」
讲话人停住，对着炉火暖融融的光出了一会儿神；随后像才发现雪茄灭了似的，拿火钳夹起一块炭点上。
「种地是苦，」卡洛姆太太搭了一句，一副人家指望她贡献谈话的气派。
「可是，如今回头想，」大卫幽幽地接着说，「最苦的还不是这个：是除波莉之外，没谁给过我一句好话。

===Original===

I s’pose I got kind o’ used to bein’ cold an’ tired; dressin’ in a snowdrift where it blowed into the attic, an’ goin’ out to fodder cattle ’fore sunup; pickin’ up stun in the blazin’ sun, an’ doin’ all the odd jobs my father set me to, an’ the older ones shirked onto me. That was the reg’lar order o’ things; but I remember I never *did* git used to never pleasin’ nobody. ’Course I didn’t expect nothin’ f’m my step-marm, an’ the only way I ever knowed I’d done my stent fur ’s father was concerned, was that he didn’t say nothin’. But sometimes the older ones ’d git settin’ ’round, talkin’ an’ laughin’, havin’ popcorn an’ apples, an’ that, an’ I’d kind o’ sidle up, wantin’ to join ’em, an’ some on ’em ’d say, ‘What *you* doin’ here? time you was in bed,’ an’ give me a shove or a cuff. Yes, ma’am,” looking up at Mrs. Cullom, “the wust on’t was that I was kind o’ scairt the hull time. Once in a while Polly ’d give me a mossel o’ comfort, but Polly wa’n’t but little older ’n me, an’ bein’ the youngest girl, was chored most to death herself.”
It had stopped snowing, and though the wind still came in gusty blasts, whirling the drift against the windows, a wintry gleam of sunshine came in and touched the widow’s wrinkled face.
“It’s amazin’ how much trouble an’ sorrer the’ is in the world, an’ how soon it begins,” she remarked, moving a little to avoid the sunlight. “I hain’t never ben able to reconcile how many good things the’ be, an’ how little most on us gits o’ them. I hain’t ben to meetin’ fer a long spell ’cause I hain’t had no fit clo’es, but I remember most of the preachin’ I’ve set under either dwelt on the wrath to come, or else on the Lord’s doin’ all things well, an’ providin’. I hope I ain’t no wickeder ’n than the gen’ral run, but it’s putty hard to hev faith in the Lord’s providin’ when you hain’t got nothin’ in the house but corn meal, an’ none too much o’ that.”
“That’s so, Mis’ Cullom, that’s so,” affirmed David. “I don’t blame ye a mite. ‘Doubts assail, an’ oft prevail,’ as the hymnbook says, an’ I reckon it’s a sight easier to have faith on meat an’ potatoes ’n it is on corn meal mush. Wa’al, as I was sayin’﻿—I hope I ain’t tirin’ ye with my goin’s on?”
“No,” said Mrs. Cullom, “I’m engaged to hear ye, but nobody ’d suppose to see ye now that ye was such a f’lorn little critter as you make out.”

===Chinese===

挨冻、受累，我想我倒惯了：在穿堂风灌顶的阁楼雪窝里穿衣裳，天不亮出去喂牛；大太阳底下捡石头，父亲派的零活，哥哥们推给我的——一切照章办事。可我记得，怎么也惯不了的是：做什么都不讨人好。后娘那里我本来就不指望；父亲那边，我那一份工完没完，我知道的惟一法子，是他没言语。可有几年，大的们围着坐，又说又笑，爆玉米花啊苹果啊什么的；我蹭蹭挨挨凑过去，想搭个伙，哪一个就说了：『*你*在这儿干什么？该上床了』，一搡，或一巴掌。是，太太，」他抬眼看卡洛姆太太，「最苦的是我从头到尾心惊肉跳。偶尔波莉给我一口安慰，可波莉大不了我多少，又是顶小的姑娘，自己叫杂活差一点儿使死。」
雪停了；风还是一阵阵刮，把雪卷子卷上窗玻璃；一线冬日的亮光照进来，落在寡妇打皱的脸上。
「这世上苦难真多，来得真早，」她挪了挪躲开日头，感慨说。「天下好东西那么多，我们大多数人到手的那么少——这一层我从来想不拢。我做礼拜断了些日子了——没件像样的衣裳——可我记得，我听过的布道，不是讲将来的震怒，就是讲上主凡事安排得好、有预备。我不敢说自己比一般人更坏；可屋里除了玉米面、还不见得管够的时候，要人信『上主有预备』，实在难。」
「正是，卡洛姆太太，正是，」大卫肯定她。「我一星半点不怪您。『疑惑来袭，屡屡得胜』——赞美诗上说的；我估摸，就着肉和土豆生信心，比就着玉米面糊容易得多。这个嘛，方才说到哪儿了——我这絮叨没叫您乏吧？」
「没有，」卡洛姆太太说，「我听着呢。只是看您如今的模样，谁也想不到您当年是那么个可怜见的小家伙。」

===Original===

“It’s jest as I’m tellin’ ye, an’ more also, as the Bible says,” returned David, and then, rather more impressively, as if he were leading up to his conclusion, “it come along to a time when I was ’twixt thirteen an’ fourteen. The’ was a cirkis billed to show down here in Homeville, an’ ev’ry barn an’ shed fer miles around had pictures stuck onto ’em of el’phants, an’ rhinoceroses, an’ ev’ry animul that went into the ark; an’ girls ridin’ bareback an’ jumpin’ through hoops, an’ fellers ridin’ bareback an’ turnin’ summersets, an’ doin’ turnovers on swings; an’ clowns gettin’ hoss-whipped, an’ ev’ry kind of a thing that could be pictered out; an’ how the’ was to be a grand percession at ten o’clock, ’ith golden chariots, an’ scripteral allegories, an’ the hull bus’nis; an’ the gran’ performance at two o’clock; admission twenty-five cents, children under twelve, at cetery, an’ so forth. Wa’al, I hadn’t no more idee o’ goin’ to that cirkis ’n I had o’ flyin’ to the moon, but the night before the show somethin’ waked me ’bout twelve o’clock. I don’t know how ’twas. I’d ben helpin’ mend fence all day, an’ gen’ally I never knowed nothin’ after my head struck the bed till mornin’. But that night, anyhow, somethin’ waked me, an’ I went an’ looked out the windo’, an’ there was the hull thing goin’ by the house. The’ was more or less moon, an’ I see the el’phant, an’ the big wagins﻿—the drivers kind o’ noddin’ over the dashboards﻿—an’ the chariots with canvas covers﻿—I don’t know how many of ’em﻿—an’ the cages of the tigers an’ lions, an’ all. Wa’al, I got up the next mornin’ at sunup an’ done my chores; an’ after breakfust I set off fer the ten-acre lot where I was mendin’ fence. The ten-acre was the farthest off of any, Homeville way, an’ I had my dinner in a tin pail so’t I needn’t lose no time goin’ home at noon, an’, as luck would have it, the’ wa’n’t nobody with me that mornin’. Wa’al, I got down to the lot an’ set to work; but somehow I couldn’t git that show out o’ my head nohow. As I said, I hadn’t no more notion of goin’ to that cirkis ’n I had of kingdom come. I’d never had two shillin’ of my own in my hull life. But the more I thought on’t the uneasier I got. Somethin’ seemed pullin’ an’ haulin’ at me, an’ fin’ly I gin in. I allowed I’d see that percession anyway if it took a leg, an’ mebbe I c’d git back ’ithout nobody missin’ me.

===Chinese===

「我说的句句是实，只多不少——像《圣经》说的，」大卫答；随后口气郑重了些，像朝结论引去——「事情就到了我十三四岁那当口。有马戏班子贴出告示，要来霍姆维尔演出；方圆几里的谷仓棚子全贴满了画：大象呀，犀牛呀，进过方舟的各样畜生呀；姑娘们光着背骑马、钻圈呀；小伙们光背骑马、翻筋斗、荡秋千上翻跟头呀；小丑挨马鞭呀——凡画得出的名堂应有尽有；还有十点钟的大游行，金色的战车呀，《圣经》寓言呀，全套；两点开演；门票两毛五，十二岁以下儿童云云，等等。这个嘛，去看那场马戏，我的念头还赶不上飞上月亮。可开演前一夜，十二点来钟，什么东西把我弄醒了。怎么醒的我说不清——我帮着修了一整天篱笆，往常头一挨枕就死到天亮。可那晚反正醒了，我下床朝窗外一望：整副人马正打屋前过。月色不大不小；我望见大象、大板车——赶车的伏在车台上打盹——蒙着帆布篷的战车，不知多少辆——老虎狮子笼，全伙。这个嘛，第二天天一亮我起来干完杂活；早饭一过就奔十里那块地——我修篱笆的地。十里地离家顶远，朝着霍姆维尔这边；午饭装在白铁桶里，省得晌午来回跑；也是运气，那天早晨没人跟我一块儿。到了地头我就干上了；可那场戏在我脑子里怎么也撵不走。我说过，去看马戏的念头，我不比升天多。我这辈子连两先令属于自己的钱都没见过。可越想心越野，有个东西又拉又拽，末了我缴械了：游行说什么也要看——豁出一条腿也要看——兴许还能赶回来，神不知鬼不觉。

===Original===

’T any rate, I’d take the chances of a lickin’ jest once﻿—fer that’s what it meant﻿—an’ I up an’ put fer the village lickity-cut. I done them four mile lively, I c’n tell ye, an’ the stun-bruises never hurt me once.
“When I got down to the village it seemed to me as if the hull population of Freeland County was there. I’d never seen so many folks together in my life, an’ fer a spell it seemed to me as if ev’rybody was a-lookin’ at me an’ sayin’, ‘That’s old Harum’s boy Dave, playin’ hookey,’ an’ I sneaked ’round dreadin’ somebody ’d give me away; but I fin’ly found that nobody wa’n’t payin’ any attention to me﻿—they was there to see the show, an’ one redheaded boy more or less wa’n’t no pertic’ler account. Wa’al, putty soon the percession hove in sight, an’ the’ was a reg’lar stampede among the boys, an’ when it got by, I run an’ ketched up with it agin, an’ walked alongside the el’phant, tin pail an’ all, till they fetched up inside the tent. Then I went off to one side﻿—it must ’a’ ben about ’leven or half-past, an’ eat my dinner﻿—I had a devourin’ appetite﻿—an’ thought I’d jest walk round a spell, an’ then light out fer home. But the’ was so many things to see an’ hear﻿—all the sideshow pictures of Fat Women, an’ Livin’ Skelitons; an’ Wild Women of Madygasker, an’ Wild Men of Borneo; an’ snakes windin’ round women’s necks; hand-orgins; fellers that played the ’cordion, an’ mouth-pipes, an’ drum an’ cymbals all to once, an’ suchlike﻿—that I fergot all about the time an’ the ten-acre lot, an’ the stun fence, an’ fust I knowed the folks was makin’ fer the ticket wagin, an’ the band begun to play inside the tent. Be I taxin’ your patience over the limit?” said David, breaking off in his story and addressing Mrs. Cullom more directly.
“No, I guess not,” she replied; “I was jest thinkin’ of a circus I went to once,” she added with an audible sigh.
“Wa’al,” said David, taking a last farewell of the end of his cigar, which he threw into the grate, “mebbe what’s comin’ ’ll int’rest ye more ’n the rest on’t has. I was standin’ gawpin’ ’round, list’nin’ to the band an’ watchin’ the folks git their tickets, when all of a suddin I felt a twitch at my hair﻿—it had a way of workin’ out of the holes in my old chip straw hat﻿—an’ somebody says to me, ‘Wa’al, sonny, what you thinkin’ of?’ he says. I looked up, an’ who do you s’pose it was? It was Billy P. Cullom!

===Chinese===

大不了挨一顿抽——反正就是这个——我撒腿就往村里跑，一路飞快。那四英里我跑得欢，告诉你，脚跟的石头伤一点儿不碍事。
「到了村里，我觉得整个弗里兰县的人全到了。我生平没见过这么多人在一处；有一阵，人人都像在看我、说：『这是老哈勒姆家的小戴夫，逃学呢。』我溜着边走，生怕有人告发；后来发现根本没人理会我——人家是来看戏的，多一个少一个红毛小子，算不得什么。这个嘛，不多时游行过来了，孩子们一阵疯抢；队伍过完，我又追上去，跟着大象并排走，白铁桶提在手里，一直跟到他们进大棚。随后我退到一边——总有十一点或十一点半吧——吃了午饭；我胃口好得吓人——想着蹓跶一圈就撒腿回家。可要看要听的东西太多了——外场画上那些胖女人、活骷髅；马达加斯加野女人、婆罗洲野人；蛇缠在女人脖子上；手摇风琴；一个人又是手风琴、又是口琴、又是鼓和钹一齐上的家伙们——叫我忘了时辰、忘了十里地、忘了石头篱笆；一晃神，人们已经往售票车涌，大棚里乐队开了。我这絮叨，没超您耐心的份吧？」大卫把故事剎住，正对卡洛姆太太问。
「没，我想没超，」她答。「我正想起自己有一回看的马戏，」她添一句，叹一声听得见的气。
「这个嘛，」大卫同雪茄头作了最后的告别，把它扔进炉膛，「往后的兴许比前头更对您胃口。我正张着嘴东张西望，听乐队，看人买票，忽然觉着头发叫人扯了一下——我的头发有个毛病，老从旧草帽的窟窿里钻出来——有人对我说：『喂，小家伙，想什么呢？』我抬头一望——您猜是谁？是比利·P·卡洛姆（Billy P. Cullom）！

===Original===

I knowed who he was, fer I’d seen him before, but of course he didn’t know me. Yes, ma’am, it was Billy P., an’ wa’n’t he rigged out to kill!”
The speaker paused and looked into the fire, smiling. The woman started forward facing him, and clasping her hands, cried, “My husband! What’d he have on?”
“Wa’al,” said David slowly and reminiscently, “near’s I c’n remember, he had on a blue broadcloth claw-hammer coat with flat gilt buttons, an’ a double-breasted plaid velvet vest, an’ pearl-gray pants, strapped down over his boots, which was of shiny leather, an’ a high pointed collar an’ blue stock with a pin in it (I remember wonderin’ if it c’d be real gold), an’ a yeller-white plug beaver hat.”
At the description of each article of attire Mrs. Cullom nodded her head, with her eyes fixed on David’s face, and as he concluded she broke out breathlessly, “Oh, yes! Oh, yes! David, he wore them very same clo’es, an’ he took me to that very same show that very same night!” There was in her face a look almost of awe, as if a sight of her long-buried past youth had been shown to her from a coffin.
Neither spoke for a moment or two, and it was the widow who broke the silence. As David had conjectured, she was interested at last, and sat leaning forward with her hands clasped in her lap.
“Well,” she exclaimed, “ain’t ye goin’ on? What did he say to ye?”
“Cert’nly, cert’nly,” responded David, “I’ll tell ye near ’s I c’n remember, an’ I c’n remember putty near. As I told ye, I felt a twitch at my hair, an’ he said, ‘What be you thinkin’ about, sonny?’ I looked up at him, an’ looked away quick. ‘I dunno,’ I says, diggin’ my big toe into the dust; an’ then, I dunno how I got the spunk to, for I was shyer ’n a rat, ‘Guess I was thinkin’ ’bout mendin’ that fence up in the ten-acre lot’s much’s anythin’,’ I says.
“ ‘Ain’t you goin’ to the cirkis?’ he says.
“ ‘I hain’t got no money to go to cirkises,’ I says, rubbin’ the dusty toes o’ one foot over t’ other, ‘nor nothin’ else,’ I says.
“ ‘Wa’al,’ he says, ‘why don’t you crawl under the canvas?’
“That kind o’ riled me, shy ’s I was. ‘I don’t crawl under no canvases,’ I says. ‘If I can’t go in same ’s other folks, I’ll stay out,’ I says, lookin’ square at him fer the fust time. He wa’n’t exac’ly smilin’, but the’ was a look in his eyes that was the next thing to it.”
“Lordy me!” sighed Mrs. Cullom, as if to herself.

===Chinese===

他我认得，先前见过；他当然不认得我。是，太太，正是比利·P.；那一身，可讲究到家了！」
讲话人停住，望着炉火微笑。那女人朝前一挺，正对着他，双手一攢，喊出来：「我的丈夫！他穿的什么？」
「这个嘛，」大卫慢悠悠、回着味儿说，「凭我记的：一件蓝色呢料的燕尾服，平的金扣子；一件双排扣格子呢的马甲；珠灰色的裤子，用带子绊进靴筒——靴子黑亮黑亮；一条高尖领，蓝领巾上别一根别针（我记得我还嘀咕过是不是真金）；一顶黄白色的长毛丝礼帽。」
每报到一件，卡洛姆太太就点一下头，眼睛钉在大卫脸上；报完，她气都喘不匀地喊出来：「哦，是！哦，是！大卫，他穿的就是这一身，当天夜里，他带我去看的就是那同一场！」她脸上近乎一种敬畏，仿佛她埋了半辈子的青春，从一口棺材里给她看了一眼。
两人有一两息没言语；先开口的还是寡妇。正如大卫所料的，她这会儿来劲了，前倾着坐，两手扣在膝头。
「后来呢，」她催，「怎么不讲？他跟你说什么来着？」
「讲，讲，」大卫应道，「我记得多少讲多少——我记得差不多全。方才说了，我觉着头发叫人一扯，他说：『想什么呢，小家伙？』我抬头看他，赶紧挪开眼。『不知道，』我说，大脚趾往土里抠；跟着——哪来的胆子，我天生怕得同耗子一样——『猜我顶想的是十里地那块篱笆，』我说。
「『你不看马戏去？』他说。
「『我看马戏没那个钱，』我说，一只脏脚在另一只脚背上蹭，『别的也没有，』我说。
「『这个嘛，』他说，『你怎么不钻帐篷底？』
「这话把我惹毛了——我那样怕生。『我不钻帐篷底，』我说。『进不去人家怎么进，我就不进，』我说——头一回正眼看他。他不算是笑；可他眼睛里那副神气，离笑只差一层。」
「老天爷！」卡洛姆太太叹了一声，像自言自语。

===Original===

“How well I can remember that look; jest as if he was laughin’ at ye, an’ wa’n’t laughin’ at ye, an’ his arm around your neck!”
David nodded in reminiscent sympathy, and rubbed his bald poll with the back of his hand.
“Wa’al,” interjected the widow.
“Wa’al,” said David, resuming, “he says to me, ‘Would you like to go to the cirkis?’ an’ with that it occurred to me that I did want to go to that cirkis more’n anythin’ I ever wanted to before﻿—nor since, it seems to me. But I tell ye the truth, I was so far f’m expectin’ to go’t I really hadn’t knowed I wanted to. I looked at him, an’ then down agin, an’ began tenderin’ up a stun-bruise on one heel agin the other instep, an’ all I says was, bein’ so dum’d shy, ‘I dunno,’ I says. But I guess he seen in my face what my feelin’s was, fer he kind o’ laughed an’ pulled out half-a-dollar an’ says: ‘D’ you think you could git a couple o’ tickits in that crowd? If you kin, I think I’ll go myself, but I don’t want to git my boots all dust,’ he says. I allowed I c’d try; an’ I guess them bare feet o’ mine tore up the dust some gettin’ over to the wagin. Wa’al, I had another scare gettin’ the tickits, fer fear someone that knowed me ’d see me with a half-a-dollar, an’ think I must ’a’ stole the money. But I got ’em an’ carried ’em back to him, an’ he took ’em an’ put ’em in his vest pocket, an’ handed me a ten-cent piece, an’ says, ‘Mebbe you’ll want somethin’ in the way of refreshments fer yourself an’ mebbe the el’phant,’ he says, an’ walked off toward the tent; an’ I stood stun still, lookin’ after him. He got off about a rod or so an’ stopped an’ looked back. ‘Ain’t you comin’?’ he says.
“ ‘Be I goin’ with *you*?” I says.
“ ‘Why not?’ he says, ‘ ’nless you’d ruther go alone,’ an’ he put his finger an’ thumb into his vest pocket. Wa’al, ma’am, I looked at him a minute, with his shiny hat an’ boots, an’ fine clo’es, an’ gold pin, an’ thought of my ragged ole shirt, an’ cotton pants, an’ ole chip hat with the brim most gone, an’ my tin pail an’ all. ‘I ain’t fit to,’ I says, ready to cry﻿—an’﻿—wa’al, he jest laughed, an’ says, ‘Nonsense,’ he says, ‘come along. A man needn’t be ashamed of his workin’ clo’es,’ he says, an’ I’m dum’d if he didn’t take holt of my hand, an’ in we went that way together.”
“How like him that was!” said the widow softly.
“Yes, ma’am, yes, ma’am, I reckon it was,” said David, nodding.

===Chinese===

「那副神气我记得多清楚：像在笑你，又不像在笑你——还有他搂着你脖子的那条胳膊！」
大卫点头，回味的同情写在脸上，拿手背蹭了蹭秃顶。
「后来呢，」寡妇催。
「这个嘛，」大卫接着讲，「他对我说：『想不想看马戏？』这一问，我才明白：我想去，比这辈子想过的什么都想——回头想来，后来也没有更想的。可我说实话：先前离看那么远，连自己想看都没觉出来。我看看他，又低下头，一只脚后跟的石头伤在另一只脚背上蹭来蹭去；我那么怕生，只憋出一句：『不知道，』我说。可我想他看出了我的心思，他轻轻一笑，摸出半块钱，说：『你看你能不能从这堆人里挤两张票出来？挤得出，我就自己进去；我嫌靴子吃土，』他说。我说试试看；我估摸我那双光脚，跑过那一段，土扬得不小。这个嘛，买票又是一场怕：怕熟人看见我攢着半块钱，当那钱是偷的。票到手，我给他送回去；他接了，揣进马甲口袋，找我一个一毛的银毫子，说：『兴许你自个儿想垫补点什么，兴许大象也想，』说完朝大棚去了；我钉在原地望他的背影。他走出约莫一竿地，站住回头。『你不来？』他说。
「『我同*您*一块儿去？』我说。
「『怎么不？』他说，『除非你情愿一个人。』指头往马甲口袋里伸了伸。这个嘛，太太，我望了他一分钟：亮堂的帽子靴子，好衣裳，金别针；再想想自己破衬衫、布裤子、帽檐快掉光的旧草帽，还有那只白铁桶。『我配不上，』我说——眼看要哭——可他笑了，说：『胡说，』他说，『来吧。干活的衣裳，人不寒碜，』他说——您信不信，他一把攢住我的手，我们两个就那么手拉手进去了。」
「他行事就是这个样子！」寡妇柔声说。
「是，太太，是，正是，」大卫点头。

===Original===

“Wa’al,” he went on after a little pause, “I was ready to sink into the ground with shyniss at fust, but that wore off some after a little, an’ we two seen the hull show, I *tell* ye. We walked ’round the cages, an’ we fed the el’phant﻿—that is, he bought the stuff an’ I fed him. I ’member﻿—he, he, he!﻿—’t he says, ‘mind you git the right end,’ he says, an’ then we got a couple o’ seats, an’ the doin’s begun.”

===Chinese===

「这个嘛，」他略停一停，接着讲，「起先我臊得恨不能钻地缝；过一阵好些了；我们两个把整场戏看全了，我*告诉*你。我们绕着兽笼蹓，我们喂大象——就是说他买吃食、我来喂。我记得——嘿，嘿，嘿！——他说：『留神，认准那一头，』他说；随后我们寻了两个座，好戏开场。」

## XL / 四十
===Original===

John leaned out of the buggy and looked back along the road, as if deeply interested in observing something which had attracted his attention, and David’s face worked oddly for a moment.
Turning south in the direction of the village, they began the descent of a steep hill, and Mr. Harum, careful of loose stones, gave all his attention to his driving. Our friend, respecting his vigilance, forebore to say anything which might distract his attention until they reached level ground, and then, “You never married again?” he queried.
“No,” was, the reply. “My matrymonial experience was ‘brief an’ to the p’int,’ as the sayin’ is.”
“And yet,” urged John, “you were a young man, and I should have supposed﻿—”
“Wa’al,” said David, breaking in and emitting his chuckling laugh, “I allow ’t mebbe I sometimes thought on’t, an’ once, about ten year after what I ben tellin’ ye, I putty much made up my mind to try another hitch-up. The’ was a woman that I seen quite a good deal of, an’ liked putty well, an’ I had some grounds fer thinkin’ ’t she wouldn’t show me the door if I was to ask her. In fact, I made up my mind I would take the chances, an’ one night I put on my best bib an’ tucker an’ started fer her house. I had to go ’cross the town to where she lived, an’ the farther I walked the fiercer I got﻿—havin’ made up my mind﻿—so ’t putty soon I was travelin’ ’s if I was ’fraid some other feller’d git there ’head o’ me. Wa’al, it was Sat’day night, an’ the stores was all open, an’ the streets was full o’ people, an’ I had to pull up in the crowd a little, an’ I don’t know how it happened in pertic’ler, but fust thing I knew I run slap into a woman with a ban’box, an’ when I looked ’round, there was a mil’nery store in full blast an’ winders full o’ bunnits. Wa’al, sir, do you know what I done? Ye don’t. Wa’al, the’ was a hoss car passin’ that run three mile out in the country in a diff’rent direction f’m where I started fer, an’ I up an’ got onto that car, an’ rode the length o’ that road, an’ got off an’ *walked back*﻿—an’ I never went near her house f’m that day to this, an’ that,” said David, “was the nearest I ever come to havin’ another pardner to my joys an’ sorro’s.”
“That was pretty near, though,” said John, laughing.

===Chinese===

约翰从单马车里探出身，顺着来路回望，像是被什么吸引住了、看得入了神；大卫的脸有一阵抽动得古怪。

车头向南，朝村子方向下一段陡坡；哈勒姆先生提防着碎石，把全副心神都放在了驾车上。我们这位朋友体恤他这份专注，忍到平地才开口，没说一句会分他心的话；到了平地，他问：「后来没再娶？」

「没有。」回答是。「俺的婚姻经验，照老话说，『短而精』。」

「可是，」约翰追问，「那时你还年轻，我还以为——」

「这个嘛，」大卫插进来，发出他那咯咯的笑，「俺认了，兴许俺有时候也想过；有一回，就是俺跟你说的那档子事之后十年上下，俺差不多拿定主意，再套一回车。有位太太，俺见得不少，也挺中意，俺也有几分把柄：俺要开这个口，她不至于叫俺吃闭门奠。真格的，俺打定主意豁出去，一天晚上，俺穿上顶好的行头，动身往她家去。她住的地方得横穿全镇，俺越走火气越壮——主意已定嘛——不大会儿，俺就走成了一路小跑，像怕别的家伙抢了先。这个嘛，那天是礼拜六晚上，铺子全开着，街上人挤人，俺在人堆里不得不放慢些；具体咋闹的俺说不清，反正俺一回神，迎头撞上一位抱着帽盒的太太；再一四下看——一家女帽店灯火通明，橱窗里一水儿的帽子。这个嘛，先生，你猜俺干了啥？你猜不着。这样：正过去一辆马拉街车，往乡下开三里，同俺要去的方向满拧；俺抬腿就上了那辆车，坐到底，下来，又*一路走了回来*——打那天到今天，俺再没沾过她家的边。这个，」大卫说，「就是俺这辈子离再娶一房、再找个同甘共苦的伴儿，最近的一回。」

「这可真够近的。」约翰笑道。

===Original===

“Wa’al,” said David, “mebbe Prov’dence might ’a’ had some other plan fer stoppin’ me ’fore I smashed the hull rig, if I hadn’t run into the mil’nery shop, but as it was, that fetched me to a stan’still, an’ I never started to run agin.”
They drove on for a few minutes in silence, which John broke at last by saying, “I have been wondering how you got on after your wife died and left you with a little child.”
“That was where Mis’ Jones come in,” said David. “Of course I got the best nurse I could, an’ Mis’ Jones ’d run in two three times ev’ry day an’ see ’t things was goin’ on as right ’s they could; but it come on that I had to be away f’m home a good deal, an’ fin’ly, come fall, I got the Joneses to move into a bigger house, where I could have a room, an’ fixed it up with Mis’ Jones to take charge o’ the little feller right along. She hadn’t but one child, a girl of about thirteen, an’ had lost two little ones, an’ so between havin’ took to my little mite of a thing f’m the fust, an’ my makin’ it wuth her while, she was willin’, an’ we went on that way till﻿—the’ wa’n’t no further occasion fur ’s he was concerned, though I lived with them a spell longer when I was at home, which wa’n’t very often, an’ after he died I was gone fer a good while. But before that time, when I was at home, I had him with me all the time I could manage. With good care he’d growed up nice an’ bright, an’ as big as the average, an’ smarter ’n a steel trap. He liked bein’ with me better ’n anybody else, and when I c’d manage to have him I couldn’t bear to have him out o’ my sight. Wa’al, as I told you, he got to be most seven year old. I’d had to go out to Chicago, an’ one day I got a telegraph sayin’ he was putty sick﻿—an’ I took the fust train East. It was ’long in March, an’ we had a breakdown, an’ run into an awful snowstorm, an’ one thing another, an’ I lost twelve or fifteen hours. It seemed to me that them two days was longer ’n my hull life, but I fin’ly did git home about nine o’clock in the mornin’. When I got to the house Mis’ Jones was on the lookout fer me, an’ the door opened as I run up the stoop, an’ I see by her face that I was too late. ‘Oh, David, David!’ she says (she’d never called me David before), puttin’ her hands on my shoulders.
“ ‘When?’ I says.
“ ‘ ’Bout midnight,’ she says.
“ ‘Did he suffer much?’ I says.

===Chinese===

「这个嘛，」大卫说，「俺要不是撞进了那家帽店，老天爷兴许也备着别的法子，赶在俺把整挂车撞散之前拦下俺；不过就事实而论，那一下叫俺站住了，俺就再没起过跑。」

两人默默又走了一程，末了还是约翰开的口：「我一直在想，你太太过世、撇给你一个小娃娃，那之后你怎么过来的。」

「这就要说到琼斯太太了。」大卫说。「俺当然请了能请到的最好的奶妈，琼斯太太也一天两三趟地跑过来，看着事事都顺当；可后来赶上俺老得出门，到了秋天，俺索性请琼斯一家搬进一座大些的房子，俺也好有间屋，同琼斯太太讲定，小家伙往后就归她照管。她只有一个孩子，一个十三岁上下的闺女，还夭过两个小的；一来看俺那小不点儿打头一眼就投她的缘，二来俺也不叫她白辛苦，她便应了；我们就这样过下去，直到——就他而言，往后用不着这样了；不过俺在家的日子又同他们住了一阵——在家的时候并不多——他走后，俺出门去了好一阵子。可在那之前，只要俺在家，能带他的时候俺都带在身边。养得精心，他长得又俊又灵，个头同一般孩子齐平，脑子比捕兽夹还灵。他最爱跟着俺，胜过跟任何人；只要能带他，俺就见不得他离了俺的眼。这个嘛，同你说过的，他快满七岁那年。俺出了趟芝加哥，一天接到电报，说他病得不轻——俺搭了头一班火车往东赶。那时节正是三月光景，车又坏了，又撞上一场吓人的暴风雪，七事八事，误了十二三个钟头。那两天，在俺的感觉里，比俺这一辈子都长；末了总算到家，早上九点来钟。俺一到，琼斯太太正候着；俺跑上门阶，门应声开了，一看她的脸，俺就知道晚了。『哦，大卫，大卫！』她说（她从没叫过俺大卫），一双手按住俺的肩膀。

「『什么时候？』俺说。

「『半夜里。』她说。

「『他受罪了没有？』俺说。

===Original===

“ ‘No,’ she says, ‘I don’t think so; but he was out of his head most of the time after the fust day, an’ I guess all the time the last twenty-four hours.’
“ ‘Do you think he’d ’a’ knowed me?’ I says. ‘Did he say anythin’?’ an’ at that,” said David, “she looked at me. She wa’n’t cryin’ when I come in, though she had ben; but at that her face all broke up. ‘I don’t know,’ she says. ‘He kept sayin’ things, an’ ’bout all we could understand was “Daddy, daddy,” ’ an’ then she throwed her apern over her face, an’﻿—”
David tipped his hat a little farther over his eyes, though, like many if not most “horsey” men, he usually wore it rather far down, and leaning over, twirled the whip in the socket between his two fingers and thumb. John studied the stitched ornamentation of the dashboard until the reins were pushed into his hands. But it was not for long. David straightened himself, and, without turning his head, resumed them as if that were a matter of course.
“Day after the fun’ral,” he went on, “I says to Mis’ Jones, ‘I’m goin’ back out West,’ I says, ‘an’ I can’t say how long I shall be gone﻿—long enough, anyway,’ I says, ‘to git it into my head that when I come back the’ won’t be no little feller to jump up an’ ’round my neck when I come into the house; but, long or short, I’ll come back some time, an’ meanwhile, as fur ’s things between you an’ me air, they’re to go on jest the same, an’ more ’n that, do you think you’ll remember him some?’ I says.
“ ‘As long as I live,’ she says, ‘jes’ like my own.’
“ ‘Wa’al,’ I says, ‘long ’s you remember him, he’ll be, in a way, livin’ to ye, an’ as long ’s that I allow to pay fer his keep an’ tendin’ jes’ the same as I have, *an’*,’ I says, ‘if you don’t let me you ain’t no friend o’ mine, an’ you *ben* a *good* one.’ Wa’al, she squimmidged some, but I wouldn’t let her say ‘No.’ ‘I’ve ’ranged it all with my pardner an’ other ways,’ I says, ‘an’ more ’n that, if you git into any kind of a scrape an’ I don’t happen to be got at, you go to him an’ git what you want.’ ”
“I hope she lived and prospered,” said John fervently.
“She lived twenty year,” said David, “an’ I wish she was livin’ now. I never drawed a check on her account without feelin’ ’t I was doin’ somethin’ for my little boy.

===Chinese===

「『没有，』她说，『俺想没有；只是打头一天起，他多半时候烧得说胡话，末了那二十四个钟头，怕是一直都烧着。』

「『你说他还认得俺不？』俺说。『他说什么了没有？』这话一出，」大卫说，「她看了俺一眼。俺进门那会儿她没哭——先前哭过；可这一句，她的脸全散了。『俺不知道，』她说。『他嘴里一直念叨，俺们能听出来的，来来回回就是「爸爸，爸爸」。』说完她把围裙蒙在脸上，然后——」

大卫把帽檐又往下压了压——其实同多数弄马的人一样（弄马的人里少有例外的），他本就戴得低；随后他俯过身，拇指食指中指捻着插在鞭筒里的马鞭转。约翰一路研究挡泥板上缝的装饰，直到缰绳塞进了他手里。可没多久。大卫直起身，头也不回，把缰绳接了回去，仿佛理所当然。

「下葬第二天，」他接着说，「俺同琼斯太太说：『俺还回西部去，』俺说，『去多久说不好——反正够久的，』俺说，『久到俺脑子能装下一件事：回来的时候，进屋再没有一个小家伙蹦起来搂俺的脖子了。可久也罢短也罢，俺总有回来的一天；这期间，你俺之间的事照旧；还不止——你能常常记着他一点不？』俺说。

「『活一天记一天，』她说，『同俺自己的一样。』

「『这个嘛，』俺说，『你记他一天，从某种意义上说，他就对你活一天；只要这样，他的吃穿照应，俺照旧出，一个子儿不少，*还有*，』俺说，『你不容俺，就是不给俺朋友的面子——而你，*一直*是*顶好*的那一个。』她扭捏了一阵，可俺不容她说『不』。『俺同俺的合伙人方方面面都安排妥了，』俺说，『再有一层：你哪天遇上什么难处，一时又寻不着俺——你去找他，要什么拿什么。』」

「但愿她长寿兴旺。」约翰恳切地说。

「她活了二十年。」大卫说，「俺巴不得她还活着。每一回为她开支票，俺都觉得，是替俺的小儿子在做一件事。

===Original===

“The’s a good many diff’rent sorts an’ kinds o’ sorro’,” he said, after a moment, “that’s in some ways kind o’ kin to each other, but I guess losin’ a child ’s a specie by itself. Of course I passed the achin’, smartin’ point years ago, but it’s somethin’ you can’t fergit﻿—that is, you can’t help feelin’ about it, because it ain’t only what the child *was* to you, but what you keep thinkin’ he’d ’a’ ben growin’ more an’ more to *be* to you. When I lost my little boy I didn’t only lose him as he was, but I ben losin’ him over an’ agin all these years. What he’d ’a’ ben when he was *so* old; an’ what when he’d got to be a big boy; an’ what he’d ’a’ ben when he went mebbe to collidge; an’ what he’d ’a’ ben afterward, an’ up to *now*. Of course the times when a man stuffs his face down into the pillers nights, passes, after a while; but while the’s some sorro’s that the happenin’ o’ things helps ye to fergit, I guess the’s some that the happenin’ o’ things keeps ye rememberin’, an’ losin’ a child ’s one on ’em.”

===Chinese===

「世上的伤心，」过了片刻他说，「门类繁多，各有各的样子，有些彼此还沾亲带故；可俺瞅，丧子这一种，自成一类。那种钻心火辣辣的阶段，俺自然早熬过去了；可这是忘不掉的东西——就是说，由不得你不去想它；因为不光是那孩子*曾是*你的什么，还因为你总在一遍遍想：他往后长下去，会*越来越是*你的什么。俺没了小儿子，丢的不只是他当年的模样；这些年，俺一直在把他丢了一回又一回。他长到*这么*大该是什么样；长成半大小子该是什么样；兴许上了大学该是什么样；再往后，一直到*如今*，又该是什么样。夜里把脸埋进枕头的那段时辰，人熬一阵，总会过去；可世上有一种伤心，事情越多越帮你忘，俺看就还有另一种，事情越多越叫你记着——丧子，就是后一种。」

## XLI / 四十一
===Original===

It was the latter part of John’s fifth winter in Homeville. The business of the office had largely increased. The new manufactories which had been established did their banking with Mr. Harum, and the older concerns, including nearly all the merchants in the village, had transferred their accounts from Syrchester banks to David’s. The callow Hopkins had fledged and developed into a competent all-’round man, able to do anything in the office, and there was a new “skeezicks” discharging Peleg’s former functions. Considerable impetus had been given to the business of the town by the new road whose rails had been laid the previous summer. There had been a strong and acrimonious controversy over the route which the road should take into and through the village. There was the party of the “nabobs” (as they were characterized by Mr. Harum) and their following, and the party of the “village people,” and the former had carried their point; but now the road was an accomplished fact, and most of the bitterness which had been engendered had died away. Yet the struggle was still matter for talk.
“Did I ever tell you,” said David, as he and his cashier were sitting in the rear room of the bank, “how Lawyer Staples come to switch round in that there railroad jangle last spring?”
“I remember,” said John, “that you told me he had deserted his party, and you laughed a little at the time, but you did not tell me how it came about.”
“I kind o’ thought I told ye,” said David.
“No,” said John, “I am quite sure you did not.”
“Wa’al,” said Mr. Harum, “the’ was, as you know, the Tenaker-Rogers crowd wantin’ one thing, an’ the Purse-Babbit lot bound to have the other, an’ run the road under the other fellers’ noses. Staples was workin’ tooth an’ nail fer the Purse crowd, an’ bein’ a good deal of a politician, he was helpin’ ’em a good deal. In fact, he was about their best card. I wa’n’t takin’ much hand in the matter either way, though my feelin’s was with the Tenaker party. I know ’t would come to a point where some money ’d prob’ly have to be used, an’ I made up my mind I wouldn’t do much drivin’ myself unless I had to, an’ not then till the last quarter of the heat. Wa’al, it got to lookin’ like a putty even thing. What little show I had made was if anythin’ on the Purse side. One day Tenaker come in to see me an’ wanted to know flat-footed which side the fence I was on.

===Chinese===

那是约翰在霍姆维尔的第五个冬天，下半段。事务所的营业大见增长。新办的几家制造厂都把银行往来放在哈勒姆先生这里；老字号——包括村里几乎全部商家——也把账户从雪切斯特（Syrchester）的银行转到了大卫门下。当年那只生涩的雀儿霍普金斯羽翼已丰，长成了个拿得起全份的通手，屋里样样来得；顶替皮尔先前那份差事的，又换了一个新的「小毛头」。新铁路去年夏天铺轨，给镇上的生意添了不小的一把劲。铁路进村、穿村走哪条线，起过一场激烈而尖刻的争斗：一党是「财主们」（哈勒姆先生的定名）及其随从，一党是「村里人」；前一党赢了；如今铁路既成事实，结下的大部分怨气也散了。可那场争夺仍是谈资。
「我同你讲过没有，」大卫同他的出纳坐在银行后屋，说，「斯台普斯（Staples）律师去年春天在那场铁路官司里怎么倒的戈？」
「我记得，」约翰说，「你同我说过他叛了党，当时你还笑了一笑；可他怎么倒的戈，你没讲。」
「我当讲过了，」大卫说。
「没有，」约翰说，「我拿得准没讲。」
「这个嘛，」哈勒姆先生说，「你也知道：一边是特纳克-罗杰斯（Tenaker-Rogers）那一伙要这样，一边是珀斯-巴比特（Purse-Babbit）那一帮非要那样，要从对头鼻子底下把路修过去。斯台普斯在替珀斯一伙死命奔走；他又是相当的政治家，帮了大忙——说实在的，是他们顶大的一张牌。我呢，两头都没大插手，虽说心向着特纳克一边。我知道事情早晚要走到花一点钱的地步，我拿定主意：不到万不得已，自己不出面赶这架马车；真要出面，也捱到最后一节。这个嘛，后来瞧着两边不相上下了。我亮的那点颜色，还偏在珀斯一边。有一天特纳克来见我，直截了当问我：这道墙我坐在哪一头。

===Original===

‘Wa’al,’ I says, ‘I’ve ben settin’ up fer shapes to be kind o’ on the fence, but I don’t mind sayin’, betwixt you an’ me, that the bulk o’ my heft is a-saggin’ your way; but I hain’t took no active part, an’ Purse an’ them thinks I’m goin’ to be on their side when it comes to a pinch.’
“ ‘Wa’al,’ he says, ‘it’s goin’ to be a putty close thing, an’ we’re goin’ to need all the help we c’n git.’
“ ‘Wa’al,’ I says, ‘I guess that’s so, but fer the present I reckon I c’n do ye more good by keepin’ in the shade. Are you folks prepared to spend a little money?’ I says.
“ ‘Yes,’ he says, ‘if it comes to that.’
“ ‘Wa’al,’ I says, ‘it putty most gen’ally does come to that, don’t it? Now, the’s one feller that’s doin’ ye more harm than some others.’
“ ‘You mean Staples?’ he says.
“ ‘Yes,’ I says, ‘I mean Staples. He don’t really care a hill o’ white beans which way the road comes in, but he thinks he’s on the pop’lar side. Now,’ I says, ‘I don’t know as it’ll be nec’sary to use money with him, an’ I don’t say ’t you could, anyway, but mebbe his yawp c’n be stopped. I’ll have a quiet word with him,’ I says, ‘an’ see you agin.’ So,” continued Mr. Harum, “the next night the’ was quite a lot of ’em in the bar of the new hotel, an’ Staples was haranguin’ away the best he knowed how, an’ bime by I nodded him off to one side, an’ we went across the hall into the settin’ room.
“ ‘I see you feel putty strong ’bout this bus’nis,’ I says.
“ ‘Yes, sir, it’s a matter of princ’ple with me,’ he says, knockin’ his fist down onto the table.
“ ‘How does the outcome on’t look to ye?’ I says. ‘Goin’ to be a putty close race, ain’t it?’
“ ‘Wa’al,’ he says, ‘ ’tween you an’ me, I reckon it is.’
“ ‘That’s the way it looks to me,’ I says, ‘an’ more’n that, the other fellers are ready to spend some money at a pinch.’
“ ‘They be, be they?’ he says.
“ ‘Yes, sir,’ I says, ‘an’ we’ve got to meet ’em halfway. Now,’ I says, takin’ a paper out o’ my pocket, ‘what I wanted to say to you is this: You ben ruther more prom’nent in this matter than most anybody﻿—fur’s talkin’ goes﻿—but I’m consid’ably int’risted. The’s got to be some money raised, an’ I’m ready,’ I says, ‘to put down as much as you be up to a couple o’ hunderd, an’ I’ll take the paper ’round to the rest; but,’ I says, unfoldin’ it, ‘I think you’d ought to head the list, an’ I’ll come next.’

===Chinese===

「『这个嘛，』我说，『我一直装作骑墙的架势；可是你知我知，我这身分量多半朝你那边坠；不过我没出头活动，珀斯他们还当紧关节要时我站他们那边呢。』
「『嗯，』他说，『这一仗咬得很紧，我们这边能拉的手全要拉。』
「『是，』我说，『我看也是；不过眼下，我估摸我待在阴凉里对你们用处更大。你们几位备下花一点钱没有？』我说。
「『备下了，』他说，『真到那一步的话。』
「『这个嘛，』我说，『十有八九总到那一步，对不对？如今有一位，给你们添的害处比谁都多。』
「『你说斯台普斯？』他说。
「『是，』我说，『我说斯台普斯。路打哪头进村，他其实一筐白豆都不在乎；他只是当自己在得人心的一头。这么着，』我说，『钱在他身上兴许用不着——也不见得用得动——可他那张嚷嚷的嘴，兴许堵得上。我去同他静静说一句，』我说，『回头再见你。』」于是，」哈勒姆先生接着说，「第二天晚上，新旅馆的酒馆里聚了他们一大伙，斯台普斯正卖着力气演说；过了一阵，我朝他一点头，引他到一边，两人穿过过道进了客厅。
「『我看这事您热得很，』我说。
「『是，先生，这对我是个原则问题，』他说，一拳捶在桌上。
「『您看结局会怎样？』我说。『要咬得很紧，对不对？』
「『这个嘛，』他说，『你我之间说说：我看是。』
「『我也是这个看法，』我说，『还不止：到了关节上，对面那伙是准备花钱的。』
「『他们敢，』他说。
「『敢，先生，』我说，『咱们只好半路迎一迎。这么着，』我从口袋里掣出一张纸，『我要同您说的是这个：这件事上您比谁都出风头——出嘴的份儿上——可我也相当入伙。总得凑一笔钱；我准备，』我说，『您出多少我出多少，出到两百为止，名单我拿去挨家走；不过，』我把纸抖开，『我看您该领衔头一名，我第二。』

===Original===

Wa’al,” said David with a chuckle and a shake of the head, “you’d ought to have seen his jaw go down. He wriggled ’round in his chair, an’ looked ten diff’rent ways fer Sunday.
“ ‘What do you say?’ I says, lookin’ square at him, ‘ ’ll you make it a couple a hunderd?’
“ ‘Wa’al,’ he says, ‘I guess I couldn’t go ’s fur ’s that, an’ I wouldn’t like to head the list anyway.’
“ ‘All right,’ I says, ‘I’ll head it. Will you say one-fifty?’
“ ‘No,’ he says, pullin’ his whiskers, ‘I guess not.’
“ ‘A hunderd?’ I says, an’ he shook his head.
“ ‘Fifty,’ I says, ‘an’ I’ll go a hunderd,’ an at that he got out his hank’chif an’ blowed his nose, an’ took his time to it. ‘Wa’al,’ I says, ‘what *do* ye say?’
“ ‘Wa’al,’ he says, ‘I ain’t quite prepared to give ye ’n answer tonight. Fact on’t is,’ he says, ‘it don’t make a cent’s wuth o’ diff’rence to me person’ly which way the dum’d road comes in, an’ I don’t jest this minute see why I should spend any money in it.’
“ ‘There’s the *princ’ple* o’ the thing,’ I says.
“ ‘Yes,’ he says, gettin’ out of his chair, ‘of course, there’s the princ’ple of the thing, an’﻿—wa’al, I’ll think it over an’ see you agin,’ he says, lookin’ at his watch. ‘I got to go now.’
“Wa’al, the next night,” proceeded Mr. Harum, “I went down to the hotel agin, an’ the’ was about the same crowd, but no Staples. The’ wa’n’t much goin’ on, an’ Purse, in pertic’ler, was lookin’ putty down in the mouth. ‘Where’s Staples?’ I says.
“ ‘Wa’al,’ says Purse, ‘he said mebbe he’d come tonight, an’ mebbe he couldn’t. Said it wouldn’t make much diff’rence; an’ anyhow he was goin’ out o’ town up to Syrchester fer a few days. I don’t know what’s come over the feller,’ says Purse. ‘I told him the time was gittin’ short an’ we’d have to git in our best licks, an’ he said he guessed he’d done about all ’t he could, an’ in fact,’ says Purse, ‘he seemed to ’a’ lost int’rist in the hull thing.’ ”
“What did you say?” John asked.
“Wa’al,” said David with a grin, “Purse went on to allow ’t he guessed somebody’s pocketbook had ben talkin’, but I didn’t say much of anythin’, an’ putty soon come away. Two three days after,” he continued, “I see Tenaker agin. ‘I hear Staples has gone out o’ town,’ he says, ‘an’ I hear, too,’ he says, ‘that he’s kind o’ soured on the hull thing﻿—didn’t care much how it did come out.’

===Chinese===

这个嘛，」大卫笑着摇头说，「他那下巴塌下去的样子，您真该看看。他在椅子里拧来拧去，眼睛朝十个方向找礼拜天。
「『您怎么说？』我直望着他说，『来两百？』
「『嗯，』他说，『我看出不到那个数；再说领衔我也不情愿。』
「『行，』我说，『我领衔。一百五？』
「『不，』他捻着胡子说，『我看不。』
「『一百？』我说；他摇头。
「『五十，』我说，『我出一百』；听到这儿他掣出手帕擤鼻子，慢慢腾腾擤了个够。『那么，』我说，『您到底*怎么*说？』
「『这个嘛，』他说，『今晚我还备不下一个回话。实情是，』他说，『这条路从哪头进村，对我个人一文钱的出入都没有；这会儿我看不出凭什么要我在里头贴钱。』
「『这里头有个*原则*问题，』我说。
「『是，』他站起来说，『当然，有原则问题，还有——嗯，我考虑考虑，再见你，』他看看表说，『我这会儿得走。』
「第二天晚上，」哈勒姆先生接着说，「我又下旅馆去；人还是差不多那一伙，独没有斯台普斯。没什么动静，珀斯尤其一副蔫相。『斯台普斯呢？』我说。
「『嗯，』珀斯说，『他说今晚兴许来，兴许来不了。说也无关紧要；反正他要出城，上雪切斯特待几天。不知这家伙中了什么邪，』珀斯说。『我告诉他日子不多了，咱们得拿出全副力气；他说他估摸他出的力差不离到头了；说实在的，』珀斯说，『他仿佛对整个这件事失了兴致。』」
「你怎么说？」约翰问。
「这个嘛，」大卫咧嘴一笑，「珀斯接着发表意见，猜是哪个的钱袋在说话；我没多言语，坐了一阵就走。过两三天，」他接着说，「我又见了特纳克。『听说斯台普斯出城了，』他说，『还听说，』他说，『他对这整个的事有点寒了心——结局怎样满不在乎了。』

===Original===

“ ‘Wa’al,’ I says, ‘when he comes back you c’n use your own judgment about havin’ a little interview with him. Mebbe somethin’ ’s made him think the’s two sides to this thing. But anyway,’ I says, ‘I guess he won’t do no more hollerin’.’
“ ‘How’s that?’ says Tenaker.
“ ‘Wa’al,’ I says, ‘I guess I’ll have to tell ye a little story. Mebbe you’ve heard it before, but it seems to be to the point. Once on a time,’ I says, ‘the’ was a big church meetin’ that had lasted three days, an’ the last evenin’ the’ was consid’able excitement. The prayin’ an’ singin’ had warmed most on ’em up putty well, an’ one o’ the most movin’ of the speakers was tellin’ ’em what was what. The’ was a big crowd, an’ while most on ’em come to be edified, the’ was quite a lot in the back part of the place that was ready fer anythin’. Wa’al, it happened that standin’ mixed up in that lot was a feller named﻿—we’ll call him Smith, to be sure of him﻿—an’ Smith was jes’ runnin’ over with power, an’ ev’ry little while when somethin’ the speaker said touched him on the funny bone he’d out with an “A﻿—men! *Yes*, Lord!” in a voice like a fact’ry whistle. Wa’al, after a little the’ was some snickerin’ an’ gigglin’ an’ scroughin’ an’ hustlin’ in the back part, an’ even some of the serioustest up in front would kind o’ smile, an’ the moderator leaned over an’ says to one of the bretherin on the platform, “Brother Jones,” he says, “can’t you git down to the back of the hall an’ say somethin’ to quiet Brother Smith? Smith’s a good man, an’ a pious man,” the moderator says, “but he’s very excitable, an’ I’m ’fraid he’ll git the boys to goin’ back there an’ disturb the meetin’.” So Jones he worked his way back to where Smith was, an’ the moderator watched him go up to Smith and jest speak to him ’bout ten seconds; an’ after that Smith never peeped once. After the meetin’ was over, the moderator says to Jones, “Brother Jones,” he says, “what did you say to Brother Smith tonight that shut him up so quick?” “I ast him fer a dollar for For’n Missions,” says Brother Jones, ‘an’, wa’al,’ I says to Tenaker, ‘that’s what I done to Staples.’ ”
“Did Mr. Tenaker see the point?” asked John, laughing.
“He laughed a little,” said David, “but didn’t quite ketch on till I told him about the subscription paper, an’ then he like to split.”
“Suppose Staples had taken you up,” suggested John.

===Chinese===

「『这个嘛，』我说，『他回来之后，要不要找他谈一谈，您自个儿斟酌。兴许有什么事叫他想起：这件事有两面。反正，』我说，『我估摸他不会再嚷嚷了。』
「『怎么说？』特纳克说。
「『嗯，』我说，『看来我得给您讲个小故事。兴许您听过；可它正对题。从前，』我说，『有一场大型教会奋兴会，开了三天；末一晚群情激奋。祷告唱诗把大伙烘得差不多了，一位顶动人的讲员正告诉大家天底下的大道理。人山人海，多数是来受教的，可会场后头也有一大批，什么都来得了。恰好，混在那一批里站着一位——就叫他史密斯吧，保险——这位史密斯正是满得往外冒，讲员的话每碰到他哪根笑筋，他就放一嗓子『阿——门！*是*的，主！』像工厂汽笛。过了一阵，后头就有人嗤嗤咕咕、推推搡搡；连前排顶严肃的也禁不住笑一笑；主席探过身对台上一位弟兄说：『琼斯弟兄，』他说，『你能不能到会场后头，同史密斯弟兄说几句，叫他安静安静？史密斯是好人，是虔诚人，』主席说，『可他太容易激动，我怕他把后头那帮小子带起来，搅了聚会。』琼斯就一路挤到史密斯跟前；主席望着他走到史密斯那里，统共说了十来秒钟的话；打那以后史密斯一声也没吭。会罨，主席对琼斯说：『琼斯弟兄，』他说，『你今晚同史密斯弟兄说了句什么，叫他收声收得这样快？』『我请他为海外传教捐一块钱，』琼斯弟兄说。『——嗯，』我对特纳克说，『我对斯台普斯干的就是这个。』」
「特纳克先生听出点子了吗？」约翰笑着问。
「他笑了笑，」大卫说，「可没大悟；等我讲了认捐单那一节，他险些笑破肚皮。」
「倘若斯台普斯接了您的招呢？」约翰出主意说。

===Original===

“Wa’al,” said David, “I didn’t think I was takin’ many chances. If, in the fust place, I hadn’t knowed Staples as well’s I did, the Smith fam’ly, so fur ’s my experience goes, has got more members ’n any other fam’ly on top of the earth.” At this point a boy brought in a telegram. David opened it, gave a side glance at his companion, and, taking out his pocketbook, put the dispatch therein.

===Chinese===

「这个嘛，」大卫说，「我没想到自己担多大风险。头一条，我对斯台普斯知根知底；再说史密斯这一姓——照我的阅历——天下哪一家姓氏也比不上它人多。」话音未落，一个小厮送进来一封电报。大卫拆开，斜眼觑一下同伴，掣出皮夹，把电报收了进去。

## XLII / 四十二
===Original===

The next morning David called John into the rear room. “Busy?” he asked.
“No,” said John. “Nothing that can’t wait.”
“Set down,” said Mr. Harum, drawing a chair to the fire. He looked up with his characteristic grin. “Ever own a hog?” he said.
“No,” said John, smiling.
“Ever feel like ownin’ one?”
“I don’t remember ever having any cravings in that direction.”
“Like pork?” asked Mr. Harum.
“In moderation,” was the reply. David produced from his pocketbook the dispatch received the day before and handed it to the young man at his side. “Read that,” he said.
John looked at it and handed it back.
“It doesn’t convey any idea to my mind,” he said.
“What?” said David, “you don’t know what ‘Bangs Galilee’ means? nor who ‘Raisin’ is?”
“You’ll have to ask me an easier one,” said John, smiling.
David sat for a moment in silence, and then, “How much money have you got?” he asked.
“Well,” was the reply, “with what I had and what I have saved since I came I could get together about five thousand dollars, I think.”
“Is it where you c’n put your hands on’t?”
John took some slips of paper from his pocketbook and handed them to David.
“H’m, h’m,” said the latter. “Wa’al, I owe ye quite a little bunch o’ money, don’t I? Forty-five hunderd! Wa’al! Couldn’t you ’a’ done better ’n to keep this here at four percent?”
“Well,” said John, “perhaps so, and perhaps not. I preferred to do this at all events.”
“Thought the old man was *safe* anyway, didn’t ye?” said David in a tone which showed that he was highly pleased.
“Yes,” said John.
“Is this all?” asked David.
“There is some interest on those certificates, and I have some balance in my account,” was the reply; “and then, you know, I have some very valuable securities﻿—a beautiful line of mining stocks, and that promising Pennsylvania property.”
At the mention of the last-named asset David looked at him for an instant as if about to speak, but if so he changed his mind.
