He sat for a moment fingering the yellow paper which carried the mystic words. Presently he said, opening the message out, “That’s from an old friend of mine out to Chicago. He come from this part of the country, an’ we was young fellers together thirty years ago. I’ve had a good many deals with him and through him, an’ he never give me a wrong steer, fur ’s I know.

===Chinese===

第二天早晨，大卫把约翰叫进里间。「忙着呢？」他问。

「不忙。」约翰说。「没什么等不得的事。」

「坐。」哈勒姆先生说，拖了把椅子到炉边。他抬眼，露出那副标志性的咧嘴笑。「养过猪没有？」

「没有。」约翰笑着答。

「动过养一头的心思没有？」

「不记得朝那个方向起过什么饕。」

「爱吃猪肉不？」哈勒姆先生问。

「适量。」回答是。大卫从钱夹里掏出前一天收到的那封电报，递给身边的年轻人。「念念。」

约翰看了看，递了回去。

「我看不出个所以然。」他说。

「什么？」大卫说，「『班克斯加利利』是什么意思你不知道？『葡萄干』是谁也不知道？」

「您得问个容易点的。」约翰笑着说。

大卫默坐了片刻，然后问：「你手头有多少钱？」

「呢，」回答是，「算上原有的、加上来了之后攒下的，凑一凑大概有五千块。」

「都是能立刻上手的？」

约翰从钱夹里抽出几张纸片，递给大卫。

「嗯，嗯。」后者说。「这个嘛，俺欠你不小一沓钱呢，是不是？四千五百！嚯！撜这儿吃四厘利，你就没有更好的去处？」

「呢，」约翰说，「也许有，也许没有。无论如何，我情愿这样。」

「反正当这老头子*稳当*，是不是？」大卫说，口气里透着他受用极了。

「是。」约翰说。

「就这些？」大卫问。

「这几张存单还有点利息，账上还剩点余额。」回答是；「再有，您知道的，我手里还有些顶值钱的证券——漂亮的一串矿业股票，还有那份大有前途的宾夕法尼亚产业。」

提到最后那项资产，大卫看了他一瞬，像要开口，可终究改了主意。他坐着捻了捻那张载着暗语的黄纸。不多时，他把电报展开，说：「这是俺一位老朋友打芝加哥发来的。他就是这一带出去的，三十年前俺俩是一起混的小伙子。同他做的生意、经他手做的生意，俺有过不老少；就俺所知，他指的路没错过一回。

===Original===

That is, I never done as he told me without comin’ out all right, though he’s give me a good many pointers I never did nothin’ about. ’Tain’t nec’sary to name no names, but ‘Bangs Galilee’ means ‘buy pork,’ an’ as I’ve ben watchin’ the market fer quite a spell myself, an’ standard pork ’s a good deal lower ’n it costs to pack it, I’ve made up my mind to buy a few thousan’ barrels fer fam’ly use. It’s a handy thing to have in the house,” declared Mr. Harum, “an’ I thought mebbe it wouldn’t be a bad thing fer you to have a little. It looks cheap to me,” he added, “an’ mebbe bime-by what you don’t eat you c’n sell.”
“Well,” said John, laughing, “you see me at table every day and know what my appetite is like. How much pork do you think I could take care of?”
“Wa’al, at the present price,” said David, “I think about four thousan’ barrels would give ye enough to eat fer a spell, an’ mebbe leave ye a few barrels to dispose of if you should happen to strike a feller later on that wanted it wuss ’n you did.”
John opened his eyes a little. “I should only have a margin of a dollar and a quarter,” he said.
“Wa’al, I’ve got a notion that that’ll carry ye,” said David. “It may go lower ’n what it is now. I never bought anythin’ yet that didn’t drop some, an’ I guess nobody but a fool ever did buy at the bottom more’n once; but I’ve had an idee for some time that it was about bottom, an’ this here telegraph wouldn’t ’a’ ben sent if the feller that sent it didn’t think so too, an’ I’ve had some other cor’spondence with him.” Mr. Harum paused and laughed a little.
“I was jest thinkin’,” he continued, “of what the Irishman said about Stofford. Never ben there, have ye? Wa’al, it’s a place eight nine mile f’m here, an’ the hills ’round are so steep that when you’re goin’ up you c’n look right back under the buggy by jes’ leanin’ over the edge of the dash. I was drivin’ ’round there once, an’ I met an Irishman with a big drove o’ hogs.
“ ‘Hello, Pat!’ I says, ‘where ’d all them hogs come from?’
“ ‘Stofford,’ he says.
“ ‘Wa’al,’ I says, ‘I wouldn’t ’a’ thought the’ was so many hogs *in* Stofford.’
“ ‘Oh, be gobs!’ he says, ‘sure they’re *all* hogs in Stofford;’ an’,” declared David, “the bears ben sellin’ that pork up in Chicago as if the hull everlastin’ West was *all* hogs.”
“It’s very tempting,” said John thoughtfully.

===Chinese===

「就是说，凡按他说的办，俺没有不顺当出锅的；他给的点子也有不少，俺压根没动。名字就不必点了——『班克斯加利利』的意思是『买猪肉』。俺自己也盯行情盯了好一阵，标准猪肉现在的价，比装箱成本还低出一截，俺拿定主意：买它几千桶，家用。家里备着这个，方便。」哈勒姆先生宣称，「俺寻思你跟着弄一点，兴许不坏。俺瞅着便宜，」他添一句，「吃不完的，过些日子兴许还能卖。」

「呢，」约翰笑道，「您天天看我上桌，知道我饭量如何。您说我吃得动多少猪肉？」

「这个嘛，照眼下的价，」大卫说，「俺瞅四千桶上下，够你吃一阵子的；兴许还能剩几桶，往后碰上个比你更饕的主，也好出手。」

约翰眼睛睁大了些。「那我的保证金只剩一块二毛五了。」他说。

「这个嘛，俺有个数：撑得住。」大卫说。「比现在再低也有可能。俺这辈子买的东西还没有一样不跌一点的；比底价再低的价，俺瞅除了傻子，没人能逮着第二回。可俺有个念头有些日子了：差不多到底了；发电报那位要不也这么想，这封电报就不会发——何况俺同他还另有通信。」哈勒姆先生顿了顿，轻笑一声。

「俺正想起，」他接着说，「那个爱尔兰人说斯托福德的那句话。没去过吧？这个嘛，离这儿八九里地，四面的山陡成这样：上坡的时候，只要从挡板边探一探头，就能顺着车底往后看。有回俺在那边转，迎面碰上个爱尔兰人，赶着一大群猪。

「『喂，帕特！』俺说，『这群猪打哪儿来的？』

「『斯托福德。』他说。

「『这个嘛，』俺说，『没想到斯托福德*里头*有这么多猪。』

「『哦，老天！』他说，『斯托福德里头可全是猪哇！』所以，」大卫宣称，「芝加哥那头卖猪肉的空头，卖得就像整个没边没沿的西部*全是*猪。」

「很诱人。」约翰沉吟着说。

===Original===

“Wa’al,” said David, “I don’t want to tempt ye exac’ly, an’ certain I don’t want to urge ye. The’ ain’t no sure things but death an’ taxes, as the sayin’ is, but buyin’ pork at these prices is buyin’ somethin’ that’s got value, an’ you can’t wipe it out. In other words, it’s buyin’ a warranted article at a price consid’ably lower ’n it c’n be produced for, an’ though it may go lower, if a man c’n *stick*, it’s bound to level up in the long run.”
Our friend sat for some minutes apparently looking into the fire, but he was not conscious of seeing anything at all. Finally he rose, went over to Mr. Harum’s desk, figured the interest on the certificates up to the first of January, endorsed them, and filling up a check for the balance of the amount in question, handed the check and certificate to David.
“Think you’ll go it, eh?” said the latter.
“Yes,” said John; “but if I take the quantity you suggest, I shall have nothing to remargin the trade in case the market goes below a certain point.”
“I’ve thought of that,” replied David, “an’ was goin’ to say to you that I’d carry the trade down as fur as your money would go, in case more margins had to be called.”
“Very well,” said John. “And will you look after the whole matter for me?”
“All right,” said David.
John thanked him and returned to the front room.
---
There were times in the months which followed when our friend had reason to wish that all swine had perished with those whom Shylock said “your prophet the Nazarite conjured the devil into;” and the news of the world in general was of secondary importance compared with the market reports. After the purchase pork dropped off a little, and hung about the lower figure for some time. Then it began to advance by degrees until the quotation was a dollar above the purchase price.
John’s impulse was to sell, but David made no sign. The market held firm for a while, even going a little higher. Then it began to drop rather more rapidly than it had advanced, to about what the pork had cost, and for a long period fluctuated only a few cents one way or the other. This was followed by a steady decline to the extent of half-a-dollar, and, as the reports came, it “looked like going lower,” which it did.

===Chinese===

「这个嘛，」大卫说，「俺倒不是要勾你，更不是要催你。老话说，除了死和税，没有保准的事；可照这个价买猪肉，买的是有实值的东西，抹不掉的。换句话说，就是拿比生产成本低一大截的价，买一件保真的货；价兴许再落，可只要人*扛得住*，长远看准得涨回来。」

我们这位朋友坐了几分钟，表面上望着炉火，实际上什么都没看见。末了他起身，走到哈勒姆先生的桌前，把存单的利息算到一月一号，背了书，又开了一张支票补足差额，支票连存单一并递给大卫。

「要干了，嗯？」后者说。

「干。」约翰说；「可要按您说的量吃进，行情一旦跌破某一点，我就再没有钱补保证金了。」

「这个俺想到了。」大卫答，「正要同你说：真要再催保证金，你那点钱撑到哪儿算哪儿，往后的俺替你垫。」

「好。」约翰说。「整桩事就托付您照看了？」

「行。」大卫说。

约翰谢过他，回了前屋。

---

往后那几个月，有几阵子，我们这位朋友真愿全天下的猪都同夏洛克说的「你们的先知拿撒勒人把鬼赶进去的那些东西」一道死绝了；同行情报比起来，天下大事都成了第二位的。买进之后，猪肉小跌了一点，在低位上荡了些日子。随后开始步步上爬，爬到比买价高出一块。

约翰的冲动是卖，大卫不动声色。行情稳了一阵，甚至还往上拱了拱。随后开始跌，跌得比涨时还快，跌回成本上下，又好长一阵子，来来回回不过几分钱。接着是稳稳的下坡，跌掉五毛；报上说来，「看跌」——它也真跌。

===Original===

In fact, there came a day when it was so “low,” and so much more “looked like going lower” than ever (as such things usually do when the “bottom” is pretty nearly reached), that our friend had not the courage to examine the market reports for the next two days, and simply tried to keep the subject out of his mind. On the morning of the third day the Syrchester paper was brought in about ten o’clock, as usual, and laid on Mr. Harum’s desk. John shivered a little, and for some time refrained from looking at it. At last, more by impulse than intention, he went into the back room and glanced at the first page without taking the paper in his hands. One of the press dispatches was headed: “Great Excitement on Chicago Board of Trade: Pork Market reported Cornered: Bears on the Run,” and more of the same sort, which struck our friend as being the most profitable, instructive, and delightful literature that he had ever come across. David had been in Syrchester the two days previous, returning the evening before. Just then he came into the office, and John handed him the paper.
“Wa’al,” he said, holding it off at arm’s length, and then putting on his glasses, “them fellers that thought they was *all* hogs up West, are havin’ a change of heart, are they? I reckoned they would ’fore they got through with it. It’s ben ruther a long pull, though, eh?” he said, looking at John with a grin.
“Yes,” said our friend, with a slight shrug of the shoulders.
“Things looked ruther colicky the last two three days, eh?” suggested David. “Did you think ‘the jig was up an’ the monkey was in the box?’ ”
“Rather,” said John. “The fact is,” he admitted, “I am ashamed to say that for a few days back I haven’t looked at a quotation. I suppose you must have carried me to some extent. How much was it?”
“Wa’al,” said David, “I kept the trade margined, of course, an’ if we’d sold out at the bottom you’d have owed me somewhere along a thousan’ or fifteen hunderd; but,” he added, “it was only in the slump, an’ didn’t last long, an’ anyway I cal’lated to carry that pork to where it would ’a’ ketched fire. I wa’n’t worried none, an’ you didn’t let on to be, an’ so I didn’t say anythin’.”
“What do you think about it now?” asked John.
“My opinion is now,” replied Mr. Harum, “that it’s goin’ to putty near where it belongs, an’ mebbe higher, an’ them ’s my advices.

===Chinese===

真有那么一天，行情跌得那样「低」，「看跌」又看得比以往任何时候都凶——这类事到了「底」快到的时候，通常正是这样——我们这位朋友连着两天没勇气看行情版，只求把这一桩从脑子里赶出去。第三天早晨十点来钟，锡拉切斯特的报纸照常送到，撜在哈勒姆先生的桌上。约翰打了个小小的寒嚔，好一阵子没敢去看。末了，与其说是有意，不如说是一股冲动，他走进里间，手都没拿，只朝头版瞥了一眼。一条电讯的标题是：「芝加哥交易所大震动：猪肉市场据报已被囤断：空头溃逃」，底下一串同类的话——我们这位朋友只觉这是他平生读过的最有利可图、最富教益、最叫人欢喜的文字。大卫前两天去了锡拉切斯特，前一夜刚回。正这时他进了行里，约翰把报纸递给他。

「这个嘛，」他把报纸伸直了胳膊扯着，又戴上眼镜，「西边那些当*满世界全是*猪的家伙，回心转意了，是吧？俺就算准他们熬不到头。这一趟拉得可不算短，嗯？」他看着约翰，咧嘴一笑。

「可不。」我们这位朋友说，微微一耸肩。

「前两三天，光景瞧着挺绞肠痧，嗯？」大卫提醒道。「你是不是当『戏散场、猴子进笼』了？」

「相当。」约翰说。「不瞒您说，」他承认，「难为情——好几天我没敢看一眼牌价。想来您多少替我垫了些。垫了多少？」

「这个嘛，」大卫说，「保证金俺当然是一直续着的；真在底上清了仓，你欠俺的，一千到一千五上下；不过，」他添一句，「那只是打了个趔趄，没久；再说，俺原本就打算把那批肉驮到它自己着起火来的地方。俺一点没慌，你也一点没露，俺就什么都没说。」

「如今您怎么看？」约翰问。

「俺如今的意见，」哈勒姆先生答，「是它快回到它该在的位子了，兴许还高——俺得到的信儿也是这个。

===Original===

We can sell now at some profit, an’ of course the bears’ll jump on agin as it goes up, an’ the other fellers’ll take the profits f’m time to time. If I was where I could watch the market, I’d mebbe try to make a turn in ’t ’casionally, but I guess as ’tis we’d better set down an’ let her take her own gait. I don’t mean to try an’ git the top price﻿—I’m alwus willin’ to let the other feller make a little﻿—but we’ve waited fer quite a spell, an’ as it’s goin’ our way, we might ’s well wait a little longer.”
“All right,” said John, “and I’m very much obliged to you.”
“Sho, sho!” said David.
It was not until August, however, that the deal was finally closed out.

===Chinese===

「眼下卖，已有些赚头；当然，它一涨，空头还得扑回来，别的人也会时不时落袋。俺要是守得着行情，兴许还倒腾它一两把；眼下这个样子，俺瞅还是坐稳了，由它自己走它的步子。俺不打算去够那个顶价——别人挣一点，俺向来乐意——只是咱们等也等了这么一大阵，如今又顺着咱们的道走，不妨再等等。」

「好。」约翰说，「太谢谢您了。」

「嗐，嗐！」大卫说。

不过，这笔买卖最终了结，已是八月的事了。

## XLIII / 四十三
===Original===

The summer was drawing to a close. The season, so far as the social part of it was concerned, had been what John had grown accustomed to in previous years, and there were few changes in or among the people whom he had come to know very well, save those which a few years make in young people: some increase of importance in demeanor on the part of the young men whose razors were coming into requisition; and the changes from short to long skirts, from braids, pigtails, and flowing-manes to more elaborate coiffures on the part of the young women. The most notable event had been the reopening of the Verjoos house, which had been closed for two summers, and the return of the family, followed by the appearance of a young man whom Miss Clara had met abroad, and who represented himself as the acknowledged fiancé of that young woman. It need hardly be said that discussions of the event, and upon the appearance, manners, prospects, etc., of that fortunate gentleman had formed a very considerable part of the talk of the season among the summer people; and, indeed, interest in the affair had permeated all grades and classes of society.
---
It was some six weeks after the settlement of the transaction in “pork” that David and John were driving together in the afternoon as they had so often done in the last five years. They had got to that point of understanding where neither felt constrained to talk for the purpose of keeping up conversation, and often in their long drives there was little said by either of them. The young man was never what is called “a great talker,” and Mr. Harum did not always “git goin’.” On this occasion they had gone along for some time, smoking in silence, each man absorbed in his thoughts. Finally David turned to his companion.
“Do you know that Dutchman Claricy Verjoos is goin’ to marry?” he asked.
“Yes,” replied John, laughing; “I have met him a number of times. But he isn’t a Dutchman. What gave you that idea?”
“I heard it was over in Germany she run across him,” said David.
“I believe that is so, but he isn’t a German. He is from Philadelphia, and is a friend of the Bradways.”
“What kind of a feller is he? Good enough for her?”
“Well,” said John, smiling, “in the sense in which that question is usually taken, I should say yes.

===Chinese===

夏天临近尾声。就社交一面说，这一季同约翰往年过惯的没有两样；他熟识的人们中间，除了岁月在年轻人身上添的那几笔，变化寥寥：小伙子一侧，剃刀开始派上用场的，气派添了几分；姑娘一侧，短裙换长裙，辫子、马尾、披发换成讲究些的发式。顶大的一桩事是弗茹（Verjoos）家的宅子重新开了门——关了两个夏天——一家人回了村，随后出现了一位年轻人：克拉拉小姐在国外结识的，自称是这位小姐公认的未婚夫。不必说，这件事，连同那位幸运先生的相貌、举止、前程等等，占了避暑客们一季闲话里相当大的一份；说实在的，这份兴味浸透了社会上各等各色。
---
猪肉那笔交易了结之后约六个星期，一天下午，大卫同约翰照这五年里常有的样子，一同赶车出去。两人到了那样一种默契：谁也不必为维持谈话勉强开口；长途驱车时，常常彼此都少话。年轻人从来算不上「能说的人」，哈勒姆先生也不永远「跑得起兴头」。这一回，两人有一阵只管抽烟，各想各的。末了大卫转向同伴。
「知道吗，克拉丽丝要嫁的那个『荷兰佬』？」他问。
「知道，」约翰笑着答。「照过几面。可他不是荷兰佬。你怎么有这个想法？」
「听说她是在德国撞见他的，」大卫说。
「那大约不假；可他不是德国人。他是费城人，布雷德韦家的朋友。」
「这人怎么样？配得上她吗？」
「嗯，」约翰微笑说，「照这个问题通常的意思，我看配得上。

===Original===

He has good looks, good manners, a good deal of money, I am told, and it is said that Miss Clara﻿—which is the main point, after all﻿—is very much in love with him.”
“H’m,” said David after a moment. “How do you git along with the Verjoos girls? Was Claricy’s ears pointed all right when you seen her fust after she come home?”
“Oh, yes!” replied John, smiling, “she and her sister were perfectly pleasant and cordial, and Miss Verjoos and I are on very friendly terms.”
“I was thinkin’,” said David, “that you an’ Claricy might be got to likin’ each other, an’ mebbe﻿—”
“I don’t think there could ever have been the smallest chance of it,” declared John hastily.
“Take the lines a minute,” said David, handing them to his companion after stopping the horses. “The nigh one’s picked up a stone, I guess,” and he got out to investigate. “The river road,” he remarked as he climbed back into the buggy after removing the stone from the horse’s foot, “is about the puttiest road ’round here, but I don’t drive it oftener jest on account of them dum’d loose stuns.” He sucked the air through his pursed-up lips, producing a little squeaking sound, and the horses started forward. Presently he turned to John:
“Did you ever think of gettin’ married?” he asked.
“Well,” said our friend with a little hesitation, “I don’t remember that I ever did, very definitely.”
“Somebody ’t you knew ’fore you come up here?” said David, jumping at a conclusion.
“Yes,” said John, smiling a little at the question.
“Wouldn’t she have ye?” queried David, who stuck at no trifles when in pursuit of information.
John laughed. “I never asked her,” he replied, in truth a little surprised at his own willingness to be questioned.
“Did ye cal’late to when the time come right?” pursued Mr. Harum.
Of this part of his history John had, of course, never spoken to David. There had been a time when, if not resenting the attempt upon his confidence, he would have made it plain that he did not wish to discuss the matter, and the old wound still gave him twinges. But he had not only come to know his questioner very well, but to be much attached to him. He knew, too, that the elder man would ask him nothing save in the way of kindness, for he had had a hundred proofs of that; and now, so far from feeling reluctant to take his companion into his confidence, he rather welcomed the idea.

===Chinese===

人长得好，举止好，听说钱也相当；而顶要紧的一条——克拉拉小姐确实很爱他。」
「嗯，」大卫过了一刻说。「你同弗茹家两位小姐处得怎样？克拉丽丝刚回来那阵，你头一回见她，耳朵没支起来？」
「哦，没有！」约翰笑着答。「她同她妹妹客气亲热得很；弗茹小姐同我如今交情很好。」
「我原先想，」大卫说，「你同克拉丽丝兴许能处出个情分来，兴许——」
「我看这一层从来就没有过一星的可能，」约翰急忙声明。
「替我拉一下缰，」大卫勒住马，把缰绳递给同伴。「里首这匹怕是踩着石头了。」他下去查看。「这条河滨路，」从马蹄里取出石子、爬回车后他说，「是这一带顶漂亮的路；就为这些该死的散石，我不常走。」他嘬起嘴唇嘬进一口气，嘬出吱的一声轻响，马起步了。不多时他转向约翰：
「想过成家没有？」他问。
「嗯，」我们这位朋友微微踌躇，「不记得很明确地想过。」
「上这儿来之前认得的人？」大卫一步抢到结论。
「是，」约翰说，被这一问引得浅浅一笑。
「人家没要你？」大卫追问——他打听事情，从来不在小节前却步。
约翰笑了。「我没开过口，」他答；说实在的，自己肯这样受盘问，连他自己也有点惊讶。
「原打算等时机到了再问？」哈勒姆先生追下去。
这段历史，约翰自然从没对大卫讲过。搁在从前，纵然不怪他探问，也要表明不愿多谈；旧伤至今还不时抽痛。可他如今不但把这位问话的人摸得透熟，还着实依恋他。他也知道，老人除非出于好意，什么也不会问他——这一层他得过的凭据有一百件；此刻非但不勉强，他倒情愿把心事向同伴交出。
况且，他也有一点好奇，想摸清这一串盘问的去向：他知道大卫行事尽管迂回，起手却很少没有目的。于是这一问以及随后几问，他都答了——像同一个女人讲自己的故事那样。

===Original===

He was, withal, a bit curious to ascertain the drift of the inquiry, knowing that David, though sometimes working in devious ways, rarely started without an intention. And so he answered the question and what followed as he might have told his story to a woman.
“An’ didn’t you never git no note, nor message, nor word of any kind?” asked David.
“No.”
“Nor hain’t ever heard a word about her f’m that day to this?”
“No.”
“Nor hain’t ever tried to?”
“No,” said John. “What would have been the use?”
“Prov’dence seemed to ’ve made a putty clean sweep in your matters that spring, didn’t it?”
“It seemed so to me,” said John.
Nothing more was said for a minute or two. Mr. Harum appeared to have abandoned the pursuit of the subject of his questions. At last he said:
“You ben here most five years.”
“Very nearly,” John replied.
“Ben putty contented, on the hull?”
“I have grown to be,” said John. “Indeed, it’s hard to realize at times that I haven’t always lived in Homeville. I remember my former life as if it were something I have read in a book. There was a John Lenox in it, but he seems to me sometimes more like a character in a story than myself.”
“An’ yet,” said David, turning toward him, “if you was to go back to it, this last five years ’d git to be that way to ye a good deal quicker. Don’t ye think so?”
“Perhaps so,” replied John. “Yes,” he added thoughtfully, “it is possible.”
“I guess on the hull, though,” remarked Mr. Harum, “you done better up here in the country ’n you might some ’ers else﻿—”
“Oh, yes,” said John sincerely, “thanks to you, I have indeed, and﻿—”
“﻿—an’﻿—ne’ mind about me﻿—you got quite a little bunch o’ money together now. I was thinkin’ ’t mebbe you might feel ’t you needn’t to stay here no longer if you didn’t want to.”
The young man turned to the speaker inquiringly, but Mr. Harum’s face was straight to the front, and betrayed nothing.
“It wouldn’t be no more ’n natural,” he went on, “an’ mebbe it would be best for ye. You’re too good a man to spend all your days workin’ fer Dave Harum, an’ I’ve had it in my mind fer some time﻿—somethin’ like that pork deal﻿—to make you a little independent in case anythin’ should happen, an’﻿—gen’ally. I couldn’t give ye no money ’cause you wouldn’t ’a’ took it even if I’d wanted to, but now you got it, why﻿—”
“I feel very much as if you had given it to me,” protested the young man.
David put up his hand.

===Chinese===

「你就从没收到过一张条子、一句口信、随便一个字？」大卫问。
「没有。」
「从那天到今天，也没听人提过她一个字？」
「没有。」
「也没设法打听过？」
「没有，」约翰说。「打听了又有什么用？」
「上天那年春天对你那一摊，看来是扫得相当干净，是不是？」
「我也这么觉着，」约翰说。
有一两分钟没话。哈勒姆先生像是放弃了这个题目。末了他说：
「你到这儿快五年了。」
「差不多，」约翰答。
「总的说，还受用？」
「渐渐受用了，」约翰说。「说真的，有时候很难相信自己不是一直住在霍姆维尔。从前那段日子，我想起来像书里读来的故事。里头也有个约翰·伦诺克斯；可他在我眼里，有时更像小说里的人物，不像我自己。」
「可是，」大卫转向他，「你要是回去了，这五年也很快会变成那个样子——比你料得快。你说是不是？」
「兴许，」约翰答。「是，」他若有所思地加一句，「有这个可能。」
「不过我猜，总的说，」哈勒姆先生说，「你在这乡下干得比在别处可能干的强——」
「哦，是，」约翰由衷地说，「多亏了你，确实——而且——」
「——嗯——我不用提——你如今手头攒下小小一笔了。我在想：你兴许会觉得，愿意的话，不必再待在这儿了。」
年轻人询问地转向说话人；可哈勒姆先生的脸正对前方，什么也不露。
「这也合乎常情，」他接着说，「兴许还是为你好。你这样的人才，不该一辈子替戴夫·哈勒姆当差；我心里盘算这事也有日子了——就照那回猪肉的买卖那样——替你挣下一点独立的本钱，万一有个万一——也作平常打算。现钱我给不了你——你也不会要；如今你有了，那——」
「我觉得，这同你送给我没什么两样，」年轻人抗议。
大卫抬起一只手。

===Original===

“No, no,” he said, “all ’t I did was to propose the thing to ye, an’ to put up a little money fer two three days. I didn’t take no chances, an’ it’s all right, an’ it’s your’n, an’ it makes ye to a certain extent independent of Homeville.”
“I don’t quite see it so,” said John.
“Wa’al,” said David, turning to him, “if you’d had as much five years ago you wouldn’t ’a’ come here, would ye?”
John was silent.
“What I was leadin’ up to,” resumed Mr. Harum after a moment, “is this: I ben thinkin’ about it fer some time, but I haven’t wanted to speak to ye about it before. In fact, I might ’a’ put it off some longer if things wa’n’t as they are, but the fact o’ the matter is that I’m goin’ to take down my sign.”
John looked at him in undisguised amazement, not unmixed with consternation.
“Yes,” said David, obviously avoiding the other’s eye, “ ‘David Harum, Banker,’ is goin’ to come down. I’m gettin’ to be an’ old man,” he went on, “an’ what with some investments I’ve got, an’ a hoss-trade once in a while, I guess I c’n manage to keep the fire goin’ in the kitchin stove fer Polly an’ me, an’ the’ ain’t no reason why I sh’d keep my sign up much of any longer. Of course,” he said, “if I was to go on as I be now I’d want ye to stay jest as you are; but, as I was sayin’, you’re to a consid’able extent independent. You hain’t no speciul ties to keep ye, an’ you ought anyway, as I said before, to be doin’ better for yourself than jest drawin’ pay in a country bank.”
One of the most impressive morals drawn from the fairy tales of our childhood, and indeed from the literature and experience of our later periods of life, is that the fulfilment of wishes is often attended by the most unwelcome results. There had been a great many times when to our friend the possibility of being able to bid farewell to Homeville had seemed the most desirable of things, but confronted with the idea as a reality﻿—for what other construction could he put upon David’s words except that they amounted practically to a dismissal, though a most kind one?﻿—he found himself simply in dismay.
“I suppose,” he said after a few moments, “that by ‘taking down your sign’ you mean going out of business﻿—”
“Figger o’ speech,” explained David.
“﻿—and your determination is not only a great surprise to me, but grieves me very much. I am very sorry to hear it﻿—more sorry than I can tell you.

===Chinese===

「不，不，」他说，「我干的无非是把事情提给你，垫了两三天的款。我没冒什么险；一切都好；钱是你的；它叫你在相当程度上独立于霍姆维尔了。」
「我不大这么看，」约翰说。
「这个嘛，」大卫转向他，「五年前你要有这么多，就不会上这儿来了，对不对？」
约翰不响。
「我要引到的是这个，」哈勒姆先生过了一刻接下去，「我盘算有些日子了，只是一直不想先开口。实情是，若不是眼下这个局面，我兴许还捱些日子；可实情是：我要摘招牌了。」
约翰望着他，毫不掩饰的惊异里掺着惊慌。
「是，」大卫说，分明避开对方的眼睛，「『大卫·哈勒姆，银行家』，要摘下来了。我上年纪了，」他接着说，「有手头的几处进项，加上隔三差五一桩马生意，波莉同我的灶膛里烧得起火；招牌再挂下去，没什么道理了。当然，」他说，「我要照现在这样办下去，就要你照旧留任；可我说了，你在相当程度上独立了。也没什么特别的牵挂拴着你；再说，方才也说过，你总该给自己谋个更好的前程，不该光在一家乡村银行支薪水。」
童年童话——以及后来岁月的文学与阅历——教给我们的顶扎心的道理之一是：愿望的实现，往往伴着顶不受欢迎的结果。我们这位朋友有过多少回，把「能够向霍姆维尔道别」想成天下第一等可心的东西；可如今它作为现实摆到面前——大卫这番话，除了实际上等于一纸辞退，虽然是最厚道的一纸，还能作什么别的解？——他发现自己只剩下慌。
「我猜，」过了几刻他说，「您说的『摘招牌』，是指歇业——」
「打个比方，」大卫解释。
「——您这个决定，不但叫我大吃一惊，还叫我非常难过。听到它我遗憾——遗憾得说不出口。

===Original===

As you remind me, if I leave Homeville I shall not go almost penniless as I came, but I shall leave with great regret, and, indeed﻿—Ah, well﻿—” he broke off with a wave of his hands.
“What was you goin’ to say?” asked David, after a moment, his eyes on the horizon.
“I can’t say very much more,” replied the young man, “than that I am very sorry. There have been times,” he added, “as you may understand, when I have been restless and discouraged for a while, particularly at first; but I can see now that, on the whole, I have been far from unhappy here. Your house has grown to be more a real home than any I have ever known, and you and your sister are like my own people. What you say, that I ought not to look forward to spending my life behind the counter of a village bank on a salary, may be true; but I am not, at present at least, a very ambitious person, nor, I am afraid, a very clever one in the way of getting on in the world; and the idea of breaking out for myself, even if that were all to be considered, is not a cheerful one. I am afraid all this sounds rather selfish to you, when, as I can see, you have deferred your plans for my sake, and after all else that you have done for me.”
“I guess I shan’t lay it up agin ye,” said David quietly.
They drove along in silence for a while.
“May I ask,” said John, at length, “when you intend to ‘take down your sign,’ as you put it?”
“Whenever you say the word,” declared David, with a chuckle and a side glance at his companion. John turned in bewilderment.
“What do you mean?” he asked.
“Wa’al,” said David with another short laugh, “fur ’s the sign ’s concerned, I s’pose we *could* stick a new one over it, but I guess it might ’s well come down; but we’ll settle that matter later on.”
John still looked at the speaker in utter perplexity, until the latter broke out into a laugh.
“Got any idee what’s goin’ onto the new sign?” he asked.
“You don’t mean﻿—”
“Yes, I do,” declared Mr. Harum, “an’ my notion ’s this, an’ don’t you say aye, yes, nor no till I git through,” and he laid his left hand restrainingly on John’s knee.
“The new sign’ll read ‘Harum & Comp’ny,’ or ‘Harum & Lenox,’ jest as you elect. You c’n put in what money you got an’ I’ll put in as much more, which’ll make cap’tal enough in gen’ral, an’ any extry money that’s needed﻿—wa’al, up to a certain point, I guess I c’n manage.

===Chinese===

您提醒得是，我离开霍姆维尔，不会再像来时那样几乎身无分文；可我走会带着大大的不舍，而且，说真的——唉——」他两手一摊，断了话头。
「你要说什么来着？」大卫过了一刻问，眼睛望着天边。
「我说不出更多了，」年轻人答，「只有一句：我非常难过。有过几阵，」他添一句，「您可想而知，我心浮气躁、垂头丧气过一阵，尤其起头；可如今我看得明白：总的说，我在这里离不幸福远得很。您的家，已经长成比我知道的任何家更像家的地方；您同您妹妹，就像我自己的亲人。您说的——我不该指望一辈子坐在一家乡村银行的柜台后头挣薪水——也许是实；可我眼下，至少，不是个雄心很大的人，说到在世上往上爬的门道，怕也不算灵光；白手起家这个念头——就算别的都不用考虑——也不叫人痛快。我看得出来，您为我推迟了自己的打算，加上您已为我做的一切；我这一篇话，在您听来恐怕相当自私。」
「我想我不记账，」大卫安安静静地说。
两人默默赶了一阵车。
「我能问一句吗，」约翰终于说，「您打算几时『摘招牌』，照您的话说？」
「您说哪天，就哪天，」大卫宣布，一声轻笑，斜眼觑着同伴。约翰惶惑地转过脸。
「您什么意思？」他问。
「这个嘛，」大卫又短短一笑，「招牌那一层，我猜咱们*也*能在旧的上头钉块新的；不过摘了也好；这件事回头再定。」
约翰还直蹬着说话人，一头的雾水；直到后者放声大笑。
「猜得着新招牌写什么吗？」他问。
「您莫不是说——」
「正是，」哈勒姆先生宣布，「我的主意是这样：我话没说完，你先别点头，也别摇头，」左手按住约翰的膝盖，拦着。
「新招牌写『哈勒姆合股公司』，或者『哈勒姆-伦诺克斯』，随你挑。你把你的钱投进来，我再照数添一份，本钱大致就够了；往后缺的活钱——嗯，到一定数目，我谅必对付得了。

===Original===

Now putty much all the new bus’nis has come in through you, an’ practically you got the hull thing in your hands. You’ll do the work about ’s you’re doin’ now, an’ you’ll draw the same sal’ry; an’ after that’s paid we’ll go snucks on anythin’ that’s left﻿—that *is*,” added David with a chuckle, “if you feel that you c’n *stan’* it in Homeville.”
---
“I wish you was married to one of our Homeville girls, though,” declared Mr. Harum later on as they drove homeward.

===Chinese===

如今新进的生意，差不多全是打你手里来的；实际上一整个摊子都在你手里。活计你还照眼前这样干；薪水照旧支；支完之后，剩下什么，咱们对半分——*那就是说*，」大卫笑着补一句，「如果你觉得你在霍姆维尔*待得住*的话。」
---
「不过我还是愿你娶咱们霍姆维尔哪家的姑娘，」归途上，哈勒姆先生发表意见。

## XLIV / 四十四
===Original===

Since the whooping-cough and measles of childhood the junior partner of Harum & Company had never to his recollection had a day’s illness in his life, and he fought the attack which came upon him about the first week in December with a sort of incredulous disgust, until one morning when he did not appear at breakfast. He spent the next week in bed, and at the end of that time, while he was able to be about, it was in a languid and spiritless fashion, and he was shaken and exasperated by a persistent cough. The season was and had been unusually inclement even for that region, where the thermometer sometimes changes fifty degrees in thirty-six hours; and at the time of his release from his room there was a period of successive changes of temperature from thawing to zero and below, a characteristic of the winter climate of Homeville and its vicinity. Dr. Hayes exhibited the inevitable quinine, iron, and all the tonics in his pharmacopoeia, with cough mixtures and sundry, but in vain. Aunt Polly pressed bottles of sovereign decoctions and infusions upon him﻿—which were received with thanks and neglected with the blackest ingratitude﻿—and exhausted not only the markets of Homeville, but her own and Sairy’s culinary resources (no mean ones, by the way) to tempt the appetite which would not respond. One week followed another without any improvement in his condition; and indeed as time went on he fell into a condition of irritable listlessness which filled his partner with concern.
“What’s the matter with him, Doc?” said David to the physician. “He don’t seem to take no more int’rist than a foundered hoss. Can’t ye do nothin’ for him?”
“Not much use dosin’ him,” replied the doctor. “Pull out all right, may be, come warm weather. Big strong fellow, but this cussed influenzy, or grip, as they call it, sometimes hits them hardest.”
“Wa’al, warm weather ’s some way off,” remarked Mr. Harum, “an’ he coughs enough to tear his head off sometimes.”
The doctor nodded. “Ought to clear out somewhere,” he said. “Don’t like that cough myself.”
“What do you mean?” asked David.
“Ought to go ’way for a spell,” said the doctor; “quit working, and get a change of climate.”
“Have you told him so?” asked Mr. Harum.
“Yes,” replied the doctor; “said he couldn’t get away.”
“H’m’m!” said David thoughtfully, pinching his lower lip between his thumb and finger.

===Chinese===

自童年的百日咳和麻疹之后，哈勒姆合股公司这位小合伙人，据他自己记得，这辈子没病过一天；十二月初头一个星期落到他头上的这场袭击，他带着一种难以置信的厌恶同它对抗，直到有一天早晨他没有下楼吃早饭。随后一星期他在床上度过；期满虽能起身，也是懒懒散散、没精打采，还叫一声纠缠不休的咳嗽摇得七颠八倒。这个季节，即便对那个寒暑表三十六小时里能跳五十度的地方来说，也叫反常；他出房门的当口，正赶上气温一连串地换挡，化冻、零度、零下——霍姆维尔一带冬季气候的看家本领。海斯（Hayes）医生搬出了药典里少不了的奎宁、铁剂和一切补药，配上咳嗽合剂和杂七杂八，全不见效。波莉姨妈把一瓶瓶灵验的汤药煎剂往他身上灌——收下时千恩万谢，撂下时忘恩负义到家——并且把霍姆维尔的市场，连同她自己同赛丽的厨房手艺（话说回来，不是弱手）全部用尽，去逗那个不肯应的胃口。一星期接一星期，病情毫无起色；日子一长，他落进一种烦躁的恹恹里，叫他的合伙人满心挂虑。
「他这是怎么了，大夫？」大卫对医生说。「他那个兴头，还赶不上一匹吃伤了的马。你就没个办法？」
「药是白喂了，」医生答。「兴许天一暖自己就好。块头大，底子壮；可这场该死的流行感冒——他们叫它『格立普』——有时专拣壮的下手。」
「这个嘛，天暖还远着呢，」哈勒姆先生说，「他有时咳得脑袋都要掉下来。」
医生点头。「该出门躲一躲，」他说，「那咳嗽我自己也不喜欢。」
「怎么讲？」大卫问。
「该出去一阵子，」医生说，「撂下活，换换气候。」
「你同他讲过？」哈勒姆先生问。
「讲过，」医生答，「他说走不开。」
「嗯——」大卫若有所思，拇指食指捻着下唇。

===Original===

A day or two after the foregoing interview, John came in and laid an open letter in front of David, who was at his desk, and dropped languidly into a chair without speaking. Mr. Harum read the letter, smiled a little, and turning in his chair, took off his glasses and looked at the young man, who was staring abstractedly at the floor.
“I ben rather expectin’ you’d git somethin’ like this. What be you goin’ to do about it?”
“I don’t know,” replied John. “I don’t like the idea of leasing the property in any case, and certainly not on the terms they offer; but it is lying idle, and I’m paying taxes on it﻿—”
“Wa’al, as I said, I ben expectin’ fer some time they’d be after ye in some shape. You got this this mornin’?”
“Yes.”
“I expect you’d sell the prop’ty if you got a good chance, wouldn’t ye?”
“With the utmost pleasure,” said John emphatically.
“Wa’al, I’ve got a notion they’ll buy it of ye,” said David, “if it’s handled right. I wouldn’t lease it if it was mine an’ I wanted to sell it, an’ yet, in the long run, you might git more out of it﻿—an’ then agin you mightn’t,” he added.
“I don’t know anything about it,” said John, putting his handkerchief to his mouth in a fit of coughing. David looked at him with a frown.
“I ben aware fer some time that the’ was a movement on foot in your direction,” he said. “You know I told ye that I’d ben int’ristid in the oil bus’nis once on a time; an’ I hain’t never quite lost my int’rist, though it hain’t ben a very active one lately, an’ some fellers down there have kep’ me posted some. The’ ’s ben oil found near where you’re located, an’ the prospectin’ points your way. The hull thing has ben kep’ as close as possible, an’ the holes has ben plugged, but the oil is there somewhere. Now it’s like this: If you lease on shares an’ they strike the oil on your prop’ty, mebbe it’ll bring you more money; but they might strike, an’ agin they mightn’t. Sometimes you git a payin’ well an’ a dry hole only a few hunderd feet apart. Nevertheless they want to drill your prop’ty. I know who the parties is. These fellers that wrote this letter are simply actin’ for ’em.”
The speaker was interrupted by another fit of coughing, which left the sufferer very red in the face, and elicited from him the word which is always greeted with laughter in a theater.

===Chinese===

上面这场谈话过后一两天，约翰进来，把一封拆开的信摆在大卫面前——大卫正在写字台前——自己乏乏地落进椅子，一言不发。哈勒姆先生读完信，微微一笑，转过椅子，摘下眼镜，打量这位年轻人；后者正出神地蹬着地板。
「我料着你要来这么一封。打算怎么办？」
「不知道，」约翰答。「把那产业租出去，这个主意我本来就不喜欢；照他们开的条件，更不喜欢；可它闲着，我还年年为它纳税——」
「这个嘛，我说过，我料他们早晚要在你身上打主意，什么样式难说。今早收到的？」
「是。」
「机会好，你肯出手吧？」
「求之不得，」约翰加重语气说。
「这个嘛，我有个预感：办得得法，他们肯买，」大卫说。「要是我要卖，我就不租；可长远算，租分成兴许进项多些——也兴许落空，」他添一句。
「这里头的事我一窍不通，」约翰说，一阵咳嗽，拿手帕掩住嘴。大卫皱眉看他。
「你们那一头有点动静，我知道有些日子了，」他说。「你知道我说过，我从前在石油那行里沾过手；兴头一直没全断，虽说近来不大活动，下面几个伙计还常给我通风。你那块地附近出了油，勘探的指向朝你这边来。整个事情捂得严严实实，井眼全堵着，可油就在那一带的什么地方。如今是这样：你要按分成出租、他们又在你地上打出了油，兴许给你带来的钱多些；可他们打得出，也打不出。有的两口井隔几百英尺，一口出油一口是干窟窿。不管怎样，他们想在你的地上钻。背后是谁我知道；写信这几位，不过是替他们跑腿。」
话音未落又是一阵咳，咳得这位受难者满脸通红，还逼出那个在戏院里总能博得哄笑的字眼。

===Original===

“Say,” said David, after a moment, in which he looked anxiously at his companion, “I don’t like that cough o’ your’n.”
“I don’t thoroughly enjoy it myself,” was the rejoinder.
“Seems to be kind o’ growin’ on ye, don’t it?”
“I don’t know,” said John.
“I was talkin’ with Doc Hayes about ye,” said David, “an’ he allowed you’d ought to have your shoes off an’ run loose a spell.”
John smiled a little, but did not reply.
“Spoke to you about it, didn’t he?” continued David.
“Yes.”
“An’ you told him you couldn’t git away?”
“Yes.”
“Didn’t tell him you wouldn’t go if you could, did ye?”
“I only told him I couldn’t go,” said John.
David sat for a moment thoughtfully tapping the desk with his eyeglasses, and then said with his characteristic chuckle:
“I had a letter f’m Chet Timson yestidy.”
John looked up at him, failing to see the connection.
“Yes,” said David, “he’s out fer a job, an’ the way he writes I guess the dander’s putty well out of him. I reckon the’ hain’t ben nothin’ much but hay in *his* manger fer quite a spell,” remarked Mr. Harum.
“H’m!” said John, raising his brows, conscious of a humane but very faint interest in Mr. Timson’s affairs. Mr. Harum got out a cigar, and, lighting it, gave a puff or two, and continued with what struck the younger man as a perfectly irrelevant question. It really seemed to him as if his senior were making conversation.
“How’s Peleg doin’ these days?” was the query.
“Very well,” was the reply.
“C’n do most anythin’ ’t’s nec’sary, can’t he?”
A brief interruption followed upon the entrance of a man, who, after saying good morning, laid a note on David’s desk, asking for the money on it. Mr. Harum handed it back, indicating John with a motion of his thumb.
The latter took it, looked at the face and back, marked his initials on it with a pencil, and the man went out to the counter.
“If you was fixed so ’t you could git away fer a spell,” said David a moment or two after the customer’s departure, “where would you like to go?”
“I have not thought about it,” said John rather listlessly.
“Wa’al, s’pose you think about it a little now, if you hain’t got no pressin’ engagement. Bus’nis don’t seem to be very rushin’ this mornin’.”
“Why?” said John.
“Because,” said David impressively, “you’re goin’ somewhere right off, quick ’s you c’n git ready, an’ you may ’s well be makin’ up your mind where.”
John looked up in surprise.

===Chinese===

「我说，」过了一刻，大卫不安地看着同伴，「你这个咳嗽我不喜欢。」
「我自己也不大受用，」回答是。
「像是在你身上越长越牢，是不是？」
「不知道，」约翰说。
「我同海斯大夫谈起你，」大卫说，「他看该给你卸下蹄铁，撒出去蹓一阵。」
约翰浅浅一笑，没有接话。
「同你提过这事，对不对？」大卫接着说。
「提过。」
「你告诉他，你走不开？」
「是。」
「没告诉他，能走你也不走吧？」
「我只告诉他，走不开，」约翰说。
大卫坐着，拿眼镜笃笃地敲了一阵桌子，随后带着他那声招牌的轻笑说：
「昨天我收到切特·蒂姆森一封信。」
约翰抬眼望他，看不出这中间的关联。
「是，」大卫说，「他正寻活儿干；照他信上那口气，火气消得差不离了。我估摸，他槽里的草料，这一阵多半只有干草。」哈勒姆先生说。
「嗯，」约翰扬了扬眉，对蒂姆森先生的事抱着一分人道而极淡的兴趣。哈勒姆先生摸出一支雪茄点上，喷了两口，接下去问了一个年轻人看来全不相干的问题——他觉得老伙计简直是在没话找话。
「皮尔这阵子干得怎么样？」是这一问。
「很好，」回答是。
「必要的活计差不多都拿得起来，对吧？」
打这时进来一位客人，道过早安，把一张票据放在大卫桌上要求兑付，插断了一下。哈勒姆先生把票递回，拇指朝约翰一指。后者接过，看看正面背面，铅笔签上自己姓名起首的字母，那人便到柜台那边去了。
「你要是安排得开，能出去一阵，」客人走后一两分钟大卫说，「你想上哪儿？」
「没想过，」约翰恹恹地说。
「这个嘛，你要没什么急事，不妨现在想一想。今儿早晨生意不忙。」
「为什么？」约翰说。
「因为，」大卫郑重地说，「你马上就要出门了，多快收拾得多快；上哪儿，你不妨这就拿定主意。」
约翰惊讶地抬头。

===Original===

“I don’t want to go away,” he said, “and if I did, how could I leave the office?”
“No,” responded Mr. Harum, “you don’t want to make a move of any kind that you don’t actually have to, an’ that’s the reason fer makin’ one. F’m what the doc said, an’ f’m what I c’n see, you got to git out o’ this dum’d climate,” waving his hand toward the window, against which the sleet was beating, “fer a spell; an’ as fur ’s the office goes, Chet Timson ’d be tickled to death to come on an’ help out while you’re away, an’ I guess ’mongst us we c’n mosey along some gait. I ain’t *quite* to the bone-yard yet myself,” he added with a grin.
The younger man sat for a moment or two with brows contracted, and pulling thoughtfully at his moustache.
“There is that matter,” he said, pointing to the letter on the desk.
“Wa’al,” said David, “the’ ain’t no tearin’ hurry ’bout that; an’ anyway, I was goin’ to make you a suggestion to put the matter into my hands to some extent.”
“Will you take it?” said John quickly. “That is exactly what I should wish in any case.”
“If you want I should,” replied Mr. Harum. “Would you want to give full power attorney, or jest have me say ’t I was instructed to act for ye?”
“I think a better way would be to put the property in your name altogether,” said John. “Don’t you think so?”
“Wa’al,” said David, thoughtfully, after a moment, “I hadn’t thought of that, but mebbe I *could* handle the matter better if you was to do that. I know the parties, an’ if the’ was any bluffin’ to be done either side, mebbe it would be better if they thought I was playin’ my own hand.”
At that point Peleg appeared and asked Mr. Lenox a question which took the latter to the teller’s counter. David sat for some time drumming on his desk with the fingers of both hands. A succession of violent coughs came from the front room. His mouth and brows contracted in a wince, and rising, he put on his coat and hat and went slowly out of the bank.

===Chinese===

「我不想出门，」他说，「再说，事务所怎么办？」
「不，」哈勒姆先生应道，「但凡不是非动不可的，你一步也不想动——所以正该动一动。照大夫的话，照我自己的眼睛，你必须离开这个该死的气候，」他朝窗上一摆手，窗上正扑着冻雨，「出去一阵；至于事务所，你不在的时候，切特·蒂姆森巴不得来搭把手；咱们几个凑合着，总走得动几步。我自己离进坟场*还*有一截呢，」他咧嘴一笑。
年轻人坐着，眉心收拢，若有所思地捻着髭须。
「还有那桩事，」他指着桌上的信说。
「这个嘛，」大卫说，「也不急在这一时；反正我正要向你提个议：这件事，放到我手里办一层。」
「您肯接？」约翰急忙说。「这正是我在任何情形下都希望的。」
「你要我接，我就接，」哈勒姆先生答。「你是要一份全权委托，还是只要我说一句：我奉你的指示代行？」
「我看更好的办法是：把那产业整个过到您的名下，」约翰说。「您看呢？」
「这个嘛，」大卫想了一刻，「这我倒没想；不过你若这样办，兴许我*更*好周旋。对方几位我认得；两边要是有一场唬人的牌，让他们当我在打我自己的手，兴许更好。」
正说着，皮尔进来问伦诺克斯先生一句话，把后者引去了出纳柜台。大卫坐着，双手手指在桌上敲了好一阵。前屋传来一连串猛咳。他嘴和眉心一缩，起身穿上外衣、戴上帽子，慢慢走出银行。

## XLV / 四十五
===Original===

The *Vaterland* was advertised to sail at one o’clock, and it wanted but fifteen or twenty minutes of the hour. After assuring himself that his belongings were all together in his stateroom, John made his way to the upper deck and leaning against the rail, watched the bustle of embarkation, somewhat interested in the people standing about, among whom it was difficult in instances to distinguish the passengers from those who were present to say farewell. Near him at the moment were two people, apparently man and wife, of middle age and rather distinguished appearance, to whom presently approached, with some evidence of hurry and with outstretched hand, a very well dressed and pleasant looking man.
“Ah, here you are, Mrs. Ruggles,” John heard him say as he shook hands.
Then followed some commonplaces of good wishes and farewells, and in reply to a question which John did not catch, he heard the lady addressed as Mrs. Ruggles say, “Oh, didn’t you see her? We left her on the lower deck a few minutes ago. Ah, here she comes.”
The man turned and advanced a step to meet the person in question. John’s eyes involuntarily followed the movement, and as he saw her approach his heart contracted sharply: it was Mary Blake. He turned away quickly, and as the collar of his ulster was about his face, for the air of the January day was very keen, he thought that she had not recognized him. A moment later he went aft around the deck-house, and going forward to the smoking-room, seated himself therein, and took the passenger list out of his pocket. He had already scanned it rather cursorily, having but the smallest expectation of coming upon a familiar name, yet feeling sure that, had hers been there, it could not have escaped him. Nevertheless, he now ran his eye over the columns with eager scrutiny, and the hands which held the paper shook a little.
There was no name in the least like Blake. It occurred to him that by some chance or error hers might have been omitted, when his eye caught the following:
William Ruggles
New York.
Mrs. Ruggles
” ”
Mrs. Edward Ruggles
” ”
It was plain to him then. She was obviously traveling with the people whom she had just joined on deck, and it was equally plain that she was Mrs. Edward Ruggles. When he looked up the ship was out in the river.

===Chinese===

「法特兰号」（*Vaterland*）的告示是一点钟启航，此刻离一点只差一刻二十分钟了。约翰先确认行李物件都齐整地聚在舱房里，然后上了上层甲板，倚着栏杆，看登船的那一派忙乱，对站在四下的人群也颇有兴致——其中有些人，还真难分清是乘客还是来送行的。此刻他近旁是两个人，看模样是夫妇，中年，气度相当堂皇；一位衣冠楚楚、面相和善的先生，带着几分匆忙、伸着手，朝他们走了过去。

「啊，您在这儿呢，拉格尔斯太太。」约翰听见他握着手说。

接着是一串祝愿道别的常谈；对一句约翰没听清的问话，他听见被称作拉格尔斯太太的那位夫人说：「哦，您没瞧见她？几分钟前我们把她留在下层甲板了。啊，她来了。」

那位先生转过身，上前一步迎那人说的人。约翰的眼睛不由自主地跟着这一动；看清走近的人，他的心猛地一缩：是玛丽·布莱克。他迅速别过脸去；一月的天气风头极锐，乌尔斯特大衣的领子拢着半张脸，他想着，她没有认出他。过了片刻，他绕过舱房朝船尾去，又折到前头的吸烟室，在里面落了座，从口袋里掏出乘客名单。这名单他先前已草草扫过一遍——本就没抱多大指望撞见熟名字；却又笃定，她的名字若真在上头，绝逃不过他的眼。尽管如此，此刻他还是急切地逐栏细搜了一遍，捧着纸的那双手，微微发抖。

名单上没有一个名字同布莱克沾一点边。他忽然想：兴许出于什么偶然或差错，她的名字漏掉了——正想着，他的目光撞见了下面几行：

威廉·拉格尔斯　　纽约。

拉格尔斯太太　　同上。

爱德华·拉格尔斯太太　　同上。

这一下全明白了。她显然是同方才在甲板上会合的那几位同行；而同样明白的是：她就是爱德华·拉格尔斯太太。他抬起头时，船已经开进了河面。

## XLVI / 四十六
===Original===

John had been late in applying for his passage, and in consequence, the ship being very full, had had to take what berth he could get, which happened to be in the second cabin. The occupants of these quarters, however, were not rated as second-class passengers. The *Vaterland* took none such on her outward voyages, and all were on the same footing as to the fare and the freedom of the ship. The captain and the orchestra appeared at dinner in the second saloon on alternate nights, and the only disadvantage in the location was that it was very far aft; unless it could be considered a drawback that the furnishings were of plain wood and plush instead of carving, gilding, and stamped leather. In fact, as the voyage proceeded, our friend decided that the afterdeck was pleasanter than the one amidships, and the cozy second-class smoking-room more agreeable than the large and gorgeous one forward.
Consequently, for a while he rarely went across the bridge which spanned the opening between the two decks. It may be that he had a certain amount of reluctance to encounter Mrs. Edward Ruggles.
The roof of the second cabin deck-house was, when there was not too much wind, a favorite place with him. It was not much frequented, as most of those who spent their time on deck apparently preferred a place nearer amidships. He was sitting there on the morning of the fifth day out, looking idly over the sea, with an occasional glance at the people who were walking on the promenade-deck below, or leaning on the rail which bounded it. He turned at a slight sound behind him, and rose with his hat in his hand. The flush in his face, as he took the hand which was offered him, reflected the color in the face of the owner, but the grayish brown eyes, which he remembered so well, looked into his, a little curiously, perhaps, but frankly and kindly. She was the first to speak.
“How do you do, Mr. Lenox?” she said.
“How do you do, Mrs. Ruggles?” said John, throwing up his hand as, at the moment of his reply, a puff of wind blew the cape of his mackintosh over his head. They both laughed a little (this was their greeting after nearly six years), and sat down.
“What a nice place!” she said, looking about her.
“Yes,” said John; “I sit here a good deal when it isn’t too windy.”
“I have been wondering why I did not get a sight of you,” she said. “I saw your name in the passenger list.

===Chinese===

约翰订舱订得迟，船又满得厉害，只好有什么铺位拿什么，落在二等舱。不过住这一区的，并不被当作二等乘客。「法特兰号」出航从不载二等票，人人船资同一、行动的范围也同一。船长和乐队隔天晚上到二等餐厅用晚餐；这片位置唯一的不便，是太靠船尾——除非把装潢算作一条：这里的陈设是素木加长毛绒，不是雕花、贴金和压花皮子。而随着航程展开，我们这位朋友认定：尾甲板比船腰的甲板惬意，二等那间暖和的小吸烟室，也比前头那间又大又华丽的更称心。

于是有一阵子，他难得横跨那道连接两片甲板的天桥。兴许，他对撞见爱德华·拉格尔斯太太，存着几分不愿。

风不大的时候，二等舱舱房的房顶是他的一处宝地。那儿人迹寥寥——在甲板上耗时光的多数人，显然更中意靠船腰的地界。启航第五天的早晨，他正坐在那儿，闲闲地望海，偶尔瞥一眼下层散步甲板上来回走动的、或倚着围栏的人。背后一声轻响，他回头，手摘下帽，站起身来。他握住递来的手时，脸上那一阵红，映着对方脸上的红；可那双灰褐的眼睛——他记得多清楚的一双——望着他的眼睛，兴许带一点好奇，却坦率，而且和善。她先开的口。

「你好吗，伦诺克斯先生？」她说。

「你好吗，拉格尔斯太太？」约翰说，答话的当口一股风把雨披的披肩兜上了头，他抬手一挡。两人都轻笑了一声（这便是他们分别近六年后的重逢礼），坐下了。

「这地方真好！」她说，四下看了看。

「是啊，」约翰说，「风不太大的时候，我常坐这儿。」

「我正纳闷怎么总瞧不见你。」她说。「我在乘客名单上见到了你的名字。

===Original===

Have you been ill?”
“I’m in the second cabin,” he said, smiling.
She looked at him a little incredulously, and he explained.
“Ah, yes,” she said, “I saw your name, but as you did not appear in the dining saloon, I thought you must either be ill or that you did not sail. Did you know that I was on board?” she asked.
It was rather an embarrassing question.
“I have been intending,” he replied rather lamely, “to make myself known to you﻿—that is, to﻿—well, make my presence on board known to you. I got just a glimpse of you before we sailed, when you came up to speak to a man who had been saying goodbye to Mr. and Mrs. Ruggles. I heard him speak their name, and looking over the passenger list I identified you as Mrs. Edward Ruggles.”
“Ah,” she said, looking away for an instant, “I did not know that you had seen me, and I wondered how you came to address me as Mrs. Ruggles just now.”
“That was how,” said John; and then, after a moment, “it seems rather odd, doesn’t it, that we should be renewing an acquaintance on an ocean steamer as we did once before, so many years ago? and that the first bit of intelligence that I have had of you in all the years since I saw you last should come to me through the passenger list?”
“Did you ever try to get any?” she asked. “I have always thought it very strange that we should never have heard anything about you.”
“I went to the house once, some weeks after you had gone,” said John, “but the man in charge was out, and the maid could tell me nothing.”
“A note I wrote you at the time of your father’s death,” she said, “we found in my small nephew’s overcoat pocket after we had been some time in California; but I wrote a second one before we left New York, telling you of our intended departure, and where we were going.”
“I never received it,” he said. Neither spoke for a while, and then:
“Tell me of your sister and brother-in-law,” he said.
“My sister is at present living in Cambridge, where Jack is at college,” was the reply; “but poor Julius died two years ago.”
“Ah,” said John, “I am grieved to hear of Mr. Carling’s death. I liked him very much.”
“He liked you very much,” she said, “and often spoke of you.”
There was another period of silence, so long, indeed, as to be somewhat embarrassing. None of the thoughts which followed each other in John’s mind was of the sort which he felt like broaching.

===Chinese===

「你病了不成？」

「我在二等舱。」他笑着说。

她有点不信地看他，他解释了一遍。

「啊，原来如此，」她说，「名字我是见着了，可餐厅里总不见你人，我只当你病了，要不就是没上船。你知道我在船上吗？」她问。

这问题相当不好接。

「我本打算，」他答得颇有些无力，「去同你相认——就是说，去——嗯，让你知道我也在船上。开船前我勿勿瞥见了你一眼，你上来同一个刚同拉格尔斯先生夫妇道别的人说话。我听见他提起他们的姓氏，再一对乘客名单，就认出你是爱德华·拉格尔斯太太。」

「啊，」她说，把目光移开了一瞬，「我不知道你看见了我；我还在纳闷，你方才怎么开口就叫我拉格尔斯太太。」

「就是这么认出来的。」约翰说；随后，停了一停：「咱们在一艘远洋轮上重续旧识，同多年前那回一模一样——这不有点奇吗？而自上次一别，这些年里我得到关于你的头一条音信，竟是从一张乘客名单上来的？」

「你想打听过吗？」她问。「我们竟从没听到过你一点消息，我一直觉得这事很怪。」

「你们走后几个星期，我上过一回那宅子，」约翰说，「管事的出去了，女仆又说不出个所以然。」

「你父亲过世那阵子我给你写的一封短信，」她说，「我们在加州住了些日子之后，在我那小外甥的大衣口袋里找着了；离开纽约之前我还写了第二封，告诉你我们要走、去哪儿。」

「我从没收到。」他说。两人都沉默了一阵，然后：

「跟我说说令姐和姐夫。」他说。

「姐姐眼下住在坎布里奇，杰克在那儿上大学。」回答是；「可怜的朱利叶斯，两年前去世了。」

「啊，」约翰说，「卡林先生过世，我听了难过。我从前很喜欢他。」

「他也很喜欢你，」她说，「常提起你。」

又是一阵沉默，长得竟有些难堪。约翰脑子里一个接一个转过的念头，没有一条是他觉得拿得出手的。

===Original===

He realized that the situation was getting awkward, and that consciousness added to the confusion of his ideas. But if his companion shared his embarrassment, neither her face nor her manner betrayed it as at last she said, turning, and looking frankly at him:
“You seem very little changed. Tell me about yourself. Tell me something of your life in the last six years.”
During the rest of the voyage they were together for a part of every day, sometimes with the company of Mrs. William Ruggles, but more often without it, as her husband claimed much of her attention and rarely came on deck; and John, from time to time, gave his companion pretty much the whole history of his later career. But with regard to her own life, and, as he noticed, especially the two years since the death of her brother-in-law, she was distinctly reticent. She never spoke of her marriage or her husband, and after one or two faintly tentative allusions, John forebore to touch upon those subjects, and was driven to conclude that her experience had not been a happy one. Indeed, in their intercourse there were times when she appeared distrait and even moody; but on the whole she seemed to him to be just as he had known and loved her years ago; and all the feeling that he had had for her then broke forth afresh in spite of himself﻿—in spite of the fact that, as he told himself, it was more hopeless than ever: absolutely so, indeed.
It was the last night of their voyage together. The Ruggleses were to leave the ship the next morning at Algiers, where they intended to remain for some time.
“Would you mind going to the afterdeck?” he asked. “These people walking about fidget me,” he added rather irritably.
She rose, and they made their way aft. John drew a couple of chairs near to the rail. “I don’t care to sit down for the present,” she said, and they stood looking out at sea for a while in silence.
“Do you remember,” said John at last, “a night six years ago when we stood together, at the end of the voyage, leaning over the rail like this?”
“Yes,” she said.
“Does this remind you of it?” he asked.
“I was thinking of it,” she said.
“Do you remember the last night I was at your house?” he asked, looking straight out over the moonlit water.
“Yes,” she said again.
“Did you know that night what was in my heart to say to you?”
There was no answer.
“May I tell you now?”

===Chinese===

他觉出局面正在变僵，这份自觉又给他心里的乱添了一层。可他的同伴即便同样窘迫，脸上做派里却一点不露；末了她转过身，坦率地望着他说：

「你几乎没变。同我说说你自己。说说这六年你的一些日子。」

此后一路航程，两人每天都做伴一阵，有时威廉·拉格尔斯太太也在，多数时候不在——她丈夫离不得她的照应，自己又难得登甲板；约翰则断断续续地，把自己后几年的履历差不多全数讲给了她。至于她自己的生活——约翰留意到，尤其是姐夫过世后的这两年——她明显地缄默。她的婚姻、她的丈夫，她绝口不提；约翰试着轻描淡写地碰过一两回，也就不再沾这两个题目，只能得出结论：她这段经历并不快活。真的，两人相处间，她时时显得心不在焉，甚至郁郁；可总体上，他觉得她还是多年前他认得、他恋着的那个人；于是当年他对她的那份情，全数重新烧了起来，由不得他——尽管照他对自己说的，这情比从前更没有指望：简直是彻底没有。

这是他们同船的最后一夜。拉格尔斯一家明早要在阿尔及尔下船，打算在那儿盘桓些时日。

「去尾甲板坐坐，可好？」他问。「这些走来走去的人搅得我心慌。」他补了一句，语气颇为烦躁。

她起身，两人一路往船尾去。约翰拖了两把躺椅到栏杆边。「我暂时不想坐。」她说；两人便站着，默默望了一会儿海。

「你还记得吗，」约翰终于开口，「六年前的那个夜晚，航程将尽，我们也这样并排倚着栏杆？」

「记得。」她说。

「此刻让你想起它了吗？」他问。

「我正在想它。」她说。

「你还记得我上你家的最后一晚吗？」他问，目光直直投向月光下的海面。

「记得。」她又说。

「那一晚，你可知道我心里要对你说的是什么？」

没有回答。

「现在，容我说出来吗？」

===Original===

he asked, giving a side glance at her profile, which in the moonlight showed very white.
“Do you think you ought?” she answered in a low voice, “or that I ought to listen to you?”
“I know,” he exclaimed. “You think that as a married woman you should not listen, and that knowing you to be one I should not speak. If it were to ask anything of you I would not. It is for the first and last time. Tomorrow we part again, and for all time, I suppose. I have carried the words that were on my lips that night all these years in my heart. I know I can have no response﻿—I expect none; but it can not harm you if I tell you that I loved you then, and have﻿—”
She put up her hand in protest.
“You must not go on, Mr. Lenox,” she said, turning to him, “and I must leave you.”
“Are you very angry with me?” he asked humbly.
She turned her face to the sea again and gave a sad little laugh.
“Not so much as I ought to be,” she answered; “but you yourself have given the reason why you should not say such things, and why I should not listen, and why I ought to say good night.”
“Ah, yes,” he said bitterly; “of course you are right, and this is to be the end.”
She turned and looked at him for a moment. “You will never again speak to me as you have tonight, will you?” she asked.
“I should not have said what I did had I not thought I should never see you again after tomorrow,” said John, “and I am not likely to do that, am I?”
“If I could be sure,” she said hesitatingly, and as if to herself.
“Well,” said John eagerly. She stood with her eyes downcast for a moment, one hand resting on the rail, and then she looked up.
“We expect to stay in Algiers about two months,” she said, “and then we are going to Naples to visit some friends for a few days, about the time you told me you thought you might be there. Perhaps it would be better if we said goodbye tonight; but if after we get home you are to spend your days in Homeville and I mine in New York, we shall not be likely to meet, and, except on this side of the ocean, we may, as you say, never see each other again. So, if you wish, you may come to see me in Naples if you happen to be there when we are. I am sure after tonight that I may trust you, may I not? But,” she added, “perhaps you would not care. I am treating you very frankly; but from your standpoint you would expect or excuse more frankness than if I were a young girl.”

===Chinese===

他问，侧眼望了望她的侧影——月光下，白得很。

「你觉得该说吗？」她低声答，「或者，我该听吗？」

「我知道，」他喊道。「你想的是：身为有夫之妇，你不该听；明知你是有夫之妇，我不该说。若是向你求什么，我不会开这个口。这是头一遭，也是末一遭。明天我们又要分别了——我想，是永别。那一晚到了我唇边的话，这些年我一直揣在心里。我知道不会有回应——我也不指望；可如果我告诉你，那时我爱着你，如今还——这总伤不着你。」

她抬起手止住他。

「不能再说了，伦诺克斯先生。」她说，转向他，「我也该走了。」

「你很生我的气吗？」他谦卑地问。

她重新把脸朝向海，发出一声小小的、悲哀的笑。

「没有我该有的那么气。」她答，「可不该说这些话的理由、不该听的理由、该道晚安的理由——你自己都说了。」

「啊，是，」他苦涩地说，「自然你是对的，这就该是终局了。」

她转过身，看了他片刻。「今晚这样的话，你不会再对我说了，是不是？」她问。

「要不是想着明天之后再见不着你，方才那些话我不会说。」约翰说，「而再见着，多半也不会了，是不是？」

「要是我能拿得准——」她迟疑着说，像是说给自己。

「拿得准什么？」约翰急切地问。她垂眼立了片刻，一只手搭在栏杆上，随后抬起头来。

「我们打算在阿尔及尔住两个月上下，」她说，「然后去那不勒斯，看望几位朋友，待几天——差不多就在你跟我说你兴许也到那儿的时节。也许今晚就道别更好；可回了国之后，你把日子放在霍姆维尔，我把日子放在纽约，再碰面的机会实在不大；而且照你说的，除非在大洋这一边，我们兴许就此永不再见。所以，你若愿意，到那不勒斯可以来看我——前提是你我恰好都在。今晚之后我信得过你，是不是？不过，」她添一句，「兴许你并不在意。我待你这般直白；可在你的立场上，换我不是个年轻姑娘，你原就料得到、也担待得起这多一分直白。」

===Original===

“I care very much,” he declared, “and it will be a happiness to me to see you on any footing, and you may trust me never to break bounds again.” She made a motion as if to depart.
“Don’t go just yet,” he said pleadingly; “there is now no reason why you should for a while, is there? Let us sit here in this gorgeous night a little longer, and let me smoke a cigar.”
At the moment he was undergoing a revulsion of feeling. His state of mind was like that of an improvident debtor who, while knowing that the note must be paid some time, does not quite realize it for a while after an extension. At last the cigar was finished. There had been but little said between them.
“I really must go,” she said, and he walked with her across the hanging bridge and down the deck to the gangway door.
“Where shall I address you to let you know when we shall be in Naples?” she asked as they were about to separate.
“Care of Cook & Son,” he said. “You will find the address in Baedeker.”
He saw her the next morning long enough for a touch of the hand and a goodbye before the bobbing, tubby little boat with its Arab crew took the Ruggleses on board.

===Chinese===

「我在意，很在意。」他声明，「任何名分之下见得着你，都是我的欢喜；你也信得过我：越界的事，不会再有。」她动了动，像要走。

「先别走。」他央求道，「眼下这片刻，你没有非走不可的道理了，是不是？让我们在这华美的夜里再坐一会儿，容我抽支雪茄。」

此刻他正经历一场心绪的倒流。他的心境，像那个不会过日子的欠债人：明知期票早晚要付，展期之后的好一阵子，却还没真切地意识到它。雪茄终于抽完了。两人之间，话说得很少。

「我真得走了。」她说；他陪她跨过悬桥，沿甲板送到舷门。

「往哪儿给你写信，告诉你我们什么时候到那不勒斯？」临别时她问。

「库克父子旅行社转。」他说。「贝德克尔指南上找得到地址。」

第二天早晨他又见了她一面——只够一握手、一道别；随后那条颠颠簸簸、圆滚滚的小船，载着阿拉伯船员，把拉格勒斯一家接了上去。

## XLVII / 四十七
===Original===

How John Lenox tried to kill time during the following two months, and how time retaliated during the process, it is needless to set forth. It may not, however, be wholly irrelevant to note that his cough had gradually disappeared, and that his appetite had become good enough to carry him through the average table d’hôte dinner. On the morning after his arrival at Naples he found a cable dispatch at the office of Cook & Son, as follows: “Sixty cash, forty stock. Stock good. Harum.”
“God bless the dear old boy!” said John fervently. The Pennsylvania property was sold at last; and if “stock good” was true, the dispatch informed him that he was, if not a rich man for modern days, still, as David would have put it, “wuth consid’able.” No man, I take it, is very likely to receive such a piece of news without satisfaction; but if our friend’s first sensation was one of gratification, the thought which followed had a drop of bitterness in it. “If I could only have had it before!” he said to himself; and indeed many of the disappointments of life, if not the greater part, come because events are unpunctual. They have a way of arriving sometimes too early, or worse, too late.
Another circumstance detracted from his satisfaction: a note he expected did not appear among the other communications waiting him at the bankers, and his mind was occupied for the while with various conjectures as to the reason, none of which was satisfactory. Perhaps she had changed her mind. Perhaps﻿—a score of things! Well, there was nothing for it but to be as patient as possible and await events. He remembered that she had said she was to visit some friends by the name of Hartleigh, and she had told him the name of their villa, but for the moment he did not remember it. In any case he did not know the Hartleighs, and if she had changed her mind﻿—as was possibly indicated by the omission to send him word﻿—well﻿—! He shrugged his shoulders, mechanically lighted a cigarette, and strolled down and out of the Piazza Martiri and across to the Largo della Vittoria. He had a half-formed idea of walking back through the Villa Nazionale, spending an hour at the Aquarium, and then to his hotel for luncheon.

===Chinese===

此后两个月约翰·伦诺克斯怎样杀时间、时间又怎样在过程中报复他，不必细表。不过记上一笔也不算离题：他的咳嗽渐渐断了根，胃口好到吃得下寻常客店的包饭。到那不勒斯的第二天早晨，他在库克父子公司（Cook & Son）的办事处收到一封电报：「六十现款，四十股票。股票可靠。哈勒姆。」
「上帝保佑这位亲爱的老人家！」约翰热热地说。宾夕法尼亚那块地终于出手了；倘若「股票可靠」属实，这封电报通知他：按现代的标准说不上富，但照大卫的说法，「值不少」。我想，谁接到这样一条消息都不会不快意；可我们这位朋友头一阵的快意之后，跟着的那念头带着一滴苦。「要是早一点到手就好了！」他自语；人生许多失意——若说不上大半——实在都因为事件不守时：它们有时来得太早，更糟的是，太晚。
另一桩事折损了他的满意：他指望的一张短简，没有出现在银行留给他的那叠信件里；一时他的心全叫种种猜测占着，没有一条猜测令人满意。也许她变了主意。也许——二十样事！唉，没奈何，只有尽量耐着，等事情自己来。他记得她说过要去访几位姓哈特利（Hartleigh）的朋友，也告诉过他那别墅的名字；一时却想不起来。反正哈特利家他不认识；她若真变了主意——没给他捎话，也许正说明这个——唉——！他耸耸肩，机械地点上一支纸烟，踱下烈士广场，穿到胜利广场那面。心里有个半成的主意：从国民花园一路走回去，在水族馆消磨一个钟头，然后回旅馆吃午饭。

===Original===

It occurred to him at the moment that there was a steamer from Genoa on the Monday following, that he was tired of wandering about aimlessly and alone, and that there was really no reason why he should not take the said steamer and go home. Occupied with these reflections, he absently observed, just opposite to him across the way, a pair of large bay horses in front of a handsome landau. A coachman in livery was on the box, and a small footman, very much coated and silk-hatted, was standing about; and, as he looked, two ladies came out of the arched entrance to the court of the building before which the equipage was halted, and the small footman sprang to the carriage door.
One of the ladies was a stranger to him, but the other was Mrs. William Ruggles; and John, seeing that he had been recognized, at once crossed over to the carriage; and presently, having accepted an invitation to breakfast, found himself sitting opposite them on his way to the Villa Violante. The conversation during the drive up to the Vomero need not be detailed. Mrs. Hartleigh arrived at the opinion that our friend was rather a dull person. Mrs. Ruggles, as he had found out, was usually rather taciturn. Neither is it necessary to say very much of the breakfast, nor of the people assembled.
It appeared that several guests had departed the previous day, and the people at table consisted only of Mr. and Mrs. Ruggles, Mary, Mr. and Mrs. Hartleigh and their two daughters, and John, whose conversation was mostly with his host, and was rather desultory. In fact, there was during the meal a perceptible air of something like disquietude. Mr. Ruggles in particular said almost nothing, and wore an appearance of what seemed like anxiety. Once he turned to his host: “When ought I to get an answer to that cable, Hartleigh? today, do you think?”
“Yes, I should say so without doubt,” was the reply, “if it’s answered promptly, and in fact there’s plenty of time. Remember that we are about six hours earlier than New York by the clock, and it’s only about seven in the morning over there.”
---
Coffee was served on the balustraded platform of the flight of marble steps leading down to the grounds below.
“Mary,” said Mrs. Hartleigh, when cigarettes had been offered, “don’t you want to show Mr. Lenox something of La Violante?”

===Chinese===

此刻他想起：下礼拜一热那亚有船；自己已经逛倦了，漫无目的又独自一人；实在没有理由不搭那条船回家。正想着，他无意识地望见，对面街前一驾漂亮的双排座四轮马车，驾着两匹配对的大枣红马。车座上坐着穿号衣的车夫；一个小个子的跟班，裹得厚厚的、戴丝帽，在旁边侍立；正看着，两位女士从马车停着的那座楼前拱形门洞里出来，小跟班一个箭步抢到车门边。
两位女士中有一位他不认得；另一位却是威廉·拉格尔斯太太。约翰看见自己已被认出，立刻穿过街走向马车；不多时，他应下了共进早餐的邀请，发现自己同她们对面而坐，正往维奥兰特别墅（Villa Violante）去。上沃梅罗山一路的谈话不必细表。哈特利太太得出的结论是：我们这位朋友是个相当乏味的人。拉格尔斯太太呢——他早已领教——向来寡言。早餐本身，以及在座的诸位，也不必多说。
头一天走了几位客人，此刻餐桌边只有拉格尔斯先生伉俪、玛丽、哈特利先生伉俪和他们的两位小姐，外加约翰；约翰的话多半同男主人应酬，也是有一搭没一搭。说实在的，这顿饭自始至终浮着一层近乎不安的气氛。拉格尔斯先生尤其几乎一言不发，一脸像是焦灼的神色。有一回他转向主人：「那封回电什么时候该到，哈特利？你说今天？」
「是，我看准没错，」回答是，「只要那边即刻回；再说时间宽裕得很。记住：我们这儿比纽约的钟早约莫六个钟头，那边才早晨七点来钟。」
---
咖啡摆在那一列通下面园子的大理石阶顶头带栏杆的平台上。
「玛丽，」敬过纸烟后，哈特利太太说，「不想带伦诺克斯先生看看拉维奥兰特的景致吗？」

===Original===

“I shall take you to my favorite place,” she said, as they descended the steps together.
The southern front of the grounds of the Villa Violante is bounded and upheld by a wall of tufa fifty feet in height and some four hundred feet long. About midway of its length a semicircular bench of marble, with a rail, is built out over one of the buttresses. From this point is visible the whole bay and harbor of Naples, and about one third of the city lies in sight, five hundred feet below. To the left one sees Vesuvius and the Sant’ Angelo chain, which the eye follows to Sorrento. Straight out in front stands Capri, and to the right the curve of the bay, ending at Posilipo. The two, John and his companion, halted near the bench, and leaned upon the parapet of the wall for a while in silence. From the streets below rose no rumble of traffic, no sound of hoof or wheel; but up through three thousand feet of distance came from here and there the voices of street-venders, the clang of a bell, and ever and anon the pathetic supplication of a donkey. Absolute quiet prevailed where they stood, save for these upcoming sounds. The April sun, deliciously warm, drew a smoky odor from the hedge of box with which the parapet walk was bordered, in and out of which darted small green lizards with the quickness of little fishes.
John drew a long breath.
“I don’t believe there is another such view in the world,” he said. “I do not wonder that this is your favorite spot.”
“Yes,” she said, “you should see the grounds﻿—the whole place is superb﻿—but this is the glory of it all, and I have brought you straight here because I wanted to see it with you, and this may be the only opportunity.”
“What do you mean?” he asked apprehensively.
“You heard Mr. Ruggles’s question about the cable dispatch?” she said.
“Yes.”
“Well,” she said, “our plans have been very much upset by some things he has heard from home. We came on from Algiers ten days earlier than we had intended, and if the reply to Mr. Ruggles’s cable is unfavorable, we are likely to depart for Genoa tomorrow and take the steamer for home on Monday. The reason why I did not send a note to your bankers,” she added, “was that we came on the same boat that I intended to write by; and Mr. Hartleigh’s man has inquired for you every day at Cook’s so that Mr. Hartleigh might know of your coming and call upon you.”

===Chinese===

「我带你上我心爱的那一处去，」两人一同下台阶时她说。
维奥兰特别墅园子的南沿，由一道五十英尺高、四百英尺来长的凝灰岩墙托住圈定。墙身中段，有一张半圆形的大理石长凳，带栏杆，从一座扶壁上凌空探出。从这一点上望得见那不勒斯整个海湾和港埠，全城约莫三分之一收在眼底，在五百英尺之下。左边是维苏威和圣安杰洛山脉，目光一路可以追到索伦托。正前方海上立着卡普里；右边是海湾的弯，收在波西利波。两人——约翰同他的同伴——在长凳附近站住，倚着矮墙的护栏，静了好一阵。底下街上来不着一辆车的辘辘，听不见蹄声轮声；只有三两千英尺的距离下，这里那里升上来小贩的叫卖、一声钟响，间或一声驴子哀哀的恳求。他们站的地方一片绝对的静，只有这些上浮的声音。四月的太阳，暖得受用，把黄杨树篱熏出一股烟一样的香——护栏步道两边就镶着这样的篱；小小的绿蜥蜴像小鱼一样利索，在篱间穿进穿出。
约翰长吸一口气。
「我不信世上还有第二幅这样的景，」他说。「这里是你心爱的去处，我不奇怪。」
「是，」她说，「园子你也该看看——整个地方都堂皇——可这一切的荣耀全在这儿；我径直把你带到这里来，因为我要同你一起看它，而这也许仅此一回。」
「什么意思？」他不无忧虑地问。
「拉格尔斯先生问回电那句话，你听见了？」她说。
「听见了。」
「嗯，」她说，「家里的几条消息把我们的计划全打乱了。我们从阿尔及尔提前了十天动身；倘若拉格尔斯先生那封电报的回音不利，明天我们就多半要动身去热那亚，礼拜一搭船回家。我没有往你银行送短简的缘故，」她添一句，「是我们搭的就是我原打算写信托的那条船；哈特利先生的仆人天天到库克公司去问，一等你到就告诉哈特利先生，好让他来拜你。」

===Original===

John gave a little exclamation of dismay. Her face was very still as she gazed out over the sea with half-closed eyes. He caught the scent of the violets in the bosom of her white dress.
“Let us sit down,” she said at last. “I have something I wish to say to you.”
He made no rejoinder as they seated themselves, and during the moment or two of silence in which she seemed to be meditating how to begin, he sat bending forward, holding his stick with both hands between his knees, absently prodding holes in the gravel.
“I think,” she began, “that if I did not believe the chances were for our going tomorrow, I would not say it today.” John bit his lip and gave the gravel a more vigorous punch. “But I have felt that I must say it to you some time before we saw the last of each other, whenever that time should be.”
“Is it anything about what happened on board ship?” he asked in a low voice.
“Yes,” she replied, “it concerns all that took place on board ship, or nearly all, and I have had many misgivings about it. I am afraid that I did wrong, and I am afraid, too, that in your secret heart you would admit it.”
“No, never!” he exclaimed. “If there was any wrong done, it was wholly of my own doing. I was alone to blame. I ought to have remembered that you were married, and perhaps﻿—yes, I did remember it in a way, but I could not realize it. I had never seen or heard of your husband, or heard of your marriage. He was a perfectly unreal person to me, and you﻿—you seemed only the Mary Blake that I had known, and as I had known you. I said what I did that night upon an impulse which was as unpremeditated as it was sudden. I don’t see how you were wrong. You couldn’t have foreseen what took place﻿—and﻿—”
“Have you not been sorry for what took place?” she asked, with her eyes on the ground. “Have you not thought the less of me since?”
He turned and looked at her. There was a little smile upon her lips and on her downcast eyes.
“No, by Heaven!” he exclaimed desperately, “I have not, and I am not sorry. Whether I ought to have said what I did or not, it was true, and I wanted you to know﻿—”
He broke off as she turned to him with a smile and a blush. The smile was almost a laugh.
“But, John,” she said, “I am not Mrs. Edward Ruggles. I am Mary Blake.”
---
The parapet was fifty feet above the terrace. The hedge of box was an impervious screen.

===Chinese===

约翰低低惊呼了一声。她的脸非常静，半阖着眼望海。他嗅到她白衣裙襟上的紫罗兰香。
「坐下吧，」她终于说，「我有话要对你说。」
落座时他没有接话；她像在斟酌怎样开头的那一两息静默里，他前倾着坐，两手把司的克夹在膝间，心不在焉地在砾石地上戳着一个个小洞。
「我想，」她开口，「若不是我认定明天多半要走，我今天也不会说。」约翰咬住嘴唇，把砾石捣得更起劲。「可我一直觉得：在我们彼此见最后一面之前——不管那是什么时候——总得对你说。」
「是船上发生的那件事吗？」他低声问。
「是，」她答，「同船上发生的一切有关——差不多一切；为它我起过许多疑悔。我怕我做错了；我还怕，你心底里也认它错。」
「不，从来没有！」他喊。「若真有错，也全是我一个人的。要怪只怪我。我本该记得你已嫁人；也许——是，我总算记得，只是没法当真。你丈夫我没见过，你的婚事我没听人提过。他对我是个全然虚的人物；而你——你只像我从前的玛丽·布莱克，同我认识的你一模一样。那晚的话，我是在一阵冲动里说的，来得突然，又全无预谋。我看不出你有什么错。后来的事你无从预见——而且——」
「后来的事，你没有后悔过吗？」她问，眼睛望着地。「打那以后，你没有看轻过我？」
他转过脸看她。她唇边、低垂的眼上都挂着一点笑。
「没有，老天在上！」他不顾一切地喊，「从来没有，我也不悔。那晚的话该不该说是一回事；它是真的，我要你知道——」
她含笑带红地转向他，他剎住了。那笑几乎就是一声笑出声的笑。
「可是约翰，」她说，「我不是爱德华·拉格尔斯太太。我是玛丽·布莱克。」

Well, and then, after a little of that sort of thing, they both began hurriedly to admire the view again, for someone was coming. But it was only one of the gardeners, who did not understand English; and confidence being once more restored, they fell to discussing﻿—everything.
“Do you think you could live in Homeville, dear?” asked John after a while.
“I suppose I shall have to, shall I not?” said Mary. “And are you, too, really happy, John?”
John instantly proved to her that he was. “But it almost makes me unhappy,” he added, “to think how nearly we have missed each other. If I had only known in the beginning that you were not Mrs. Edward Ruggles!”
Mary laughed joyously. The mistake which a moment before had seemed almost tragic now appeared delightfully funny.
“The explanation is painfully simple,” she answered. “Mrs. Edward Ruggles﻿—the real one﻿—did expect to come on the *Vaterland*, whereas I did not. But the day before the steamer sailed she was summoned to Andover by the serious illness of her only son, who is at school there. I took her ticket, got ready overnight﻿—I like to start on these unpremeditated journeys﻿—and here I am.” John put his arm about her to make sure of this, and kept it there﻿—lest he should forget. “When we met on the steamer and I saw the error you had made I was tempted﻿—and yielded﻿—to let you go on uncorrected. But,” she added, looking lovingly up into John’s eyes, “I’m glad you found out your mistake at last.”

好了，随后，那类事情小小一阵过后，两人都赶忙重新赞叹起风景来，因为有人来了。来的不过是个园丁，不懂英语；胆子一旦复元，他们又谈论起来——什么都谈。
「你看你住得惯霍姆维尔吗，亲爱的？」过了一阵约翰问。
「我想我非住惯不可，是不是？」玛丽说。「你也真的快活吗，约翰？」
约翰当场向她证明了他是。「可是一想到我们差点错过，我又几乎要不快活，」他添一句，「早知道你不是爱德华·拉格尔斯太太就好了！」
玛丽欢畅地笑了。方才还近乎悲壮的那场误会，此刻显得妙不可言。
「解释起来简单得叫人难过，」她答。「爱德华·拉格尔斯太太——真正的那位——本来是要搭*伐特兰号*（Vaterland）的；我本不是。开船前一天，她独子在安多弗上学，得了重病，把她叫了去。我顶了她的票，连夜收拾——我就喜欢这种说走就走的行程——于是到了这儿。」约翰伸出胳膊搂住她，落实这一点，并且一直搂着——怕忘。「船上相遇、看出你认错了人的那会儿，我起了个念头——也就依了——由你错下去。不过，」她仰头爱爱地望进约翰的眼睛，添一句，「你到底发现了这个错，我高兴。」

## XLVIII / 四十八
===Original===

A fortnight later Mr. Harum sat at his desk in the office of Harum & Co. There were a number of letters for him, but the one he opened first bore a foreign stamp, and was postmarked “Napoli.” That he was deeply interested in the contents of this epistle was manifest from the beginning, not only from the expression of his face, but from the frequent “wa’al, wa’als” which were elicited as he went on; but interest grew into excitement as he neared the close, and culminated as he read the last few lines.
“Scat my **cats**!” he cried, and, grabbing his hat and the letter, he bolted out of the back door in the direction of the house, leaving the rest of his correspondence to be digested﻿—any time.

===Chinese===

两星期后，哈勒姆先生坐在哈勒姆公司办事处的书桌前。桌上有他一搭信，可他头一封拆的，贴着外国邮票，邮戳是「拿坡里」。这封信的内容如何攋住了他，打头就一目了然——不光看他的脸色，也看他一路读下去不时冒出的那声声「这个嘛、这个嘛」；读到将近收尾，兴味烧成了激动；末了几行一读，烧到了顶。

「去他*猫』的！」他喊了一声，一把抓起帽子和信，从后门夺路而出，直奔住宅去了——余下的那搭信，随便哪年哪月再消化吧。

## XV / 十五
===Original===

When John went back to the office after the noonday intermission it was manifest that something had happened to Mr. Timson, and that the something was of a nature extremely gratifying to that worthy gentleman. He was beaming with satisfaction and rustling with importance. Several times during the afternoon he appeared to be on the point of confiding his news, but in the face of the interruptions which occurred, or which he feared might check the flow of his communication, he managed to restrain himself till after the closing of the office. But scarcely were the shutters up (at the willing hands of Peleg Hopkins) when he turned to John and, looking at him sharply, said, “Has Dave said anythin’ ’bout my leavin’?”
“He told me he expected you would stay as long as might be necessary to get me well started,” said John noncommittally, mindful of Mr. Harum’s injunction.
“Jest like him,” declared Chet. “Jest like him for all the world; but the fact o’ the matter is ’t I’m goin’ to-morro’. I s’pose he thought,” reflected Mr. Timson, “thet he’d ruther you’d find it out yourself than to have to break it to ye, ’cause then, don’t ye see, after I was gone he c’d lay the hull thing at my door.”
“Really,” said John, “I should have said that he ought to have told me.”
“Wa’al,” said Chet encouragingly, “mebbe you’ll git along somehow, though I’m ’fraid you’ll have more or less trouble; but I told Dave that as fur ’s I c’d see, mebbe you’d do ’s well ’s most anybody he c’d git that didn’t know any o’ the customers, an’ hadn’t never done any o’ this kind o’ work before.”
“Thank you very much,” said John. “And so you are off tomorrow, are you?”
“Got to be,” declared Mr. Timson. “I’d ’a’ liked to stay with you a spell longer, but the’s a big concern f’m out of town that as soon as they heard I was at libe’ty wrote for me to come right along up, an’ I s’pose I hadn’t ought to keep ’em waitin’.”
“No, I should think not,” said John, “and I congratulate you upon having located yourself so quickly.”
“Oh!” said Mr. Timson, with ineffable complacency, “I hain’t give myself no worry; I hain’t lost no sleep. I’ve allowed all along that Dave Harum’d find out that he wa’n’t the unly man that needed my kind o’ work, an’ I ain’t meanin’ any disrispect to you when I say ’t﻿—”
“Just so,” said John. “I quite understand. Nobody could expect to take just the place with him that you have filled.

午间休息过后约翰回到事务所，一看便知蒂姆森先生身上出了事，而且那件事对这位可敬的先生极为受用。他满面红光，浑身都是分量的窸窣。一下午他有几回眼看要吐露他的新闻；可或是有人打搅，或是他怕话头被人截断，硬是捱到了打烊。百叶窗刚上（皮尔·霍普金斯手脚麻利），他就转向约翰，眼睛一利：「戴夫提过我走的事没有？」
「他同我说，料想您会待到把我带顺手为止，」约翰不置可否地说，记着哈勒姆先生的嘱咐。
「他就是这个样子，」切特宣布。「活脱脱就是这个样子；可实情是：我明天就走。我猜他想着，」蒂姆森先生寻思，「让你自己发觉，总比他亲口告诉你强；因为那样，你明白吧，等我走了，这一整个的事就可以推到我头上。」
「说实在的，」约翰说，「我看他本该告诉我。」
「嗯，」切特鼓励他说，「兴许你们凑合得过去，虽说麻烦多少会有；我同戴夫讲过：就我看，一个不认得主顾、又没干过这一行的人，你算顶使得的了。」
「多谢夸奖，」约翰说。「这么说您明天动身？」
「非走不可，」蒂姆森先生宣布。「本来想多陪你几日；可是城外有一家大商号，一听说我闲下了，立时来信请我即刻上工；人家等着，不好叫人家多等。」
「是不好，」约翰说。「您这样快就有了归宿，恭喜。」
「哦！」蒂姆森先生带着说不出的自满说，「我自己一点儿没犯愁；一宿好觉没缺过。我向来估摸着：戴夫·哈勒姆早晚会发现，需要我这一路本事的不止他一家——我这么说，对您可没有半点不敬——」
「正是，」约翰说。「我全明白。他身边您那个位子，谁也别想坐得同您一样。

===Original===

And, by the way,” he added, “as you are going in the morning, and I may not see you again, would you kindly give me the last balance sheets of the two ledgers and the bill-book. I suppose, of course, that they are brought down to the first of the month, and I shall want to have them.”
“Oh, yes, cert’nly, of course﻿—wa’al I guess Dave’s got ’em,” replied Chet, looking considerably disconcerted, “but I’ll look ’em up in the mornin’. My train don’t go till ten o’clock, an’ I’ll see you ’bout any little last thing in the mornin’﻿—but I guess I’ve got to go now on account of a lot of things. You c’n shut up, can’t ye?”
Whereupon Mr. Timson made his exit, and not long afterward David came in. By that time everything had been put away, the safe and vault closed, and Peleg had departed with the mail and his freedom for the rest of the day.
“Wa’al,” said Mr. Harum, lifting himself to a seat on the counter, “how’ve you made out? All OK?”
“Yes,” replied John, “I think so.”
“Where’s Chet?”
“He went away some few minutes ago. He said he had a good many things to attend to as he was leaving in the morning.”
“E-um’m!” said David incredulously. “I guess ’t won’t take him long to close up his matters. Did he leave ev’rything in good shape? Cash all right, an’ so on?”
“I think so,” said John. “The cash is right I am sure.”
“How ’bout the books?”
“I asked him to let me have the balance sheets, and he said that you must have them, but that he would come in in the morning and﻿—well, what he said was that he would see me in the morning, and, as he put it, look after any little last thing.”
“E-um’m!” David grunted. “He won’t do no such a thing. We’ve seen the last of him, you bet, an’ a good riddance. He’ll take the nine o’clock tonight, that’s what he’ll do. Drawed his pay, I guess, didn’t he?”
“He said he was to be paid for this month,” answered John, “and took sixty dollars. Was that right?”
“Yes,” said David, nodding his head absently. “What was it he said about them statements?” he inquired after a moment.
“He said he guessed you must have them.”
“E-um’m!” was David’s comment. “What’d he say about leavin’?”
John laughed and related the conversation as exactly as he could.
“What’d I tell ye,” said Mr. Harum, with a short laugh. “Mebbe he won’t go till to-morro’, after all,” he remarked.

===Chinese===

「对了，」他添一句，「您明早就走，往后未必再见：麻烦把两本账簿和票据簿的末次余额表给我。我想总轧到月初了；我要留着。」
「哦，是，当然——这个嘛，我想在戴夫手里，」切特答，相当狼狈，「明早我找一找。我的火车十点才开；有什么零星末了的事，明早我同你交代——可我这会儿得走了，一摊子事呢。门你会锁的吧？」
说罢蒂姆森先生扬长而去；不多时大卫进来。那时一切都已归置停当，保险柜和金库都锁了；皮尔带着邮件和下半天的自由走了。
「嗯，」哈勒姆先生把自己抬上柜台坐着，「混得怎样？都还顺手？」
「是，」约翰答，「我看顺手。」
「切特呢？」
「几分钟前走了。说明早动身，有一摊子事要料理。」
「嗯——嗯！」大卫不信地哼了一声。「我看他料理不了多久。咱们见他的最后一面了，没错——走了干净。他准搭今晚九点那班，他就干这个。工钱支走了吧？」
「他说本月照付，」约翰答，「支了六十块。对不对？」
「对，」大卫点头，心不在焉。「报表那事他怎么说？」过了一刻他问。
「他说想是在您手里。」
「嗯——嗯！」大卫评了一句。「走的事他怎么说？」
约翰笑了，把那场谈话尽原样学了。
「我说什么来着，」哈勒姆先生短短一笑。「这么着，说不定他倒捱到明天才走，」他说。

“Polly,” said David, “I’ve ast the young feller to come to tea, but don’t you say the word ‘Eagle,’ to him. You c’n show your ign’rance ’bout all the other kinds of birds an’ animals you ain’t familiar with,” said the unfeeling brother, “but leave eagles alone.”

From a social point of view the entertainment could not be described as a very brilliant success. Our friend was tired and hungry. Mr. Harum was unusually taciturn, and Mrs. Bixbee, being under her brother’s interdict as regarded the subject which, had it been allowed discussion, might have opened the way, was at a loss for generalities. But John afterward got upon terms of the friendliest nature with that kindly soul.
