从社交的角度看，这一席款待算不得如何辉煌的成功。我们这位朋友又累又饿；哈勒姆先生异乎寻常地少言；比克斯比太太呢——她哥哥禁了她那个也许能开路的话题——一时想不出正经闲话来。可约翰后来同这位好心肠的人处得再友好不过。

## XVI / 十六
===Original===

Some weeks after John’s assumption of his duties in the office of David Harum, Banker, that gentleman sat reading his New York paper in the “wing settin’-room,” after tea, and Aunt Polly was occupied with the hemming of a towel. The able editorial which David was perusing was strengthening his conviction that all the intelligence and virtue of the country were monopolized by the Republican party, when his meditations were broken in upon by Mrs. Bixbee, who knew nothing and cared less about the Force Bill or the doctrine of protection to American industries.
“You hain’t said nothin’ fer quite a while about the bank,” she remarked. “Is Mr. Lenox gittin’ along all right?”
“Guess he’s gittin’ into condition as fast as c’d be expected,” said David, between two lines of his editorial.
“It must be awful lonesome fer him,” she observed, to which there was no reply.
“Ain’t it?” she asked, after an interval.
“Ain’t what?” said David, looking up at her.
“Awful lonesome,” she reiterated.
“Guess nobody ain’t ever very lonesome when you’re ’round an’ got your breath,” was the reply. “What you talkin’ about?”
“I ain’t talkin’ about you, ’t any rate,” said Mrs. Bixbee. “I was sayin’ it must be awful lonesome fer Mr. Lenox up here where he don’t know a soul hardly, an’ livin’ at that hole of a tavern.”
“I don’t see ’t you’ve any cause to complain long’s he don’t,” said David, hoping that it would not come to his sister’s ears that he had, for reasons of his own, discouraged any attempt on John’s part to better his quarters, “an’ he hain’t ben very lonesome daytimes, I guess, so fur, ’thout he’s ben makin’ work fer himself to kill time.”
“What do you mean?”
“Wa’al,” said David, “we found that Chet hadn’t done more ’n to give matters a lick an’ a promise in most a year. He done just enough to keep up the day’s work an’ no more an’ the upshot on’t is that John’s had to put in consid’able time to git things straightened out.”
“What a shame!” exclaimed Aunt Polly.
“Keeps him f’m bein’ lonesome,” remarked her brother with a grin.
“An’ he hain’t had no time to himself!” she protested. “I don’t believe you’ve made up your mind yet whether you’re goin’ to like him, an’ I don’t believe he’ll *stay* anyway.”
“I’ve told more ’n forty-leven times,” said Mr.

===Chinese===

约翰到「大卫·哈勒姆，银行业」上工几个星期后的一天，用过晚茶，那位先生坐在厢房起居室里读他的纽约报，波莉姨妈正给一条毛巾缭边。大卫细读的那篇能干社论，正把他那个信念越夺越实：全国的聪明和德行，尽归共和党一家垄断；恰在此时，比克斯比太太打断了他的玄思——力法案也好，保护美国工业的学说也好，她一概不知，更不关心。

「你有好一阵子没提银行的事了。」她说。「伦诺克斯先生还顺当吧？」

「瞅他进入状态的速度，快得不能再指望了。」大卫说，眼睛没离开社论的行间。

「他准冷清得厉害。」她评论；无人应声。

「是不是？」过了一阵她问。

「什么是不是？」大卫说，抬眼看她。

「冷清得厉害。」她重申。

「只要有你在场、气还匀着，谁也冷清不到哪儿去。」回答是。「你说的什么？」

「反正说的不是你。」比克斯比太太说。「我是说伦诺克斯先生，在这上头，一个人也不认得几个，又住着那么个狗窝客栈，准冷清得厉害。」

「他不抱怨，我看你也没什么可抱怨的。」大卫说——巴望着别让妹妹听去：他自己另有盘算，曾拦着约翰别去改善住处。「再说白天他大约不怎么冷清，至少眼下——除非他自己没事找事杀时间。」

「这话怎么讲？」

「这个嘛，」大卫说，「俺们发现，切特这大半年，凡事都只来了个『先应付、回头再说』。他干的刚够把当天的活对付过去，多一点也不干；结果就是：约翰为把一切理顺，搭进去不少工夫。」

「真不像话！」波莉姨妈叹道。

「省得他冷清。」她弟弟咧嘴评了一句。

「可他连点自己的工夫都没有！」她抗议。「我看你连喜不喜欢他都没拿定主意；再说我看他反正*待不住*。」

「俺说过何止四十七回了，」哈勒姆先生说：

===Original===

Harum, looking up over his paper, “that I thought we was goin’ to make a hitch of it, an’ he cert’nly hain’t said nuthin’ ’bout leavin’, an’ I guess he won’t fer a while, tavern or no tavern. He’s got a putty stiff upper lip of his own, I reckon,” David further remarked, with a short laugh, causing Mrs. Bixbee to look up at him inquiringly, which look the speaker answered with a nod, saying, “Me an’ him had a little go-round today.”
“You hain’t had no *words*, hev ye?” she asked anxiously.
“Wa’al, we didn’t have what ye might call *words*. I was jest tryin’ a little experiment with him.”
“Humph,” she remarked, “you’re alwus tryin’ exper’ments on somebody, an’ you’ll be liable to git ketched at it some day.”
“Exceptin’ on you,” said David. “You don’t think I’d try any experiments on you, do ye?”
“Me!” she cried. “You’re at me the hull endurin’ time, an’ you know it.”
“Wa’al, but Polly,” said David insinuatingly, “you don’t know how int’restin’ you *be*.”
“Glad you think so,” she declared, with a sniff and a toss of the head. “What you ben up to with Mr. Lenox?”
“Oh, nuthin’ much,” replied Mr. Harum, making a feint of resuming his reading.
“Be ye goin’ to tell me, or﻿—air ye too *’shamed* on’t?” she added with a little laugh, which somewhat turned the tables on her teasing brother.
“Wa’al, I laid out to try an’ read this paper,” he said, spreading it out on his lap, “but,” resignedly, “I guess ’tain’t no use. Do you know what a count’fit bill is?” he asked.
“I dunno ’s I ever see one,” she said, “but I s’pose I do. They’re agin the law, ain’t they?”
“The’s a number o’ things that’s agin the law,” remarked David dryly.
“Wa’al?” ejaculated Mrs. Bixbee after a moment of waiting.
“Wa’al,” said David, “the’ ain’t much to tell, but it’s plain I don’t git no peace till you git it out of me. It was like this: The young feller’s took holt everywhere else right off, but handlin’ the money bothered him consid’able at fust. It was slow work, an’ I c’d see it myself; but he’s gettin’ the hang on’t now. Another thing I expected he’d run up agin was count’fits. The’ ain’t so very many on ’em round nowadays, but the’ is now an’ then one. He allowed to me that he was liable to get stuck at fust, an’ I reckoned he would.

===Chinese===

他抬眼越过报纸，「俺瞅俺们能合上套；他呢，走不走的事半个字没提，俺估摸一阵子也不会——住不住客栈的再说。他那片嘴唇，倒是自带一股硬气。」大卫又补一句，短笑一声，惹得比克斯比太太抬眼探究地看他；对这一看，说话的人点头作答，说：「俺同他今儿小小过了一回合。」

「你们没*拌嘴*吧？」她急问。

「这个嘛，还没到你们说的那种*拌嘴*。俺就是拿他试了个小实验。」

「哼，」她说，「你就爱在人身上做实验，早晚有一天叫人当场拿住。」

「就是不在于你。」大卫说。「你不会以为我会在你身上做实验吧？」

「我！」她喊。「你成天价拿我开涮，你自己知道。」

「这个嘛，波莉，」大卫循循善诱，「你不知道你这个人*多*有意思。」

「你这么想就好。」她哼一声、头一扬，宣布。「你同伦诺克斯先生搞什么名堂来着？」

「哦，没啥大不了的。」哈勒姆先生答，装出接着看报的样子。

「你是打算讲给我听呢，还是——*臊得*讲不出口？」她笑着补一句，这一下倒把拿她打趣的弟弟将了个半军。

「这个嘛，俺本来打算把这报纸读完，」他说，把报纸摊在腿上，「不过，」一副认命的口气，「瞅着是不成了。你知道假票子是什么不？」他问。

「见没见过不好说，」她说，「想来是知道的。那玩意儿犯法，不是吗？」

「犯法的东西可多了。」大卫干巴巴地说。

「然后呢？」比克斯比太太等了片刻，催了一声。

「这个嘛，」大卫说，「没啥可讲的好戏；可明摆着，不掏给你，俺落不着安生。是这么回事：那年轻人别的处处一上手就来，唯独经手钱票，头里很让他犯了一阵难。慢工，俺自己瞧得见；如今门道摸出来了。还有一样俺料到他要撞的，就是假票。如今市面上不算多，可隔三差五有那么一张。他同俺说过，头里保不齐栽跟头；俺也算准了。

But I never said nuthin’ about it, nor ast no questions until today; an’ this afternoon I come in to look ’round, an’ I says to him, ‘What luck have you had with your money? Git any bad?’ I says. ‘Wa’al,’ he says, colorin’ up a little, ‘I don’t know how many I may have took in an’ paid out agin without knowin’ it,’ he says, ‘but the’ was a couple sent back from New York out o’ that package that went down last Friday.’ ”
“ ‘What was they?’ I says.
“ ‘A five an’ a ten,’ he says.
“ ‘Where be they?’ I says.
“ ‘They’re in the draw there﻿—they’re ruther int’restin’ objects of study,’ he says, kind o’ laughin’ on the wrong side of his mouth.
“ ‘Countin’ ’em in the cash?’ I says, an’ with that he kind o’ reddened up agin. ‘No, sir,’ he says, ‘I charged ’em up to my own account, an’ I’ve kept ’em to compare with.’
“ ‘You hadn’t ought to done that,’ I says.
“ ‘You think I ought to ’a’ put ’em in the fire at once?’ says he.
“ ‘No,’ I says, ‘that wa’n’t what I meant. Why didn’t you mix ’em up with the other money, an’ let ’em go when you was payin’ out? Anyways,’ I says, ‘you charge ’em up to profit an’ loss if you’re goin’ to charge ’em to anythin’, an’ let me have ’em,’ I says.
“ ‘What’ll you do with ’em?’ he says to me, kind o’ shuttin’ his jaws together.
“ ‘I’ll take care on ’em,’ I says. ‘They mayn’t be good enough to send down to New York,’ I says, ‘but they’ll go around here all right﻿—jest as good as any other,’ I says, ‘long ’s you keep ’em movin’.’ ”
“David Harum!” cried Polly, who, though not quite comprehending some of the technicalities of detail, was fully alive to the turpitude of the suggestion. “I hope to gracious he didn’t think you was in earnest. Why, s’pose they was passed around, wouldn’t somebody git stuck with ’em in the long run? You know they would.” Mrs. Bixbee occasionally surprised her brother with unexpected penetration, but she seldom got much recognition of it.
“I see by the paper,” he remarked, “that the’ was a man died in Pheladelphy one day last week,” which piece of barefaced irrelevancy elicited no notice from Mrs. Bixbee.
“What more did he say?” she demanded.
“Wa’al,” responded Mr.

===Chinese===

「可这事俺一个字没提、一个问题没问，直到今儿。今儿下晌俺进来转转，同他说：『钱上头手气如何？收着坏票子没有？』俺说。『这个嘛，』他说，脸微微一红，『不知不觉里收进又付出去过多少，我说不好，』他说，『不过上礼拜五下去的那一包，纽约退回来两张。』」

「『哪两张？』俺说。

「『一张五块，一张十块。』他说。

「『撜哪儿了？』俺说。

「『就在那抽屉里——倒是挺有看头的两件研究物。』他说，那笑扯在嘴的错误一侧。

「『记进现金里了？』俺说；这一下他又红了红。『没有，先生，』他说，『记在我自己账上了，留着两张做比对的样。』

「『这个你不该。』俺说。

「『您觉得我该当时就塞炉子里？』他说。

「『不是，』俺说，『俺不是那个意思。你怎么不把它们掺进旁的钱里，付款的时候一并放出去？再说，』俺说，『真要记账，也记到盈亏账上去，完了交给俺。』俺说。

「『您拿它们做什么？』他问俺，牙关有点咬紧。

「『俺来照管。』俺说。『送去纽约，它们兴许不够格，』俺说，『可在这地界上照样使得——同旁的票子一样好使，』俺说，『只要别叫它们停着。』」

「大卫·哈勒姆！」波莉喊道——细节上有些门道她虽没全参透，这提议的缺德她可是全听明白了。「老天保佑，他可别当你是在正经说话。你想，票子真撒出去，到头来总有人捏在手里吃亏，是不是？你知道会的。」比克斯比太太时不时会拿出一份出人意料的眼力，惊她弟弟一惊；只是这份眼力，难得换来多少承认。

「报纸上说，」他说，「上礼拜哪天，费城死了个人。」这一手脸不红心不跳的顾左右而言他，比克斯比太太只当没听见。

「他还说什么了？」她追问。

「这个嘛，」哈勒姆先生答：

===Original===

Harum with a laugh, “he said that he didn’t see why I should be a loser by his mistakes, an’ that as fur as the bills was concerned they belonged to him, an’ with that,” said the narrator, “Mister Man gits ’em out of the draw an’ jest marches into the back room an’ puts the dum things int’ the fire.”
“He done jest right,” declared Aunt Polly, “an’ you know it, don’t ye now?”
“Wa’al,” said David, “f’m his standpoint﻿—f’m his standpoint, I guess he did, an’,” rubbing his chin with two fingers of his left hand, “it’s a putty dum good standpoint too. I’ve ben lookin’,” he added reflectively, “fer an honest man fer quite a number o’ years, an’ I guess I’ve found him; yes’m, I guess I’ve found him.”
“An’ be you goin’ to let him lose that fifteen dollars?” asked the practical Polly, fixing her brother with her eyes.
“Wa’al,” said David, with a short laugh, “what c’n I do with such an obst’nit critter ’s he is? He jest backed into the britchin’, an’ I couldn’t do nothin’ with him.” Aunt Polly sat over her sewing for a minute or two without taking a stitch.
“I’m sorry you done it,” she said at last.
“I dunno but I did make ruther a mess of it,” admitted Mr. Harum.

===Chinese===

他笑了一声，「他说，他不明白凭什么他的过失要俺吃亏；再说那两张票子，是谁的就是谁的——话音未落，」讲述人说，「这位爷从抽屉里取出票子，抬脚就进了里间，把那两张该死的东西塞进了炉子。」

「他干得对极了。」波莉姨妈宣布，「你自己心里也清楚，是不是？」

「这个嘛，」大卫说，「站在他的立场上——站在他的立场上，俺瞅他干得对；还有，」左手两根手指搓着下巴，「那也是个顶顶好的立场。俺这些年，」他若有所思地添一句，「一直在寻一个诚实的人；俺瞅，寻着了；是啦，俺瞅寻着了。」

「那这十五块钱，你就打算让他白丢？」讲求实际的波莉问，两眼钉住弟弟。

「这个嘛，」大卫说，短笑一声，「他那么头一头的倔牲口，俺能咋办？他一屁股倒进尻带里，俺拿他没辙。」波莉姨妈对着针线坐了一两分钟，一针没下。

「你干这事，俺遗憾。」她终于说。

「俺瞅，兴许还真办了个稀碎。」哈勒姆先生认了。

## XVII / 十七
===Original===

It was the 23rd of December, and shortly after the closing hour. Peleg had departed and our friend had just locked the vault when David came into the office and around behind the counter.
“Be you in any hurry?” he asked.
John said he was not, whereupon Mr. Harum hitched himself up onto a high office stool, with his heels on the spindle, and leaned sideways upon the desk, while John stood facing him with his left arm upon the desk.
“John,” said David, “do ye know the Widdo’ Cullom?”
“No” said John, “but I know who she is﻿—a tall, thin woman, who walks with a slight stoop and limp. I noticed her and asked her name because there was something about her looks that attracted my attention﻿—as though at some time she might have seen better days.”
“That’s the party,” said David. “She has seen better days, but she’s eat an’ drunk sorro’ mostly fer goin’ on thirty year, an’ darned little else good share o’ the time, I reckon.”
“She has that appearance certainly,” said John.
“Yes sir,” said David, “she’s had a putty tough time, the widdo’ has, an’ yet,” he proceeded after a momentary pause, “the’ was a time when the Culloms was some o’ the kingpins o’ this hull region. They used to own quarter o’ the county, an’ they lived in the big house up on the hill where Doc Hays lives now. That was considered to be the finest place anywheres ’round here in them days. I used to think the Capitol to Washington must be somethin’ like the Cullom house, an’ that Billy P. (folks used to call him Billy P. ’cause his father’s name was William an’ his was William Parker), an’ that Billy P. ’d jest ’s like ’s not be president. I’ve changed my mind some on the subject of presidents since I was a boy.”
Here Mr. Harum turned on his stool, put his right hand into his sack-coat pocket, extracted therefrom part of a paper of “Maple Dew,” and replenished his left cheek with an ample wad of “fine-cut.” John took advantage of the break to head off what he had reason to fear might turn into a lengthy digression from the matter in hand by saying, “I beg pardon, but how does it happen that Mrs. Cullom is in such circumstances? Has the family all died out?”
“Wa’al,” said David, “they’re most on ’em dead, all on ’em, in fact, except the widdo’s son Charley, but as fur ’s the family ’s concerned, it more ’n *died* out﻿—it *gin* out! ’D ye ever hear of Jim Wheton’s calf?

===Chinese===

十二月二十三日，打烊后不久。皮尔走了，我们这位朋友刚锁上金库，大卫走进办公室，绕到柜台后头。
「赶时间不？」他问。
约翰说不赶；哈勒姆先生于是把自己跨上一张高脚账凳，鞋跟钩在凳杆上，侧身倚着写字台；约翰左臂搭在台面上，面对他站着。
「约翰，」大卫说，「卡洛姆寡妇你认得不？」
「不认得，」约翰说，「不过知道是谁——瘦高个儿，走路微微佝背带点瘸。我留意过她，打听过名字，因为她模样上有一样东西惹我注意——仿佛从前也有过好日子。」
「就是这位，」大卫说。「她是过过好日子；可是整整快三十年，她吃的喝的主要是愁，我估摸，一多半的日子连别的都没得吃。」
「她那副样子确实像，」约翰说。
「是，先生，」大卫说，「她过的日子够呛，寡妇过的；可是，」他顿了一顿，「当年，卡洛姆一家是这一带数得着的顶梁柱。全县他们家曾有四分之一，住在山上那所大宅里——如今海斯医生住的那所。那些年，四下里算它顶体面的一处。我那时候想，华盛顿的国会大厦，想必也就同卡洛姆家差不多；还想比利·P.（大伙儿叫他比利·P.，因为他父亲叫威廉，他自己叫威廉·帕克）早晚八成当总统。打小时候起，我对总统这个题目的看法改了些。」
说到这里，哈勒姆先生在凳子上拧过身，右手伸进便装口袋，摸出半包「枫露」，往左腮帮子里添了一大团细切嚼烟。约翰趁着这个空当，把他担心会岔成长途闲谈的话头拦回来：「对不起——卡洛姆太太怎么会落到这个地步？家里人都没了？」
「这个嘛，」大卫说，「多半死了——全死了，除了寡妇的儿子查利；不过就这一家子说，岂止是*死*绝了——是*芭*绝了！吉姆·惠顿家那头小牛，你听说过没有？」

===Original===

Wa’al, Jim brought three or four veals into town one spring to sell. Dick Larrabee used to peddle meat them days. Dick looked ’em over an’ says, ‘Look here, Jim,’ he says, ‘I guess you got a “deakin” in that lot,’ he says. ‘I dunno what you mean,’ says Jim. ‘Yes, ye do, goll darn ye!’ says Dick, ‘yes, ye do. You didn’t never kill that calf, an’ you know it. That calf died, that’s what that calf done. Come, now, own up,’ he says. ‘Wa’al,’ says Jim, ‘I didn’t *kill* it, an’ it didn’t *die* nuther﻿—it jest kind o’ *gin out*.’ ”
John joined in the laugh with which the narrator rewarded his own effort, and David went on: “Yes, sir, they jes’ petered out. Old Billy, Billy P.’s father, inheritid all the prop’ty﻿—never done a stroke of work in his life. He had a collidge education, went to Europe, an’ all that’, an’ before he was fifty year old he hardly ever come near the old place after he was growed up. The land was all farmed out on shares, an’ his farmers mostly bamboozled him the hull time. He got consid’able income, of course, but as things went along and they found out how slack he was they kept bitin’ off bigger chunks all the time, an’ sometimes he didn’t git even the core. But all the time when he wanted money﻿—an’ he wanted it putty often I tell ye﻿—the easiest way was to stick on a morgidge; an’ after a spell it got so ’t he’d have to give a morgidge to pay the int’rist on the other morgidges.”
“But,” said John, “was there nothing to the estate but land?”
“Oh, yes,” said David, “old Billy’s father left him some consid’able pers’nal, but after that was gone he went into the morgidge bus’nis as I tell ye. He lived mostly up to Syrchester and around, an’ when he got married he bought a place in Syrchester and lived there till Billy P. was about twelve or thirteen year old, an’ he was about fifty. By that time he’d got ’bout to the end of his rope, an’ the’ wa’n’t nothin’ for it but to come back here to Homeville an’ make the most o’ what the’ was left﻿—an’ that’s what he done, let alone that he didn’t make the most on’t to any pertic’ler extent. Mis’ Cullom, his wife, wa’n’t no help to him. She was a city woman an’ didn’t take to the country no way, but when she died it broke old Billy up wus ’n ever. She peaked an’ pined, an’ died when Billy P. was about fifteen or so. Wa’al, Billy P.

===Chinese===

「这个嘛，吉姆有一年春天，运了三四头小牛进镇卖。那年头迪克·拉腊比常走街串巷卖肉。迪克相了相，说：『听着，吉姆，』他说，『我瞧你这批里头有一位「执事」，』他说。『不懂你什么意思，』吉姆说。『懂，你懂，见你的鬼！』迪克说，『你懂。那头小牛不是你宰的，你自己清楚。那头小牛是死的，它就是那么回事。来，招了吧，』他说。『嗯，』吉姆说，『我没*宰*它，它也*没死*——它就是有点儿*芭掉了*。』」
叙述人自己先笑了，约翰跟着笑；大卫接着讲：「是，先生，他们就是那么芭掉的。老比利——比利·P.的父亲——把产业整个继承下来，这辈子没干过一天活。他受过大学教育，去过欧洲，等等；不到五十岁上，长大成人之后就几乎不着老宅的边。地全按分成放出去，佃户们从头到尾糊弄他。进项当然还有可观的一份；可是日子一长，人家摸透了他的松，一口一口越咬越大，有时连核都没给他剩下。可他一要钱——我告诉你，他要钱勤得很——顶便当的法子就是往上贴一笔抵押；过一阵，竟要借新抵押去付旧抵押的利。」
「可是，」约翰说，「那分家产里就没有别的，光有地？」
「哦，有，」大卫说，「老比利的父亲给他留下相当一批动产；那批糟完，他就干起了抵押的营生，如我所说。他多半住在雪切斯特一带；成了家，就在雪切斯特买了处宅子住着，直到比利·P.十二三岁，他本人五十来岁。到那时他的绳子也快到头了，没奈何，只好回霍姆维尔来，把剩下的这点儿尽力经营——他也确实这么办了，只是「尽力」经营得实在有限。卡洛姆太太，他那位太太，帮不上他。她是城里女人，怎么也过不惯乡下；可她一死，老比利垮得更狠。她恹恹地瘦下去，比利·P.十五岁上下就没了。嗯，比利·P.

an’ the old man wrastled along somehow, an’ the boy went to collidge fer a year or so. How they ever got along ’s they did I dunno. The’ was a story that some far-off relation left old Billy some money, an’ I guess that an’ what they got off’m what farms was left carried ’em along till Billy P. was twenty-five or so, an’ then he up an’ got married. That was the crownin’ stroke,” remarked David. “She was one o’ the village girls﻿—respectable folks, more ’n ordinary good lookin’ an’ high steppin’, an’ had had some schoolin’. But the old man was prouder ’n a cock-turkey, an’ thought nobody wa’n’t quite good enough fer Billy P., an’ all along kind o’ reckoned that he’d marry some money an’ git a new start. But when he got married﻿—on the quiet, you know, cause he knowed the old man would kick﻿—wa’al, that killed the trick, an’ the old man into the bargain. It took the gumption all out of him, an’ he didn’t live a year. Wa’al, sir, it was curious, but, ’s I was told, putty much the hull village sided with the old man. The Culloms was kind o’ kings in them days, an’ folks wa’n’t so one-man’s-good’s-anotherish as they be now. They thought Billy P. done wrong, though they didn’t have nothin’ to say ’gainst the girl neither﻿—an’ she’s very much respected, Mis’ Cullom is, an’ as fur’s I’m concerned, I’ve alwus guessed she kept Billy P. goin’ full as long ’s anyone could. But ’t wa’n’t no use﻿—that is to say, the sure thing come to pass. He had a nom’nal title to a good deal o’ prop’ty, but the equity in most on’t if it had ben to be put up wa’n’t enough to pay fer the papers. You see, the’ ain’t never ben no real cash value in farm prop’ty in these parts. The’ ain’t ben hardly a dozen changes in farm titles, ’cept by inher’tance or foreclosure, in thirty years. So Billy P. didn’t make no effort. Int’rist’s one o’ them things that keeps right on nights an’ Sundays. He jest had the deeds made out and handed ’em over when the time came to settle. The’ was some village lots though that was clear, that fetched him in some money from time to time until they was all gone but one, an’ that’s the one Mis’ Cullom lives on now. It was consid’able more’n a lot﻿—in fact, a putty sizable place. She thought the sun rose an’ set where Billy P. was, but she took a crotchit in her head, and wouldn’t ever sign no papers fer that, an’ lucky fer him too.

===Chinese===

同老头子两个勉强往前挣扎，孩子上过一年上下大学。他们怎么撑下来的，我说不清。有传说，说哪门子远房亲戚给老比利留过一笔钱；我猜，是那笔钱加上剩下几处田庄的出息，把他们撑到比利·P.二十五岁上下；随后他成家了。那是压垮的一击，」大卫说。「她是村里姑娘——正经人家，相貌出挑，心气也高，念过些书。可老头子骄傲得像只开屏的公火鸡，认为谁也配不上比利·P.，一直暗暗盘算他这门亲事要娶进一份财产，重振家业。可他成了家——是偷偷成的，你知道，因为他知道老头子要炸——这一下，把那点算盘连同老头子本人一齐送了终。老头子的心气全散了，没活过一年。说来也怪，据人说，全村差不多都站在老头子一边。卡洛姆家那些年是有点王者气派；人们也不像如今这样见谁都一样看待。他们认为比利·P.错了——可对姑娘本人也说不出个不字——卡洛姆太太如今很受敬重；就我看，我一直疑心，撑着比利·P.撑那么久的，正是她。可没用——就是说，命里注定的来了。他名下还挂着一大宗产业的空名，可真要拍卖，里头的净值付不清文书钱。你知道，这一带田产的现钱价值，从来就没有过。三十年里，田产换手，除了继承和没收抵债，捾共没几回。所以比利·P.也没挣扎。利钱是那样一种东西，夜里和礼拜天照走不停。到时候，他叫人把契纸立好，交出去完事。只有几块镇上的地皮是净的，隔三差五卖一块，给他进几个钱，直到卖得只剩一块——寡妇如今住的那一块。那原不止是一块宅基地——实实在在是块像样的产业。她当年把比利·P.当太阳敬；可她脑瓜里有一股拗劲，那块地死也不肯画押——也算他走运。

===Original===

The’ was a house on to it, an’ he had a roof over his head anyway when he died six or seven years after he married, an’ left her with a boy to raise. How she got along all them years till Charley got big enough to help, I swan! I don’t know. She took in sewin’ an’ washin’, an’ went out to cook an’ nurse, an’ all that, but I reckon the’ was now an’ then times when they didn’t overload their stomechs much, nor have to open the winders to cool off. But she held onto that prop’ty of her’n like a pup to a root. It was putty well out when Billy P. died, but the village has growed up to it. The’s some good lots could be cut out on’t, an’ it backs up to the river where the current’s enough to make a mighty good power fer a ’lectric light. I know some fellers that are talkin’ of startin’ a plant here, an’ it ain’t out o’ sight that they’d pay a good price fer the river front, an’ enough land to build on. Fact on’t is, it’s got to be a putty valu’ble piece o’ prop’ty, more ’n she cal’lates on, I reckon.”
Here Mr. Harum paused, pinching his chin with thumb and index finger, and mumbling his tobacco. John, who had listened with more attention than interest﻿—wondering the while as to what the narrative was leading up to﻿—thought something might properly be expected of him to show that he had followed it, and said, “So Mrs. Cullom has kept this last piece clear, has she?”
“No,” said David, bringing down his right hand upon the desk with emphasis, “that’s jest what she hain’t done, an’ that’s how I come to tell ye somethin’ of the story, an’ more on’t ’n you’ve cared about hearin’, mebbe.”
“Not at all,” John protested. “I have been very much interested.”
“You have, have you?” said Mr. Harum. “Wa’al, I got somethin’ I want ye to do. Day after to-morro’ ’s Chris’mus, an’ I want ye to drop Mis’ Cullom a line, somethin’ like this, ‘That Mr. Harum told ye to say that that morgidge he holds, havin’ ben past due fer some time, an’ no int’rist havin’ ben paid fer, let me see, more’n a year, he wants to close the matter up, an’ he’ll see her Chris’mus mornin’ at the bank at nine o’clock, he havin’ more time on that day; but that, as fur as he can see, the bus’nis won’t take very long’﻿—somethin’ like that, you understand?”

===Chinese===

「地上有座房子，成婚六七年后他死的时候，总算有个屋顶；撇下她，带一个要拉扯的男孩。那些年，直到查利长大搭得上手，她怎么过的，天晓得！我不知道。她接针线、接浆洗，出门给人烧饭、看护，什么都来；我估摸，也不时有些日子，娘俩的胃没多少可添的负担，倒也不用开窗散热。可她那块地攢得死紧，像小狗咬住树根。比利·P.死时，那块地还荒在村边上；如今村子长上来，把它包进去了。上头能划出几块好宅基，背靠大河，水势够推动一套顶好的电灯厂。我知道有几位正合计在这儿办厂；河沿那一线，外加够盖房的地，他们肯出好价，不是没影的事。实情是，那块产业如今值钱了——比她自己估的值钱，我料想。」
哈勒姆先生说到这里打住，拇指食指捻着下巴，嚼着烟。约翰一路听得专注多过兴趣——心里纳闷这段叙述要引到哪里去——觉得总该应个声，表示听懂了，便说：「这么说，卡洛姆太太这最后一块地，一直保得干干净净？」
「不，」大卫说，右手往台面上一按，「恰恰是她没有——这正是我要给你讲这段来历的缘故；兴许讲得比你爱听的还长。」
「哪里，」约翰拦道，「我一直听得很有兴致。」
「是吗，是吗？」哈勒姆先生说。「这个嘛，我有一件事要你办。后天是圣诞节；我要你给卡洛姆太太捐一句话，大意如此：『奉哈勒姆先生之命转告：他手上那笔抵押，逾期已有相当时日，利息——我想想——也已一年多分文未付；他要把这件事了结。圣诞节上午九点，他在银行等她，那天他工夫宽裕；至于这件事本身，依他看，费不了多大工夫』——大意如此，明白？」

===Original===

“Very well, sir,” said John, hoping that his employer would not see in his face the disgust and repugnance he felt as he surmised what a scheme was on foot, and recalled what he had heard of Harum’s hard and unscrupulous ways, though he had to admit that this, excepting perhaps the episode of the counterfeit money, was the first revelation to him personally. But this seemed very bad to him.
“All right,” said David cheerfully, “I s’pose it won’t take you long to find out what’s in your stockin’, an’ if you hain’t nothin’ else to do Chris’mus mornin’ I’d like to have you open the office and stay ’round a spell till I git through with Mis’ Cullom. Mebbe the’ ’ll be some papers to fill out or witniss or somethin’; an’ have that skeezicks of a boy make up the fires so’st the place’ll be warm.”
“Very good, sir,” said John, hoping that the interview was at an end.
But the elder man sat for some minutes apparently in a brown study, and occasionally a smile of sardonic cunning wrinkled his face. At last he said: “I’ve told ye so much that I may as well tell ye how I come by that morgidge. ’Twont take but a minute, an’ then you can run an’ play,” he added with a chuckle.
“I trust I have not betrayed any impatience,” said John, and instantly conscious of his infelicitous expression, added hastily, “I have really been very much interested.”
“Oh, no,” was the reply, “you hain’t *betrayed* none, but I know old fellers like me gen’rally tell a thing twice over while they’re at it. Wa’al,” he went on, “it was like this. After Charley Cullom got to be some grown he helped to keep the pot a-bilin’, ’n they got on some better. ’Bout seven year ago, though, he up an’ got married, an’ then the fat ketched fire. Finally he allowed that if he had some money he’d go West ’n take up some land, ’n git along like pussly ’n a flower gard’n. He ambitioned that if his mother ’d raise a thousan’ dollars on her place he’d be sure to take care of the int’rist, an’ prob’ly pay off the princ’ple in almost no time. Wa’al, she done it, an’ off he went. She didn’t come to me fer the money, because﻿—I dunno﻿—at any rate she didn’t, but got it of ’Zeke Swinney.
“Wa’al, it turned out jest ’s any fool might ’ve predilictid, fer after the first year, when I reckon he paid it out of the thousan’, Charley never paid no int’rist.

===Chinese===

「遵命，先生，」约翰说，一面盼着雇主别从他脸上看出他此刻的厌恶与反感——他猜到了这是一个什么圈套，也想起他听人说过的哈勒姆那些狠辣不择手段的名声；虽然他得认账，除去那桩假钞的插曲，这是头一回当着他的面露出来。可这一手在他看来实在下作。
「好，」大卫高高兴兴地说，「我猜用不了多久你就知道你袜子里装的什么了；圣诞早上你要没别的安排，我想请你把办公室开了，在附近待一阵，等我同卡洛姆太太办完。兴许有文书要填、要见证什么的；再叫那个小猴崽子把炉子生好，屋里暖着。」
「好的，先生，」约翰说，只盼这场谈话到此为止。
可老人又坐了好几分钟，像在出神，脸上不时皱起一丝讥诣的狡黠。末了他开口：「我都讲了这么多了，索性把那笔抵押的来历也讲给你。费不了一分钟，讲完你自去玩耍，」他笑着添一句。
「但愿我没有露出不耐烦，」约翰说；随即自觉这话不妥，急忙补上：「我实在一直很有兴致。」
「哦，没有，」回答是，「你没有*露出来*；不过我知道，我这样的老家伙讲起一件事来，多半要顺带讲两遍。这个嘛，」他接着说，「是这样。查利·卡洛姆长大些以后，帮着维持锅开，日子好过一些。可是约莫七年前，他也成了家；这下肥肉上了火。末了他盘算：有笔钱的话，他就西部去领一块地，像花圃里的薄荷一样发家。他雄心勃勃：母亲若肯拿她的宅子抵一千块，利息他保管，本金多半转眼就清。这个嘛，她办了，他走了。钱没找我借——为什么，说不清——反正没找；找的齐克·斯温尼。
「后来的结局，任何傻瓜都料得到：头一年，我估摸他从那一千里付了利；往后，查利一文利也没付过。

===Original===

The second year he was jest gettin’ goin’, an’ the next year he lost a hoss jest as he was cal’latin’ to pay, an’ the next year the grasshoppers smote him, ’n so on; an’ the outcome was that at the end of five years, when the morgidge had one year to run, Charley’d paid one year, an’ she’d paid one, an’ she stood to owe three years’ int’rist. How old Swinney come to hold off so was that she used to pay the cuss ten dollars or so ev’ry six months ’n git no credit fer it, an’ no receipt an’ no witniss, ’n he knowed the prop’ty was improving all the time. He may have had another reason, but at any rate he let her run, and got the shave reg’lar. But at the time I’m tellin’ you about he’d begun to cut up, an’ allowed that if she didn’t settle up the int’rist he’d foreclose, an’ I got wind on’t an’ I run across her one day an’ got to talkin’ with her, an’ she gin me the hull narration. ‘How much do you owe the old critter?’ I says. ‘A hunderd an’ eighty dollars,’ she says, ‘an’ where I’m goin’ to git it,’ she says, ‘the Lord only knows.’ ‘An’ He won’t tell ye, I reckon,’ I says. Wa’al, of course I’d known that Swinney had a morgidge because it was a matter of record, an’ I knowed him well enough to give a guess what his game was goin’ to be, an’ more’n that I’d had my eye on that piece an’ parcel an’ I figured that he wa’n’t any likelier a citizen ’n I was.” (“Yes,” said John to himself, “where the carcase is the vultures are gathered together.”)
“ ‘Wa’al,’ I says to her, after we’d had a little more talk, ‘s’posen you come ’round to my place to-morro’ ’bout ’leven o’clock, an’ mebbe we c’n cipher this thing out. I don’t say positive that we kin,’ I says, ‘but mebbe, mebbe.’ So that afternoon I sent over to the county seat an’ got a description an’ had a second morgidge drawed up fer two hundred dollars, an’ Mis’ Cullom signed it mighty quick. I had the morgidge made one day after date, ’cause, as I said to her, it was in the nature of a temp’rary loan, but she was so tickled she’d have signed most anythin’ at that pertic’ler time. ‘Now,’ I says to her, ‘you go an’ settle with old Step-an’-fetch-it, but don’t you say a word where you got the money,’ I says. ‘Don’t ye let on nothin’﻿—stretch that conscience o’ your’n if nes’sary,’ I says, ‘an’ be pertic’ler if he asks you if Dave Harum give ye the money you jest say, “No, he didn’t.”

===Chinese===

第二年他「正要起步」；第三年眼看要付了，死了一匹马；再一年蝗虫又灭他；如此等等。到头来，五年期满、抵押只剩一年到期时，查利付过一年，她付过一年，她还欠着三年的利。老斯温尼所以一直按着不动，是她每半年塞给那老东西十块上下——白塞，没账、没收条、没见证——而他也知道那块地一直在看涨。兴许另有缘故；总之他由她拖，刮皮照刮不误。可到我说的这个当口，他开始发作了，放话说利息再不结清就收地。我得了风声，有一天路上撞见她，攀谈起来，她把全部始末给了我一听。『你欠那老货多少？』我说。『一百八，』她说，『打哪儿弄去，』她说，『只有老天爷知道。』『他也不会告诉你，我猜，』我说。这个嘛，斯温尼有抵押我当然早知道——那是有案可查的事；他的算盘往哪边打，我也摸得出几分；还不止：那块地我早就眼热；论做本县的良民，他不比我强哪儿去。」（「是，」约翰心里说，「尸首在哪里，秃鹫就聚在哪里。」）
「『这个嘛，』多谈几句后我对她说，『明儿十一点来我那儿一趟，兴许咱们能把这笔账解开。不敢打包票，』我说，『兴许，兴许。』于是当天下午我派人去县里，抄了地契简况，叫人立了一纸二百块的第二抵押，卡洛姆太太三下五除二就签了。我把抵押立成出票后一日，因为——我对她说——这在性质上是一笔暂借；可她欢喜得那样，那个节骨眼上签什么都肯。『好了，』我对她说，『你去同那老「随叫随到」把账结了；可钱打哪儿来，一个字也别提，』我说。『什么也别露——必要的话，把你的良心抻一抻，』我说，『记住：他要是问你，是不是戴夫·哈勒姆给的，你就答：「不是他给的。」』

===Original===

That wont be no lie,’ I says, ‘because I aint *givin’* it to ye,’ I says. Wa’al, she done as I told her. Of course Swinney suspicioned fust off that I was mixed up in it, but she stood him off so fair an’ square that he didn’t know jest what *to* think, but his claws was cut fer a spell, anyway.
“Wa’al, things went on fer a while, till I made up my mind that I ought to relieve Swinney of some of his anxieties about worldly bus’nis, an’ I dropped in on him one mornin’ an’ passed the time o’ day, an’ after we’d eased up our minds on the subjects of each other’s health an’ suchlike I says, ‘You hold a morgidge on the Widder Cullom’s place, don’t ye?’ Of course he couldn’t say nothin’ but ‘yes.’ ‘Does she keep up the int’rist all right?’ I says. ‘I don’t want to be pokin’ my nose into your bus’nis,’ I says, ‘an’ don’t tell me nothin’ you don’t want to.’ Wa’al, he knowed Dave Harum was Dave Harum, an’ that he might ’s well spit it out, an’ he says, ‘Wa’al, she didn’t pay nothin’ fer a good while, but last time she forked over the hull amount. ‘But I hain’t no notion,’ he says, ‘that she’ll come to time agin.’ ‘An’ s’posin’ she don’t,’ I says, ‘you’ll take the prop’ty, won’t ye?’ ‘Don’t see no other way,’ he says, an’ lookin’ up quick, ‘unless you overbid me,’ he says. ‘No,’ I says, ‘I ain’t buyin’ no real estate jest now, but the thing I come in fer,’ I says, ‘leavin’ out the pleasure of havin’ a talk with you, was to say that I’d take that morgidge off’m your hands.’
“Wa’al, sir, he, he, he, he! Scat my —﻿—﻿—! At that he looked at me fer a minute with his jaw on his neck, an’ then he hunched himself, ’n drawed in his neck like a mud turtle. ‘No,’ he says, ‘I ain’t sufferin’ fer the money, an’ I guess I’ll keep the morgidge. It’s putty near due now, but mebbe I’ll let it run a spell. I guess the secur’ty’s good fer it.’ ‘Yes,’ I says, ‘I reckon you’ll let it run long enough fer the widder to pay the taxes on’t once more anyhow; I guess the secur’ty’s good enough to take that resk; but how ’bout *my* secur’ty?’ I says. ‘What d’you mean?’ he says. ‘I mean,’ says I, ‘that I’ve got a second morgidge on that prop’ty, an’ I begin to tremble fer my secur’ty.

这不算撒谎，』我说，『因为我并没有*给*你，』我说。这个嘛，她照办了。斯温尼当然一开头就疑心有我掺和；可她顶得那样四平八稳，他反倒不知*该*怎么想了；不过他的爪子好歹叫剪了一阵。
「事情这样走了一程，直到我拿定主意：该替斯温尼分担几分尘世营生的焦虑了。一天早晨我踱过去，寒暄已毕，互相把健康之类的题目放宽了心，我说：『你手里有一笔卡洛姆寡妇那块地的抵押，对不对？』他除了「有」没的可说。『利钱她照付？』我说。『我不是要伸鼻子进你的生意，』我说，『不愿说的一个字别说。』这个嘛，他知道戴夫·哈勒姆是戴夫·哈勒姆，不如干脆倒出来；他说：『嗯，她好一阵子一个子儿没付；可上一回，她把全数都捧来了。』「不过，」他说，「再照付，我看她是不能了。」「那要是不能，」我说，「你就收地，是不是？」「没第二条路，」他说；又猛一抬眼：「除非你出价压我，」他说。「不，」我说，「我眼下不置地产；我进来，」我说，「拋开同你说话的乐趣不谈——是要说：那笔抵押，我接过来。」
「这个嘛，先生，嘿，嘿，嘿，嘿！见了个鬼——！他下巴搭到脖子上，直蹬了我一分钟；随后他把身子一缩，脖子一收，活只泥龟。『不，』他说，『我不缺钱使；抵押我留着。眼看快到期，兴许再由它拖一阵。保得住，我想。』『是，』我说，『我料你由它拖，拖到寡妇再替它纳一茬税；这点风险，保金大约担得起；可是*我*的保金呢？』我说。『什么意思？』他说。『意思是这样，』我说，『我手里有那块地的第二抵押，我开始为我的保金发抖了。

===Original===

You’ve jest told me,’ I says, ‘that you’re goin’ to foreclose an’ I cal’late to protect myself, an’ I *don’t* cal’late,’ I says, ‘to have to go an’ bid on that prop’ty, an’ put in a lot more money to save my investment, unless I’m ’bleeged to﻿—not *much*! an’ you can jest sign that morgidge over to me, an’ the sooner the quicker,’ I says.”
David brought his hand down on his thigh with a vigorous slap, the fellow of the one which, John could imagine, had emphasized his demand upon Swinney. The story, to which he had at first listened with polite patience merely, he had found more interesting as it went on, and, excusing himself, he brought up a stool, and mounting it, said, “And what did Swinney say to that?” Mr. Harum emitted a gurgling chuckle, yawned his quid out of his mouth, tossing it over his shoulder in the general direction of the waste basket, and bit off the end of a cigar which he found by slapping his waistcoat pockets. John got down and fetched him a match, which he scratched in the vicinity of his hip pocket, lighted his cigar (John declining to join him on some plausible pretext, having on a previous occasion accepted one of the brand), and after rolling it around with his lips and tongue to the effect that the lighted end described sundry eccentric curves, located it firmly with an upward angle in the left-hand corner of his mouth, gave it a couple of vigorous puffs, and replied to John’s question.
“Wa’al, ’Zeke Swinney was a perfesser of religion some years ago, an’ mebbe he is now, but what he said to me on this pertic’ler occasion was that he’d see me in hell fust, an’ *then* he wouldn’t.
“ ‘Wa’al,’ I says, ‘mebbe you won’t, mebbe you will, it’s alwus a pleasure to meet ye,’ I says, ‘but in that case this morgidge bus’nis’ll be a question fer our executors,’ I says, ‘fer *you* don’t never foreclose that morgidge, an’ don’t you fergit it,’ I says.
“ ‘Oh, you’d like to git holt o’ that prop’ty yourself. I see what you’re up to,’ he says.
“ ‘Look a-here, ’Zeke Swinney,’ I says, ‘I’ve got an int’rist in that prop’ty, an’ I propose to p’tect it. You’re goin’ to sign that morgidge over to me, or I’ll foreclose and surrygate ye,’ I says, ‘unless you allow to bid in the prop’ty, in which case we’ll see whose weasel-skin’s the longest. But I guess it won’t come to that,’ I says. ‘You kin take your choice,’ I says.

===Chinese===

你方才亲口说要收地；我要保我自己，而我*不*打算，』我说，『跑去那块地上出价，再填进一注钱救我的本——除非万不得已——想得美！你干脆把那笔抵押签转给我，越快越好，』我说。」
大卫一巴掌拍在自己大腿上——这一掌的孪生兄弟，约翰想得出，当时怎样敲在斯温尼的桌上。这段故事，他起初只是出于礼貌耐着性子听，越听越有味；此刻他告了个便，搬来一张凳子跨上去，问：「斯温尼怎么说？」哈勒姆先生发出一声咕噜般的轻笑，把嘴里的烟团吐出来，朝身后废纸篓的大致方向一甩，拍着马甲口袋摸出一支雪茄，咬掉头。约翰下来替他划了根火柴——他就在后裤袋附近一擦——点上雪茄（约翰借口婉谢了同抽，这个牌子他领教过一回），拿嘴唇和舌头把它盘弄了一阵，点着的那头画出好几道古怪的弧线，末了把它牢牢安顿在左嘴角，冲上一个角度，猛喷两口，这才回答约翰的问话。
「这个嘛，齐克·斯温尼多年前是位信教的教授——兴许如今还是；可他那天对我说的原话是：先让他下地狱见我，*到时候*还不肯见。
「『嗯，』我说，『见得着见不着，天知道；同你见面永远是件乐事，』我说，『不过那样的话，这桩抵押的事就归咱们的遗嘱执行人去办了，』我说，『因为你那笔抵押，*永远*收不成，这话你记住，』我说。
「『哦，你想自己吞那块地。你的算盘我看穿了，』他说。
「『听着，齐克·斯温尼，』我说，『我在那块地上有权益，我打算护住它。你把那笔抵押签转给我；不然我就收地，叫你靠边，』我说，『除非你打算自己出价吃进——那样咱们就比比谁的黄鼠狼皮长。可我看不至于，』我说，『两条路随你挑，』我说。

===Original===

‘Whether I want to git holt o’ that prop’ty myself ain’t neither here nor there. Mebbe I do, an’ mebbe I don’t, but anyways,’ I says, ‘*you* don’t git it, nor wouldn’t ever, for if I can’t make you sign over, I’ll either do what I said or I’ll back the widder in a defence fer usury. Put that in your pipe an’ smoke it,’ I says.
“ ‘What do you mean?’ he says, gittin’ half out his chair.
“ ‘I mean this,’ I says, ‘that the fust six months the widder couldn’t pay she gin you ten dollars to hold off, an’ the next time she gin you fifteen, an’ that you’ve bled her fer shaves to the tune of sixty odd dollars in three years, an’ then got your int’rist in full.’
“That riz him clean out of his chair,” said David. “ ‘She can’t prove it,’ he says, shakin’ his fist in the air.
“ ‘Oh, ho! ho!’ I says, tippin’ my chair back agin the wall. ‘If Mis’ Cullom was to swear how an’ where she paid you the money, givin’ chapter an’ verse, and showin’ her own mem’randums even, an’ I was to swear that when I twitted you with gittin’ it you didn’t deny it, but only said that she couldn’t *prove* it, how long do you think it ’ould take a Freeland County jury to find agin ye? I allow, ’Zeke Swinney,’ I says, ‘that you wa’n’t born yestyd’y, but you ain’t so old as you look, not by a dum sight!’ an’ then how I did laugh!
“Wa’al,” said David, as he got down off the stool and stretched himself, yawning, “I guess I’ve yarned it enough fer one day. Don’t fergit to send Mis’ Cullom that notice, an’ make it up an’ up. I’m goin’ to git the thing off my mind this trip.”
“Very well, sir,” said John, “but let me ask, did Swinney assign the mortgage without any trouble?”
“O Lord! yes,” was the reply. “The’ wa’n’t nothin’ else fer him to do. I had another twist on him that I hain’t mentioned. But he put up a great show of doin’ it to obleege me. Wa’al, I thanked him an’ so on, an’ when we’d got through I ast him if he wouldn’t step over to the Eagil an’ take somethin’, an’ he looked kind o’ shocked an’ said he never drinked nothin’. It was ’gin his princ’ples, he said. Ho, ho, ho, ho! Scat my —﻿—﻿—! Princ’ples!” and John heard him chuckling to himself all the way out of the office.

===Chinese===

『我自己想不想要那块地，不相干。兴许想，兴许不想；可横竖，』我说，『*你*拿不到，永远拿不到：你不肯签转，我就照方才说的办；再不然，我帮寡妇打一场高利贷的官司。这个你装烟点上慢慢咂吧去吧，』我说。
「『什么意思？』他说，半截从椅子里蹿起来。
「『意思是这样，』我说，『寡妇头一个半年付不出，塞了你十块求缓；再一回塞了你十五；三年里你刮她的头寸、零割的肉，前后六十好几块——利钱又照数全收。』
这一下把他整个儿从椅子里掀了起来，」大卫说。「『她拿不出凭据，』他朝空中挥着拳说。
「『哦——嚯！嚯！』我说，把椅子往后一仰，靠上墙。『卡洛姆太太若是一五一十宣誓，说她何时何地把钱付给你，日子地点件件有着落，连她自己的流水账都摆出来；我再宣誓说，我拿这话点你，你没否认，只说她拿不出*凭据*——你说，弗里兰县的陪审团要裁你输，得用几分钟？我承认，齐克·斯温尼，』我说，『你不是昨天才生的；可你也没有看起来那么老——差得远！』说完我那个笑哟！」
「这个嘛，」大卫一面跨下凳子、伸着懒腰打呵欠，一面说，「今天的闲篇儿够本了。别忘给卡洛姆太太送那句话；字斟句酌地写。这一回我要把这件事从心里卸下来。」
「好的，先生，」约翰说，「请问：斯温尼转让抵押，没费事吧？」
「老天，没费事，」回答是。「他没第二条路。我手里还捏着他一把柄，没提呢。可他偏偏摆出一副「专诚帮忙」的大架势。我谢了谢他，等等；办完我请他移步鹰旅店喝一口，他还装出一副受惊的样子，说他从不沾酒，说那违他的原则。嚯，嚯，嚯，嚯！见了个鬼——！原则！」约翰听着他一路嘿嘿地笑出办公室去。

## XVIII / 十八
===Original===

Considering John’s relations with David Harum, it was natural that he should wish to think as well of him as possible, and he had not (or thought he had not) allowed his mind to be influenced by the disparaging remarks and insinuations which had been made to him, or in his presence, concerning his employer. He had made up his mind to form his opinion upon his own experience with the man, and so far it had not only been pleasant but favorable, and far from justifying the half-jeering, half-malicious talk that had come to his ears. It had been made manifest to him, it was true, that David was capable of a sharp bargain in certain lines, but it seemed to him that it was more for the pleasure of matching his wits against another’s than for any gain involved. Mr. Harum was an experienced and expert horseman, who delighted above all things in dealing in and trading horses, and John soon discovered that, in that community at least, to get the best of a “hoss-trade” by almost any means was considered a venial sin, if a sin at all, and the standards of ordinary business probity were not expected to govern those transactions.
David had said to him once when he suspected that John’s ideas might have sustained something of a shock, “A hoss-trade ain’t like anythin’ else. A feller may be straighter ’n a string in ev’rythin’ else, an’ never tell the truth﻿—that is, the hull truth﻿—about a hoss. I trade hosses with hoss-traders. They all think they know as much as I do, an’ I dunno but what they do. They hain’t learnt no diff’rent anyway, an’ they’ve had chances enough. If a feller come to me that didn’t think he knowed anythin’ about a hoss, an’ wanted to buy on the square, he’d git, fur’s I knew, square treatment. At any rate I’d tell him all ’t I knew. But when one o’ them smart Alecks comes along and cal’lates to do up old Dave, why he’s got to take his chances, that’s all. An’ mind ye,” asserted David, shaking his forefinger impressively, “it ain’t only them fellers. I’ve ben wuss stuck two three time by church members in good standin’ than anybody I ever dealed with. Take old Deakin Perkins. He’s a terrible feller fer church bus’nis; c’n pray an’ psalm-sing to beat the Jews, an’ in spiritual matters c’n read his title clear the hull time, but when it comes to hoss-tradin’ you got to git up very early in the mornin’ or he’ll skin the eyeteeth out of ye. Yes, sir!

===Chinese===

就约翰同大卫·哈勒姆的关系而论，他自然愿意把这个人往最好里想；那些当着他、或当他的面说过的、关于东家的贬语和影射，他没有（或者自认没有）让它们左右自己。他拿定主意：对这个人的评判，只凭自己的亲身经手；至今为止，那经历不仅愉快，而且偏于好评，同他听到的那些半讥半毒的闲话全然对不上。诚然，他已见识过大卫在几路买卖上砍得下狠价；可在他看来，那多半是为了斗智的乐趣，不为那点实利。哈勒姆先生是位老练的马客，最大的乐子就在贩马换马；约翰很快发现，至少在那一方乡里，「马生意」上不择手段占上风，算轻罪——如果算罪的话——而寻常买卖里的诚信规矩，是不被指望来管这类交易的。

有一回，大卫疑心约翰的观念受了点震动，对他说：「马生意同啥都不一样。一个人旁的样样笔直得像根线，可提起马，就是不说实话——就是说，不全说实话。俺同马贩子做马生意。他们个个当自己懂得不比俺少；俺看兴许真不比俺少。反正他们没学出别的来，机会也给足过他们了。要有谁上俺这儿来，自认对马一窍不通，想照实买，那据俺所知，他得到的就是照实的待遇。至少，俺知道的都告诉他。可那些自作聪明的宝贝蛋子，算计着要收拾老戴夫，那就各安天命，就这么回事。还有你记着，」大卫郑重其事地晃着食指，「不光是那路人。俺吃过的大亏，有两三回是教那些口碑载道的教友给的，比谁给的都狠。就说老珀金斯执事。教会的事务他顶热心；祷告、唱诗，犹太人都赛不过他；灵性的事上，产权状他念得从头到尾清清白白；可一到了马生意——你得起得比鸡早，不然他能把你连犬齿带皮剥了去。真的，先生！

===Original===

Scat my —﻿—﻿—! I believe the old critter *makes* hosses! But the deakin,” added David, “he, he, he, he! the deakin hain’t hardly spoke to me fer some consid’able time, the deakin hain’t. He, he, he!
“Another thing,” he went on, “the’ ain’t no gamble like a hoss. You may think you know him through an’ through, an’ fust thing you know he’ll be cuttin’ up a lot o’ didos right out o’ nothin’. It stands to reason that sometimes you let a hoss go all on the square﻿—as you know him﻿—an’ the feller that gits him don’t know how to hitch him or treat him, an’ he acts like a diff’rent hoss, an’ the feller allows you swindled him. You see, hosses gits used to places an’ ways to a certain extent, an’ when they’re changed, why they’re apt to act diff’rent. Hosses don’t know but dreadful little, really. Talk about hoss sense﻿—wa’al, the’ ain’t no such thing.”
Thus spoke David on the subject of his favorite pursuit and pastime, and John thought then that he could understand and condone some things he had seen and heard, at which at first he was inclined to look askance. But this matter of the Widow Cullom’s was a different thing, and as he realized that he was expected to play a part, though a small one, in it, his heart sank within him that he had so far cast his fortunes upon the good will of a man who could plan and carry out so heartless and cruel an undertaking as that which had been revealed to him that afternoon. He spent the evening in his room trying to read, but the widow’s affairs persistently thrust themselves upon his thoughts. All the unpleasant stories he had heard of David came to his mind, and he remembered with misgiving some things which at the time had seemed regular and right enough, but which took on a different color in the light in which he found himself recalling them. He debated with himself whether he should not decline to send Mrs. Cullom the notice as he had been instructed, and left it an open question when he went to bed.
He wakened somewhat earlier than usual to find that the thermometer had gone up, and the barometer down. The air was full of a steady downpour, half snow, half rain, about the most disheartening combination which the worst climate in the world﻿—that of central New York﻿—can furnish.

===Chinese===

「去他的———！俺都疑心那老东西会*下*马驹子！不过执事嘛，」大卫又添一句，「嘿、嘿、嘿、嘿！执事有相当一阵子不大同俺说话了，执事是不大说了。嘿、嘿、嘿！

「还有一样，」他接着说，「天底下没有哪样赌局赛得过马。你当你把它里外摸透，一转眼，它平白无故就给你耍一串花活。按理说，有时候你照实——照你对它的了解——放一匹马出去，得马那家伙不懂怎么套它、怎么待它，它就换了一副马样，那人就咬定你坑了他。你瞧，马对地方、对规矩，有一定的习惯；一换，行为就容易变。要说马懂什么——说实在的，马懂的那点东西少得可怜。什么『马的灵性』——这个嘛，压根没那回事。」

关于他最钟爱的事业和消遣，大卫如是说；约翰当时觉得，自己见过的、听过的一些起初让他斜眼相看的事，如今可以理解、可以宽宥了。可卡勒姆寡妇这件事是另一码事；待他明白自己竟要在这事里充当一个角色——哪怕小小的角色——他的心沉了下去：自己竟把身家托付给了这样一个人的善意，一个能谋划、能施行那天下午向他摊开的这般狠毒营生的人。一晚上他待在屋里想读点书，寡妇的事却缠着念头不肯走。他听过的关于大卫的一切不愉快的故事全涌了上来；又有几件当时看着规规矩矩、并无不妥的事，如今在他重忆它们的光线里，变了颜色，让他不安。他同自己辩论：是不是该抗命，不把那纸通知发给卡勒姆太太；上床时，这问题还悬着。

他醒得比平常略早，发现温度计升了，气压计降了。天空下着一场不紧不慢的雨雪各半，这大约是全世界最坏的气候——纽约州中部——所能端出的最败人兴致的一种搭配。

===Original===

He passed rather a busy day in the office in an atmosphere redolent of the unsavory odors raised by the proximity of wet boots and garments to the big cylinder stove outside the counter, a compound of stale smells from kitchen and stable.
After the bank closed he dispatched Peleg Hopkins, the office boy, with the note for Mrs. Cullom. He had abandoned his half-formed intention to revolt, but had made the note not only as little peremptory as was compatible with a clear intimation of its purport as he understood it, but had yielded to a natural impulse in beginning it with an expression of personal regret﻿—a blunder which cost him no little chagrin in the outcome.
Peleg Hopkins grumbled audibly when he was requested to build the fires on Christmas day, and expressed his opinion that “if there warn’t Bible agin workin’ on Chris’mus, the’ ’d ort ter be”; but when John opened the door of the bank that morning he found the temperature in comfortable contrast to the outside air. The weather had changed again, and a blinding snowstorm, accompanied by a buffeting gale from the northwest, made it almost impossible to see a path and to keep it. In the central part of the town some tentative efforts had been made to open walks, but these were apparent only as slight and tortuous depressions in the depths of snow. In the outskirts, the unfortunate pedestrian had to wade to the knees.
As John went behind the counter his eye was at once caught by a small parcel lying on his desk, of white note paper, tied with a cotton string, which he found to be addressed, “Mr. John Lenox, Esq., Present,” and as he took it up it seemed heavy for its size.
Opening it, he found a tiny stocking, knit of white wool, to which was pinned a piece of paper with the legend, “A Merry Christmas from Aunt Polly.” Out of the stocking fell a packet fastened with a rubber strap. Inside were five ten-dollar gold pieces and a slip of paper on which was written, “A Merry Christmas from Your Friend David Harum.” For a moment John’s face burned, and there was a curious smarting of the eyelids as he held the little stocking and its contents in his hand. Surely the hand that had written “Your Friend” on that scrap of paper could not be the hand of an oppressor of widows and orphans.

===Chinese===

这一天他在行里过得相当忙，空气里氤氲着一股湿靴湿衣凑在柜台外那台大圆筒炉边烘出来的浊味——厨房味同马厩味的陈年化合物。

银行关门后，他差遣听差的男孩佩莱格·霍普金斯（Peleg Hopkins），把那张字条送给卡勒姆太太。那个半途而废的造反念头他放弃了；可他写那张字条，不但把它写得同「明白交代来意」这个底线所能兼容的程度一样委婉，还顺着一股天性的冲动，开头先写了一句自己心里的歉意——这个岔子，后来叫他懊恼不已。

让他圣诞节生炉子，佩莱格·霍普金斯咕哝出声，还发表了意见：「要是圣经里没有一条反对圣诞做工的，就该添一条」；可圣诞早晨约翰推开关行的大门，发觉屋里气温同外头一比，舒坦得很。天又变了，一场雪暴卷着西北风劈头盖脸，想看清道、想踩住道，都几乎不能。镇中心有几段试开的步道，可它们只在深雪里显出一道浅浅的、七拐八弯的凹痕。到了镇子外缘，倒霉的行人只好趙雪，深及膝盖。

约翰绕进柜台，一眼就被桌上一个小包裹勾住：白信纸包的，棉线捆着，封皮写着「约翰·伦诺克斯先生台启」；一上手，就那点大小来说，沉得出奇。

拆开，是只小小的袜子，白毛线织的，别着一张纸条，题着：「波莉姨妈贺圣诞」。袜子里掉出一个皮筋箍着的小包。里头是五枚十元金币，还有一张纸条，写着：「你的朋友大卫·哈勒姆，贺圣诞」。有一瞬，约翰的脸发烧，捏着那只小袜子和它的内容，眼皮奇异地刺痛。在那张纸片上写下「你的朋友」的手，断不会同时是欺压寡妇孤儿的手。

===Original===

“This,” said John to himself, “is what he meant when ‘he supposed it wouldn’t take me long to find out what was in my stocking.’ ”
---
The door opened and a blast and whirl of wind and snow rushed in, ushering the tall, bent form of the Widow Cullom. The drive of the wind was so strong that John vaulted over the low cash counter to push the door shut again. The poor woman was white with snow from the front of her old worsted hood to the bottom of her ragged skirt.
“You are Mrs. Cullom?” said John. “Wait a moment till I brush off the snow, and then come to the fire in the back room. Mr. Harum will be in directly, I expect.”
“Be I much late?” she asked. “I made ’s much haste ’s I could. It don’t appear to me ’s if I ever see a blusteriner day, ’n I ain’t as strong as I used to be. Seemed as if I never would git here.”
“Oh, no,” said John, as he established her before the glowing grate of the Franklin stove in the bank parlor, “not at all. Mr. Harum has not come in himself yet. Shall you mind if I excuse myself a moment while you make yourself as comfortable as possible?” She did not apparently hear him. She was trembling from head to foot with cold and fatigue and nervous excitement. Her dress was soaked to the knees, and as she sat down and put up her feet to the fire John saw a bit of a thin cotton stocking and her deplorable shoes, almost in a state of pulp. A snow-obliterated path led from the back door of the office to David’s house, and John snatched his hat and started for it on a run. As he stamped off some of the snow on the veranda the door was opened for him by Mrs. Bixbee. “Lord sakes!” she exclaimed. “What on earth be you cavortin’ ’round for such a mornin’ ’s this without no overcoat, an’ on a dead run? What’s the matter?”
“Nothing serious,” he answered, “but I’m in a great hurry. Old Mrs. Cullom has walked up from her house to the office, and she is wet through and almost perished. I thought you’d send her some dry shoes and stockings, and an old shawl or blanket to keep her wet skirt off her knees, and a drop of whisky or something. She’s all of a tremble, and I’m afraid she will have a chill.”
“Certain! certain!” said the kind creature, and she bustled out of the room, returning in a minute or two with an armful of comforts. “There’s a pair of bedroom slips lined with lamb’s wool, an’ a pair of woolen stockin’s, an’ a blanket shawl.

===Chinese===

「这个，」约翰自语，「就是他那句『想必用不了多久你就会知道袜子里装了什么』的意思。」

---

门开了，一股风雪灌进来，送进卡勒姆寡妇那高高偊偊的身影。风势太猛，约翰翻过低低的出纳柜，把门重新顶上。那可怜的女人，从旧绒线兜帽的前脸到破裙子的底边，一身雪白。

「您是卡勒姆太太吧？」约翰说。「等等，我先给您掸掸雪，再请到里间炉边坐。哈勒姆先生想必就来。」

「俺迟得狠不？」她问。「能赶的俺都赶了。俺瞅着，这么撒野的天，俺活这么大没见过；俺也不比从前硬朗了。一路上只当走不到头。」

「不迟，一点也不。」约翰说着，把她安置在银行客厅那座烧得通红的富兰克林炉前。「哈勒姆先生自己还没到。您先怎么舒坦怎么待着，容我告退片刻，您介意吗？」她显然没听见。她从头到脚地抖——冻的，乏的，还有神经的激动。裙裾湿到膝盖，她坐下把脚伸向炉火时，约翰看见一小截薄棉袜，和那双惨不忍睹的鞋——几乎泡成了烂泥。行里后门通往大卫家的小径已被雪抹平，约翰抓起帽子一路跑了过去。他在廊下跺掉些雪，门开了，是比克斯比太太。「老天爷！」她嚷。「这么个早晨，你连大衣都不穿，还一路狂奔，疯什么？出什么事了？」

「没大事，」他答，「就是十万火急。卡勒姆老太太从她家一路走到行里，浑身湿透，人快冻僵了。我想请您给她捎双干鞋干袜，再拿条旧披肩或毯子，把她湿裙子同膝盖隔开，再滴点威士忌什么的。她整个人抖成一片，我怕她要发寒。」

「这就办！这就办！」这善良人说，风风火火出了屋，一两分钟抱回来一怀的暖和物。「这是一双羔羊毛衬里的拖鞋，一双毛线袜，一条毯披肩。

===Original===

This here petticut, ’tain’t what ye’d call bran’ new, but it’s warm and comf’table, an’ I don’t believe she’s got much of anythin’ on ’ceptin’ her dress, an’ I’ll git ye the whisky, but”﻿—here she looked deprecatingly at John﻿—“it ain’t gen’ally known ’t we keep the stuff in the house. I don’t know as it’s right, but though David don’t hardly ever touch it he will have it in the house.”
“Oh,” said John, laughing, “you may trust my discretion, and we’ll swear Mrs. Cullom to secrecy.”
“Wa’al, all right,” said Mrs. Bixbee, joining in the laugh as she brought the bottle; “jest a minute till I make a passel of the things to keep the snow out. There, now, I guess you’re fixed, an’ you kin hurry back ’fore she ketches a chill.”
“Thanks very much,” said John as he started away. “I have something to say to you besides ‘Merry Christmas,’ but I must wait till another time.”
When John got back to the office David had just preceded him.
“Wa’al, wa’al,” he was saying, “but you be in a putty consid’able state. Hullo, John! what you got there? Wa’al, you air the stuff! Slips, blanket-shawl, petticut, stockin’s﻿—wa’al, you an’ Polly ben puttin’ your heads together, I guess. What’s that? Whisky! Wa’al, scat my —﻿—﻿—! I didn’t s’pose wild hosses would have drawed it out o’ Polly to let on the’ was any in the house, much less to fetch it out. Jest the thing! Oh, yes ye are, Mis’ Cullom﻿—jest a mouthful with water,” taking the glass from John, “jest a spoonful to git your blood a-goin’, an’ then Mr. Lenox an’ me’ll go into the front room while you make yourself comf’table.”
“Consarn it all!” exclaimed Mr. Harum as they stood leaning against the teller’s counter, facing the street, “I didn’t cal’late to have Mis’ Cullom hoof it up here the way she done. When I see what kind of a day it was I went out to the barn to have the cutter hitched an’ send for her, an’ I found ev’rythin’ topsy-turvy. That dum’d uneasy sorril colt had got cast in the stall, an’ I ben fussin’ with him ever since. I clean forgot all ’bout Mis’ Cullom till jest now.”
“Is the colt much injured?” John asked.
“Wa’al, he won’t trot a twenty gait in some time, I reckon,” replied David. “He’s wrenched his shoulder some, an’ mebbe strained his inside. Don’t seem to take no int’rist in his feed, an’ that’s a bad sign. Consarn a hoss, anyhow! If they’re wuth anythin’ they’re more bother ’n a teethin’ baby.

===Chinese===

「这条衬裙，说新算不上新，可它暖和、舒坦；俺看她除了那身裙子，里头八成没多少货色了。威士忌俺这就去拿，不过」——说到这儿她朝约翰投来劝阻的一眼——「家里存着这物件，一般没人知道。对不对俺说不上；大卫自己虽然几乎不沾，可他非要家里备着。」

「哦，」约翰笑道，「我的嘴您放得了心，回头再叫卡勒姆太太也起个誓。」

「这个嘛，成。」比克斯比太太说，取酒瓶来时也跟着笑；「稍等，俺把这一摊打个包，别灌雪。好了，齐了；你赶紧回去，趁她还没发寒。」

「多谢。」约翰临走说。「除了『圣诞快乐』，我还有话要对您说——只好留到下回了。」

约翰回到行里时，大卫刚比他早一步到。

「这个嘛，这个嘛，」他正说着，「你这可真成了个了不起的人物了。哟，约翰！你抱的什么？嚯，好家伙！拖鞋、毯披肩、衬裙、袜子——这个嘛，你同波莉碰过头了吧，俺猜。那是什么？威士忌！嚯，去他的———！俺原当野马也拖不出波莉的口风、认家里有这玩意儿，更别说抱出来了。正合适！哦，喝得喝得，卡勒姆太太——兑水，一小口，」他从约翰手里接过杯子，「一小勺，让血走起来；完了伦诺克斯先生同俺去前屋，您自管舒坦。」

「真是糟心！」两人倚着出纳柜、面朝大街站着，哈勒姆先生叹道，「俺没算到卡勒姆太太会靠两条腿走上这一趟。一看今天这天气，俺就去马房叫套雪橇去接她，结果里头翻了天。那匹该死的闹腾沙毛驹在栏里打了个滚卡住了，俺伺候它伺候到这会儿。卡勒姆太太的事，俺忘了个干净，刚才才想起来。」

「驹子伤得重不重？」约翰问。

「这个嘛，一时半会儿，二十节的步子它是迈不出来了，俺瞅。」大卫答。「肩膀拧了些，里头兴许也伤了。草料它瞧着不香——这不是好兆头。唉，这马！但凡值点钱的，比出牙的娃娃还磨人。

===Original===

Alwus some dum thing ailin’ ’em, an’ I took consid’able stock in that colt too,” he added regretfully, “an’ I could ’a’ got putty near what I was askin’ fer him last week, an’ putty near what he was wuth, an’ I’ve noticed that most gen’ally alwus when I let a good offer go like that, some cussed thing happens to the hoss. It ain’t a bad idee, in the hoss bus’nis anyway, to be willin’ to let the other feller make a dollar once ’n a while.”
After that aphorism they waited in silence for a few minutes, and then David called out over his shoulder, “How be you gettin’ along, Mis’ Cullom?”
“I guess I’m fixed,” she answered, and David walked slowly back into the parlor, leaving John in the front office. He was annoyed to realize that in the bustle over Mrs. Cullom and what followed, he had forgotten to acknowledge the Christmas gift; but, hoping that Mr. Harum had been equally oblivious, promised himself to repair the omission later on. He would have preferred to go out and leave the two to settle their affair without witness or hearer, but his employer, who, as he had found, usually had a reason for his actions, had explicitly requested him to remain, and he had no choice. He perched himself upon one of the office stools and composed himself to await the conclusion of the affair.

===Chinese===

「身上总有哪件该死的毛病。那匹驹子俺原本也上了心，」他惋惜地添一句，「上礼拜，俺差点就按俺要的价出了手——差不多就是它值的价；可俺留意到一条，十有八九，这样的好价一放走，马身上就要出点遭瘟的事。所以说，做马生意，肯时不时让对面那主儿挣一块，不算坏主意。」

这条格言之后，两人默等了几分钟；随后大卫回头喊：「弄得咋样了，卡勒姆太太？」

「齐了，俺瞅。」她答；大卫慢慢踱回客厅，把约翰留在前头营业室。他懊恼地发觉，忙乱卡勒姆太太这一场、连同其后种种，他竟忘了谢那份圣诞礼；不过他巴望哈勒姆先生同样没留神，暗自许愿回头补上。他本想出去，让两位无人旁观无人旁听地了结他们的事；可东家——他已领教过，此人行事向来有名堂——明明白白要他留下，他别无选择。他在一张柜台圆凳上坐定，安下心等这桩事的结局。

## XX / 二十
===Original===

The widow was looking at David with shining eyes and devouring his words. All the years of trouble and sorrow and privation were wiped out, and she was back in the days of her girlhood. Ah, yes! how well she remembered him as he looked that very day﻿—so handsome, so splendidly dressed, so debonair; and how proud she had been to sit by his side that night, observed and envied of all the village girls.
“I ain’t goin’ to go over the hull show,” proceeded David, “well ’s I remember it. The’ didn’t nothin’ git away from me that afternoon, an’ once I come near to stickin’ a piece o’ gingerbread into my ear ’stid o’ my mouth. I had my ten-cent piece that Billy P. give me, but he wouldn’t let me buy nothin’; an’ when the gingerbread man come along he says, ‘Air ye hungry, Dave? (I’d told him my name), air ye hungry?’ Wa’al, I was a growin’ boy, an’ I was hungry putty much all the time. He bought two big squares an’ gin me one, an’ when I’d swallered it, he says, ‘Guess you better tackle this one too,’ he says, ‘I’ve dined.’ I didn’t exac’ly know what ‘dined’ meant, but﻿—he, he, he, he!﻿—I tackled it,” and David smacked his lips in memory.
“Wa’al,” he went on, “we done the hull programmy﻿—gingerbread, lemonade﻿—*pink* lemonade, an’ he took some o’ that﻿—popcorn, peanuts, pep’mint candy, cin’mun candy﻿—scat my —﻿—﻿—! an’ he payin’ fer ev’rythin’﻿—I thought he was jest made o’ money! An’ I remember how we talked about all the doin’s; the ridin’, an’ jumpin’, an’ summersettin’, an’ all﻿—fer he’d got all the shyniss out of me for the time﻿—an’ once I looked up at him, an’ he looked down at me with that curious look in his eyes an’ put his hand on my shoulder. Wa’al, now, I tell ye, I had a queer, crinkly feelin’ go up an’ down my back, an’ I like to up an’ cried.”
“Dave,” said the widow, “I kin see you two as if you was settin’ there front of me. He was alwus like that. Oh, my! Oh, my! David,” she added solemnly, while two tears rolled slowly down her wrinkled face, “we lived together, husban’ an’ wife, fer seven year, an’ he never give me a cross word.”
“I don’t doubt it a mossel,” said David simply, leaning over and poking the fire, which operation kept his face out of her sight and was prolonged rather unduly.

===Chinese===

寡妇眼睛发亮地望着大卫，一个字一个字地吞。多年的愁苦与匮乏一扫而空，她回到了做姑娘的日子。啊，是！那一天他的模样她记得多清楚——那样俊，那样一身好衣裳，那样倜傥；那天夜里坐在他身边，受全村姑娘的注视与眼红，她多骄傲。
「整场戏我不从头讲了，」大卫接着说，「虽说我都记得。那天下午什么也没从我眼皮底下溜掉；有一回我险些把一块姜饼塞进耳朵里，没塞进嘴。比利·P.给我的那枚一毛的银毫子我揣着；他什么都不让我买；卖姜饼的过来，他说：『饿不饿，戴夫？』（我的名字我告诉过他）『饿不饿？』这个嘛，我是半大孩子，差不多永远饿着。他买了两大方，给我一方；我咽下去，他说：『这块你也对付了吧，』他说，『我用过饭了。』『用过饭』是什么意思我不很懂，可是——嘿，嘿，嘿，嘿！——我对付了。」大卫咂着嘴回味。
「这个嘛，」他接着说，「全套节目我们走了一遍——姜饼，柠檬水——*粉红*的柠檬水，这个他也喝了几口——爆米花，花生，薄荷糖，桂皮糖——见了个鬼——！样样他付钱——我当他整个人是钱做的！我还记得我们聊那一切的名堂：骑马呀，钻圈呀，翻筋斗呀——他一时把我身上的怕生全收走了——有一回我抬头看他，他低头看我，眼睛里那副说不出的神气，一只手搭上我的肩。这个嘛，我告诉你，一股说不清的麻酥酥顺着我的脊梁骨爬上爬下，我险些哭出来。」
「戴夫，」寡妇说，「你们两个，就像坐在我眼前一样。他一贯是这个样子。哦，天哪！哦，天哪！大卫，」她庄严地添一句，两行泪慢慢滚下她打皱的脸，「我们做夫妻做了七年，他没同我说过一句重话。」
「我一星半点也不疑心，」大卫简单地说，俯身捅了捅炉火；这一操作把他的脸挪出了她的视线，且拖得略嫌过长。

===Original===

Finally he straightened up and, blowing his nose as it were a trumpet, said:
“Wa’al, the cirkis fin’ly come to an end, an’ the crowd hustled to git out ’s if they was afraid the tent ’d come down on ’em. I got kind o’ mixed up in ’em, an’ somebody tried to git my tin pail, or I thought he did, an’ the upshot was that I lost sight o’ Billy P., an’ couldn’t make out to ketch a glimpse of him nowhere. An’ *then* I kind o’ come down to earth, kerchug! It was five o’clock, an’ I had better ’n four mile to walk﻿—mostly up hill﻿—an’ if I knowed anything ’bout the old man, an’ I thought I *did*, I had the all-firedist lickin’ ahead of me ’t I’d ever got, an’ that was sayin’ a good deal. But, boy ’s I was, I had grit enough to allow ’twas wuth it, an’ off I put.”
“Did he lick ye much?” inqured Mrs. Cullom anxiously.
“Wa’al,” replied David, “he done his best. He was layin’ fer me when I struck the front gate﻿—I knowed it wa’n’t no use to try the back door, an’ he took me by the ear﻿—most pulled it off﻿—an’ marched me off to the barn shed without a word. I never see him so mad. Seemed like he couldn’t speak fer a while, but fin’ly he says, ‘Where you ben all day?’
“ ‘Down t’ the village,’ I says.
“ ‘What you ben up to down there?’ he says.
“ ‘Went to the cirkis,’ I says, thinkin’ I might ’s well make a clean breast on’t.
“ ‘Where ’d you git the money?’ he says.
“ ‘Mr. Cullom took me,’ I says.
“ ‘You lie,’ he says. ‘You stole the money somewheres, an’ I’ll trounce it out of ye, if I kill ye,’ he says.
“Wa’al,” said David, twisting his shoulders in recollection, “I won’t harrer up your feelin’s. ’S I told you, he done his best. I was willin’ to quit long ’fore he was. Fact was, he overdone it a little, an’ he had to throw water in my face ’fore he got through; an’ he done that as thorough as the other thing. I was somethin’ like a chickin jest out o’ the cistern. I crawled off to bed the best I could, but I didn’t lay on my back fer a good spell, I c’n tell ye.”
“You poor little critter,” exclaimed Mrs. Cullom sympathetically. “You poor little critter!”
“ ’Twas more’n wuth it, Mis’ Cullom,” said David emphatically. “I’d had the most enjoy’ble day, I might say the only enjoy’ble day, ’t I’d ever had in my hull life, an’ I hain’t never fergot it. I got over the lickin’ in course of time, but I’ve ben enjoyin’ that cirkis fer forty year.

===Chinese===

末了他直起身，像吹号似的擤了鼻子，说：
「这个嘛，马戏到底散了场，人群挤着往外涌，仿佛怕大棚塌下来砸着。我夹在人堆里，有人想抢我的白铁桶——反正我当他是——结果是我把比利·P.挤丢了，哪儿也瞅不见他一眼。*这一下*我才落回地上，扑通！五点了，还有四英里上下要走——多半是上坡——照我对老头子的了解——我当自己*了解*——前头等着我的是这辈子顶厉害的一顿打，而这话的分量不轻。可是，孩子归孩子，我那点胆气够我认定：值。撒腿就走。」
「他狠狠打了你？」卡洛姆太太焦急地问。
「这个嘛，」大卫答，「他尽力了。我一进前门他就候着——后门我知趣没试——他揪住我耳朵——差点揪下来——一言不发，把我押到牲口棚棚子里。我从没见他火成那样。像有一阵子说不出话；末了他说：『一天在哪儿来？』
「『下村里去了，』我说。
「『下去干什么了？』他说。
「『看马戏了，』我说，想着索性和盘托出。
「『钱哪儿来的？』他说。
「『卡洛姆先生带我去的，』我说。
「『你撒谎，』他说。『钱是你哪儿偷的；今天不打出来，我把你打死，』他说。」
「这个嘛，」大卫回想得肩膀直拧，「我就不搅你的心了。说了，他尽力了；他还没尽兴我先服了。实情是，他打得过了点，末了还得往我脸上泼水才收场；泼也泼得同打一样彻底。我活像刚从水缸里捞出来的一只鸡。我连滚带爬上了床；好一阵子没法仰着躺，我告诉你。」
「你这可怜的小家伙，」卡洛姆太太满心怜惜地喊。「你这可怜的小家伙！」
「值，大大地值，卡洛姆太太，」大卫加重语气说。「我过了顶快活的一天——可以说，一辈子就快活了那一天——我永远没忘。那顿打，慢慢也就好了；那场马戏，我享用了四十年。

===Original===

The’ wa’n’t but one thing to hender, an’ that’s this, that I hain’t never ben able to remember﻿—an’ to this day I lay awake nights tryin’ to﻿—that I said ‘Thank ye’ to Billy P., an’ I never seen him after that day.”
“How’s that?” asked Mrs. Cullom.
“Wa’al,” was the reply, “that day was the turnin’ point with me. The next night I lit out with what duds I c’d git together, an’ as much grub ’s I could pack in that tin pail; an’ the next time I see the old house on Buxton Hill the’ hadn’t ben no Harums in it fer years.”
Here David rose from his chair, yawned and stretched himself, and stood with his back to the fire. The widow looked up anxiously into his face. “Is that all?” she asked after a while.
“Wa’al, it is an’ it ain’t. I’ve got through yarnin’ about Dave Harum at any rate, an’ mebbe we’d better have a little confab on your matters, seein’ ’t I’ve got you ’way up here such a mornin’ ’s this. I gen’ally do bus’nis fust an’ talkin’ afterward,” he added, “but I kind o’ got to goin’ an’ kept on this time.”
He put his hand into the breast pocket of his coat and took out three papers, which he shuffled in review as if to verify their identity, and then held them in one hand, tapping them softly upon the palm of the other, as if at a loss how to begin. The widow sat with her eyes fastened upon the papers, trembling with nervous apprehension. Presently he broke the silence.
“About this here morgidge o’ your’n,” he said, “I sent ye word that I wanted to close the matter up, an’ seein’ ’t you’re here an’ come fer that purpose, I guess we’d better make a job on’t. The’ ain’t no time like the present, as the sayin’ is.”
“I s’pose it’ll hev to be as you say,” said the widow in a shaking voice.
“Mis’ Cullom,” said David solemnly, “*you* know, an’ I know, that I’ve got the repitation of bein’ a hard, graspin’, schemin’ man. Mebbe I be. Mebbe I’ve ben hard done by all my hull life, an’ have had to be; an’ mebbe, now ’t I’ve got ahead some, it’s got to be second nature, an’ I can’t seem to help it. ‘Bus’nis is bus’nis’ ain’t part of the golden rule, I allow, but the way it gen’ally runs, fur ’s I’ve found out, is, ‘Do unto the other feller the way he’d like to do unto you, an’ do it fust.’

===Chinese===

只有一样碍事：我始终想不起来——到今天我还夜里睁着眼想——那天我对比利·P.说过『谢谢』没有；还有，打那天起，我再没见过他。」
「怎么会？」卡洛姆太太问。
「这个嘛，」回答是，「那一天是我一生的转折点。第二夜我就收拾起凑得拢的破烂，连同白铁桶里塞得下的干粮，跑了；等我再见巴克斯顿山上那所老屋，里头的哈勒姆一家已经走空多年了。」
说到这里，大卫从椅子里起身，打个呵欠，伸个懒腰，背朝炉火站着。寡妇焦灼地仰起脸。「就这些？」过了一阵她问。
「这个嘛，是，也不是。戴夫·哈勒姆的闲篇儿，横竖讲完了；这样的大清早，我把您请到这么老高的地方来，咱们还是谈谈您那桩事的好。我平日的规矩，先办正事，后说话，」他添一句，「这一回不知怎么讲上了头，就没收住。」
他把手伸进上衣胸袋，掏出三张纸，一张张翻检，像验明正身，随后拢在一只手里，拿另一只手掌轻轻拍着，像不知从何开口。寡妇坐着，两眼钉在那几张纸上，紧张得直抖。过了片刻他打破沉默。
「您这笔抵押，」他说，「我捎话说要了结；您既然来了，又是为这个来的，咱们干脆办了吧。常言道，趁热打铁。」
「我想，只有照您说的办了，」寡妇声音发颤地说。
「卡洛姆太太，」大卫郑重地说，「*您*知道，我也知道：我的名声，是个又狠、又贪、又奸的人。兴许我是。兴许我这辈子一直叫人狠着待，不得不狠；兴许如今我稍稍起来了，狠成了第二天性，改也改不掉。『生意是生意』不算金律里的一条，我认账；可照我查明的，这世道的通行跑法是：『对方想怎么对付你，你就先怎么对付他——而且抢先一步。』

===Original===

But, if you want to keep this thing a-runnin’ as it’s goin’ on now fer a spell longer, say one year, or two, or even three, you may, only I’ve got somethin’ to say to ye ’fore ye elect.”
“Wa’al,” said the poor woman, “I expect it ’d only be pilin’ up wrath agin’ the day o’ wrath. I can’t pay the int’rist now without starvin’, an’ I hain’t got no one to bid in the prop’ty fer me if it was to be sold.”
“Mis’ Cullom,” said David, “I said I’d got somethin’ more to tell ye, an’ if, when I git through, you don’t think I’ve treated you right, includin’ this mornin’s confab, I hope you’ll fergive me. It’s this, an’ I’m the only person livin’ that ’s knowin’ to it, an’ in fact I may say that I’m the only person that ever was really knowin’ to it. It was before you was married, an’ I’m sure he never told ye, fer I don’t doubt he fergot all about it, but your husband, Billy P. Cullom, that was, made a small investment once on a time, yes, ma’am, he did, an’ in his kind of careless way it jes’ slipped his mind. The amount of cap’tal he put in wa’n’t large, but the rate of int’rist was uncommon high. Now, he never drawed no dividends on’t, an’ they’ve ben ’cumulatin’ fer forty year, more or less, at compound int’rist.”
The widow started forward, as if to rise from her seat. David put his hand out gently and said, “Jest a minute, Mis’ Cullom, jest a minute, till I git through. Part o’ that cap’tal,” he resumed, “consistin’ of a quarter an’ some odd cents, was invested in the cirkis bus’nis, an’ the rest on’t﻿—the cap’tal, an’ all the cash cap’tal that I started in bus’nis with﻿—was the ten cents your husband give me that day, an’ here,” said David, striking the papers in his left hand with the back of his right, “*here* is the *dividends*! This here second morgidge, not bein’ on record, may jest as well go onto the fire﻿—it’s gettin’ low﻿—an’ here’s a satisfaction piece which I’m goin’ to execute now, that’ll clear the thousan’ dollar one. Come in here, John,” he called out.
The widow stared at David for a moment speechless, but as the significance of his words dawned upon her, the blood flushed darkly in her face. She sprang to her feet and, throwing up her arms, cried out: “My Lord! My Lord! Dave! Dave Harum! Is it true?﻿—tell me it’s true! You ain’t foolin’ me, air ye, Dave?

===Chinese===

不过，这东西您要愿意照现在这样再让它跑一阵——一年，两年，甚至三年——也可以；只是您选定之前，我还有话对您说。」
「嗯，」可怜的女人说，「我料着不过是把忿怒越积越多，积到忿怒的日子。如今这利息，不饿死我付不出；真要拍卖，也没有人替我出价。」
「卡洛姆太太，」大卫说，「我说我还有话，等我讲完，您要觉得我待您不地道——连今儿早晨这场闲谈一并算上——望您包涵。是这样：这件事的底细，如今活着的只有我一个人知道；说实在的，从来真知底细的，也只有我一个。那是您出阁以前的事，我拿得准他从没对您讲过——我不疑心他忘了个精光——您丈夫，已故的比利·P·卡洛姆，早年做过一笔小小的投资。是，太太，真做过；他那人粗心大意，转身就忘了。投的本钱不大；利息却高得离奇。他一文红利也没支取；利滚利，四十年上下，一直滚到今天。」
寡妇猛地朝前一挺，像要离座。大卫轻轻伸手：「稍待，卡洛姆太太，稍待，容我讲完。那笔本钱，」他接下去，「一部分——两毛五分带几个零头——投在马戏那行上；其余的——连同我起家做生意时的全部现钱本钱——就是您丈夫那天给我的那一毛。喏，」大卫右手背敲着左手的纸，「喏，这就是*红利*！这张第二抵押，没上过登记，进火正好——火也矮了；这里是清偿文书，我这就签，签了那一千块的也就清了。进来吧，约翰，」他朝外喊。
寡妇瞪着大卫，一时说不出话；可他那番话的意思一透进她心里，血就浓浓地涌上她的脸。她一跃而起，双臂高举，喊：「我的主！我的主！戴夫！戴夫·哈勒姆！是真的吗？——告诉我真的是！你不是哄我吧，戴夫？

===Original===

You wouldn’t fool a poor old woman that never done ye no harm, nor said a mean word agin ye, would ye? Is it true? an’ is my place clear? an’ I don’t owe nobody anythin’﻿—I mean, no money? Tell it agin. Oh, tell it agin! Oh, Dave! it’s too good to be true! Oh! Oh! Oh, *my*! an’ here I be cryin’ like a great baby, an’, an’ ”﻿—fumbling in her pocket﻿—“I do believe I hain’t got no hank’chif﻿—Oh, thank ye,” to John; “I’ll do it up an’ send it back to-morrer. Oh, what made ye do it, Dave?”
“Set right down an’ take it easy, Mis’ Cullom,” said David soothingly, putting his hands on her shoulders and gently pushing her back into her chair. “Set right down an’ take it easy.﻿—Yes,” to John, “I acknowledge that I signed that.”
He turned to the widow, who sat wiping her eyes with John’s handkerchief.
“Yes, ma’am,” he said. “It’s as true as anythin’ kin be. I wouldn’t no more fool ye, ye know I wouldn’t, don’t ye? than I’d﻿—jerk a hoss,” he asseverated. “Your place is clear now, an’ by this time to-morro’ the’ won’t be the scratch of a pen agin it. I’ll send the satisfaction over fer record fust thing in the mornin’.”
“But, Dave,” protested the widow, “I s’pose ye know what you’re doin’﻿—?”
“Yes,” he interposed, “I cal’late I do, putty near. You ast me why I done it, an’ I’ll tell ye if ye want to know. I’m payin’ off an old score, an’ gettin’ off cheap, too. That’s what I’m doin’! I thought I’d hinted up to it putty plain, seein’ ’t I’ve talked till my jaws ache; but I’ll sum it up to ye if you like.”
He stood with his feet aggressively wide apart, one hand in his trousers pocket, and holding in the other the “morgidge,” which he waved from time to time in emphasis.
“You c’n estimate, I reckon,” he began, “what kind of a bringin’-up I had, an’ what a poor, mis’able, God-fersaken, scairt-to-death little forlorn critter I was; put upon, an’ snubbed, an’ jawed at till I’d come to believe myself﻿—what was rubbed into me the hull time﻿—that I was the most all-’round no-account animul that was ever made out o’ dust, an’ wa’n’t ever likely to be no diff’rent. Lookin’ back, it seems to me that﻿—exceptin’ of Polly﻿—I never had a kind word said to me, nor a day’s fun. Your husband, Billy P. Cullom, was the fust man that ever treated me human up to that time. He give me the only enjoy’ble time ’t I’d ever had, an’ I don’t know ’t anythin’ ’s ever equaled it since.

===Chinese===

你总不肯哄一个从没害过你、也没说过你一句坏话的穷老婆子吧，肯不肯？是真的吗？我的地净了？我谁也不欠了——我是说，不欠钱了？再说一遍。哦，再说一遍！哦，戴夫！这好得不像真的！哦！哦！哦，*天*哪！瞧我哭得像个大娃娃，还，还，」——她往口袋里摸——「我准是一块手帕也没带——哦，谢谢，」对约翰说；「我洗好了明天送还。哦，戴夫，你这是图什么呀？」
「坐下，稳稳坐下，卡洛姆太太，」大卫安抚她说，两手按住她的肩，轻轻把她送回椅子里。「坐下，稳稳坐下。——是，」对约翰说，「这张是我签的，我认。」
他转向还在拿约翰的手帕抹眼睛的寡妇。
「是，太太，」他说，「千真万确，真得不能再真。我绝不哄您——您知道我不会，对不对？——哄您，好比，好比坑一匹马。」他声明。「您的地如今净了；到明天这时候，上头连一笔画的道道都没有了。清偿文书明早头一件事，我送去登记。」
「可是戴夫，」寡妇拦道，「你自己知道你在干什么吧——？」
「知道，」他插进来，「我算知道，差不离。您问我图什么；想知道我就告诉您。我在还一笔旧账，还占了大便宜。我干的就是这个！我当自己已经讲得够明白了——讲到腮帮子发酸；不过您乐意，我再给您归拢归拢。」
他两脚气势汹汹地分开站着，一只手插在裤袋里，另一只手举着那张「抵押」，不时挥一挥加重语气。
「我受的什么教养，我当年是个怎样可怜、可卑、老天不收、吓得半死的小瘪三，您想也想得出：又欺又骂又数落，数落到我自己都信了——他们一天到晚往我身上揉的就是这个——信自己是上帝造出来顶没出息的一头畜生，永远变不出第二种样子。如今回头看，除了波莉，没人对我说过一句好话，也没让我快活过一天。您丈夫，比利·P·卡洛姆，是头一个把我当人待的人。他把惟一快活的一天给了我自己——后来有什么比得上它的，我也不知道。

===Original===

He spent money on me, an’ he give me money to spend﻿—that had never had a cent to call my own﻿—*an’*, Mis’ Cullom, he took me by the hand, an’ he talked to me, an’ he gin me the fust notion ’t I’d ever had that mebbe I wa’n’t only the scum o’ the earth, as I’d ben teached to believe. I told ye that that day was the turnin’ point of my life. Wa’al, it wa’n’t the lickin’ I got, though that had somethin’ to do with it, but I’d never have had the spunk to run away’s I did if it hadn’t ben for the heartenin’ Billy P. gin me, an’ never knowed it, an’ never knowed it,” he repeated mournfully. “I alwus allowed to pay some o’ that debt back to him, but seein’ ’s I can’t do that, Mis’ Cullom, I’m glad an’ thankful to pay it to his widdo’.”
“Mebbe he knows, Dave,” said Mrs. Cullom softly.
“Mebbe he does,” assented David in a low voice.
Neither spoke for a time, and then the widow said: “David, I can’t thank ye ’s I ought ter﻿—I don’t know how﻿—but I’ll pray for ye night an’ mornin’ ’s long ’s I got breath. An’, Dave,” she added humbly, “I want to take back what I said about the Lord’s providin’.”
She sat a moment, lost in her thoughts, and then exclaimed, “Oh, it don’t seem ’s if I c’d wait to write to Charley!”
“I’ve wrote to Charley,” said David, “an’ told him to sell out there an’ come home, an’ to draw on me fer any balance he needed to move him. I’ve got somethin’ in my eye that’ll be easier an’ better payin’ than fightin’ grasshoppers an’ drought in Kansas.”
“Dave Harum!” cried the widow, rising to her feet, “you ought to ’a’ ben a king!”
“Wa’al,” said David with a grin, “I don’t know much about the kingin’ bus’nis, but I guess a cloth cap ’n’ a hoss whip ’s more ’n my line than a crown an’ scepter. An’ now,” he added, “ ’s we’ve got through ’th our bus’nis, s’pose you step over to the house an’ see Polly. She’s expectin’ on ye to dinner. Oh, yes,” replying to the look of deprecation in her face as she viewed her shabby frock, “you an’ Polly c’n prink up some if you want to, but we can’t take ‘No’ fer an answer Chris’mus day, clo’es or no clo’es.”
“I’d really like ter,” said Mrs. Cullom.
“All right then,” said David cheerfully. “The path is swep’ by this time, I guess, an’ I’ll see ye later. Oh, by the way,” he exclaimed, “the’s somethin’ I fergot.

===Chinese===

他在我身上花钱，还给我钱让我花——我一个子儿也没自己拥有过——*还有*，卡洛姆太太，他攢住我的手，同我说话，头一回叫我起了个念头：兴许我不光是人家教我信的那层地皮上的浮土。我同您说，那一天是我一生的转折。这个嘛，转折不在挨的那顿打——打也有一份功劳——可若不是比利·P.那一次不知不受给我的胆，我横不下心逃出来；他到死也不知道，到死也不知道，」他凄凄地重复。「那笔账，我一直打算还他几分；既然还不到他本人手里，卡洛姆太太，能还给他遗下的寡妇，我高兴，我感激。」
「兴许他知道呢，戴夫，」卡洛姆太太柔声说。
「兴许知道，」大卫低声应道。
两人静了一阵；随后寡妇说：「大卫，谢也谢不到份上——我不会谢——可我要为你祷告，早一遭晚一遭，只要我还有一口气。还有，戴夫，」她谦卑地添一句，「我先前说『上主有预备』那些话，我收回。」
她坐着出了一会儿神，随后喊：「哦，给查利写信，我一刻也等不了！」
「我给查利写了，」大卫说，「叫他那边出手，回家来；搬家短的钱，尽管出我的票子。我眼里正有一桩事，又轻省、又来钱，强过在堪萨斯同蝗虫和旱灾打仗。」
「戴夫·哈勒姆！」寡妇起身喊，「你本该当国王！」
「这个嘛，」大卫咧嘴一笑，「国王这门营生我不大懂；不过王冠同权杖怕不合我的路数，布帽子加马鞭更对我的劲。好了，」他添一句，「正事办完，请挪步到我家，看看波莉。她等你吃圣诞饭呢。哦，行，」他应着她低头看旧衣裳时的推辞神色，「你同波莉要乐意，拾掇拾掇也使得；可圣诞节这天，说什么也不能『不』，穿什么算什么。」
「我真想去，」卡洛姆太太说。
「那就定了，」大卫欢欢喜喜地说。「小路这会儿该扫出来了；回头见。哦，对了，」他喊起来，「还有一桩忘了。

===Original===

I want to make you a proposition, ruther an onusual one, but seein’ ev’rythin’ is as ’tis, perhaps you’ll consider it.”
“Dave,” declared the widow, “if I could, an’ you ast for it, I’d give ye anythin’ on the face o’ this mortal globe!”
“Wa’al,” said David, nodding and smiling, “I thought that mebbe, long ’s you got the int’rist of that investment we ben talkin’ about, you’d let me keep what’s left of the princ’pal. Would ye like to see it?”
Mrs. Cullom looked at him with a puzzled expression without replying.
David took from his pocket a large wallet, secured by a strap, and, opening it, extracted something enveloped in much faded brown paper. Unfolding this, he displayed upon his broad fat palm an old silver dime black with age.
“There’s the cap’tal,” he said.

我向你提个议——相当不寻常的议；不过事已如此，兴许你肯斟酌。」
「戴夫，」寡妇宣布，「只要办得到，只要你开口，这凡世地面上任何东西我都给！」
「这个嘛，」大卫点头微笑说，「我是这么想：那笔投资，方才咱们谈的，利息既然归了你，本钱剩下的，就归我留着。想不想看看它？」
卡洛姆太太一脸疑惑地望着他，没有作答。
大卫从口袋里摸出一只带袢的大皮夹，打开，取出一样用褪了色的牛皮纸层层裹着的东西。一层层解开，摊在他宽厚的手掌上：一枚旧银毫，一角的，年头把它浸得乌黑。
「这就是本钱，」他说。

## XXI / 二十一
===Original===

John walked to the front door with Mrs. Cullom, but she declined with such evident sincerity his offer to carry her bundle to the house that he let her out of the office and returned to the back room. David was sitting before the fire, leaning back in his chair with his hands thrust deep in his trousers pockets. He looked up as John entered and said, “Draw up a chair.”
John brought a chair and stood by the side of it while he said, “I want to thank you for the Christmas remembrance, which pleased and touched me very deeply; and,” he added diffidently, “I want to say how mortified I am﻿—in fact, I want to apologize for﻿—”
“Regrettin’?” interrupted David with a motion of his hand toward the chair and a smile of great amusement. “Sho, sho! Se’ down, se’ down. I’m glad you found somethin’ in your stockin’ if it pleased ye, an’ as fur’s that regret o’ your’n was concerned﻿—wa’al﻿—wa’al, I liked ye all the better for’t, I did fer a fact. He, he, he! Appearances was ruther agin me, wasn’t they, the way I told it.”
“Nevertheless,” said John, seating himself, “I ought not to have﻿—that is to say, I ought to have known﻿—”
“How could ye,” David broke in, “When I as good as told ye I was cal’latin’ to rob the old lady? He, he, he, he! Scat my —﻿—﻿—! Your face was a picture when I told ye to write that note, though I reckon you didn’t know I noticed it.”
John laughed and said, “You have been very generous all through, Mr. Harum.”
“Nothin’ to brag on,” he replied, “nothin’ to brag on. Fur ’s Mis’ Cullom’s matter was concerned, ’twas as I said, jes’ payin’ off an old score; an’ as fur ’s your stockin’, it’s really putty much the same. I’ll allow you’ve earned it, if it’ll set any easier on your stomach.”
“I can’t say that I have been overworked,” said John with a slight laugh.
“Mebbe not,” rejoined David, “but you hain’t ben overpaid neither, an’ I want ye to be satisfied. Fact is,” he continued, “my gettin’ you up here was putty consid’able of an experiment, but I ben watchin’ ye putty close, an’ I’m more’n satisfied. Mebbe Timson c’d beat ye at figurin’ an’ countin’ money when you fust come, an’ knowed more about the pertic’ler points of the office, but outside of that he was the biggist dumb-head I ever see, an’ you know how he left things. He hadn’t no tack, fer one thing.

===Chinese===

约翰送卡勒姆太太走到前门；他要替她把小包送到家，她推辞得那样实心实意，他只好送她出门，自己回了里间。大卫坐在炉火前，仰在椅子里，双手深深插在裤袋里。约翰进来，他抬眼说：「拖把椅子。」

约翰搬来椅子，站在椅边说：「我要谢谢您的圣诞心意，它让我欢喜，也让我深深感动；还有，」他训训地补上，「我要说，我实在惭愧——其实，我想为——道歉。」

「后悔了？」大卫打断他，手朝椅子一摆，一脸大大的有趣。「嗐，嗐！坐，坐。袜子里的东西你中意，俺就高兴；至于你那点后悔嘛——这个嘛——这个嘛，俺反倒因此更看中你，真的。嘿，嘿，嘿！照俺那个讲法，局面可着实对俺不利，是不是？」

「话虽如此，」约翰落座，「我不该——或者说，我本该知道——」

「你咋能知道，」大卫截进来，「俺都差明差白告诉你俺要打劫老太太了？嘿、嘿、嘿、嘿！去他的———！俺叫你写那张字条时，你那张脸真是一幅画——俺瞅你自己不知道俺瞧见了。」

约翰笑了：「从头到尾，您都大方得很，哈勒姆先生。」

「没啥可夸的。」他答，「没啥可夸的。卡勒姆太太那桩，正如俺说的，不过是清一笔旧账；你那袜子嘛，其实也差不多是一回事。你要心里摮得平些，俺就认你挣下了它。」

「可说不上操劳过度。」约翰轻笑一声说。

「兴许没操劳过度，」大卫接话，「可你也没多拿一分钱，俺要你心满意足。实情是，」他接着说，「把你弄上来，是桩相当不小的实验；可俺一直盯你盯得紧，俺满意得不能再满意。你刚来时，算数点钱兴许蒂姆森比你强，行里各处的细目他懂的也多；可除此之外，他是俺平生见过最大的木头脑袋——他掠下的那摊子你也见了。头一样：他没一点分寸。

===Original===

Outside of summin’ up figures an’ countin’ money he had a faculty fer gettin’ things t’other-end to that beat all. I’d tell him a thing, an’ explain it to him two three times over, an’ he’d say ‘Yes, yes,’ an’, scat my —﻿—﻿—! when it came to carryin’ on’t out, he hadn’t sensed it a mite﻿—jes’ got it which-end-t’other. An talk! Wa’al, I think it must ’a’ ben a kind of disease with him. He really didn’t mean no harm, mebbe, but he couldn’t no more help lettin’ out anythin’ he knowed, or thought he knowed, than a settin’ hen c’n help settin’. He kep’ me on tenterhooks the hull endurin’ time.”
“I should say he was honest enough, was he not?” said John.
“Oh, yes,” replied David with a touch of scorn, “he was honest enough fur ’s money matters was concerned; but he hadn’t no tack, nor no sense, an’ many a time he done more mischief with his gibble-gabble than if he’d took fifty dollars out an’ out. Fact is,” said David, “the kind of honesty that won’t actually steal ’s a kind of fool honesty that’s common enough; but the kind that keeps a feller’s mouth shut when he hadn’t ought to talk ’s about the scurcest thing goin’. I’ll jes’ tell ye, fer example, the last mess he made. You know Purse, that keeps the gen’ral store? Wa’al, he come to me some months ago, on the quiet, an’ said that he wanted to borro’ five hunderd. He didn’t want to git no endorser, but he’d show me his books an’ give me a statement an’ a chattel morgidge fer six months. He didn’t want nobody to know ’t he was anyway pushed fer money because he wanted to git some extensions, an’ so on. I made up my mind it was all right, an’ I done it. Wa’al, about a month or so after he come to me with tears in his eyes, as ye might say, an’ says, ‘I got somethin’ I want to show ye,’ an’ handed out a letter from the house in New York he had some of his biggist dealin’s with, tellin’ him that they regretted”﻿—here David gave John a nudge﻿—“that they couldn’t give him the extensions he ast for, an’ that his paper must be paid as it fell due﻿—some twelve hunderd dollars. ‘Somebody ’s leaked,’ he says, ‘an’ they’ve heard of that morgidge, an’ I’m in a putty scrape,’ he says.
“ ‘H’m’m,’ I says, ‘what makes ye think so?’

===Chinese===

「除了合计数目、点数钱票，他还天生一门本事：把事情拿反，反得天下无双。俺交代他一件事，掰开揉碎讲他两三遍，他『是了、是了』；等真做出来——去他的———！他一点没往心里去——正反拿了个满拧。还有那张嘴！这个嘛，俺看在他是一种病。他兴许真没坏心，可他憋不住心里那点货——或自以为的货——就像抱窝的母鸡憋不住抱窝。他成天把俺吊在锥子尖上。」

「要我说，他还算诚实，对吧？」约翰说。

「哦，是，」大卫答，带一丝不屑，「钱上的事他诚实够用；可他没分寸、没脑子，他那嘴碎碎出来的祸，多少回比明抢五十块还大。实情是，」大卫说，「那种不至于偷的诚实，是傻诚实，满地都是；可那种在该闭嘴时闭得住的嘴——是天底下顶稀罕的货。俺就给你举个例子，他捅的最后一个娄子。你认得珀斯，开杂货铺的那位？这个嘛，几个月前他悄悄来找俺，说要借五百。他不想找保人，可愿意给俺看账本、开清单，再押一张六个月的动产抵押。他不想叫任何人知道他手头紧——他还想求几家宽限些日子，如此等等。俺搋量着没问题，办了。这个嘛，个把月后，他几乎是含着泪来找俺，说：『俺有个东西给你看。』掏出一封信，纽约一家他有大宗来往的商号来的，说是很遗憾」——大卫在这儿捅了约翰一下——「他申请的宽限给不了，票据到期必须照付——一千二百块上下。『有人走漏了风声，』他说，『他们听着那笔抵押了，俺这一下麻烦大了。』他说。

「『嗯哼，』俺说，『你咋这么想？』
