“ ‘Can’t be nothin’ else,’ he says; ‘I’ve dealt with them people fer years an’ never ast fer nothin’ but what I got it, an’ now to have ’em round up on me like this, it can’t be nothin’ but what they’ve got wind o’ that chattel morgidge,’ he says.
“ ‘H’m’m,’ I says. ‘Any o’ their people ben up here lately?’ I says.
“ ‘That’s jest it,’ he says. ‘One o’ their travellin’ men was up here last week, an’ he come in in the afternoon as chipper as you please, wantin’ to sell me a bill o’ goods, an’ I put him off, sayin’ that I had a putty big stock, an’ so on, an’ he said he’d see me agin in the mornin’﻿—you know that sort of talk,’ he says.
“ ‘Wa’al,’ I says, ‘did he come in?’
“ ‘No,’ says Purse, ‘he didn’t. I never set eyes on him agin, an’ more’n that,’ he says, ‘he took the first train in the mornin’, an’ now,’ he says, ‘I expect I’ll have ev’ry last man I owe anythin’ to buzzin’ ’round my ears.’
“ ‘Wa’al,’ I says, ‘I guess I see about how the land lays, an’ I reckon you ain’t fur out about the morgidge bein’ at the bottom on’t, an’ the’ ain’t no way it c’d ’a’ leaked out ’ceptin’ through that dum’d chuckle-head of a Timson. But this is the way it looks to me﻿—you hain’t heard nothin’ in the village, have ye?’ I says.
“ ‘No,’ he says. ‘Not *yit*,’ he says.
“ ‘Wa’al, ye won’t, I don’t believe,’ I says, ‘an’ as fur as that drummer is concerned, you c’n bet,’ I says, ‘that he didn’t nor won’t let on to nobody but his own folks﻿—not till *his* bus’nis is squared up, an’ more ’n that,’ I says, ‘seein’ that your trouble ’s ben made ye by one o’ my help, I don’t see but what I’ll have to see ye through,’ I says. ‘You jes’ give me the address of the New York parties, an’ tell me what you want done, an’ I reckon I c’n fix the thing so ’t they won’t bother ye. I don’t believe,’ I says, ‘that anybody else knows anythin’ yet, an’ I’ll shut up Timson’s yawp so ’s it’ll stay shut.’ ”

“Oh,” replied David, “Purse went off head up an’ tail up. He said he was everlastin’ly obliged to me, an’﻿—he, he, he!﻿—he said ’twas more ’n he expected.

「『嗯哼，』俺说。『他们的人最近有上来过的没有？』俺说。

「『正说着这个，』他说。『他们一个跑外销的上礼拜来过，下晌进俺店里，那个精神抖撇，要塞给俺一单货；俺推了，说存货不小、等等，他就说明儿早上再来——你知道那路话。』他说。

「『这个嘛，』俺说，『后来来了没有？』

「『没来，』珀斯说，『压根没来。不光没来，』他说，『他赶了第二天一早的头班车走了；这下好了，』他说，『俺欠着钱的，怕要挨个儿围上俺嗡嗡了。』

「『这个嘛，』俺说，『地形俺瞅明白了；你估摸根子在抵押上，俺瞅估得不离谱，而这风声，除了打蒂姆森那个该死的呆鹅嘴里，没第二处漏得出去。可俺是这么看的——村里的闲话，你一句没听着吧？』俺说。

「『没。』他说。『*还没*。』他说。

===Original===

You see I charged him what I thought was right on the ’rig’nal deal, an’ he squimmidged some, an’ I reckon he allowed to be putty well bled if I took holt agin; but I done as I agreed on the extension bus’nis, an’ I’m on his paper for twelve hunderd fer nothin’, jest because that nikum-noddy of a Timson let that drummer bamboozle him into talkin’. I found out the hull thing, an’ the very day I wrote to the New York fellers fer Purse, I wrote to Gen’ral Wolsey to find me somebody to take Timson’s place. I allowed I’d ruther have somebody that didn’t know nobody, than such a clackin’ ole he-hen as Chet.”
“I should have said that it was rather a hazardous thing to do,” said John, “to put a total stranger like me into what is rather a confidential position, as well as a responsible one.”
“Wa’al,” said David, “in the fust place I knew that the Gen’ral wouldn’t recommend no deadbeat nor no skin, an’ I allowed that if the raw material was OK, I could break it in; an’ if it wa’n’t I should find it out putty quick. Like a young hoss,” he remarked, “if he’s sound an’ kind, an’ got gumption, I’d sooner break him in myself ’n not﻿—fur’s my use goes﻿—an’ if I can’t, nobody can, an’ I get rid on him. You understand?”
“Yes,” said John with a smile.
“Wa’al,” continued David, “I liked your letter, an’ when you come I liked your looks. Of course I couldn’t tell jest how you’d take holt, nor if you an’ me ’d hitch. An’ then agin, I didn’t know whether you could stan’ it here after livin’ in a city all your life. I watched ye putty close﻿—closter ’n you knowed of, I guess. I seen right off that you was goin’ to fill your collar, fur’s the work was concerned, an’ though you didn’t know nobody much, an’ couldn’t have no amusement to speak on, you didn’t mope nor sulk, an’ what’s more﻿—though I know I advised ye to stay there fer a spell longer when you spoke about boardin’ somewhere else﻿—I know what the Eagle tavern is in winter; summer, too, fer that matter, though it’s a little better then, an’ I allowed that air test ’d be final. He, he, he! Putty rough, ain’t it?”
“It is, rather,” said John, laughing. “I’m afraid my endurance is pretty well at an end. Elright’s wife is ill, and the fact is, that since day before yesterday I have been living on what I could buy at the grocery﻿—crackers, cheese, salt fish, canned goods, et cetera.”
“Scat my —﻿—﻿—!” cried David. “Wa’al! Wa’al!

===Chinese===

「你是知道的，头一笔买卖俺收的费是俺认为公道的数，他还扭捏了一阵；俺估摸他打好了谱：俺再插手，非被他放血放到贫血不可。可宽限那一桩，俺怎么应的怎么办的；他那十二百的票据上，俺白白担着名——就因为蒂姆森那个憨货叫那个跑外销的哄开了牙。整桩事俺查了个底朝天；给珀斯往纽约写信的当天，俺就给沃尔西将军写了信，请他寻个人来顶蒂姆森。俺情愿要一个谁也不认得的，也不要切特这么一只呱嗒的老母鸡——公的。」

「要我说，这可是桩相当冒险的事。」约翰说，「把我这么个全然的外人，放进一个既机要、又担干系的位置。」

「这个嘛，」大卫说，「头一条，俺知道将军荐来的，绝不会是混子也不会是滑头；俺认定：坯子只要正，调教的事俺来；不正，俺也很快就试得出来。就像一匹年轻马，」他说，「只要皮实、性子好、有灵性，合不合用先不论，俺情愿自己亲手调——俺调不出来的，谁也调不出来，那俺就打发了它。明白？」

「明白。」约翰含笑说。

「这个嘛，」大卫接着说，「你的信俺中意；你人一来，模样俺也中意。当然，你会上手到什么份上、你同俺对不对榫，俺说不准。再说，你在城里长到这么大，这地方你熬不熬得住，俺也不知道。俺把你盯得紧——比你晓得的还紧，俺瞅。俺一眼看出：论干活，你这副肩膀撑得起来；你虽然谁也不熟、也没什么消遣可言，可你不蔫也不怨；还有——俺自知你提换地方住的时候，俺劝你再在客栈待一阵——雄鹰客栈的冬天什么样，俺知道；夏天也一样，不过好上一点——俺认定那阵风就是终考。嘿、嘿、嘿！够哈吧？」

「何止够哈。」约翰笑道。「我怕是熬到头了。埃尔赖特的太太病了；实情是，打前天起，我就靠杂货铺买的过活——饼干、干酪、咸鱼、罐头，等等。」

「去他的———！」大卫喊。「嚯！嚯！

===Original===

That’s too dum’d bad! Why on earth﻿—why, you must be *hungry*! Wa’al, you won’t have to eat no salt herrin’ today, because Polly ’n I are expectin’ ye to dinner.”
Two or three times during the conversation David had gone to the window overlooking his lawn and looked out with a general air of observing the weather, and at this point he did so again, coming back to his seat with a look of satisfaction, for which there was, to John, no obvious reason. He sat for a moment without speaking, and then, looking at his watch, said: “Wa’al, dinner ’s at one o’clock, an’ Polly’s a great one fer bein’ on time. Guess I’ll go out an’ have another look at that pesky colt. You better go over to the house ’bout quarter to one, an’ you c’n make your t’ilet over there. I’m ’fraid if you go over to the Eagle it’ll spoil your appetite. She’d think it might, anyway.”
So David departed to see the colt, and John got out some of the books and busied himself with them until the time to present himself at David’s house.

===Chinese===

「这就太不像话了！怎么搞的——你这不是*饿着*么！这个嘛，今儿的咸鲱鱼你免了——波莉同俺正等你吃正餐呢。」

这番谈话里，大卫已有两三回踱到俯瞰草坪的窗前，摆出一副观天象的架势张望；此刻他又去了一趟，回到座位时一脸满足——满足的缘由，约翰看不出来。他默坐了片刻，看看表，说：「这个嘛，正餐一点开，波莉最讲究准时。俺出去再瞅瞅那匹闹腾的驹子。你差一刻一点上家那边去，梳洗也在那边。俺怕你再回雄鹰跑一趟，胃口就毁了——反正她会这么想。」

于是大卫看驹子去了；约翰取出几本账，埋头对付，直到该去大卫家报到的时辰。

## XXII / 二十二
===Original===

“Why, Mis’ Cullom, I’m real glad to see ye. Come right in,” said Mrs. Bixbee as she drew the widow into the “wing settin’ room,” and proceeded to relieve her of her wraps and her bundle. “Set right here by the fire while I take these things of your’n into the kitchen to dry ’em out. I’ll be right back”; and she bustled out of the room. When she came back Mrs. Cullom was sitting with her hands in her lap, and there was in her eyes an expression of smiling peace that was good to see.
Mrs. Bixbee drew up a chair, and seating herself, said: “Wa’al, I don’t know when I’ve seen ye to git a chance to speak to ye, an’ I was real pleased when David said you was goin’ to be here to dinner. An’ my! how well, you’re lookin’﻿—more like Cynthy Sweetland than I’ve seen ye fer I don’t know when; an’ yet,” she added, looking curiously at her guest, “you ’pear somehow as if you’d ben cryin’.”
“You’re real kind, I’m sure,” responded Mrs. Cullom, replying to the other’s welcome and remarks seriatim; “I guess, though, I don’t look much like Cynthy Sweetland, if I do feel twenty years younger ’n I did a while ago; an’ I have ben cryin’, I allow, but not fer sorro’, Polly Harum,” she exclaimed, giving the other her maiden name. “Your brother Dave comes putty nigh to bein’ an angel!”
“Wa’al,” replied Mrs. Bixbee with a twinkle, “I reckon Dave might hev to be fixed up some afore he come out in that pertic’ler shape, but,” she added impressively, “es fur as bein’ a *man* goes, he’s ’bout ’s good ’s they make ’em. I know folks thinks he’s a hard bargainer, an’ closefisted, an’ some on ’em that ain’t fit to lick up his tracks says more’n that. He’s got his own ways, I’ll allow, but down at bottom, an’ all through, I know the’ ain’t no better man livin’. No, ma’am, the’ ain’t, an’ what he’s ben to me, Cynthy Cullom, nobody knows but me﻿—an’﻿—an’﻿—mebbe the Lord﻿—though I hev seen the time,” she said tentatively, “when it seemed to me ’t I knowed more about my affairs ’n He did,” and she looked doubtfully at her companion, who had been following her with affirmative and sympathetic nods, and now drew her chair a little closer, and said softly: “Yes, yes, I know. I ben putty doubtful an’ rebellious myself a good many times, but seems now as if He had had me in His mercy all the time.” Here Aunt Polly’s sense of humor asserted itself. “What’s Dave ben up to now?” she asked.

===Chinese===

「哎呀，卡勒姆太太，见到你俺真高兴。快里边请。」比克斯比太太一边说，一边把寡妇往「厢房客厅」里让，动手替她解下披肩、接过包袱。「你就坐这儿，挨着炉火，俺把这些东西拿厨房去烘烘干，马上回来。」说着便风风火火出了屋。待她回来，卡勒姆太太已经坐定，两手叠在膝头，眼里含着一汪笑意融融的安详，瞧着就叫人心里舒坦。
比克斯比太太拖过一把椅子坐下，开腔道：「这个嘛，都记不清上回逮着机会跟你说话是哪年月了，大卫说你要来这儿吃饭，俺心里真高兴。哎呀，你气色多好——活脱脱又是当年的辛西·斯威特兰，多少年没见你这模样了；不过嘛，」她打量着客人，好奇地添了一句，「你瞧着倒像是哭过。」
「多谢你的好话，」卡勒姆太太把对方的欢迎和夸赞一条条挨个儿应承下来，「不过我看，我比不得辛西·斯威特兰了——虽说自觉比前些日子年轻了二十岁；哭是哭过，我认，可不是为伤心事，波莉·哈勒姆，」她唤出对方的闺名。「你弟弟戴夫呀，差一丁点儿就够得上一个天使了！」
「这个嘛，」比克斯比太太眼里一闪，「俺寻思戴夫要真摆出那副模样，怕是得先拾掇拾掇才成；不过，」她郑重其事地补上一句，「单论当个*男人*，他算是这世上顶好的了。俺知道外头有人说他买卖上死抠、手头紧，有些人连舔他脚印子的资格都没有，嘴还更损。他是有他自个儿的一套，俺认账，可打骨子里、从头到尾，俺就知道活着的人里头，再没有比他更好的了。没有，太太，真没有。他待俺的好处，辛西·卡勒姆，天底下只有俺一个人知道——兴许——兴许还有主知道——不过俺也有过那么些时候，」她试探着说，「觉着俺自个儿的家事，俺比他知道得还清楚。」她拿不准地望了望同伴；对方一路点头附和，满脸同情，这时把椅子挪近了些，轻声说：「是，是，我明白。我自己也不知疑惑过、顶撞过多少回，可如今回头看，主的慈怜原来一直罩着我。」说到这儿，波莉姑妈的幽默劲儿又冒了上来。「戴夫这回又干什么好事啦？」她问。

===Original===

And then the widow told her story, with tears and smiles, and the keen enjoyment which we all have in talking about ourselves to a sympathetic listener like Aunt Polly, whose interjections pointed and illuminated the narrative. When it was finished she leaned forward and kissed Mrs. Cullom on the cheek.
“I can’t tell ye how glad I be for ye,” she said; “but if I’d known that David held that morgidge, I could hev told ye ye needn’t hev worried yourself a mite. He wouldn’t never have taken your prop’ty, more’n he’d rob a hen-roost. But he done the thing his own way﻿—kind o’ fetched it round fer a Merry Chris’mus, didn’t he? Curious,” she said reflectively, after a momentary pause, “how he lays up things about his childhood,” and then, with a searching look at the Widow Cullom, “you didn’t let on, an’ I didn’t ask ye, but of course you’ve heard the things that some folks says of him, an’ natchally they got some holt on your mind. There’s that story about ’Lish, over to Whitcom﻿—you heard somethin’ about that, didn’t ye?”
“Yes,” admitted the widow, “I heard somethin’ of it, I s’pose.”
“Wa’al,” said Mrs. Bixbee, “you never heard the hull story, ner anybody else really, but I’m goin’ to tell it to ye﻿—”
“Yes,” said Mrs. Cullom assentingly.
Mrs. Bixbee sat up straight in her chair with her hands on her knees and an air of one who would see justice done.
“ ’Lish Harum,” she began, “wa’n’t only half-brother to Dave. He was hull-brother to me, though, but notwithstandin’ that, I will say that a meaner boy, a meaner growin’ man, an’ a meaner man never walked the earth. He wa’n’t satisfied to git the best piece an’ the biggist piece﻿—he hated to hev anyone else git anythin’ at all. I don’t believe he ever laughed in his life, except over some kind o’ suff’rin’﻿—man or beast﻿—an’ what’d tickle him the most was to be the means on’t. He took pertic’ler delight in abusin’ an’ tormentin’ Dave, an’ the poor little critter was jest as ’fraid as death of him, an’ good reason. Father was awful hard, but he didn’t go out of his way; but ’Lish never let no chance slip. Wa’al, I ain’t goin’ to give you the hull fam’ly hist’ry, an’ I’ve got to go into the kitchen fer a while ’fore dinner, but what I started out fer ’s this: ’Lish fin’ly settled over to Whitcom.”
“Did he ever git married?” interrupted Mrs. Cullom.
“Oh, yes,” replied Mrs.

于是寡妇讲起了自己的事，一时落泪，一时带笑，带着咱们人人皆有的那股痛快劲儿——对着波莉姑妈这样肯掏心窝子的听客讲自个儿的事；何况她一声声插话，句句点在节骨眼上，把故事照得透亮。讲完了，她探身过去，在卡勒姆太太脸颊上亲了一下。
「俺说不出俺有多替你高兴，」她说，「可俺要是早知道那张抵押捏在戴维手里，早就告诉你了：你犯不着愁一丝一毫。他断不会夺你的房产，就像他不会去掏鸡窝。只不过他办事有他自个儿的路数——绕了个弯儿，办成一份圣诞礼，是不是？」她顿了顿，若有所思地说：「也怪，小时候的事，他攒得那么牢。」接着她拿锐利的眼光盯住卡勒姆寡妇：「你没露口风，俺也没问，可外头那些闲话你自然是听过的，难怪在你心里扎了些根。惠特科姆那边关于伊莱什的那桩事——你也听说过一点的，是吧？」
「是，」寡妇承认，「多少听说过一些，我想。」
「这个嘛，」比克斯比太太说，「从头到尾的整个儿，你没听过，旁人其实也没真听过，俺今儿个就讲给你听——」
「好。」卡勒姆太太连声应着。
比克斯比太太在椅子上坐得笔直，两手按着膝盖，一副非把公道讨回来不可的架势。
「伊莱什·哈勒姆，」她开了腔，「只是戴夫的半拉子兄弟；跟俺倒是同爹同娘的亲兄弟。可话虽这么说，俺还是要讲：天底下再没走过比他更坏的小子、更坏的半大小子、更坏的成年汉子。他自己占着最好的那份、最大的那份还不满足——他见不得旁人得着一星半点儿。俺看他这辈子就没痛痛快快笑过，除非是笑话什么遭罪的东西——人也罢，牲口也罢——顶顶叫他受用的，还是这罪由他亲手造出来。他拿作践戴夫、折磨戴夫当特别的乐子，可怜的小东西怕他怕得要命，也不是没有缘由。爹是狠，可他不专门寻岔儿；伊莱什却回回不肯放过。这个嘛，俺不打算把全家的老底都翻给你听，饭前俺还得去厨房张罗一阵，俺要说的正事儿是这个：伊莱什后来上惠特科姆落了户。」
「他成过家吗？」卡勒姆太太插了一句。
「哦，成过，」比克斯比

Bixbee, “he got married when he was past forty. It’s curious,” she remarked, in passing, “but it don’t seem as if the’ was ever yit a man so mean but he c’d find some woman was fool enough to marry him, an’ she was a putty decent sort of a woman too, f’m all accounts, an’ good lookin’. Wa’al, she stood him six or seven year, an’ then she run off.”
“With another man?” queried the widow in an awed voice. Aunt Polly nodded assent with compressed lips.
“Yes’m,” she went on, “she left him an’ went out West somewhere, an’ that was the last of *her*; an’ when her two boys got old enough to look after themselves a little, they quit him too, an’ they wa’n’t no way growed up neither. Wa’al, the long an’ the short on’t was that ’Lish got goin’ down hill ev’ry way, health an’ all, till he hadn’t nothin’ left but his disposition, an’ fairly got onter the town. The’ wa’n’t nothin’ for it but to send him to the county house, onless somebody ’d s’port him. Wa’al, the committee knew Dave was his brother, an’ one on ’em come to see him to see if he’d come forwud an’ help out, an’ he seen Dave right here in this room, an’ Dave made me stay an’ hear the hull thing. Man’s name was Smith, I remember, a peaked little man with long chin whiskers that he kep’ clawin’ at with his fingers. Dave let him tell his story, an’ he didn’t say nothin’ fer a minute or two, an’ then he says, ‘What made ye come to me?’ he says. ‘Did he send ye?’

「是呐，」她接着讲，「她扔下他，上西部什么地方去了，打那以后就没了*她*的音讯；后来两个儿子稍稍能自个儿照应自个儿了，也都撇了他走人——那会儿他们离长大成人还远着呢。这个嘛，长话短说，伊莱什是里里外外一路败下去，连身子骨一起败，败到末了就剩下一副坏脾气，实打实成了镇上的包袱。没了法子，只能送他进县济贫院，除非有人肯出钱养他。这个嘛，委员会的人知道戴夫是他兄弟，便有一位上门来，探探他肯不肯出面帮衬一把。他见戴夫，就在俺们今儿个待的这间屋里，戴夫还叫俺留下来，从头听到尾。那人姓史密斯，俺记得，一个干瘦的小个子，下巴蓄着长胡子，手指头没完没了地去挠。戴夫由着他把来意讲完，自个儿有一两分钟没吭声，然后才说：『你怎么单找到俺头上来了？』他说，『是他打发你来的？』

「『他这么说的，是吗？』戴夫说。
「『大意如此。』史密斯说。

Now, honest,’ he says, ‘*is* the’ man, woman, or child in Whitcom that knows ’Lish Harum that’s got a good word fer him? or ever knowed of his doin’ or sayin’ anythin’ that hadn’t got a mean side to it some way? “My!” exclaimed Mrs. Cullom. “What did Mr. Smith say to that?”
“Wa’al,” replied Mrs. Bixbee, “he didn’t say nuthin’ at fust, not in so many words. He sot fer a minute clawin’ away at his whiskers﻿—an’ he’d got both hands into ’em by that time﻿—an’ then he made a move as if he gin the hull thing up an’ was goin’. Dave set lookin’ at him, an’ then he says, ‘You ain’t goin’, air ye?’
“ ‘Wa’al,’ says Smith, ‘feelin’ ’s you do, I guess my arrant here ain’t goin’ t’ amount to nothin’, an’ I may ’s well.’

『说句实在的，』他说，『惠特科姆认得伊莱什·哈勒姆的男人、女人、娃娃里头——*到底*有没有一个，肯替他说句好话？他做过的、说过的，有哪一件、哪一句不带着一股子刻薄的邪气？他老婆是不是彻头彻尾被他逼走的？他两个儿子不是一能走远道儿就赶紧撇了他？*再说了*，』戴夫说，『要是有人请你凭你所能想出来的，描一副顶顶下作的无赖坯子，你描出来的——凭良心说！』戴夫说，『凭良心——是不是跟伊莱什·哈勒姆一个模子，就像两颗霰弹丸，谁也不比谁差半分？』」
「天哪！」卡勒姆太太叫出声来，「史密斯先生听了这个怎么说？」
「这个嘛，」比克斯比太太答道，「他起先一声不吭，一句囫囵话也没憋出来。他坐了那么一分钟，光顾着薅胡子——到这会儿两只手都埋进胡子里去了——然后他动了动身子，像是把这趟差事整个撂下，要走。戴夫坐着瞅他，随后说：『你不是要走吧，嗯？』
「『这个嘛，』史密斯说，『你既然是这么个心思，俺这趟来怕是白搭，俺不如——』
「『不忙，你先坐一会儿。』戴夫说，『你要是肯老老实实、直直白白回俺的话，俺还有事跟你说。来吧，』他说。
「『这个嘛，』史密斯说，脸上挂出一副认栽的笑，『你算是把他看透了。俺来见你，原不是替伊莱什·哈勒姆本人奔走，俺是替惠特科姆全镇来的。』接着他抖起几分精神，说：『伊莱什落得什么下场，』他说，『俺才不管呢；就他那个人说，俺也不知道谁肯管；可他如今成了镇上养的人，你若不把他从俺们手里接过去，总得有个安置。』
「戴夫转过脸来对俺说，那口气认真得很：『波莉，让他上这儿来，你乐意不？』
「『戴夫·哈勒姆！』俺说，『你琢磨的什么呀！也不看看他是什么东西、一向是什么东西，他待你又是什么样！老天爷！』俺说，『你不会真这么想吧！』
「『不乐意。』他说，脸上挂着一种恶狠狠的笑，俺从没在他脸上见过那样的笑，『不乐意！这个屋顶底下不成，俺名下随便哪个屋顶底下都不成——哪怕俺拢共只有一间牛棚——还有，』他转向史密斯说，『俺要跟你说的是这么个事儿：你这趟办得没毛病，俺挑不出你的错儿。

But I want you to go back an’ say to ’Lish Harum that you’ve seen me, an’ that I told you that not one cent of my money nor one mossel o’ my food would ever go to keep him alive one minute of time; that if I had an empty hogpen I wouldn’t let him sleep in’t overnight, much less to bunk in with a decent hog. You tell him that I said the poorhouse was his proper dwellin’, barrin’ the jail, an’ that it ’d have to be a dum’d sight poorer house ’n I ever heard of not to be a thousan’ times too good fer him.’ ”
“My!” exclaimed Mrs. Cullom again. “I can’t really ’magine it of Dave.”
“Wa’al,” replied Mrs. Bixbee, “I told ye how set he is on his young days, an’ nobody knows how cruel mean ’Lish used to be to him; but I never see it come out of him so ugly before, though I didn’t blame him a mite. But I hain’t told ye the upshot: ‘Now,’ he says to Smith, who set with his mouth gappin’ open, ‘you understand how I feel about the feller, an’ I’ve got good reason for it. I want you to promise me that you’ll say to him, word fer word, jes’ what I’ve said to you about him, an’ I’ll do this: You folks send him to the poorhouse, an’ let him git jes’ what the rest on ’em gits﻿—no more an’ no less﻿—as long ’s he lives. When he dies you git him the tightest coffin you kin buy, to keep him f’m spilin’ the earth as long as may be, an’ then you send me the hull bill. But this has got to be between you an’ me only. You c’n tell the rest of the committee what you like, *but* if you ever tell a livin’ soul about this here understandin’, an’ I find it out, I’ll never pay one cent, an’ you’ll be to blame. I’m willin’, on them terms, to stan’ between the town of Whitcom an’ harm; but fer ’Lish Harum, not one sumarkee! Is it a barg’in?’ Dave says.
“ ‘Yes, sir,’ says Smith, puttin’ out his hand. ‘An’ I guess,’ he says, ‘f’m all ’t I c’n gather, thet you’re doin’ all ’t we could expect, an’ more too,’ an’ off he put.”
“How ’d it come out?” asked Mrs. Cullom.
“ ’Lish lived about two year,” replied Aunt Polly, “an’ Dave done as he agreed, but even then when he come to settle up, he told Smith he didn’t want no more said about it ’n could be helped.”
“Wa’al,” said Mrs. Cullom, “it seems to me as if David did take care on him after all, fur ’s spendin’ money was concerned.”
“That’s the way it looks to me,” said Mrs. Bixbee, “but David likes to think t’other.

可俺要你回去告诉伊莱什·哈勒姆，说你见着俺了，说俺亲口讲了：俺的钱一分一厘、俺的粮食一口一粒，都甭想拿去多续他一分钟的命；俺就算有一口空猪圈，也不留他过一夜，更别提叫他去跟一头体面的猪同圈。你告诉他，俺说了，贫民院才是他正经的归宿——除了大牢不算；就是那贫民院，也得比俺听说过的再寒酸一千倍，才不嫌便宜了他。』」
「天哪！」卡勒姆太太又叫起来，「戴夫能说出这种话，我真没法儿想象。」
「这个嘛，」比克斯比太太答道，「俺不是说了嘛，他把他小时候的事看得多重，伊莱什当年又把他整治得多狠，也没人知道；可这股火气从他心里冒出来，冒得这么凶，俺从前还没见过——虽说俺一点儿也不怪他。不过结局俺还没告诉你呢：『听好，』他对史密斯说——史密斯坐在那儿，嘴巴张得老大——『这个人是什么东西，你如今明白俺是副什么心思了，俺自有充分的缘由。你要应下俺一件事：把俺这番话一字不差、原原本本说给他听，俺呢，就做这么几件事：你们把他送进贫民院，他活一天，就让他跟里头旁人得一样的一份——不多一分，不少一毫。他死了，你们给他买一口市面上顶严实的棺材，好叫他尽可能久点儿别糟践地皮，然后把账单整个儿寄给俺。可这事儿只许你我两个人知道。委员会那边，你爱怎么说怎么说，*可是*这层约定你只要漏给一个活人，叫俺知道了，俺一个子儿也不认，到时可怨不得俺。照这些条件，俺愿意替惠特科姆镇挡一挡祸害；可要论伊莱什·哈勒姆——一个铜板也没有！这买卖成不成？』戴夫说。
「『成，先生。』史密斯说着伸出手来。『俺瞧着，』他说，『就俺打听得来的这些，您做的已经超出俺们指望的了。』说完他就动身走了。」
「后来怎么收场的呢？」卡勒姆太太问。
「伊莱什又活了两年上下，」波莉姑妈答道，「戴夫也照约定的办了；可到末了结账的时候，他还嘱咐史密斯，这事儿越少提起越好。」
「这个嘛，」卡勒姆太太说，「照我看，到头来在花钱上头，戴维到底是照管了他。」
「俺瞧着也是这么回事，」比克斯比太太说，「可戴夫自个儿偏乐意那么想。

He meant to be awful mean, an’ he was﻿—as mean as he could﻿—but the fact is, he didn’t reelly know how. My sakes! Cynthy (looking at the clock), I’ll hev to excuse myself fer a spell. 他存心要当个顶刻薄的人，也真当上了——要多刻薄有多刻薄——可实情是，他压根儿不知道怎么个坏法。哎呀俺的天！（望了望钟）辛西，俺得告个便，失陪一会儿。你要是想赶在饭前梳拾梳拾，就进俺卧房去，床上摆了几样东西，兴许你用得上。」说着她急匆匆出了屋。

## XXIII / 二十三
===Original===

David’s house stood about a hundred feet back from the street, facing the east. The main body of the house was of two stories (through which ran a deep bay in front), with mansard roof. On the south of the main body of the house were two stories of the “wing,” in which were the “settin’ room,” Aunt Polly’s room, and, above, David’s quarters. Ten minutes or so before one o’clock John rang the bell at the front door.
“Sairy’s busy,” said Mrs. Bixbee apologetically as she let him in, “an’ so I come to the door myself.”
“Thank you very much,” said John. “Mr. Harum told me to come over a little before one, but perhaps I ought to have waited a few minutes longer.”
“No, it’s all right,” she replied, “for mebbe you’d like to wash an’ fix up ’fore dinner, so I’ll jes’ show ye where to,” and she led the way upstairs and into the “front parlor bedroom.”
“There,” she said, “make yourself comf’table, an’ dinner’ll be ready in about ten minutes.”
For a moment John mentally rubbed his eyes. Then he turned and caught both of Mrs. Bixbee’s hands and looked at her, speechless. When he found words he said: “I don’t know what to say, nor how to thank you properly. I don’t believe you know how kind this is.”
“Don’t say nothin’ about it,” she protested, but with a look of great satisfaction. “I done it jest t’ relieve my mind, because ever sence you fust come, I ben worryin’ over your bein’ at that nasty tavern,” and she made a motion to go.
“You and your brother,” said John earnestly, still holding her hands, “have made me a gladder and happier man this Christmas day than I have been for a very long time.”
“I’m glad on’t,” she said heartily, “an’ I hope you’ll be comf’table an’ contented here. I must go now an’ help Sairy dish up. Come down to the settin’ room when you’re ready,” and she gave his hands a little squeeze.
“Aunt Po﻿—, I beg pardon, Mrs. Bixbee,” said John, moved by a sudden impulse, “do you think you could find it in your heart to complete my happiness by giving me a kiss? It’s Christmas, you know,” he added smilingly.
Aunt Polly colored to the roots of her hair. “Wa’al,” she said, with a little laugh, “seein’ ’t I’m old enough to be your mother, I guess ’t won’t hurt me none,” and as she went down the stairs she softly rubbed her lips with the side of her forefinger.
John understood now why David had looked out of the bank window so often that morning.

===Chinese===

大卫的房子离街面往里退了百来英尺，正面朝东。主楼两层，正面开着一溜深深的凸窗，顶上是孟莎式屋顶。主楼南边接着两层的「厢房」，里头是「起居室」和波莉姑妈的屋子，楼上则是大卫的住处。差十分一点，约翰在前门拉响了门铃。
「赛丽正忙，」比克斯比太太开门让他进来，带三分歉意说，「俺就自个儿来应门了。」
「多谢您，」约翰说，「哈勒姆先生叫我差一点之前过来，也许我该再多等几分钟的。」
「不用，正好，」她答道，「兴许你想饭前洗洗、拾掇拾掇，俺领你去地方。」说着她在头里带路，上楼进了「前客厅卧房」。
「喏，」她说，「你自个儿随意，饭十来分钟就得。」
约翰一时在心里直揉眼睛。随后他转过身，一把攥住比克斯比太太的两只手，望着她说不出话来。等找着话，他说：「我不知道说什么好，也不知道怎么正经谢您。我想您自个儿都未必知道，这一手有多厚道。」
「快别提这个，」她嘴上推辞，脸上却是大大的满足，「俺这么做，图个心里松快——打你头一回来，俺就惦记你住在那么个糟心客栈里。」说着她作势要走。
「您和您弟弟，」约翰还握着她的手，诚恳地说，「叫我这个圣诞节，成了多少年以来最快活、最幸福的人。」
「俺高兴着呢，」她由衷地说，「盼你在这儿住得舒坦、住得安生。俺得去帮赛丽上菜了。拾掇好了就下起居室来。」她轻轻捏了捏他的手。
「波莉姑——请您原谅，比克斯比太太，」约翰一时冲动，说，「您看您心里能不能再成全我一桩，赏我一个亲？今儿可是圣诞节，」他笑着添了一句。
波莉姑妈红到了头发根。「这个嘛，」她带笑说，「俺当你的娘都绰绰有余了，俺琢磨着这亏不了什么。」她下楼去了，下楼时拿食指侧面轻轻蹭了蹭嘴唇。
约翰这才明白，那天早上大卫为什么一趟一趟朝银行窗外张望。

===Original===

All his belongings were in Aunt Polly’s best bedroom, having been moved over from the Eagle while he and David had been in the office. A delightful room it was, in immeasurable contrast to his squalid surroundings at that hostelry. The spacious bed, with its snowy counterpane and silk patchwork “comf’table” folded on the foot, the bright fire in the open stove, the big bureau and glass, the soft carpet, the table for writing and reading standing in the bay, his books on the broad mantel, and his dressing things laid out ready to his hand, not to mention an ample supply of *dry* towels on the rack.
The poor fellow’s life during the weeks which he had lived in Homeville had been utterly in contrast with any previous experience. Nevertheless he had tried to make the best of it, and to endure the monotony, the dullness, the entire lack of companionship and entertainment with what philosophy he could muster. The hours spent in the office were the best part of the day. He could manage to find occupation for all of them, though a village bank is not usually a scene of active bustle. Many of the people who did business there diverted him somewhat, and most of them seemed never too much in a hurry to stand around and talk the sort of thing that interested them. After John had got acquainted with his duties and the people he came in contact with, David gave less personal attention to the affairs of the bank; but he was in and out frequently during the day, and rarely failed to interest his cashier with his observations and remarks.
But the long winter evenings had been very bad. After supper, a meal which revolted every sense, there had been as many hours to be got through with as he found wakeful, an empty stomach often adding to the number of them, and the only resource for passing the time had been reading, which had often been well-nigh impossible for sheer physical discomfort. As has been remarked, the winter climate of the middle portion of New York State is as bad as can be imagined. His light was a kerosene lamp of half-candle power, and his appliance for warmth consisted of a small wood stove, which (as David would have expressed it) “took two men an’ a boy” to keep in action, and was either red hot or exhausted.

===Chinese===

他全部的家当都已经搬进了波莉姑妈顶好的卧室——趁他和大卫在办事处的工夫，从雄鹰旅店运了过来。好一间叫人欢喜的屋子，跟那客栈里的腌臜住处，一天一地。宽敞的床，雪白的床罩，床脚叠着一床绸子拼花的厚被；敞式炉里火光熊熊；大衣柜带穿衣镜；地毯软和；凸窗里摆着读写用的桌子；他的书搁在宽宽的壁炉台上；梳妆用具顺手放着——更别提架子上足足一摞*干*毛巾了。
这个可怜的小伙子住在霍姆维尔的几个星期，跟他从前的一切经历全都反着来。尽管如此，他还是尽力往好里过，拿出他攒得出的那点豁达，去捱那单调、那沉闷、那没伴儿也没消遣的日子。在办事处过的那几个钟头，是一天里最好的时光。他总能给这些时间找着事由，虽说一家乡村银行通常谈不上什么热火朝天。来办业务的人里头，好些都能给他解解闷，而且多数人似乎从没有忙到不肯站着聊几句的地步——聊的自然是他们各自惦记的那一类事。待约翰摸熟了业务、认熟了打交道的人，大卫对银行的事就不那么事事亲躬了；可他一天里总要进出好几趟，而他那一套见闻议论，很少有不逗乐他这位出纳的。
只是那一个一个漫长的冬夜实在难熬。晚饭过后——那顿饭样样倒人胃口——还有多少个钟头要睁着眼捱过去；肚子常常是空的，又给钟头添了数目；消磨时辰的法子只有一样：读书，可往往因为浑身不自在，连书都几乎读不下去。前头说过，纽约州中部地方的冬天，气候坏得只有更坏的才想象得出。他的灯，是一盏火力顶半支蜡烛的煤油灯；取暖的家什，是一只小柴炉——用大卫的话说，「得两个大人搭上个小孩」才伺候得转，而且不是烧得通红，就是烧得干干净净。

===Original===

As from the depths of a spacious lounging chair he surveyed his new surroundings, and contrasted them with those from which he had been rescued out of pure kindness, his heart was full, and it can hardly be imputed to him as a weakness that for a moment his eyes filled with tears of gratitude and happiness﻿—no less.
Indeed, there were four happy people at David’s table that Christmas day. Aunt Polly had “smartened up” Mrs. Cullom with collar and cuffs, and in various ways which the mind of man comprehendeth not in detail; and there had been some arranging of her hair as well, which altogether had so transformed and transfigured her that John thought that he should hardly have known her for the forlorn creature whom he had encountered in the morning. And as he looked at the still fine eyes, large and brown, and shining for the first time in many a year with a soft light of happiness, he felt that he could understand how it was that Billy P. had married the village girl.
Mrs. Bixbee was grand in black silk and lace collar fastened with a shell-cameo pin not quite as large as a saucer, and John caught the sparkle of a diamond on her plump left hand﻿—David’s Christmas gift﻿—with regard to which she had spoken apologetically to Mrs. Cullom:
“I told David that I was ever so much obliged to him, but I didn’t want a dimun’ more’n a cat wanted a flag, an’ I thought it was jest throwin’ away money. But he would have it﻿—said I c’d sell it an’ keep out the poorhouse some day, mebbe.”
David had not made much change in his usual raiment, but he was shaved to the blood, and his round red face shone with soap and satisfaction. As he tucked his napkin into his shirt collar, Sairy brought in the tureen of oyster soup, and he remarked, as he took his first spoonful of the stew, that he was “hungry ’nough t’ eat a graven imidge,” a condition that John was able to sympathize with after his two days of fasting on crackers and such provisions as he could buy at Purse’s. It was, on the whole, he reflected, the most enjoyable dinner that he ever ate. Never was such a turkey; and to see it give way under David’s skillful knife﻿—wings, drumsticks, second joints, side bones, breast﻿—was an elevating and memorable experience. And such potatoes, mashed in cream; such boiled onions, turnips, Hubbard squash, succotash, stewed tomatoes, celery, cranberries, “currant jell!” Oh!

===Chinese===

他从一把宽敞的安乐椅深处打量这新的四下，拿它跟方才那番纯粹的善心把他从里头捞出来的旧居一比，心里满满当当；他眼里一时涌上感激与幸福的泪水——这总不能算他软弱吧。一点儿也不能。
说真的，那个圣诞节，大卫的饭桌边坐着四个幸福的人。波莉姑妈拿领子、袖口，再加上一番男人心里没法细究的手艺，把卡勒姆太太「拾掇鲜亮」了；头发也重新篦过一回，这些合在一起，把她改造得脱胎换骨、容光焕发，约翰想，他简直认不出她就是早上遇见的那个凄惶人了。他望着那双依然好看的眼睛——大大的，棕色的，多年来头一回闪着一汪柔和的幸福的光——他觉得自己能明白，当年比利·P怎么会娶了这位村里的姑娘。
比克斯比太太穿一身黑绸，气派堂堂，花边领子上别着一枚贝雕别针，个头比茶碟只差一点儿；约翰又瞧见她那胖乎乎的左手上钻光一闪——大卫的圣诞礼物。关于这枚戒指，她对卡勒姆太太还赔过一番话：
「俺告诉戴维，俺承他的情，承得不得了，可俺要钻戒做什么？猫要旗子做什么？俺说这是白白糟蹋钱。可他非叫俺收下——说俺兴许哪天能把它卖了，省得进贫民院。」
大卫的行头倒没怎么换，只是胡子刮得干干净净，差点见了血，红扑扑的圆脸泛着肥皂和心满意足的光。他把餐巾掖进衬衫领口，赛丽端进牡蛎汤盅，他舀起第一勺，说自己是「饿得能吃下一尊石像」——约翰就着饼干、还有珀斯铺子里能买到的那些吃食，斋戒了两天，这个滋味他深有同感。他心想，总的来说，这是他平生吃过的最痛快的一顿饭。天底下没有那样的火鸡；看它在大卫熟练的刀下节节退让——翅膀、腿、二茬关节、边骨、胸脯——是一桩提气而难忘的经历。还有那样的土豆，奶油里捣的；那样的煮洋葱、芜菁、哈伯德南瓜、豆煮玉米、炖番茄、芹菜、蔓越莓、「醋栗冻」！噢！

===Original===

and to “top off” with, a mince pie to die for and a pudding (new to John, but just you try it some time) of steamed Indian meal and fruit, with a sauce of cream sweetened with shaved maple sugar.
“What’ll you have?” said David to Mrs. Cullom, “dark meat? white meat?”
“Anything,” she replied meekly, “I’m not partic’ler. Most any part of a turkey’ll taste good, I guess.”
“All right,” said David. “Don’t care means a little o’ both. I alwus know what to give Polly﻿—piece o’ the second jint an’ the last-thing-over-the-fence. Nice ’n rich fer scraggly folks,” he remarked. “How fer you, John?﻿—little o’ both, eh?” and he heaped the plate till our friend begged him to keep something for himself.
“Little too much is jest right,” he asserted.
When David had filled the plates and handed them along﻿—Sairy was for bringing in and taking out; they did their own helping to vegetables and “passin’ ”﻿—he hesitated a moment, and then got out of his chair and started in the direction of the kitchen door.
“What’s the matter?” asked Mrs. Bixbee in surprise. “Where you goin’?”
“Woodshed,” said David.
“Woodshed!” she exclaimed, making as if to rise and follow.
“You set still,” said David. “Somethin’ I fergot.”
“What on earth!” she exclaimed, with an air of annoyance and bewilderment. “What do you want in the woodshed? Can’t you set down an’ let Sairy git it for ye?”
“No,” he asserted with a grin. “Sairy might sqush it. It must be putty meller by this time.” And out he went.
“Manners!” ejaculated Mrs. Bixbee. “You’ll think (to John) we’re reg’ler heathin.”
“I guess not,” said John, smiling and much amused.
Presently Sairy appeared with four tumblers which she distributed, and was followed by David bearing a bottle. He seated himself and began a struggle to unwire the same with an icepick. Aunt Polly leaned forward with a look of perplexed curiosity.
“What you got there?” she asked.
“Vewve Clikot’s universal an’ suv’rin remedy,” said David, reading the label and bringing the corners of his eye and mouth almost together in a wink to John, “fer toothache, earache, burns, scalds, warts, dispepsy, fallin’ o’ the hair, windgall, ringbone, spavin, disapp’inted affections, an’ pips in hens,” and out came the cork with a *wop*, at which both the ladies, even Mrs. Cullom, jumped and cried out.
“David Harum,” declared his sister with conviction, “I believe thet that’s a bottle of champagne.”

===Chinese===

还有「压轴」的：一张叫人死也值得的肉馅派，一道布丁（约翰头一回见，可你哪天务必一试）——玉米面蒸的，里头和着果干，浇奶油沙司，甜味是枫糖刨花熬的。
「你用哪样？」大卫问卡勒姆太太，「深色的肉？浅色的肉？」
「什么都行，」她温顺地说，「我不挑。火鸡哪块都好吃，我想。」
「好嘞，」大卫说，「说不挑，就是两样都来点儿。波莉爱吃什么俺从来门儿清——二茬关节加『篱笆最后一样』，油水厚，瘦人吃了贴膘。」他说。「约翰你呢？——两样都来点儿，嗯？」他往盘子里堆，堆得咱这位朋友直求他给自个儿留点儿。
「多点儿才正好。」他一口咬定。
待大卫把盘子盛满递过去——赛丽管端进端出，蔬菜和「传递」都他们自个儿动手——他迟疑了一下，从椅子上起身，朝厨房门走去。
「怎么了？」比克斯比太太吃了一惊，「你上哪儿去？」
「柴棚。」大卫说。
「柴棚！」她叫起来，作势要起身跟上。
「你坐着别动，」大卫说，「落了样东西。」
「老天爷！」她又急又懵，「柴棚里有什么要拿的？你不能坐下，叫赛丽给你取去？」
「不行，」他咧嘴一笑，「赛丽会把它压坏的。这会儿准捂醇了。」说着他出去了。
「真是的！」比克斯比太太脱口道，「你会以为（对约翰说）俺们跟野人差不多了。」
「我看不像。」约翰笑着，给逗得不行。
不多会儿，赛丽端来四只平底大酒杯，挨个儿摆下；大卫跟着进来，手里一瓶酒。他坐下，动手拿冰锥跟瓶口的铁丝箍较劲。波莉姑妈探过身来，一脸又疑惑又好奇。
「你拿的什么？」她问。
「凯歌寡妇牌万应灵丹，」大卫念着标签，眼角嘴角一齐朝约翰那边挤了挤，「主治牙疼、耳朵疼、烧伤、烫伤、瘊子、消化不良、脱发、软肿、环骨瘤、飞节内肿、失恋，兼治母鸡生嗉病」——嘭的一声，软木塞蹦了出来，两位太太——连卡勒姆太太也在内——都吓了一跳，叫出声来。
「大卫·哈勒姆，」他姐姐斩钉截铁地说，「俺敢说，那是一瓶香槟。

===Original===

“If it ain’t,” said David, pouring into his tumbler, “I ben swindled out o’ four shillin’,” and he passed the bottle to John, who held it up tentatively, looking at Mrs. Bixbee.
“No, thank ye,” she said with a little toss of the head, “I’m a son o’ temp’rence. I don’t believe,” she remarked to Mrs. Cullom, “thet that bottle ever cost *less* ’n a dollar.” At which remarks David apparently “swallered somethin’ the wrong way,” and for a moment or two was unable to proceed with his dinner. Aunt Polly looked at him suspiciously. It was her experience that, in her intercourse with her brother, he often laughed utterly without reason﻿—so far as she could see.
“I’ve always heard it was dreadful expensive,” remarked Mrs. Cullom.
“Let me give you some,” said John, reaching toward her with the bottle. Mrs. Cullom looked first at Mrs. Bixbee and then at David.
“I don’t know,” she said. “I never tasted any.”
“Take a little,” said David, nodding approvingly.
“Just a swallow,” said the widow, whose curiosity had got the better of scruples. She took a swallow of the wine.
“How do ye like it?” asked David.
“Well,” she said as she wiped her eyes, into which the gas had driven the tears, “I guess I could get along if I couldn’t have it regular.”
“Don’t taste good?” suggested David with a grin.
“Well,” she replied, “I never did care any great for cider, and this tastes to me about as if I was drinkin’ cider an’ snuffin’ horseradish at one and the same time.”
“How’s that, John?” said David, laughing.
“I suppose it’s an acquired taste,” said John, returning the laugh and taking a mouthful of the wine with infinite relish. “I don’t think I ever enjoyed a glass of wine so much, or,” turning to Aunt Polly, “ever enjoyed a dinner so much,” which statement completely mollified her feelings, which had been the least bit in the world “set edgeways.”
“Mebbe your app’tite’s got somethin’ to do with it,” said David, shoveling a knife-load of good things into his mouth. “Polly, this young man’s ben livin’ on crackers an’ salt herrin’ fer a week.”
“My land!” cried Mrs. Bixbee with an expression of horror. “Is that reelly so? ’Tain’t now, reelly?”
“Not quite so bad as that,” John answered, smiling; “but Mrs. Elright has been ill for a couple of days and﻿—well, I have been foraging around Purse’s store a little.”
“Wa’al, of all the mean shames!” exclaimed Aunt Polly indignantly.

===Chinese===

「要不是，」大卫说着往自己杯里倒，「俺就是叫人坑了四个先令。」他把瓶递给约翰；约翰试探地举着，望向比克斯比太太。
「不，谢谢你，」她小脑袋一扬，「俺是戒酒会的。俺就不信，」她对卡勒姆太太说，「那瓶东西能不值*一块*钱。」这话一出，大卫显然「让什么东西呛着了」，有一两分钟没法接着吃饭。波莉姑妈疑疑惑惑地瞧他。凭她的经验，跟兄弟打交道的日子里，他常常笑得毫无来由——至少她瞧不出来。
「老听说这东西贵得吓人。」卡勒姆太太说。
「我给您倒一点儿吧。」约翰说着，拿瓶朝她递过去。卡勒姆太太先看看比克斯比太太，再看看大卫。
「我不知道，」她说，「我从没尝过。」
「来一点儿吧。」大卫点头赞许。
「就一小口。」寡妇说——好奇心到底压过了顾忌。她抿了一口。
「觉得怎么样？」大卫问。
「呃，」她一边擦眼睛——气泡的气直冲得她流泪——一边说，「要是常年喝不上，俺想俺也捱得过去。」
「味道不好？」大卫咧着嘴启发。
「呃，」她答道，「俺从来就不怎么稀罕苹果酒，这玩意儿喝着，俺觉着就像一边灌苹果酒、一边嗅辣根末儿似的。」
「怎么样，约翰？」大卫笑问。
「我看这是要慢慢培养的口味，」约翰回笑，美滋滋地呷了一大口，「我可从没这么受用过一杯酒，也——」他转向波莉姑妈，「从没这么受用过一顿饭。」这话把她方才隐隐「竖起来」的火气熨得平平整整。
「兴许也有你肚饿的功劳，」大卫说，一刀铲起满满一口好东西送进嘴里，「波莉，这小伙子啃饼干、就咸鲱鱼，啃了一个礼拜了。」
「俺的天！」比克斯比太太一脸惊骇地叫起来，「当真？不会是真的吧？」
「没那么惨，」约翰笑着答，「不过埃尔赖特太太病了两天，呃——我是在珀斯铺子里打了两天游击。」
「这个嘛，天下顶顶刻薄的糟践事儿！」波莉姑妈愤愤地叫道。

===Original===

“David Harum, you’d ought to be ridic’lous t’ allow such a thing.”
“Wa’al, I never!” said David, holding his knife and fork straight up in either fist as they rested on the table, and staring at his sister. “I believe if the meetin’-house roof was to blow off you’d lay it onto me somehow. I hain’t ben runnin’ the Eagle tavern fer quite a consid’able while. You got the wrong pig by the ear as usual. Jest you pitch into him,” pointing with his fork to John. “It’s his funeral, if anybody’s.”
“Wa’al,” said Aunt Polly, addressing John in a tone of injury, “I do think you might have let somebody know; I think you’d ortter ’ve known﻿—”
“Yes, Mrs. Bixbee,” he interrupted, “I did know how kind you are and would have been, and if matters had gone on so much longer I should have appealed to you, I should have indeed; but really,” he added, smiling at her, “a dinner like this is worth fasting a week for.”
“Wa’al,” she said, mollified again, “you won’t git no more herrin’ ’nless you ask fer ’em.”
“That is just what your brother said this morning,” replied John, looking at David with a laugh.

===Chinese===

「大卫·哈勒姆，容得下这种事，你可真行。」
「这个嘛，反了！」大卫说，两把刀叉直挺挺攥在左右拳里，立在桌上，瞪着他姐姐。「俺看呀，礼拜堂的屋顶要是叫风掀了，你也能算到俺头上。雄鹰客栈俺可有些日子没开了。你照例又揪错了猪耳朵。要训，你训他去，」叉子朝约翰一指，「要说谁自作自受，是他。」
「这个嘛，」波莉姑妈转向约翰，一副受了委屈的腔调，「俺可想你总该捎个信儿给人；俺想你总该如何如何——」
「是，比克斯比太太，」他打断道，「我确实知道您心善，知道您早乐意照应我；事情要再这么拖下去，我真要来求您了，真的；不过说实在的，」他笑着对她说，「这样一顿饭，饿一个礼拜也值。」
「这个嘛，」她又软了下来，「可你再要吃鲱鱼，不吭声可就没你的份儿了。」
「今儿早上您弟弟正是这么说的。」约翰答道，笑着望向大卫。

## XXIV / 二十四
===Original===

The meal proceeded in silence for a few minutes. Mrs. Cullom had said but little, but John noticed that her diction was more conventional than in her talk with David and himself in the morning, and that her manner at the table was distinctly refined, although she ate with apparent appetite, not to say hunger. Presently she said, with an air of making conversation, “I suppose you’ve always lived in the city, Mr. Lenox?”
“It has always been my home,” he replied, “but I have been away a good deal.”
“I suppose folks in the city go to theaters a good deal,” she remarked.
“They have a great many opportunities,” said John, wondering what she was leading up to. But he was not to discover, for David broke in with a chuckle.
“Ask Polly, Mis’ Cullom,” he said. “She c’n tell ye all about the theater, Polly kin.” Mrs. Cullom looked from David to Mrs. Bixbee, whose face was suffused.
“Tell her,” said David, with a grin.
“I wish you’d shet up,” she exclaimed. “I shan’t do nothin’ of the sort.”
“Ne’ mind,” said David cheerfully, “*I’ll* tell ye, Mis’ Cullom.”
“Dave Harum!” expostulated Mrs. Bixbee, but he proceeded without heed of her protest.
“Polly an’ I,” he said, “went down to New York one spring some years ago. Her nerves was some wore out ’long of diff’rences with Sairy about clearin’ up the woodshed, an’ bread risin’s, an’ not bein’ able to suit herself up to Purse’s in the qual’ty of silk velvit she wanted fer a Sunday-go-to-meetin’ gown, an’ I thought a spell off ’d do her good. Wa’al, the day after we got there I says to her while we was havin’ breakfust﻿—it was picked-up el’phant on toast, near ’s I c’n remember, wa’n’t it, Polly?”
“That’s as near the truth as most o’ the rest on’t so fur,” said Polly with a sniff.
“Wa’al, I says to her,” he proceeded, untouched by her scorn, “ ‘How’d you like to go t’ the theater? You hain’t never ben,’ I says, ‘an’ now you’re down here you may jest as well see somethin’ while you got a chanst,’ I says. Up to that *time*” he remarked, as it were in passing, “she’d ben somewhat pre*juced* ’ginst theaters, an’﻿—”
“Wa’al,” Mrs. Bixbee broke in, “I guess what we see that night was cal’lated﻿—”
“You hold on,” he interposed. “I’m tellin’ this story. You had a chanst to an’ wouldn’t. Anyway,” he resumed, “she allowed she’d try it once, an’ we agreed we’d go somewheres that night.

===Chinese===

席间静了几分钟。卡勒姆太太话不多，可约翰注意到，她的谈吐比早上跟大卫和他自己说话时更见规矩，餐桌上的举止也分明是文雅人家出身，虽说她吃得胃口大开，简直称得上饿了。过了一会儿，她摆出找话的架势说：「您想必一直在城里住吧，莱诺克斯先生？」
「那儿一直是我的家，」他答道，「不过我出门的时候多。」
「城里人想必常上戏园子吧。」她说。
「他们的机会是多。」约翰说，心里琢磿她要引到哪儿去。可他没琢磿出来，因为大卫一声笑插了进来。
「问波莉呀，卡勒姆太太，」他说，「戏园子的事她全门儿清，波莉门儿清。」卡勒姆太太的目光从大卫挪到比克斯比太太脸上，后者满脸涨得通红。
「讲给她听。」大卫咧嘴说。
「你给我闭嘴！」她嚷道，「俺才不干那种事呢。」
「不要紧，」大卫乐呵呵地说，「*俺*来讲，卡勒姆太太。」
「戴维·哈勒姆！」比克斯比太太想拦，可他不理会她的抗议，径自讲下去。
「几年前有个春天，波莉跟俺一块儿下纽约去了。她的神经有些熬坏了——起因是跟赛丽为收拾柴棚拌嘴，为发面拌嘴，还有上珀斯铺子，愣是买不着合意的绸丝绒，要做一件礼拜天见客的袍子——俺寻思让她出去散散心准有好处。这个嘛，到那儿的第二天，吃早饭的时候，俺跟她说——照俺记得，吃的是现成象肉吐司，是不是，波莉？」
「到眼下为止，这话跟你后头的一样，也就离实情差得不多。」波莉嗤了一声。
「这个嘛，俺跟她说，」他不受她讥诮的影响，接着讲，「『想不想上戏园子瞧瞧？你从来没去过，』俺说，『如今人都下来了，有机会还不瞧点什么，』俺说。顺带说一句，」他像随口一提，「到那*会儿*为止，她对戏园子多少存着些偏*见*，还有——」
「这个嘛，」比克斯比太太插进来，「俺瞧那天晚上俺们看的玩意儿，分明是算准了——」
「你等等，」他拦住她，「这故事是俺来讲。你有机会讲，你不讲。反正，」他接下去，「她答应了试一回，俺们就说定当晚去个什么地方。

But somethin’ happened to put it out o’ my mind, an’ I didn’t think on’t agin till I got back to the hotel fer supper. So I went to the feller at the newsstand an’ says, ‘Got any show-tickits fer tonight?’
“ ‘Theater?’ he says.
“ ‘I reckon so,’ I says.
“ ‘Wa’al,’ he says, ‘I hain’t got nothin’ now but two seats fer ‘Clyanthy.’
“ ‘Is it a good show?’ I says﻿—‘moral, an’ so on? I’m goin’ to take my sister, an’ she’s a little pertic’ler about some things,’ I says. He kind o’ grinned, the feller did. ‘I’ve took my wife twice, an’ she’s putty pertic’ler herself,’ he says, laughin.’ ”
“She must ’a’ ben,” remarked Mrs. Bixbee with a sniff that spoke volumes of her opinion of “the feller’s wife.” David emitted a chuckle.
“Wa’al,” he continued, “I took the tickits on the feller’s recommend, an’ the fact of his wife’s bein’ so pertic’ler, an’ after supper we went. It was a mighty handsome place inside, gilded an’ carved all over like the outside of a cirkis wagin, an’ when we went in the orchestry was playin’ an’ the people was comin’ in, an’ after we’d set a few minutes I says to Polly, ‘What do you think on’t?’ I says.
“ ‘I don’t see anythin’ very unbecomin’ so fur, an’ the people looks respectable enough,’ she says.
“ ‘No jail birds in sight fur ’s ye c’n see so fur, be they?’ I says. He, he, he, he!”
“You needn’t make me out more of a gump ’n I was,” protested Mrs. Bixbee. “An’ you was jest as﻿—” David held up his finger at her.
“Don’t you sp’ile the story by discountin’ the sequil. Wa’al, putty soon the band struck up some kind of a dancin’ tune, an’ the curt’in went up, an’ a girl come prancin’ down to the footlights an’ begun singin’ an’ dancin’, an’, scat my —﻿—﻿—! to all human appearances you c’d ’a’ covered ev’ry dum thing she had on with a postage stamp.” John stole a glance at Mrs. Cullom. She was staring at the speaker with wide-open eyes of horror and amazement.
“I guess I wouldn’t go very *fur* into pertic’lers,” said Mrs. Bixbee in a warning tone.
David bent his head down over his plate and shook from head to foot, and it was nearly a minute before he was able to go on. “Wa’al,” he said, “I heard Polly give a kind of a gasp an’ a snort, ’s if someone ’d throwed water ’n her face. But she didn’t say nothin’, an’, I swan!

可后来出了点事，俺把这事忘了，直到回旅馆吃晚饭才想起来。俺就走到卖报的柜台那儿，问那伙计：『今儿晚上有戏票没有？』
「『戏园子？』他说。
「『俺想是。』俺说。
「『这个嘛，』他说，『俺眼下就剩两张《克莱安西》的了。』
「『戏好不好？』俺说——『正派不正派？俺要带俺姐姐去，她有些事上讲究。』俺说。那伙计咧了咧嘴。『俺带俺媳妇去过两回，俺媳妇自个儿也顶讲究。』他笑着说。」
「她准是够讲究的。」比克斯比太太嗤了一声，对「那伙计的媳妇」的品评尽在不言中。大卫噗嗤一乐。
「这个嘛，」他接着讲，「俺听那伙计的保荐，又看他媳妇那么讲究，就把票买了，晚饭后俺们去了。场子里头真是气派，贴金雕花，满处都是，活像马戏车的外皮；俺们进去的时候，乐队正奏着，看客陆陆续续进来。坐了几分钟，俺跟波莉说：『你瞧着怎么样？』俺说。
「『到眼下还没瞧出什么不体面的，人瞅着也都够体面。』她说。
「『照眼面前看，牢里的鸟儿一只也瞧不见，是不是？』俺说。嘿嘿嘿！」
「你犯不着把俺编排得比俺那会儿还傻。」比克斯比太太抗议，「再说你自个儿也照样——」大卫朝她竖起一根手指。
「别拆台，后头还有下文呢。这个嘛，不大会儿，乐队奏起一支什么舞曲，幕升上去了，一个姑娘蹦蹦跳跳来到脚灯前，又唱又跳，哎呀俺的——！明摆着，她身上那一丁点儿行头，一张邮票就贴得过来。」约翰偷眼瞧卡勒姆太太。她正瞪着讲话的人，眼睛睁得溜圆，又惊又骇。
「俺看你还是别往细里说。」比克斯比太太警告的口气。

I didn’t dast to look at her fer a spell; an’ putty soon in come a hull crowd more girls that had left their clo’es in their trunks or somewhere, singin’, an’ dancin’, an’ weavin’ ’round on the stage, an’ after a few minutes I turned an’ looked at Polly. He, he, he, he!”
“David Harum!” cried Mrs. Bixbee, “ef you’re goin’ to discribe any more o’ them scand’lous goin’s on I sh’ll take my victuals into the kitchin. *I* didn’t see no more of ’em,” she added to Mrs. Cullom and John, “after that fust trollop appeared.”
“I don’t believe she did,” said David, “fer when I turned she set there with her eys shut tighter ’n a drum, an’ her mouth shut too so’s her nose an’ chin most come together, an’ her face was red enough so ’t a streak o’ red paint ’d ’a’ made a white mark on it. ‘Polly,’ I says, ‘I’m afraid you ain’t gettin’ the wuth o’ your money.’
“ ‘David Harum,’ she says, with her mouth shut all but a little place in the corner toward me, ‘if you don’t take me out o’ this place, I’ll go without ye,’ she says.

When Polly Bixbee,” said David impressively, “puts that foot o’ her’n *down* somethin’s got to sqush, an’ don’t you fergit it.” Mrs. Bixbee made no acknowledgment of this tribute to her strength of character. “Yes,” he said, with a solemn bend of the head, as if in answer to a question, “I squshed. I says to her, ‘All right. Bixbee’s head, “the feller went an’ leaned up agin the wall.”

“I reckon you haven’t followed theater-goin’ much after that,” she said to her hostess.
“No, ma’am,” Mrs. Bixbee replied with emphasis, “you better believe I hain’t. I hain’t never thought of it sence without tinglin’ all over. David threw back his head and roared with laughter. Mrs. Bixbee looked at him with unmixed scorn. “If I couldn’t help makin’ a﻿—” she began, “I’d﻿—”

For a time nothing more was said which did not relate to the replenishment of plates, glasses, and cups. Finally David cleaned up his plate with his knife blade and a piece of bread, and pushed it away with a sigh of fullness, mentally echoed by John.
“I feel ’s if a child could play with me,” he remarked. “What’s comin’ now, Polly?”
“The’s a mince pie, an’ Injun puddin’ with maple sugar an’ cream, an’ ice cream,” she replied.
“Mercy on us!” he exclaimed. “I guess I’ll have to go an’ jump up an’ down on the verandy. How do you feel, John?

太太的头顶望过去，「那伙计就走开，往墙根上一靠。」
「大卫·哈勒姆！」比克斯比太太嚷起来，「这是一句彻头彻尾的*瞎话*。你根本没跟谁说过话，还有——还有——谁都知道俺比你大不过四岁。」
「这个嘛，你瞧，波莉，」她兄弟用一种把人气个半死的圆滑腔调答道，「那伙计跟俺们不熟，他不过照表面下断语。」
波莉姑妈向约翰求援：「他够不够——够——俺都说不上来什么！」
「我真不明白你怎么跟他过活。」约翰笑着说。
卡勒姆太太脸上浮着一层浅笑，像是知道眼前有什么可乐的事，又拿不准可乐在哪儿。这位寡妇可怜见儿的，多半把什么都当正经。
「我想打那以后您就不大上戏园子了吧。」她对女主人说。
「可不，太太，」比克斯比太太重重地说，「你只管信。俺打那回起，想起来就浑身发麻。俺相信，」她一口咬定，「要不是为了俺，戴维是能把那场戏看完的；可是千真万确，辛西·卡勒姆，俺瞧的那一小眼就把俺臊的——到了该上床的时候，俺是摸黑脱的衣裳。」
大卫仰头大笑。比克斯比太太拿全然的鄙夷望着他。「俺要是忍不住说出一——」她起了个头，「俺就——」
「哎呀主啊！波莉，」大卫打断她，「出门千万包严实了。你要着了凉，那点怕人笑话的心思再一内攻，你可就真真儿地交代了。」这话得到了它应得的待遇——无人理睬；大卫重新扑向他的饭，说要「补上耽误的工夫」——虽说事实上，讲话讲得最多的一直是他，而且讲的同时，嘴上那另一桩营生也一刻没停。
有一阵子，席上的话不出添盘添杯添碗的范围。末了，大卫拿刀背和一块面包把盘子刮得干干净净，把它往前一推，叹出一声心满意足的饱嗝——约翰心里也正有同感。
「俺觉着，小孩都能拿俺耍着玩了。」他说，「底下还有什么，波莉？」
「有肉馅派，有枫糖奶油印第安布丁，还有冰激凌。」她答道。
「老天爷！」他叫起来，「俺看俺得去廊子上蹦跶蹦跶了。你觉着怎么样，约翰？
