Harum in conclusion, “is wuth its weight in gold, *jest about*.”
“David Harum,” declared Aunt Polly, “you’d ort to be ’shamed o’ yourself.”
“Wa’al,” said David with an air of meekness, “if I’ve done anythin’ I’m sorry for, I’m willin’ to be forgi’n. Now, s’posin’﻿—”
“I’ve heard enough ’bout s’posin’ fer one day,” said Mrs. Bixbee decisively, “unless it’s s’posin’ you finish your dinner so’s’t Sairy c’n git through her work sometime.”

===Chinese===

「称起斤两来，差不多真抵得上同等分量的黄金。」
「大卫·哈勒姆，」波莉婶婶宣称，「你该臊得慌。」
「呃，」大卫一脸温顺地说，「我要是做了什么亏心事，我情愿求人饶恕。这么说吧，假设——」
「今天『假设』两个字我听够了，」比克斯比太太斩钉截铁地说，「除非你假设把这顿饭吃完，也好叫萨莉有个收工的时候。」

## XXXIX / 三十九
===Original===

The road they were on was a favorite drive with the two men, and at the point where they had now arrived David always halted for a look back and down upon the scene below them﻿—to the south, beyond the intervening fields, bright with maturing crops, lay the village; to the west the blue lake, winding its length like a broad river, and the river itself a silver ribbon, till it was lost beneath the southern hills.
Neither spoke. For a few minutes John took in the scene with the pleasure it always afforded him, and then glanced at his companion, who usually had some comment to make upon anything which stirred his admiration or interest. He was gazing, not at the landscape, but apparently at the top of the dashboard. “Ho, hum,” he said, straightening the reins, with a “clk” to the horses, and they drove along for a while in silence﻿—so long, in fact, that our friend, while aware that the elder man did not usually abandon a topic until he had “had his say out,” was moved to suggest a continuance of the narrative which had been rather abruptly broken off, and in which he had become considerably interested.
“Was your wife pleased?” he asked at last.
“Where was I?” asked the other in return.
“You were on your way home with your purchase,” was the reply.
“Oh, yes,” Mr. Harum resumed. “It was a little after tea time when I got to the house, an’ I thought prob’ly I’d find her in the settin’ room waitin’ fer me; but she wa’n’t, an’ I went up to the bedroom to find her, feelin’ a little less sure o’ things. She was settin’ lookin’ out o’ winder when I come in, an’ when I spoke to her she didn’t give me no answer except to say, lookin’ up at the clock, ‘What’s kept ye like this?’
“ ‘Little matter o’ bus’nis,’ I says, lookin’ as smilin’ ’s I knew how, an’ holdin’ the box behind me.
“ ‘What you got there?’ she says, slewin’ her head ’round to git a sight at it.
“ ‘Little matter o’ bus’nis,’ I says agin, bringin’ the box to the front an’ feelin’ my face straighten out ’s if you’d run a flat iron over it. She seen the name on the paper.
“ ‘You ben spendin’ your time there, have ye?’ she says, settin’ up in her chair an’ pointin’ with her finger at the box. ‘*That’s* where you ben the last half hour, hangin’ ’round with them minxes in Mis’ Shoolbred’s. What’s in that box?’ she says, with her face a-blazin’.

===Chinese===

他们走的这条路，是两人心爱的一条驾车道；到了眼下这处地方，大卫总要停一停，回头俯瞰脚下的景致——南边，隔着一片庄稼渐熟的亮堂田野，卧着村庄；西边是蓝色的湖，像一条宽河蜿蜒铺展；河道本身是一根银带，直到没进南面的群山底下。
谁也没说话。约翰把景致饱看了几分钟——它一向叫他心怡——随后瞟了一眼同伴；往常凡有打动他的叹赏或兴味的事，他总有一番议论。这一刻他凝望着的却不是山水，倒像是挡泥板的顶。「呵，哼。」他说，把缰绳抻直，朝牲口「嘬」了一声；两人闷头赶了一程——闷得实在久了，咱这位朋友明知老人家不到「把话说尽」不撒手一个话头，也不由得想请他把方才突然中断、而自己又听出了兴头的那段事接下去。
「您太太高兴吗？」他终于问。
「俺说到哪儿了？」对方反问。
「您正带着买来的东西回家。」是回答。
「哦，是了，」哈勒姆先生接下去。「俺到家的时候过茶点不一会儿，俺寻思多半能在起居室里堵着她等俺；可她不在，俺上卧室去找，心里对时局有点儿没底了。俺进去的时候，她正坐着朝窗外望；俺一开口，她也不答话，只抬眼看看钟，说：『什么事把你绊成这样？』
「『一桩小小的买卖，』俺说，脸上尽量堆着笑，把盒子背在身后。
「『你手里拿的什么？』她说，脑袋一拧，要瞅它一眼。
「『一桩小小的买卖，』俺又说了一遍，把盒子转到身前，只觉着脸绷得平平整整，像叫熨斗熨过一道。她看见了纸上的字号。
「『你把工夫都耗在那儿了，是不是？』她说，在椅子上坐直了，手指头点着那盒子。『这半个钟头你就是这么过的——泡在舒尔布雷德太太那儿那帮狐狸精中间。盒子里装的什么？』她说，脸烧得通红。

===Original===

“ ‘Now, Lizy,’ I says, ‘I wa’n’t there ten minutes if I was that, an’ I ben buyin’ you a bunnit.’
“ ‘*You﻿—ben﻿—buyin’﻿—me﻿—a﻿—bunnit*?’ she says, stifnin’ up stiffer ’n a stake.
“ ‘Yes,’ I says, ‘I heard you say somethin’ ’bout a spring bunnit, an’ I thought, seein’ how economicle you was, that I’d buy you a nicer one ’n mebbe you’d feel like yourself. I thought it would please ye,’ I says, tryin’ to rub her the right way.
“ ‘Let me see it,’ she says, in a voice dryer ’n a lime-burner’s hat, pressin’ her lips together an’ reachin’ out fer the box. Wa’al, sir, she snapped the string with a jerk an’ sent the cover skimmin’ across the room, an’ then, as she hauled the parcel out of the box, she got up onto her feet. Then she tore the paper off on’t an’ looked at it a minute, an’ then took it ’tween her thumb an’ finger, like you hold up a dead rat by the tail, an’ held it off at the end of her reach, an’ looked it all over, with her face gettin’ even redder if it could. Fin’ly she says, in a voice ’tween a whisper ’n a choke:
“ ‘What’d you pay fer the thing?’
“ ‘Fifteen dollars,’ I says.
“ ‘Fifteen *dollars*?’ she says.
“ ‘Yes,’ I says, ‘don’t ye like it?’ Wa’al,” said David, “she never said a word. She drawed in her arm an’ took holt of the bunnit with her left hand, an’ fust she pulled off one thing an’ dropped it on the floor, fur off as she c’d reach, an’ then another, an’ then another, an’ then, by gum! she went at it with both hands jest as fast as she could work ’em, an’ in less time ’n I’m tellin’ it to ye she picked the thing cleaner ’n any chicken you ever see, an’ when she got down to the carkis she squeezed it up between her two hands, give it a wring an’ a twist like it was a wet dish towel, an’ flung it slap in my face. Then she made a half turn, throwin’ back her head an’ grabbin’ into her hair, an’ give the awfullest screechin’ laugh﻿—one screech after another that you c’d ’a’ heard a mile﻿—an’ then throwed herself face down on the bed, screamin’ an’ kickin’. Wa’al, sir, if I wa’n’t at my wits’ end, you c’n have my watch an’ chain.
“She wouldn’t let me touch her no way, but, as luck had it, it was one o’ the times when we had a hired girl, an’ hearin’ the noise she come gallopin’ up the stairs.

===Chinese===

「『莉齐，』俺说，『俺在那儿统共没待十分钟，俺是给你买了顶帽子。』
「『你——给——俺——买了——一顶——帽子？』她说，硬得比木桩子还直。
「『是，』俺说，『俺听你念叨过开春要顶新帽子；俺寻思，瞧你过日子那么省，俺就给你买顶好点儿的，兴许比你自己舍得出手买的好些。俺想这总归讨你欢心，』俺说，想顺着毛捋。
「『拿来俺瞧。』她说，嗓音干得赛石灰窑工的帽子，嘴唇抿紧，伸手来够盒子。这个嘛，先生，她一扯把线绳崩断，盒盖甩得满屋飞；跟着把纸包从盒子里拖出来，人也就站起来了。她把外包纸撕了，端详了一分钟，随后大拇指和食指把它拈起来——像拎着条死老鼠的尾巴——直伸到胳膊够不着的地方，前后左右打量，脸要还能更红的话就又红了一层。末了她开口，声音一半是气音一半是哽咽：
「『这玩意儿你花了多少钱？』
「『十五块。』俺说。
「『十五*块*？』她说。
「『是，』俺说，『你不欢喜？』这个嘛，」大卫说，「她一个字没说。她收回胳膊，左手攥住那帽子，先揪下一样东西，扬手撂在地上，撂得要多远有多远；又一样，又一样；跟着，好家伙！她两只手一齐上，怎么快怎么来，比俺说给你听的功夫还短，就把那顶帽子拾掇得比你所见的任何一只鸡都干净；拾掇到只剩个架子，她两手把它一攥，拧一把、绞一把，像拧一块洗碗的湿布，啪的摔在俺脸上。随后她半旋个身，头往后一甩，两手插进头发里，发出一声顶凄厉的尖笑——一声接一声，一里地外都听得见——然后一头栽倒在床上，脸朝下，又叫又踢。这个嘛，先生，俺要是没黔驴技穷，你把俺的怀表和表链拿去。
「她怎么也不让俺碰，可赶巧，那阵子俺们正雇着个丫头，她听见动静，咚咚咚奔上楼来。

===Original===

She wa’n’t a young girl, an’ she had a face humbly ’nough to keep her awake nights, but she had some sense, an’﻿—‘You’d bether run fer the docther,’ she says, when she see the state my wife was in. You better believe I done the heat of my life,” said David, “an’ more luck, the doctor was home an’ jest finishin’ his tea. His house an’ office wa’n’t but two three blocks off, an’ in about a few minutes me an’ him an’ his bag was leggin’ it fer my house, though I noticed he didn’t seem to be ’n as much of a twitter ’s I was. He ast me more or less questions, an’ jest as we got to the house he says:
“ ‘Has your wife had any thin’ to ’larm or shock her this evenin’?’
“ ‘Nothin’ ’t I know on,’ I says, ‘ ’cept I bought her a new bunnit that didn’t seem to come quite up to her idees.’ At that,” remarked Mr. Harum, “he give me a funny look, an’ we went in an’ upstairs.
“The hired girl,” he proceeded, “had got her quieted down some, but when we went in she looked up, an’ seein’ me, set up another screech, an’ he told me to go downstairs an’ he’d come down putty soon, an’ after a while he did.
“ ‘Wa’al?’ I says.
“ ‘She’s quiet fer the present,’ he says, takin’ a pad o’ paper out o’ his pocket, an’ writin’ on it.
“ ‘Do you know Mis’ Jones, your next-door neighbor?’ he says. I allowed ’t I had a speakin’ acquaintance with her.
“ ‘Wa’al,’ he says, ‘fust, you step in an’ tell her I’m here an’ want to see her, and ast her if she won’t come right along; an’ then you go down to my office an’ have these things sent up; an’ then,’ he says, ‘you go down town an’ send this’﻿—handin’ me a note that he’d wrote an’ put in an envelope﻿—‘up to the hospital﻿—better send it up with a hack, or, better yet, go yourself,’ he says, ‘an’ hurry. You can’t be no use here,’ he says. ‘I’ll stay, but I want a nurse here in an hour, an’ less if possible.’ I was putty well scared,” said David, “by all that, an’ I says, ‘Lord,’ I says, ‘is she as bad off as that? What is it ails her?’
“ ‘Don’t you know?’ says the doc, givin’ me a queer look.
“ ‘No,’ I says, ‘she hain’t ben fust rate fer a spell back, but I couldn’t git nothin’ out of her what was the matter, an’ don’t know what pertic’ler thing ails her now, unless it’s that dum’d bunnit,’ I says.
“At that the doctor laughed a little, kind as if he couldn’t help it.

===Chinese===

她不是个年轻姑娘了，那张脸也丑得够她夜里睡不着觉，可她有些见识，而且——『你顶好快去请大夫，』她瞧见俺媳妇那副光景，说。你只管信，俺这辈子没跑过那么一阵，」大卫说，「更走运，大夫在家，正喝完茶。他的住宅兼诊所离俺家不过两三个街区，不几分钟，俺、他、还有他的药箱，一路小跑直奔俺家——虽说俺瞧他不像俺那样火烧眉毛。他或多或少问了俺几句，到家门口的时候他说：
「『今儿晚上，尊夫人可曾遇上什么惊吓或是刺激？』
「『俺所知道的没有，』俺说，『只有俺给她买了顶新帽子，好像不大合她的心意。』一听这话，」哈勒姆先生说，「他拿古怪的眼神看了俺一眼，俺们就进屋上了楼。
「那丫头，」他接着讲，「已经哄得她安静了些；可俺们一进去，她抬头瞧见俺，又是一声尖叫；大夫叫俺下楼去，说他一会儿就下来，过了一阵他下来了。
「『怎么样？』俺说。
「『眼下算是安静了，』他说，从兜里掏出一沓纸，往上写着什么。
「『您认得隔壁的琼斯太太吧？』他说。俺表示跟她有点头之交。
「『这个嘛，』他说，『你先过去一趟，告诉她俺在这儿，要见她，问问她能不能这就过来；然后你到俺诊所去，叫人把这些东西送上来；再然后，』他说，『你下城里去，把这封』——他递给俺一张写好装了封的信——『送到医院去——顶好搭辆马车送去，或者，更好，你自个儿跑一趟，』他说，『要快。你在这儿帮不上忙，』他说，『俺留下。可俺要一个看护，一个钟头之内到，越快越好。』俺叫这一切唬得不轻，」大卫说，「俺就说：『老天爷，』俺说，『她病到那个地步了？她到底是什么毛病？』
「『您不知道？』大夫说，拿古怪的眼神瞧俺。
「『不知道，』俺说，『她前一阵子就一直不大利索，可俺从她嘴里掏不出个病因，如今也说不准是哪一桩事磨她，除非是那顶倒霉的帽子，』俺说。
「一听这话，大夫笑了笑，那样子像是憋不住。

===Original===

“ ‘I don’t think that was hully to blame,’ he says; ‘may have hurried matters up a little﻿—somethin’ that was liable to happen any time in the next two months.’
“ ‘You don’t mean it?’ I says.
“ ‘Yes,’ he says. ‘Now you git out as fast as you can. Wait a minute,’ he says. ‘How old is your wife?’
“ ‘F’m what she told me ’fore we was married,’ I says, ‘she’s thirty-one.’
“ ‘Oh!’ he says, raisin’ his eyebrows. ‘All right; hurry up, now,’
“I dusted around putty lively, an’ inside of an hour was back with the nurse, an ’jest after we got inside the door﻿—” David paused thoughtfully for a moment and then, lowering his tone a little, “jest as we got inside the front door, a door upstairs opened an’ I heard a little ‘Waa! waa!’ like it was the leetlist kind of a new lamb﻿—an’ I tell you,” said David, with a little quaver in his voice, and looking straight over the off horse’s ears, “nothin’ ’t I ever heard before nor since ever fetched me, right where I *lived*, as that did. The nurse, she made a dive fer the stairs, wavin’ me back with her hand, an’ I﻿—wa’al﻿—I went into the settin’ room, an﻿—wa’al﻿—ne’ mind.
“I dunno how long I set there list’nin’ to ’em movin’ ’round overhead, an’ wonderin’ what was goin’ on; but fin’ly I heard a step on the stair an’ I went out into the entry, an’ it was Mis’ Jones. ‘How be they?’ I says.
“ ‘We don’t quite know yet,’ she says. ‘The little boy is a nice formed little feller,’ she says, ‘an’ them childern very often grow up, but he is *very little*,’ she says.
“ ‘An’ how ’bout my wife?’ I says.
“ ‘Wa’al,’ she says, ‘we don’t know jes’ yet, but she is quiet now, an’ we’ll hope fer the best. If you want me,’ she says, ‘I’ll come any time, night or day, but I must go now. The doctor will stay all night, an’ the nurse will stay till you c’n git someone to take her place,’ an’ she went home, an’,” declared David, “you’ve hearn tell of the ‘salt of the earth,’ an’ if that woman wa’n’t more on’t than a hoss c’n draw down hill, the’ ain’t no such thing.”
“Did they live?” asked John after a brief silence, conscious of the bluntness of his question, but curious as to the sequel.
“The child did,” replied David; “not to grow up, but till he was ’twixt six an’ seven; but my wife never left her bed, though she lived three four weeks.

===Chinese===

「『俺想那倒不能全怪它，』他说，『兴许把它催快了些——那本来就是往后两个月里随时要来的事。』
「『当真？』俺说。
「『当真，』他说，『现在你有多快闪人多快。慢着，』他说，『尊夫人多大年纪？』
「『照她婚前跟俺讲的，』俺说，『三十一。』
「『哦！』他说，眉毛一抬，『行了；快去吧。』
「俺手脚着实麻利，不到一个钟头，就领着看护回来了；俺们前脚跨进门——」大卫若有所思地顿了顿，然后把嗓音压低了些——「俺们前脚进门厅，楼上一扇门开了，俺听见一小声『哇！哇！』，像顶小顶小的一只新羊羔——俺告诉你，」大卫说，嗓音里带着一丝颤，眼睛直望着外侧那匹马的耳朵，「俺这辈子，之前之后，再没有什么声音像那一声，一下打到俺*心坎上*。看护一头朝楼梯冲，抬手把俺往后一拦；俺呢——这个嘛——俺进了起居室，然后——算了，不提了。
「俺在里头坐了多久，听着他们头顶上挪来挪去，猜着外头到底怎么个光景，俺说不上；末了俺听见楼梯上有了脚步，俺出去到过道里，是琼斯太太。『他们怎么样？』俺说。
「『还拿不准，』她说，『小男孩是个周正的小家伙，』她说，『这样的孩子往往养得大，可他*实在小得可怜*，』她说。
「『俺媳妇呢？』俺说。
「『这个嘛，』她说，『眼下还说不好；她这会儿安静了，咱们往好里盼。你要用得着俺，』她说，『俺白天黑夜随叫随到；可俺这会儿得走了。大夫会守一整夜，看护也会守到你寻着接替的人。』她回家了；」大卫宣称，「『世上的盐』你总听说过——那女人要不是比一匹马下坡拉得动的还多，世上就没有那个东西了。」
「他们都没事吗？」短暂沉默后约翰问，自觉问得直白，可又实在惦记下文。
「孩子活下来了，」大卫答，「没长大——活到六七岁之间；俺媳妇再没下过床，虽说又熬了三四个礼拜。

===Original===

She never seemed to take no int’rist in the little feller, nor nothin’ else much; but one day﻿—it was Sunday, long to the last﻿—she seemed a little more chipper ’n usual. I was settin’ with her, an’ I said to her how much better she seemed to be, tryin’ to chirk her up.
“ ‘No,’ she says, ‘I ain’t goin’ to live.’
“ ‘Don’t ye say that,’ I says.
“ ‘No,’ she says, ‘I ain’t, an’ I don’t care.’
“I didn’t know jest what to say, an’ she spoke agin:
“ ‘I want to tell you, Dave,’ she says, ‘that you’ve ben good an’ kind to me.’
“ ‘I’ve tried to,’ I says, ‘an’ Lizy,’ I says, ‘I’ll never fergive myself about that bunnit, long ’s I live.’
“ ‘That hadn’t really nothin’ to do with it,’ she says, ‘an’ you meant all right, though,’ she says, almost in a whisper, an’ the’ came across her face, not a smile exac’ly, but somethin’ like a little riffle on a piece o’ still water, ‘that bunnit *was* enough to kill most *any*body.’ ”

===Chinese===

她好像对那小家伙再没起过什么兴头，对旁的事也大抵如此；可有一天——是个礼拜天，已经拖到最后了——她瞧着比平日稍微爽利些。俺守在她旁边，说与她听她气色好多了，想宽她的心。
「『不，』她说，『俺活不成了。』
「『快别这么说，』俺说。
「『不，』她说，『俺活不成，俺也不在乎。』
「俺一时不知说什么好，她又开口：
「『俺想告诉你，戴夫，』她说，『你一直待俺好，待俺亲。』
「『俺是尽力来着，』俺说，『还有莉齐，』俺说，『那顶帽子的事，俺一辈子不能饶俺自个儿。』
「『那实在算不得什么，』她说，『你原是一番好意，』她说，声音低得几乎是气音；她脸上掠过的不是笑，倒像一泓静水上细细的一道涟漪——『那顶帽子，*确实*够送掉大多数*任何*人的命。』」

## XXXV / 三十五
===Original===

After dinner John went to his room and David and his sister seated themselves on the “verandy.” Mr. Harum lighted a cigar and enjoyed his tobacco for a time in silence, while Mrs. Bixbee perused, with rather perfunctory diligence, the columns of her weekly church paper.
“I seen a sight fer sore eyes this mornin’,” quoth David presently.
“What was that?” asked Aunt Polly, looking up over her glasses.
“Claricy Verjoos fer one part on’t,” said David.
“The Verjooses hev come, hev they? Wa’al, that’s good. I hope she’ll come up an’ see me.”
David nodded. “An’ the other part on’t was,” he said, “she an’ that young feller of our’n was walkin’ together, an’ a putty slick pair they made too.”
“Ain’t she purty?” said Mrs. Bixbee.
“They don’t make ’em no puttier,” affirmed David; “an’ they was a nice pair. I couldn’t help thinkin’,” he remarked, “what a nice hitch up they’d make.”
“Guess the’ ain’t much chance o’ that,” she observed.
“No, I guess not either,” said David.
“He hain’t got anythin’ to speak of, I s’pose, an’ though I reckon she’ll hev prop’ty some day, all that set o’ folks seems to marry money, an’ someone’s alwus dyin’ an’ leavin’ some on ’em some more. The’ ain’t nothin’ truer in the Bible,” declared Mrs. Bixbee with conviction, “ ’n that sayin’ thet them that has gits.”
“That’s seemin’ly about the way it runs in gen’ral,” said David.
“It don’t seem right,” said Mrs. Bixbee, with her eyes on her brother’s face. “Now there was all that money one o’ Mis’ Elbert Swayne’s relations left her last year, an’ Lucy Scramm, that’s poorer ’n poverty’s back kitchin, an’ the same relation to him that Mis’ Swayne was, only got a thousan’ dollars, an’ the Swaynes rich already. Not but what the thousan’ was a godsend to the Scramms, but he might jest as well ’a’ left ’em comf’tibly off as not, ’stid of pilin’ more onto the Swaynes that didn’t need it.”
“Does seem kind o’ tough,” David observed, leaning forward to drop his cigar ash clear of the veranda floor, “but that’s the way things goes, an’ I’ve often had to notice that a man’ll sometimes do the foolishist thing or the meanest thing in his hull life after he’s dead.”
“You never told me,” said Mrs. Bixbee, after a minute or two, in which she appeared to be following up a train of reflection, “much of anythin’ about John’s matters. Hain’t he ever told you anythin’ more ’n what you’ve told me?

===Chinese===

饭后约翰回自己房里去了，大卫同他妹妹在「游廊」上坐下。哈勒姆先生点起一支雪茄，冈头享受了一阵烟草；比克斯比太太捧着她那份教会周报，那股勤勉多少带点敷衍。
「今儿早上我可见着件养眼的事，」大卫过了一阵说。
「什么事？」波莉婶婶从眼镜上头抬起眼来问。
「一半是克拉丽丝·弗茹，」大卫说。
「弗茹家来了，是么？呃，好哇。我盼着她上来看我。」
大卫点头。「另一半是，」他说，「她同咱们那个小伙子正并肩走着，凑成挺精神的一对儿。」
「她长得标致吧？」比克斯比太太说。
「再标致也造不出来了，」大卫断言；「这一对儿也真般配。我不由得想，」他说，「他俩要是配成一双，多好。」
「我看没什么指望，」她说。
「是啊，我看也没有，」大卫说。
「他大概没什么家底可言；她呢，虽说早晚会有产业——那一伙人好像专同钱结亲，又总有谁死了，再给他们哪一位撞下一笔。圣经里再没有哪句，」比克斯比太太深信不疑地宣称，「比『有的还要给他』更真的了。」
「大面上看，世道也就是这么个走法，」大卫说。
「这瞧着不对，」比克斯比太太眼睛望着兄弟的脸说。「就说去年埃尔伯特·斯温太太的一位亲戚留给她的那一大注钱；露西·斯克拉姆呢，穷得赛过穷的后厨房，论亲戚同那位故人是同一样的远近，只得了一千块——斯温家本来就有钱。那一千块对斯克拉姆家固然是雪中送炭，可他留着的人舒舒服服也是留，何苦再往不缺钱的斯温家上头堆呢。」
「瞧着是有点儿狠，」大卫说着，欠身把雪茄灰弹到游廊外头去，「可世道就这样；我常不得不留意到，一个人一辈子顶糊涂、顶刻薄的事，有时候是在他死了以后干的。」
「你从来没跟我细说过，」过了一两分钟，比克斯比太太说——这一阵她像是顺着一条思路在想——「约翰的那些事。他跟你说过的，就没有多出你告诉我的这些么？

===Original===

or don’t ye want me to know? Didn’t his father leave anythin’?”
“The’ was a little money,” replied her brother, blowing out a cloud of smoke, “an’ a lot of unlikely chances, but nothin’ to live on.”
“An’ the’ wa’n’t nothin’ for ’t but he had to come up here?” she queried.
“He’d ’a’ had to work on a salary somewhere, I reckon,” was the reply. “The’ was one thing,” added David thoughtfully after a moment, “that’ll mebbe come to somethin’ some time, but it may be a good while fust, an’ don’t you ever let on to him nor nobody else ’t I ever said anythin’ about it.”
“I won’t open my head to a livin’ soul,” she declared. “What was it?”
“Wa’al, I don’t know ’s I ever told ye,” he said, “but a good many years ago I took some little hand in the oil bus’nis, but though I didn’t git in as deep as I wish now ’t I had, I’ve alwus kept up a kind of int’rist in what goes on in that line.”
“No, I guess you never told me,” she said. “Where you goin’?” as he got out of his chair.
“Goin’ to git my cap,” he answered. “Dum the dum things! I don’t believe the’s a fly in Freeland County that hain’t danced the wild kachuky on my head sence we set here. Be I much specked?” he asked, as he bent his bald poll for her inspection.
“Oh, go ’long!” she cried, as she gave him a laughing push.
“ ’Mongst other-things,” he resumed, when he had returned to his chair and relighted his cigar, “the’ was a piece of about ten or twelve hunderd acres of land down in Pennsylvany havin’ some coal on it, he told me he understood, but all the timber, ten inch an’ over, ’d ben sold off. He told me that his father’s head clerk told him that the old gentleman had tried fer a long time to dispose of it; but it called fer too much to develop it, I guess; ’t any rate he couldn’t, an’ John’s got it to pay taxes on.”
“I shouldn’t think it was wuth anythin’ to him but jest a bill of expense,” observed Mrs. Bixbee.
“Tain’t now,” said David, “an’ mebbe won’t be fer a good while; still, it’s wuth somethin’, an’ I advised him to hold onto it on gen’ral princ’ples. I don’t know the pertic’ler prop’ty, of course,” he continued, “but I do know somethin’ of that section of country, fer I done a little prospectin’ ’round there myself once on a time. But it wa’n’t in the oil territory them days, or wa’n’t known to be, anyway.”
“But it’s eatin’ itself up with taxes, ain’t it?” objected Mrs. Bixbee.

===Chinese===

「还是你不愿叫我知道？他父亲就没留下点什么？」
「有一点钱，」她兄弟答道，喷出一口烟，「还有一大堆指望不上的机会，可没有能赖以为生的。」
「他就非得上这儿来，没有第二条路？」她问。
「依我看，他反正得在什么地方挣份薪水干活，」对方答。
「倒有一样东西，」大卫过了一刻若有所思地补道，「兴许早晚值点什么，可也许得先等上好一阵；你千万别对他、也别对任何人露半个字，说我提起过它。」
「我同一个活人不张嘴，」她宣称。「是什么？」
「呃，不知我同你说过没有，」他说，「多年以前，石油那行我小小沾过一手；虽说如今后悔当年陷得不够深，那一行的动静我却始终留着几分兴味。」
「没有，我看你没说过，」她说。「你上哪儿去？」这时他从椅子里起来了。
「去拿我的帽子，」他答。「见鬼的见鬼东西！我看弗里兰全县的苍蝇，打咱们坐下起，没有一只没在我脑袋上跳过狂野的卡丘恰。我头上点子多不多？」他弯下那颗秃脑袋请她验看。
「去你的吧！」她喊，笑着推了他一把。
「这里头有一样，」他回到椅子上重新点着雪茄，接着说，「宾夕法尼亚有一片地，一千一二百英亩，底下有点煤，据他说是这么个底；十英寸以上的树木全卖掉了。他父亲的掌柜头儿告诉他，老先生想脱手想了很久；可要开发它，垫的本太大，我猜；反正他脱不成，如今轮到约翰替它缴税。」
「我看它于他一文不值，就是一张开销单，」比克斯比太太说。
「眼下不值，」大卫说，「兴许好一阵都不值；可它总有点值，我劝他照老规矩攋住不放。那宗产业具体什么样，我自然不知道，」他接着说，「可那一带地方我知道个大概，早年我自己还在那儿探过一小回。只是那时候它不算油田，反正没人知道是。」
「可它这不是拿税钱自己啿自己么？」比克斯比太太反驳。

“Wa’al,” he replied, “it’s free an’ clear, an’ the taxes ain’t so very much﻿—though they do stick it to an outside owner down there﻿—an’ the p’int is here: I’ve alwus thought they didn’t drill deep enough in that section. The’ was some little traces of oil the time I told ye of, an’ I’ve heard lately that the’s some talk of a move to test the territory agin, an’, if anythin’ was to be found, the young feller’s prop’ty might be wuth somethin’, but,” he added, “of course the’ ain’t no tellin’.”

「呃，」他答道，「它是无债一身轻，税也不是顶重——虽说那边对个外地业主是要敲一敲的——要紧的是这一条：我一向觉得，那一带钻井钻得不够深。我同你说过的那回，就见过点儿油苗；近来又听说，有人正张罗着再试试那片矿区。真找着了什么，小伙子那宗产业兴许就值了钱；不过，」他补一句，「当然，谁也说不准。」

## XXXVI / 三十六
===Original===

“Well,” said Miss Verjoos, when her sister overtook her, Mr. Euston having stopped at his own gate, “you and your latest discovery seemed to be getting on pretty well from the occasional sounds which came to my ears. What is he like?”
“He’s charming,” declared Miss Clara.
“Indeed,” remarked her sister, lifting her eyebrows. “You seem to have come to a pretty broad conclusion in a very short period of time. ‘Charming’ doesn’t leave very much to be added on longer acquaintance, does it?”
“Oh, yes it does,” said Miss Clara, laughing. “There are all degrees: Charming, very charming, most charming, and *perfectly* charming.”
“To be sure,” replied the other. “And there is the descending scale: Perfectly charming, most charming, very charming, charming, very pleasant, quite nice, and, oh, yes, well enough. Of course you have asked him to call.”
“Yes, I have,” said Miss Clara.
“Don’t you think that mamma﻿—”
“No, I don’t,” declared the girl with decision. “I know from what Mr. Euston said, and I know from the little talk I had with him this morning, from his manner and﻿—je ne sais quoi﻿—that he will be a welcome addition to a set of people in which every single one knows just what every other one will say on any given subject and on any occasion. You know how it is.”
“Well,” said the elder sister, smiling and half shutting her eyes with a musing look, “I think myself that we all know each other a little too well to make our affairs very exciting. Let us hope the new man will be all you anticipate, and,” she added with a little laugh, and a side glance at her sister, “that there will be enough of him to go ’round.”
It hardly needs to be said that the aristocracy of Homeville and all the summer visitors and residents devoted their time to getting as much pleasure and amusement out of their life as was to be afforded by the opportunities at hand: Boating, tennis, riding, driving; an occasional picnic, by invitation, at one or the other of two very pretty waterfalls, far enough away to make the drive there and back a feature; as much dancing in an informal way as could be managed by the younger people; and a certain amount of flirtation, of course (but of a very harmless sort), to supply zest to all the rest. But it is not intended to give a minute account of the life, nor to describe in detail all the pursuits and festivities which prevailed during the season.

===Chinese===

「哟，」姐姐被妹妹追上时说——尤斯顿先生已在自己家门口停下——「你和你新近发掘的这位，听着倒处得不坏，一星半点儿的声音都进了俺耳朵。他是个什么样的人？」
「他魅力十足。」克拉拉小姐宣称。
「是吗，」姐姐挑了挑眉，「这么短的工夫，你就得出这么宽的一个结论。『魅力十足』——再处下去，可添的词儿就不多了，是吧？」
「哦，多得很呢。」克拉拉小姐笑着说，「档次有的是：有魅力、很有魅力、最有魅力，还有*完美地*有魅力。」
「可不，」对方答道，「还有往下降的那一串呢：完美地有魅力、最有魅力、很有魅力、有魅力、很愉快、挺好，哦，还有，还过得去。你当然请他去府上了。」
「请了。」克拉拉小姐说。
「你不觉得妈妈——」
「不觉得。」姑娘斩钉截铁。「俺从尤斯顿先生的话里知道，也从今儿早上跟他那几句交谈里知道——从他的举止，还有——那个说不清的什么——知道他到了那样一伙人里，准受欢迎；那一伙里，人人早就把别人在任何题目、任何场合要说什么背得滚瓜烂熟。你知道那个味儿。」
「这个嘛，」姐姐笑着说，眼睛半阖，一副出神的模样，「俺自己也想，咱们彼此熟得过了头，熟得咱们的事儿都没什么看头了。但愿这位新人样样如你所料，还有，」她轻轻一笑，斜睟妹妹一眼，「但愿他一个人够大伙儿分的。」
不必多说：霍姆维尔的贵族们，连同全部避暑的游客和住户，都把工夫花在手边的机会所能挤出的欢乐上——划船，网球，骑马，驾车；偶尔应人之邀，去两处都很秀美的瀑布中的一处野餐，路远得恰到好处，来回一趟车马自成一番景致；年轻人还有多少不算正式的舞可跳；当然，还少不了一定量级的调情（不过全然无害的那种），给其余一切添点儿作料。但这本书并不打算把那种生活记账似的一一开列，也不打算细描一季里流行的全部营生与宴游。

===Original===

Enough to say that our friend soon had opportunity to partake in them as much and often as was compatible with his duties. His first call at Lakelawn happened to be on an evening when the ladies were not at home, and it is quite certain that upon this, the occasion of his first essay of the sort, he experienced a strong feeling of relief to be able to leave cards instead of meeting a number of strange people, as he had thought would be likely.
One morning, some days later, Peleg Hopkins came in with a grin and said, “The’s some folks eout in front wants you to come eout an’ see ’em.”
“Who are they?” asked John, who for the moment was in the back room and had not seen the carriage drive up.
“The two Verjoos gals,” said Peleg with another distortion of his freckled countenance. “One on ’em hailed me as I was comin’ in and ast me to ast you to come eout.” John laughed a little as he wondered what their feeling would be were they aware that they were denominated as the “Verjoos gals” by people of Peleg’s standing in the community.
“We were so sorry to miss your visit the other evening,” said Miss Clara, after the usual salutations.
John said something about the loss having been his own, and after a few remarks of no special moment the young woman proceeded to set forth her errand.
“Do you know the Bensons from Syrchester?” she asked.
John replied that he knew who they were but had not the pleasure of their acquaintance.
“Well,” said Miss Clara, “they are extremely nice people, and Mrs. Benson is very musical; in fact, Mr. Benson does something in that line himself. They have with them for a few days a violinist, Fairman I think his name is, from Boston, and a pianist﻿—what was it, Juliet?”
“Schlitz, I think,” said Miss Verjoos.
“Oh, yes, that is it, and they are coming to the house tonight, and we are going to have some music in an informal sort of way. We shall be glad to have you come if you can.”
“I shall be delighted,” said John sincerely. “At what time?”
“Any time you like,” she said; “but the Bensons will probably get there about half-past eight or nine o’clock.”
“Thank you very much, and I shall be delighted,” he repeated.
Miss Clara looked at him for a moment with a hesitating air.
“There is another thing,” she said.
“Yes?”
“Yes,” she replied, “I may as well tell you that you will surely be asked to sing.

===Chinese===

只消说：咱这位朋友很快就得了机会，在公务许可的范围内多多地、常常地参加进去。他头一回到莱克劳恩拜访，偏偏挑了个女主人们都不在家的晚上；而他这头一回实践此道，能留下名片、不必会那一屋子他原以为多半躲不开的生人，实在叫他有说不出的轻松。
几天后的一个上午，佩莱格·霍普金斯龇着牙进来：「前头有几位，要你出去见见。」
「谁呀？」约翰问；他这会儿在里屋，没看见马车赶到。
「弗茹家那两个丫头。」佩莱格说，那张麻斑脸又是一番歪扭。「俺进来的时候，其中一个招呼俺，叫俺请你出去。」约翰不由失笑，心想这两位要是知道自己在佩莱格这般身份的人嘴里是「弗茹家那两个丫头」，不知作何感想。
「那天晚上没遇上您，真叫我们遗憾。」客套过之后，克拉拉小姐说。
约翰说了几句损失在他自己一头的话；几不相干的应酬之后，姑娘直奔来意。
「您认得瑟切斯特的本森一家吗？」她问。
约翰答说知道有这么一家，无缘结识。
「是这样，」克拉拉小姐说，「他们是顶好的人家；本森太太极懂音乐，其实本森先生自己也在那一行里有点营生。他们家里这几日有位小提琴手，费尔曼，俺想是这名字，波士顿来的；还有一位钢琴师——叫什么来着，朱丽叶？」
「施利茨，我想是。」弗茹小姐说。
「哦，对，就是这个。他们今晚要来舍下，我们要不拘形式地弄点儿音乐。您要是得空，我们很盼您来。」
「我很乐意，」约翰诚恳地说，「几点？」
「随您什么时候都行，」她说，「不过本森一家大概八点半或九点到。」
「多谢，我一定来。」他又说了一遍。
克拉拉小姐迟疑地望了他片刻。
「还有一件事。」她说。
「嗯？」
「是，」她答，「俺不妨直说，您准会被请唱歌。

===Original===

Quite a good many people who have heard you in the quartet in church are anxious to hear you sing alone, Mrs. Benson among them.”
John’s face fell a little.
“You do sing other than church music, do you not?” she asked.
“Yes,” he admitted, “I know some other music.”
“Do you think it would be a bore to you.”
“No,” said John, who indeed saw no way out of it; “I will bring some music, with pleasure, if you wish.”
“That’s very nice of you,” said Miss Clara, “and you will give us all a great deal of pleasure.”
He looked at her with a smile.
“That will depend,” he said, and after a moment, “Who will play for me?”
“I had not thought of that,” was the reply. “I think I rather took it for granted that you could play for yourself. Can’t you?”
“After a fashion, and simple things,” he said, “but on an occasion I would rather not attempt it.”
The girl looked at her sister in some perplexity.
“I should think,” suggested Miss Verjoos, speaking for the second time, “that Mr. or Herr Schlitz would play your accompaniments, particularly if Mrs. Benson were to ask him, and if he can play for the violin I should fancy he can for the voice.”
“Very well,” said John, “we will let it go at that.” As he spoke David came round the corner of the bank and up to the carriage.
“How d’y’ do, Miss Verjoos? How air ye, Miss Claricy?” he asked, taking off his straw hat and mopping his face and head with his handkerchief. “Guess we’re goin’ to lose our sleighin’, ain’t we?”
“It seems to be going pretty fast,” replied Miss Clara, laughing.
“Yes’m,” he remarked, “we sh’ll be scrapin’ bare ground putty soon now if this weather holds on. How’s the old hoss now you got him agin?” he asked. “Seem to ’ve wintered putty well? Putty chipper, is he?”
“Better than ever,” she affirmed. “He seems to grow younger every year.”
“Come, now,” said David, “that ain’t a-goin’ to do. I cal’lated to sell ye another hoss *this* summer anyway. Ben dependin’ on’t in fact, to pay a dividend. The bankin’ bus’nis has been so neglected since this feller come that it don’t amount to much any more,” and he laid his hand on John’s shoulder, who colored a little as he caught a look of demure amusement in the somber eyes of the elder sister.
“After that,” he said, “I think I had better get back to my neglected duties,” and he bowed his adieus.

===Chinese===

教堂四重唱里听过您的好些人，都巴望听您独唱，本森太太就是其中一位。」

「凑合能，简单的行，」他说，「可这种场合，我宁愿不试。」
姑娘有些为难地望向姐姐。
「俺想，」弗茹小姐第二次开腔，「施利茨先生会替您伴奏的，尤其要是本森太太开口请他；他既然能给小提琴伴奏，俺想给嗓子伴奏也不在话下。」
「好极了，」约翰说，「就这么定了。」话音未落，大卫从银行拐角绕出来，走到马车跟前。
「你好哇，弗茹小姐？你好，克拉丽西小姐？」他摘下草帽，拿手绢抹着脸和头顶。「俺们那点雪橇道怕是保不住了，是不是？」
「化得是快。」克拉拉小姐笑着答。
「是呐，」他说，「这天气再这么撑着，俺们很快就得刮光地皮了。你那匹老马回到手里怎么样？」他问，「过冬过得还体面？还精神？」
「比哪年都好，」她一口咬定，「他瞧着一年比一年年轻。」
「喎，快别这样，」大卫说，「俺本来就盘算好*今*年夏天再卖你一匹的。实话跟你交代，俺还指着这个开股息呢。自打这小子来了，银行这摊生意没人搭理，眼看就不成个买卖了。」他一只手搭上约翰的肩膀；约翰撞见大小姐那双幽深的眼睛里一丝端庄的笑意，脸微微一红。
「既然如此，」他说，「我看我还是回我那摊没人搭理的公务去吧。」他鞠躬道了别。

===Original===

“No, sir,” said Miss Clara to David, “you must get your dividend out of someone else this summer.”
“Wa’al,” said he, “I see I made a mistake takin’ such good care on him. Guess I’ll have to turn him over to Dug Robinson to winter next year. Ben havin’ a little visit with John?” he asked. Miss Clara colored a little, with something of the same look which John had seen in her sister’s face.
“We are going to have some music at the house tonight, and Mr. Lenox has kindly promised to sing for us,” she replied.
“He has, has he?” said David, full of interest. “Wa’al, he’s the feller c’n do it if anybody can. We have singin’ an’ music up t’ the house ev’ry Sunday night﻿—me an’ Polly an’ him﻿—an’ it’s fine. Yes, ma’am, I don’t know much about music myself, but I c’n beat time, an’ he’s got a stack o’ music more’n a mile high, an’ one o’ the songs he sings’ll jest make the windows rattle. That’s my fav’rit,” averred Mr. Harum.
“Do you remember the name of it?” asked Miss Clara.
“No,” he said; “John told me, an’ I guess I’d know it if I heard it; but it’s about a feller sittin’ one day by the org’n an’ not feelin’ exac’ly right﻿—kind o’ tired an’ out o’ sorts an’ not knowin’ jes’ where he was drivin’ at﻿—jes’ joggin’ ’long with a loose rein fer quite a piece, an’ so on; an’ then, by an’ by, strikin’ right into his gait an’ goin’ on stronger ’n stronger, an’ fin’ly finishin’ up with an A﻿—men that carries him quarter way round the track ’fore he c’n pull up. That’s my fav’rit,” Mr. Harum repeated, “ ’cept when him an’ Polly sings together, an’ if that ain’t a show﻿—pertic’lerly Polly﻿—I don’t want a cent. No, ma’am, when him an’ Polly gits good an’ goin’ you can’t see ’em fer dust.”
“I should like to hear them,” said Miss Clara, laughing, “and I should particularly like to hear your favorite, the one which ends with the Amen﻿—the very *large* A﻿—men.”
“Seventeen hands,” declared Mr. Harum. “Must you be goin’? Wa’al, glad to have seen ye. Polly’s hopin’ you’ll come an’ see her putty soon.”
“I will,” she promised. “Give her my love, and tell her so, please.”
They drove away and David sauntered in, went behind the desks, and perched himself up on a stool near the teller’s counter as he often did when in the office, and John was not particularly engaged.
“Got you roped in, have they?” he said, using his hat as a fan. “Scat my —﻿—﻿—! but ain’t this a ringtail squealer?”

===Chinese===

「不行，先生，」克拉拉小姐对大卫说，「今年夏天，您的股息得从别人身上开出来。」
「这个嘛，」他说，「俺明白了，把他保养得太好是俺的失算。明年俺得把他交给杜格·罗宾逊去过冬。方才跟约翰小叙来着？」他问。克拉拉小姐微微一红，神情跟约翰在她姐姐脸上见过的那副有几分像。
「我们今晚要在家弄点儿音乐，莱诺克斯先生好心答应唱一个。」她答。
「是吗，他答应啦？」大卫说，兴味十足。「这个嘛，要说有人唱得了，就是这小子。俺们家如今每礼拜天晚上都唱都奏——俺、波莉、他——好得很。是，太太，俺自个儿不大懂音乐，可俺会打拍子；他的乐谱堆得比一里高的烟囱还高，他唱的歌里有一支，唱起来窗玻璃都哗哗响。那是俺的心头好。」哈勒姆先生一口担保。
「歌名您记得吗？」克拉拉小姐问。
「记不得，」他说，「约翰跟俺说过，俺想俺一听就能认出来；说的是一个小子，有一天坐在风琴跟前，浑身不大得劲——有点儿乏，有点儿蔫，也说不清自己到底要往哪儿去——就像松着缰绳溜溜达达遛了好一程，这么一路的；跟着，不知不觉铆上自个儿的步点，越走越有劲，末了一个『阿——门』收尾，那一嗓子驮着他沿跑道又冲出四分之一里才勒得住。那是俺的心头好，」哈勒姆先生重申，「只除了他跟波莉合唱的时候；那个要还不算一出好戏——尤其是波莉——俺一个子儿也不要。是，太太，他俩正经跑起来，你隔着尘土都瞧不见人影。」
「俺想听听他们，」克拉拉小姐笑着说，「尤其想听听您那支心头好，那支拿阿门收尾的——顶*大*的那个阿——门。」
「十七掌高的。」哈勒姆先生宣称。「这就得走了？这个嘛，见着你们真高兴。波莉盼你早点儿去看她呢。」
「俺一定去，」她应承，「替俺问候她，就这么跟她说，劳驾。」
马车走了，大卫溜溜达达进了银行，绕到桌子后头，照他没事时常有的那样，在出纳柜台边的一张高凳上蹲下来。
「把你套进来了，是不是？」他拿帽子扇着风。「哎呀俺的——！这还不算一场顶刮刮的热闹么？」

===Original===

“It is very hot,” responded John.
“Miss Claricy says you’re goin’ to sing fer ’em up to their house tonight.”
“Yes,” said John, with a slight shrug of the shoulders, as he pinned a paper strap around a pile of bills and began to count out another.
“Don’t feel very fierce for it, I guess, do ye?” said David, looking shrewdly at him.
“Not very,” said John, with a short laugh.
“Feel a little skittish ’bout it, eh?” suggested Mr. Harum. “Don’t see why ye should﻿—anybody that c’n put up a tune the way you kin.”
“It’s rather different,” observed the younger man, “singing for you and Mrs. Bixbee and standing up before a lot of strange people.”
“H-m, h-m,” said David with a nod; “diff’rence ’tween joggin’ along on the road an’ drivin’ a fust heat on the track; in one case the’ ain’t nothin’ up, an’ ye don’t care whether you git there a little more previously or a little less; an’ in the other the’s the crowd, an’ the judges, an’ the stake, an’ your record, an’ mebbe the pool box into the barg’in, that’s all got to be considered. Feller don’t mind it so much after he gits fairly off, but thinkin’ on’t beforehand ’s fidgity bus’nis.”
“You have illustrated it exactly,” said John, laughing, and much amused at David’s very characteristic, as well as accurate, illustration.
---
“My!” exclaimed Aunt Polly, when John came into the sitting room after dinner dressed to go out. “My, don’t he look nice? I never see you in them clo’es. Come here a minute,” and she picked a thread off his sleeve and took the opportunity to turn him round for the purpose of giving him a thorough inspection.
“That wa’n’t what you said when you see me in *my* gold-plated harniss,” remarked David, with a grin. “You didn’t say nothin’ putty to me.”
“Humph! I guess the’s some diff’rence,” observed Mrs. Bixbee with scorn, and her brother laughed.
“How was you cal’latin’ to git there?” he asked, looking at our friend’s evening shoes.
“I thought at first I would walk,” was the reply, “but I rather think I will stop at Robinson’s and get him to send me over.”
“I guess you won’t do nothin’ o’ the sort,” declared David. “Tom’s all hitched to take you over, an’ when you’re ready jes’ ring the bell.”
“You’re awfully kind,” said John gratefully, “but I don’t know when I shall be coming home.”
“Come back when you git a good ready,” said Mr. Harum.

===Chinese===

「天真热。」约翰应道。
「克拉丽西小姐说，今晚你要上他们家唱歌。」
「是。」约翰说着，微微一耸肩，一边拿纸带把一搭钞票扎起，一边点起另一搭。
「心里不怎么来劲，俺猜，嗯？」大卫精明地望着他说。
「不怎么来劲。」约翰短短一笑。
「有点儿发怦，啊？」哈勒姆先生提点他，「俺看犯不上——能把调门立起来立得像你那样的。」
「这不一样，」年轻人说，「唱给你和比克斯比太太听是一回事，站到一屋子生人跟前是另一回事。」
「嗯，嗯，」大卫点头，「就好比道上信步溜达，跟在赛道上跑头一档正式比赛的分别：前一种，什么都不押着，早到一步晚到一步全不相干；后一种呢，有看台，有裁判，有彩头，有你的纪录，兴许还饶上赌注匣，哪一样都得掂量。起跑顺了，后头倒不怎么怦；就是临场前那一阵想，最是叫人坐立不安。」
「您比得再准没有了。」约翰笑着说；大卫这比喻又本色又地道，把他逗得不轻。
---
「哎呀！」约翰晚饭后衣冠齐整地走进起居室，波莉姑妈叫起来，「哎呀，瞧他这模样多齐整！这身穿戴俺可头一回见。过来站一站。」她从他袖子上拈下一根线头，顺势把他转了个圈，上上下下好一番检阅。
「俺穿俺那套『镀金马具』那回，你可不是这话，」大卫咧嘴说，「你可没说俺一句囫囵好话。」
「哼！俺看是有点儿分别。」比克斯比太太鄙夷地说，她兄弟哈哈一笑。
「你盘算怎么过去？」他望着咱这位朋友的晚礼鞋问。
「我本想走着去，」是回答，「不过我想顺路在罗宾逊家停一停，请他把我送过去。」
「俺看你还是省了这份心，」大卫宣布，「汤姆早套好了，专等你过去；你一预备停当，拉铃就是。」
「您们太好了，」约翰感激地说，「可我说不准什么时候回来。」
「你什么时候尽兴什么时候回。」哈勒姆先生说。

“If you keep him an’ the hoss waitin’ a spell, I guess they won’t take cold this weather.”

===Chinese===

「你就是叫他跟马多等上一阵，这样的天气，俺想他们也不会着凉。」

## XXXVII / 三十七
===Original===

The Verjoos house, of old red brick, stands about a hundred feet back from the north side of the Lake Road, on the south shore of the lake. Since its original construction a porte-cochère has been built upon the front. A very broad hall, from which rises the stairway with a double turn and landing, divides the main body of the house through the middle. On the left, as one enters, is the great drawing room; on the right a parlor opening into a library; and beyond, the dining room, which looks out over the lake. The hall opens in the rear upon a broad, covered veranda, facing the lake, with a flight of steps to a lawn which slopes down to the lake shore, a distance of some hundred and fifty yards.
John had to pass through a little flock of young people who stood near and about the entrance to the drawing room, and having given his package of music to the maid in waiting, with a request that it be put upon the piano, he mounted the stairs to deposit his hat and coat, and then went down.
In the south end of the drawing room were some twenty people sitting and standing about, most of them the elders of the families who constituted society in Homeville, many of whom John had met, and nearly all of whom he knew by sight and name. On the edge of the group, and halfway down the room, were Mrs. Verjoos and her younger daughter, who gave him a cordial greeting; and the elder lady was kind enough to repeat her daughter’s morning assurances of regret that they were out on the occasion of his call.
“I trust you have been as good as your word,” said Miss Clara, “and brought some music.”
“Yes, it is on the piano,” he replied, looking across the room to where the instrument stood.
The girl laughed. “I wish,” she said, “you could have heard what Mr. Harum said this morning about your singing, particularly his description of ‘The Lost Chord,’ and I wish that I could repeat it just as he gave it.”

===Chinese===

弗茹家的宅子是老红砖砌的，坐落在湖滨路北侧——湖的南岸——约莫一百英尺深的地方。初建之后，正面前头又添了一座车廊。一道极宽的门厅把宅子正身从当中分开，楼梯就从门厅里拔起，两折一台。进门左手是大客厅；右手一间起居室，通书房；再往里是餐厅，窗外正望湖面。门厅后头通一道宽宽的有顶游廊，面湖；一段石阶下去是一片草坪，缓缓斜向湖岸，总共约一百五十码。
约翰得从聚在大客厅门口一带的一小群年轻人中间穿过去；他把乐谱卷交给侍立的女仆，托她擞到钢琴上，先上楼安顿了帽子外衣，再下来。
大客厅南头坐着站着约有二十位，多半是构成霍姆维尔上流社会的那几家的长辈；其中许多约翰见过，几乎全部他都识面知名。在这群人的边上、屋子中腰，是弗茹太太和她的小女儿；母女俩热诚地招呼他；老太太还客客气气地把女儿早上那番「那天您来正好不在、真是抱歉」的话重叙了一遍。
「我相信你说到做到，」克拉拉小姐说，「把乐谱带来了。」
「带了，在钢琴上了，」他答，目光越过屋子落在那架琴上。
姑娘笑了。「真希望你，」她说，「亲耳听听哈勒姆先生今儿早上怎么议论你唱的歌，尤其是他那段对《失落的和音》的描摹；更希望我能一字不差地学给你听。」

===Original===

“It’s about a feller sittin’ one day by the org’n,” came a voice from behind John’s shoulder, so like David’s as fairly to startle him, “an’ not feelin’ exac’ly right﻿—kind o’ tired an’ out o’ sorts, an’ not knowin’ jes’ where he was drivin’ at﻿—jes’ joggin’ along with a loose rein fer quite a piece, an’ so on; an’ then, by an’ by, strikin’ right into his gait an’ goin’ on stronger an’ stronger, an’ fin’ly finishin’ up with an A﻿—men that carries him quarter way ’round the track ’fore he c’n pull up.” They all laughed except Miss Verjoos, whose gravity was unbroken, save that behind the dusky windows of her eyes, as she looked at John, there was for an instant a gleam of mischievous drollery.
“Good evening, Mr. Lenox,” she said. “I am very glad to see you,” and hardly waiting for his response, she turned and walked away.
“That is Juliet all over,” said her sister. “You would not think to see her ordinarily that she was given to that sort of thing, but once in a while, when she feels like it﻿—well﻿—pranks! She is the funniest creature that ever lived, I believe, and can mimic and imitate any mortal creature. She sat in the carriage this morning, and one might have fancied from her expression that she hardly heard a word, but I haven’t a doubt that she could repeat every syllable that was uttered. Oh, here come the Bensons and their musicians.”
John stepped back a pace or two toward the end of the room, but was presently recalled and presented to the newcomers. After a little talk the Bensons settled themselves in the corner at the lower end of the room, where seats were placed for the two musicians, and our friend took a seat near where he had been standing. The violinist adjusted his folding music rest. Miss Clara stepped over to the entrance door and put up her finger at the young people in the hall. “After the music begins,” she said, with a shake of the head, “if I hear one sound of giggling or chattering, I will send every one of you young heathen home. Remember now! This isn’t your party at all.”
“But, Clara, dear,” said Sue Tenaker (aged fifteen), “if we are very good and quiet do you think they would play for us to dance a little by and by?”
“Impudence!” exclaimed Miss Clara, giving the girl’s cheek a playful slap and going back to her place. Miss Verjoos came in and took a chair by her sister. Mrs.

===Chinese===

「说的是一个人有一天坐在管风琴跟前，」约翰肩后响起一个嗓音，像大卫像得叫人吓一跳，「身子不大对劲——有点儿乏，有点儿烦，自己也不知道要往哪儿去——松着缰绳溜溜达达走了老大一程，如此这般；随后呢，渐渐上了步子，一步紧似一步，末了用一声阿——门收尾，那一声带着他绕着跑道跑过四分之一圈，才收得住。」众人都笑了，唯独弗茹小姐的庄重纹丝不动；只是她望着约翰时，那两扇幽暗的窗后头，闪过一瞬淘气的狡黠。
「晚上好，伦诺克斯先生，」她说。「见到你很高兴。」话音未落，不等他答腔，她转身走开了。
「朱丽叶就是这么个人，」她姐姐说。平日见她，你想不到她会来这一手；可偶尔兴头一来——嗐——全是恶作剧！我相信天底下再没有比她更滑稽的人了，什么活物她都学得惟妙惟肖。今儿早上她坐在马车里，那神情看着像是半个字也没听进；可她说得出方才每一个音节，这一点我毫不怀疑。哦，本森家的和他们请的乐师来了。」
约翰朝屋子那头退开一两步，随即又被叫住，引见给新来的几位。寒暄了几句，本森夫妇在屋子那头的角落里落座，那里给两位乐师安了座位；我们这位朋友在方才站着的地方近旁坐下了。小提琴手支起他的折叠谱架。克拉拉小姐踱到门口，朝门厅里的年轻人竖起一根手指。「音乐一开始，」她说，摇着头，「只要叫我听见一声嗤笑、一句叽喳，我就把你们这帮小异教徒一个不落全轰回家。记住了！这晚会压根儿不是给你们开的。」
「可是，克拉拉，亲爱的，」休·特纳克说（芳龄十五），「要是我们乖乖的、安安静静的，你说待会儿他们肯为我们奏一曲，让我们跳一会儿舞么？」
「放肆！」克拉拉小姐喊道，玩笑地拍了一下姑娘的脸蛋，回到自己座位。弗茹小姐进来，在她姐姐身边坐下。本森太太

===Original===

Benson leaned forward and raised her eyebrows at Miss Clara, who took a quick survey of the room and nodded in return. Herr Schlitz seated himself on the piano chair, pushed it a little back, drew it a little forward to the original place, looked under the piano at the pedals, took out his handkerchief and wiped his face and hands, and after arpeggioing up and down the keyboard, swung into a waltz of Chopin’s (“Opus 34, Number 1”), a favorite of our friend’s, and which he would have thoroughly enjoyed﻿—for it was splendidly played﻿—if he had not been uneasily apprehensive that he might be asked to sing after it. And while on some accounts he would have been glad of the opportunity to “have it over,” he felt a cowardly sense of relief when the violinist came forward for the next number. There had been enthusiastic applause at the north end of the room, and more or less clapping of hands at the south end, but not enough to impel the pianist to supplement his performance at the time. The violin number was so well received that Mr. Fairman added a little minuet of Boccherini’s without accompaniment, and then John felt that his time had surely come. But he had to sit, drawing long breaths, through a Liszt fantasie on themes from Faust before his suspense was ended by Miss Clara, who was apparently mistress of ceremonies and who said to him, “Will you sing now, Mr. Lenox?”
He rose and went to the end of the room where the pianist was sitting. “I have been asked to sing,” he said to that gentleman. “Can I induce you to be so kind as to play for me?”
“I am sure he will,” said Mrs. Benson, looking at Herr Schlitz.
“Oh, yes, I blay for you if you vant,” he said. “Vhere is your moosic?” They went over to the piano. “Oh, ho! Jensen, Lassen, Helmund, Grieg﻿—you zing dem?”
“Some of them,” said John. The pianist opened the Jensen album.
“You want to zing one of dese?” he asked.
“As well as anything,” replied John, who had changed his mind a dozen times in the last ten minutes and was ready to accept any suggestion.
“Ver’ goot,” said the other. “Ve dry dis: ‘Lehn deine wang’ an meine Wang’ ’.” His face brightened as John began to sing the German words. In a measure or two the singer and player were in perfect accord, and as the former found his voice the ends of his fingers grew warm again.

===Chinese===

朝前欠身，朝克拉拉小姐扬了扬眉；后者把屋子飞快扫了一圈，点头回礼。施利茨先生在琴凳上坐下，把凳子往后挪了挪，又往前拉回原处，弯腰看了看琴底下踏板，掏出手帕擦了脸和手，然后在键盘上上下下刮了一遍琶音，就势荡进了一支肖邦的圆舞曲（「作品三十四之一」）——那是我们这位朋友的至爱，他也本可以彻底陶醉一番——因为弹得实在漂亮——若不是心里七上八下，怕的是弹完之后要轮到他唱。虽说从某些方面讲，他巴不得有机会「把它了掉」，可轮到小提琴手上前奏下一曲时，他还是生出一种怡懦的如释重负。屋子北头响起热烈的掌声，南头也多少拍了几下手，只是还不足以催动钢琴家当场加演。小提琴那一曲大受欢迎，费尔曼先生又无伴奏加了一小段博凯里尼的小步舞曲；这时约翰觉得，他的时刻真真切切到了。可他还得深一口浅一口地坐着，挨完一首李斯特根据《浮士德》主题写的幻想曲；他的煎熬才由克拉拉小姐来终结——她显然是今晚的司仪——她对他说：「现在请你唱，好么，伦诺克斯先生？」
他起身走到钢琴家坐着的那头。「有人点我唱，」他对那位先生说。「能不能劳您大驾，替我伴奏？」
「他一定肯的，」本森太太望着施利茨先生说。
「哦，肯，你要唱我就弹，」他说。「你的乐谱在哪里？」两人走到钢琴边。「哦嗬！延森、拉森、赫尔蒙德、格里格——这些你都唱？」
「唱过一些，」约翰说。钢琴家翻开延森的曲集。
「你想唱这里头的一首？」他问。
「哪首都行，」约翰答；这十分钟里他已经改了十几回主意，什么提议都肯接受了。
「很好，」对方说。「我们试试这首：《把你的脸颊贴上我的脸颊》。」约翰开口唱德文歌词，他的脸亮了起来。一两小节之后，唱的弹的已完全合拍；唱的人嗓子一顺，指尖的暖意也回来了。

===Original===

At the end of the song the applause was distributed about as after the Chopin waltz.
“*Sehr schön!*” exclaimed Herr Schlitz, looking up and nodding; “you must zing zome more,” and he played the first bars of “Marie, am Fenster sitzest du,” humming the words under his breath, and quite oblivious of anyone but himself and the singer.
“*Zierlich*,” he said when the song was done, reaching for the collection of Lassen. “Mit deinen blauen Augen,” he hummed, keeping time with his hands, but at this point Miss Clara came across the room, followed by her sister.
“Mrs. Tenaker,” she said, laughing, “asked me to ask you, Mr. Lenox, if you wouldn’t please sing something they could understand.”
“I have a song I should like to hear you sing,” said Miss Verjoos. “There is an obligato for violin and we have a violinist here. It is a beautiful song﻿—‘Tosti’s Beauty’s Eyes.’ Do you know it?”
“Yes,” he replied.
“Will you sing it for me?” she asked.
“With the greatest pleasure,” he answered.
Once, as he sang the lines of the song, he looked up. Miss Verjoos was sitting with her elbows on the arm of her chair, her cheek resting upon her clasped hands and her dusky eyes were fastened upon his face. As the song concluded she rose and walked away. Mrs. Tenaker came over to the piano and put out her hand.
“Thank you so much for your singing, Mr. Lenox,” she said. “Would you like to do an old woman a favor?”
“Very much so,” said John, smiling and looking first at Mrs. Tenaker and then about the room, “but there are no old women here as far as I can see.”
“Very pretty, sir, very pretty,” she said, looking very graciously at him. “Will you sing ‘Annie Laurie’ for me?”
“With all my heart,” he said, bowing. He looked at Herr Schlitz, who shook his head.
“Let me play it for you,” said Mrs. Benson, coming over to the piano.
“Where do you want it?” she asked, modulating softly from one key to another.
“I think D flat will be about right,” he replied. “Kindly play a little bit of it.”
The sound of the symphony brought most of even the young people into the drawing room. At the end of the first verse there was a subdued rustle of applause, a little more after the second, and at the end of the song so much of a burst of approval as could be produced by the audience. Mrs. Benson looked up into John’s face and smiled.

===Chinese===

一曲唱罢，掌声的分布，同肖邦圆舞曲之后差不多。
「*美极了*（Sehr schön）！」施利茨先生抬头点着脑袋喊道；「你一定得再唱几首，」说着他弹起《玛丽，你坐在窗前》的头几小节，嗓子底下哼着词，眼里除了自己同唱歌的人，再没有别人。
「*雅致*（Zierlich），」这首唱完他说，伸手去够拉森的曲集。《用你蓝色的眼睛》，他哼着，两只手打着拍子；可就在这时，克拉拉小姐穿过屋子走来，她姐姐跟着。
「特纳克太太，」她笑着说，「托我问问您，伦诺克斯先生，能不能请您唱点儿他们听得懂的。」
「有一支歌，我很想听你唱，」弗茹小姐说。「带一段小提琴助奏，咱们这儿正好有一位小提琴手。那是一支很美的歌——托斯蒂的《美人之眼》。你会么？」
「会，」他答。
「为我唱它，好么？」她问。
「不胜荣幸，」他答。
唱到中途有一刻，他抬起了眼。弗茹小姐坐着，臂肘支在扶手上，面颊倚着交握的双手，那双幽暗的眼睛定在他的脸上。歌唱完，她起身走开了。特纳克太太踱到钢琴边，伸出手来。
「多谢你的歌，伦诺克斯先生，」她说。「愿意帮老太太一个忙么？」
「非常愿意，」约翰笑着说，先看看特纳克太太，又望望满屋，「只是就我所见，这儿一位老太太也没有。」
「嘴真甜，先生，真甜，」她一脸和气地望着他说。「肯为我唱一支《安妮·劳丽》么？」
「全心全意，」他欠身说。他望向施利茨先生，那位摇了摇头。
「我来替你弹，」本森太太说着走到钢琴边。
「你要什么调？」她问，手指从一个调轻柔地滑向另一个调。
「我想降 D 大约正好，」他答。「麻烦先过一小段。」
前奏一起，连年轻人也大半涌进了客厅。第一段唱完，一阵压低了的掌声窸窣；第二段之后多了一点；到全曲终了，满座能拿出多少喝彩，就拿出多少。本森太太抬眼望着约翰的脸，笑了。

===Original===

“We appear to have scored the success of the evening,” she said with a touch of sarcasm. Miss Clara joined them.
“What a dear old song that is!” she said. “Did you see Aunt Charlie (Mrs. Tenaker) wiping her eyes?﻿—and that lovely thing of Tosti’s! We are ever so much obliged to you, Mr. Lenox.”
John bowed his acknowledgments.
“Will you take Mrs. Benson out to supper? There is a special table for you musical people at the east end of the veranda.”
“Is this merely a segregation or a distinction?” said John as they sat down.
“We shall have to wait developments to decide that point, I should say,” replied Mrs. Benson. “I suppose that fifth place was put on the off chance that Mr. Benson might be of our party, but,” she said, with a short laugh, “he is probably nine fathoms deep in a flirtation with Sue Tenaker. He shares Artemas Ward’s tastes, who said, you may remember, that he liked little girls﻿—big ones too.”
A maid appeared with a tray of eatables, and presently another with a tray on which were glasses and a bottle of Pommery Sec. “Miss Clara’s compliments,” she said.
“What do you think now?” asked Mrs. Benson, laughing.
“Distinctly a distinction, I should say,” he replied.
“*Das ist nicht so schlecht*,” grunted Herr Schlitz as he put half a pâté into his mouth, “bot I vould brefer beer.”
“The music has been a great treat to me,” remarked John. “I have heard nothing of the sort for two years.”
“You have quite contributed your share of the entertainment,” said Mrs. Benson.
“You and I together,” he responded, smiling.
“You have got a be-oodifool woice,” said Herr Schlitz, speaking with a mouthful of salad, “und you zing ligh a moosician, und you bronounce your vorts very goot.”
“Thank you,” said John.
After supper there was more singing in the drawing room, but it was not of a very classical order. Something short and taking for violin and piano was followed by an announcement from Herr Schlitz.
“I zing you a zong,” he said. The worthy man “breferred beer,” but had, perhaps, found the wine quicker in effect, and in a tremendous bass voice he roared out, “Im tiefen Keller sitz’ ich hier, auf einem Fass voll Reben,” which, if not wholly understood by the audience, had some of its purport conveyed by the threefold repetition of “*trinke*” at the end of each verse.

===Chinese===

「看来今夜的成功叫我们挣下了，」她略带讥诮地说。克拉拉小姐过来了。
「多好的一支老歌呀！」她说。「你见着查理婶婶（特纳克太太）擦眼睛没有？——还有托斯蒂那支可爱的曲子！真是多谢你了，伦诺克斯先生。」
约翰欠身致谢。
「请护送本森太太去用晚餐好吗？游廊东头给你们乐师们设了一桌专席。」
「这算是隔离呢，还是优待？」落座后约翰说。
「这一条嘛，依我看得等事态发展再作定论，」本森太太答。「我猜那第五个位子是抱着万一的心思摆的，万一本森先生也算我们一伙；可是，」她短促一笑，「他这会儿多半正同休·特纳克调情，一猛子扎下去九英寻深。他的口味同阿蒂默斯·沃德一样——你总记得沃德说过，他喜欢小姑娘——大姑娘也喜欢。」
一个女仆端着一托盘吃食来了，随后又一个端着杯子和一瓶波美里干香槟。「克拉拉小姐敬上的，」她说。
「现在你怎么说？」本森太太笑着问。
「分明是优待了，」他答。
「*这不算差*（Das ist nicht so schlecht），」施利茨先生把半个肉酱饼塞进嘴里，咕哝道，「不过我还是更爱啤酒。」
「今晚的音乐于我是一顿大飨，」约翰说。「这一路的东西，我有两年没听过了。」
「余兴里你那份也出得足足的，」本森太太说。
「你我合算的，」他微笑着应道。
「你有一副漂——亮滴嗓音，」施利茨先生满嘴沙拉地说，「你唱起来像个音乐家，你滴字咬得也非趁。」
「谢谢，」约翰说。
晚饭之后，客厅里又有歌唱，格调却不怎么古典。小提琴钢琴合奏了一支短小上口的曲子之后，施利茨先生宣布了一件事。「我给你们唱一支歌，」他说。这位可敬的人「更爱啤酒」，只是兴许发觉葡萄酒上头更快；他放开一副惊天动地的男低音吼道：「我坐在这深深的酒窖里，坐在一桶葡萄美酒上」——听众纵然不全听得懂，可每段末尾那三声「*喝呀*（trinke）」，总把大意送到家了。

===Original===

Then a deputation waited upon John, to ask in behalf of the girls and boys if he knew and could sing “Solomon Levi.”
“Yes,” he said, sitting down at the piano, “if you’ll all sing with me,” and it came to pass that that classic, followed by “Bring Back My Bonnie to Me,” “Paddy Duffy’s Cart,” “There’s Music in the Air,” and sundry other ditties dear to all hearts, was given by “the full strength of the company” with such enthusiasm that even Mr. Fairman was moved to join in with his violin; and when the “Soldier’s Farewell” was given, Herr Schlitz would have sung the windows out of their frames had they not been open. Altogether, the evening’s programme was brought to an end with a grand climax.
“Thank you very much,” said John as he said good night to Mrs. Verjoos. “I don’t know when I have enjoyed an evening so much.”
“Thank *you* very much,” she returned graciously. “You have given us all a great deal of pleasure.”
“Yes,” said Miss Verjoos, giving her hand with a mischievous gleam in her half-shut eyes, “I was enchanted with ‘Solomon Levi.’ ”

===Chinese===

随后一个代表团晋见约翰，代表姑娘小伙们请问，他识不识得、会不会唱《所罗门·利维》。
「会，」他说着在钢琴前坐下，「只要你们都跟我一块儿唱。」于是那支经典，连同《把我的邦妮带回我身边》《帕迪·达菲的马车》《空气里有音乐》，以及众所心爱的其他若干小曲，由「全班人马」以如此的热情轮番献演，连费尔曼先生也感动得操起了小提琴加入合唱；到唱《士兵的告别》时，若不是窗子本来开着，施利茨先生真能把窗框唱出去。总而言之，晚会的节目在一片壮丽的高潮中收了场。
「多谢您，」约翰向弗茹太太道晚安时说。「我都不记得几时这么尽兴过一晚上。」
「该*我们*多谢你，」她雍容地答道。「你带给了我们大家许多快乐。」
「是啊，」弗茹小姐伸出手来，半阖的眼里闪着狡黠，「《所罗门·利维》把我迷住了。」

## XXXVIII / 三十八
===Original===

David and John had been driving for some time in silence. The elder man was apparently musing upon something which had been suggested to his mind. The horses slackened their gait to a walk as they began the ascent of a long hill. Presently the silence was broken by a sound which caused John to turn his head with a look of surprised amusement﻿—Mr. Harum was singing. The tune, if it could be so called, was scaleless, and these were the words:

> “*Mon*day *mor*nin’ I *mar*ried me a *wife*,

*Think*in’ to *lead* a *more* contented *life*;

*Fid*dlin’ an’ *danc*in’ *the*’ was *played*,

To *see* how un*happy* poor *I* was *made*.
> “*Tues*day *morn*in’, *‘bout* break o’ *day*,

*While* my *head* on the *pil*ler did *lay*,

She *tuned* up her *clack*, an’ *scold*ed *more*

*Than* I *ever* heard be*fore*.”

===Chinese===

大卫和约翰驾车走了一程，谁也没言语。老人家心里显然转着一件勾出来的事。上长坡了，两匹马放缓成走步。忽然寂静里冒出一个声音，叫约翰又惊又乐地回过头——哈勒姆先生在唱歌。那调子——要是也能叫调子——不入任何音阶，词是这样的：

> 「礼拜一清早，俺娶了个媳妇，

指望往后的日子多几分安舒；

提琴也拉了，舞也跳了，

倒瞧瞧俺这苦命人落了个什么。

> 「礼拜二清早，天刚放亮，

俺的脑袋还枕在枕头上，

她那副聒噪嗓子一开腔，

骂出来的名堂，比俺这辈子听过的都高涨。

===Original===

“Never heard me sing before, did ye?” he said, looking with a grin at his companion, who laughed and said that he had never had that pleasure. “Wa’al, that’s all ’t I remember on’t,” said David, “an’ I dunno ’s I’ve thought about it in thirty year. The’ was a number o’ verses which carried ’em through the rest o’ the week, an’ ended up in a case of ’sault an’ battery, I rec’lect, but I don’t remember jest how. Somethin’ we ben sayin’ put the thing into my head, I guess.”
“I should like to hear the rest of it,” said John, smiling.
David made no reply to this, and seemed to be turning something over in his mind. At last he said:
“Mebbe Polly’s told ye that I’m a wid’wer.”
John admitted that Mrs. Bixbee had said as much as that.
“Yes, sir,” said David, “I’m a wid’wer of long standin’.”
No appropriate comment suggesting itself to his listener, none was made.
“I hain’t never cared to say much about it to Polly,” he remarked, “though fer that matter Jim Bixbee, f’m all accounts, was about as poor a shack as ever was turned out, I guess, an’﻿—”
John took advantage of the slight hesitation to interpose against what he apprehended might be a lengthy digression on the subject of the deceased Bixbee by saying:
“You were quite a young fellow when you were married, I infer.”
“Two or three years younger ’n you be, I guess,” said David, looking at him, “an’ a putty green colt too in some ways,” he added, handing over the reins and whip while he got out his silver tobacco box and helped himself to a liberal portion of its contents. It was plain that he was in the mood for personal reminiscences.
“As I look back on’t now,” he began, “it kind o’ seems as if it must ’a’ ben some other feller, an’ yet I remember it all putty dum’d well too﻿—all but one thing, an’ that the biggist part on’t, an’ that is how I ever come to git married at all. She was a widdo’ at the time, an’ kep’ the boardin’ house where I was livin’. It was up to Syrchester. I was better lookin’ them days ’n I be now﻿—had more hair at any rate﻿—though,” he remarked with a grin, “I was alwus a better goer than I was a looker. I was doin’ fairly well,” he continued, “but mebbe not so well as was thought by some.
“Wa’al, she was a good-lookin’ woman, some older ’n I was. She seemed to take some shine to me. I’d roughed it putty much alwus, an’ she was putty clever to me.

===Chinese===

「头一回听俺唱吧？」他咧嘴望着同伴说；对方笑答从没得过这份耳福。「这个嘛，俺记得的就这些，」大卫说，「有三十年没想起它了。后头还有好几段，把一个礼拜剩下的日子唱完，末了以一场打架斗殴收场，俺记得是，怎么收的记不清了。想来是俺们方才哪句话，把这玩意儿勾上了俺的心头。」
「我倒想听听后头的。」约翰微笑说。
大卫没接这话，像是心里又翻起什么。末了他说：
「波莉兴许跟你说过，俺是个鳏夫。」
约翰承认比克斯比太太说过这个话。
「是，先生，」大卫说，「俺是个鳏夫，年头还不短。」
听客想不出得体的话，便一句没说。
「俺从来不大多跟波莉提这个，」他说，「虽说讲起来，吉姆·比克斯比照各方的说法，也是天下少有的窝囊废——还有——」
约翰趁这一顿，赶紧拦住他担心要扯远的亡故比克斯比专题，插话道：「我猜您成亲那会儿还相当年轻。」
「比你小两三岁吧，俺估摸，」大卫望着他说，「而且有几处也是个顶青的生马驹子。」他补一句，把缰绳和鞭子递过去，腾手掏出银烟盒，满满装了一斗。他这会儿显然正是讲身世的心情。
「如今回头看，」他开了腔，「倒像那是别的什么小子的事；可俺又桩桩件件记得清清楚楚——就除了一样，而那偏偏是顶大的一块：俺到底怎么会成的亲。她那阵子是个寡妇，开着俺寄宿的那个公寓，在瑟切斯特。俺那些日子比如今耐看——至少头发多些——不过，」他咧嘴一乐，「俺向来是跑起来比看起来的强。俺的日子过得不算坏，」他接着说，「可兴许没有有些人想的那么好。
「这个嘛，她是个好看的女人，比俺大几岁。她好像对俺有点意思。俺一向糙惯了，她待俺也怪厚道。

===Original===

She was a good talker, liked a joke an’ a laugh, an’ had some education, an’ it come about that I got to beauin’ her ’round quite a consid’able, and used to go an’ set in her room or the parlor with her sometimes evenin’s an’ all that, an’ I wouldn’t deny that I liked it putty well.”
It was some minutes before Mr. Harum resumed his narrative. The reins were sagging over the dashboard, held loosely between the first two fingers and thumb of his left hand, while with his right he had been making abstracted cuts at the thistles and other eligible marks along the roadside.
“Wa’al,” he said at last, “we was married, an’ our wheels tracked putty well fer quite a consid’able spell. I got to thinkin’ more of her all the time, an’ she me, seemin’ly. We took a few days off together two three times that summer, to Niag’ry, an’ Saratogy, an’ ’round, an’ had real good times. I got to thinkin’ that the state of matrimony was a putty good institution. When it come along fall, I was doin’ well enough so ’t she could give up bus’nis, an’ I hired a house an’ we set up housekeepin’. It was really more on my account than her’n, fer I got to kind o’ feelin’ that when the meat was tough or the pie wa’n’t done on the bottom that I was ’sociated with it, an’ gen’ally I wanted a place of my own. But,” he added, “I guess it was a mistake, fur ’s she was concerned.”
“Why?” said John, feeling that some show of interest was incumbent.
“I reckon,” said David, “ ’t she kind o’ missed the comp’ny an’ the talk at table, an’ the goin’s on gen’ally, an’ mebbe the work of runnin’ the place﻿—she was a great worker﻿—an’ it got to be some diff’rent, I s’pose, after a spell, settin’ down to three meals a day with jest only me ’stid of a tableful, to say nothin’ of the evenin’s. I was glad enough to have a place of my own, but at the same time I hadn’t ben used to settin’ ’round with nothin’ pertic’ler to do or say, with somebody else that hadn’t neither, an’ I wa’n’t then nor ain’t now, fer that matter, any great hand fer readin’. Then, too, we’d moved into a diff’rent part o’ the town where my wife wa’n’t acquainted. Wa’al, anyway, fust things begun to drag some﻿—she begun to have spells of not speakin’, an’ then she begun to git notions about me. Once in a while I’d have to go down town on some bus’nis in the evenin’.

===Chinese===

她会说话，爱笑话爱热闹，也有些文化；一来二去，俺就围着她献起殷勤来，献得还不少，有些晚上常去她屋里或客厅坐坐，这一路的事。俺不否认，俺受用得很。」
有好几分钟，哈勒姆先生没有接着讲。缰绳松松搭在挡泥板上，左手两根指头和拇指虚虚拢着；右手一路朝路边的蓟草和一切够得着的靶子心不在焉地甩鞭子。
「这个嘛，」他终于说，「俺们成了亲，两只车轮顺着同一道辙走了好一阵。俺一天比一天多想着她，她瞧着也一天比一天多想着俺。那年夏天俺们一道歇了两三回工，去尼亚加拉、萨拉托加一带转了转，过得真痛快。俺渐渐觉得，婚姻这个制度怪不赖。到了秋天，俺的日子好到她可以把买卖撂下不做了，俺赁了一处房，俺们自立了门户。说实在的，多一半是为俺自个儿：肉老了、派底下没烤透的时候，俺渐渐觉着这些跟俺有了干系，况且俺总归想有个自个儿的家。不过，」他添一句，「就她来说，俺想这是个错招。」
「为什么？」约翰说，觉得总得表示一下兴味。
「俺估摸，」大卫说，「她是想那一屋子饭客、饭桌上的谈笑、人来客往的热闹了，兴许还有操持那摊生意的忙活——她是个顶能干的人——过上一阵，一天三顿、对面只坐着俺一个，跟从前一桌人比，滋味总归不同，更别说一个个晚上了。有个自个儿的家，俺高兴还来不及；可话说回来，俺也没惯过跟一个没话找话的人一块干坐着——俺自个儿也无话可找；再说，念书这个营生，俺那时谈不上，如今也谈不上。还有，俺们搬到了城里另一头，俺媳妇那边人生地不熟。这个嘛，反正，起头的事儿就渐渐拖沓了——她开始一阵一阵不说话，跟着开始对俺起疑心。俺时不时晚上得进城办事。

===Original===

She didn’t seem to mind it at fust, but bom-by she got it into her head that the’ wa’n’t so much bus’nis goin’ on as I made out, an’ though along that time she’d set sometimes mebbe the hull evenin’ without sayin’ anythin’ more ’n yes or no, an’ putty often not that, yet if I went out there’d be a flare-up; an’ as things went on the’d be spells fer a fortni’t together when I couldn’t any time of day git a word out of her hardly, unless it was to go fer me ’bout somethin’ that mebbe I’d done an’ mebbe I hadn’t﻿—it didn’t make no diff’rence. An’ when them spells was on, what she didn’t take out o’ me she did out o’ the house﻿—diggin’ an’ scrubbin’, takin’ up carpits, layin’ down carpits, shiftin’ the furniture, eatin’ one day in the kitchin an’ another in the settin’ room, an’ sleepin’ most anywhere. She wa’n’t real well after a while, an’ the wuss she seemed to feel, the fiercer she was fer scrubbin’ an’ diggin’ an’ upsettin’ things in gen’ral, an’ bom-by she got so she couldn’t keep a hired girl in the house more ’n a day or two at a time. She either wouldn’t have ’em, or they wouldn’t stay, an’ more ’n half the time we was without one. This can’t int’rist you much, can it?” said Mr. Harum, turning to his companion.
“On the contrary,” replied John, “it interests me very much. I was thinking,” he added, “that probably the state of your wife’s health had a good deal to do with her actions and views of things, but it must have been pretty hard on you all the same.”
“Wa’al, yes,” said David, “I guess that’s so. Her health wa’n’t jes’ right, an’ she showed it in her looks. I noticed that she’d pined an’ pindled some, but I thought the’ was some natural criss-crossedniss mixed up into it too. But I tried to make allow’nces an’ the best o’ things, an’ git along ’s well ’s I could; but things kind o’ got wuss an’ wuss. I told ye that she begun to have notions about me, an’ ’tain’t hardly nec’sary to say what shape they took, an’ after a while, mebbe a year ’n a half, she got so ’t she wa’n’t satisfied to know where I was *nights*﻿—she wanted to know where I was *daytimes*. Kind o’ makes me laugh now,” he observed, “it seems so redic’lous; but it wa’n’t no laughin’ matter then. If I looked out o’ winder she’d hint it up to me that I was watchin’ some woman.

===Chinese===

起先她好像不介意，可后来她认定了，城里没那么多生意好忙；虽说那一阵她能整晚整晚坐着，除了『是』『不』多半一个字不吐——常常连这两个字也免——可俺一出门就有一场火。日子这么走下去，有一搭没一搭就是半个月，一天里无论什么时辰俺休想从她嘴里掏出一句话，除非是冲着俺来，为一件俺兴许干过、兴许没干过的事——那全不相干。那种阵头一上来，她不在俺身上出气，就在房子身上出气——又刨又刷，掀地毯，铺地毯，挪家具，今儿在厨房开饭，明儿在起居室开饭，睡觉更是随哪儿倒哪儿。过了一阵她身子就不大好了，而且她越是难受，刷的刨的、翻箱倒柜的火气越旺；到后来，雇来的丫头在她家没有一个待得住两天的。不是她不要，就是人家不肯留，一大半日子家里没个帮手。这些个你听不进多少兴味吧？」哈勒姆先生转过脸对同伴说。
「恰恰相反，」约翰答，「我听着很有兴味。我方才在想，」他添一句，「多半是尊夫人的身子骨，跟她的行事、跟她看事情的眼光大有关连；可饶是这么着，您也实在难捱。」
「这个嘛，是，」大卫说，「俺想是这个理。她的身子骨不大利索，脸色也看得出来。俺瞧她消瘦憔悴了些，可俺当时还当，里头也掺着几分天生的别扭。俺试着体谅，往好处想，能凑合多好凑合多好；可事情还是一糟一糟下去。俺跟你说过她起头对俺起疑心，那些疑心走的什么路数，俺不必细说；过了一年半上下，她不满足于知道俺*夜里*在哪儿了——她要知道俺*白天*在哪儿。如今想想俺都想笑，」他说，「瞧着那么荒唐；可那时候一点儿也不好笑。俺朝窗外望一眼，她就要点俺：是不是瞅上哪个女人了。

===Original===

She grudged me even to look at a picture paper; an’ one day when we happened to be walkin’ together she showed feelin’ about one o’ them wooden Injun women outside a cigar store.”
“Oh, come now, Mr. Harum,” said John, laughing.
“Wa’al,” said David with a short laugh, “mebbe I did stretch that a little; but ’s I told ye, she wanted to know where I was daytimes well ’s nights, an’ ev’ry once ’n a while she’d turn up at my bus’nis place, an’ if I wa’n’t there she’d set an’ wait fer me, an’ I’d either have to go home with her or have it out in the office. I don’t mean to say that all the sort of thing I’m tellin’ ye of kep’ up all the time. It kind o’ run in streaks; but the streaks kep’ comin’ oftener an’ oftener, an’ you couldn’t never tell when the’ was goin’ to appear. Matters ’d go along putty well fer a while, an’ then, all of a sudden, an’ fer nothin’ ’t I could see, the’ ’d come on a thunder shower ’fore you c’d git in out o’ the wet.”
“Singular,” said John thoughtfully.
“Yes, sir,” said David. “Wa’al, it come along to the second spring, ’bout the first of May. She’d ben more like folks fer about a week mebbe ’n she had fer a long spell, an’ I begun to chirk up some. I don’t remember jest how I got the idee, but f’m somethin’ she let drop I gathered that she was thinkin’ of havin’ a new bunnit. I will say this for her,” remarked David, “that she was an economical woman, an’ never spent no money jest fer the sake o’ spendin’ it. Wa’al, we’d got along so nice fer a while that I felt more ’n usual like pleasin’ her, an’ I allowed to myself that if she wanted a new bunnit, money shouldn’t stand in the way, an’ I set out to give her a supprise.”
They had reached the level at the top of the long hill and the horses had broken into a trot, when Mr. Harum’s narrative was interrupted and his equanimity upset by the onslaught of an excessively shrill, active, and conscientious dog of the “yellow” variety, which barked and sprang about in front of the mares with such frantic assiduity as at last to communicate enough of its excitement to them to cause them to bolt forward on a run, passing the yellow nuisance, which, with the facility of long practice, dodged the cut which David made at it in passing. It was with some little trouble that the horses were brought back to a sober pace.
“Dum that dum’d dog!”

===Chinese===

连画报俺多瞧一眼她都不乐意；有一天俺们碰巧一道走路，雪茄铺子外头那木头印第安女人，她也拿出心事来。」
「得了吧，哈勒姆先生。」约翰笑起来。
「这个嘛，」大卫干笑一声，「那一条兴许有几分夸大；可俺跟你说了，她白天要知道俺在哪儿，晚上也要知道，隔三差五还上俺的铺子去；俺要不在，她就坐下等，俺要么跟她回家，要么在铺子里当场了断。俺不是说俺讲的这一路事常年不断。它是一阵一阵的；可一阵比一阵来得勤，你永远说不准哪阵要来。事情能顺顺当当走一程，跟着，说变就变，也看不出个所以然，雷阵雨说来就来了，躲都躲不及。」
「真怪。」约翰若有所思。
「是，先生，」大卫说。「这个嘛，这就到了第二个春天，五月初。她有个把礼拜比平常像样了些，俺也提起点精神。俺记不得那念头是怎么起的，反正是从她漏的什么话里，俺咂摸出她在想一顶新帽子。俺得替她说一句，」大卫评道，「她是个节俭的女人，从不为花钱而花钱。这个嘛，那一阵俺们处得那么好，俺比平常更想讨她欢喜；俺跟自个儿盘算，她要新帽子，钱不算回事，俺就着手给她来个突然惊喜。」
他们已经上到长坡顶上的平路，两匹马放开了小跑；哈勒姆先生的叙述忽然被打断，他的泰然也失了守——一条嗓门奇尖、劲头奇足、责任心奇重的黄狗，冲着两匹母马前前后后狂吠猛扑，那份奋不顾身到底把它的激动传给了马，马撒开蹄子直冲过去，越过了那条黄毛祸害；那狗凭着多年练出的功夫，闪过了大卫路过时朝它甩的一鞭。费了点事，马才收回到稳当的步子。
「那倒霉狗真倒霉透了！」

===Original===

exclaimed David with fervor, looking back to where the object of his execrations was still discharging convulsive yelps at the retreating vehicle, “I’d give a five-dollar note to git one good lick at him. I’d make him holler ‘pen-an’-ink’ *once*! Why anybody’s willin’ to have such a dum’d, wuthless, pestiferous varmint as that ’round ’s more ’n I c’n understand. I’ll bet that the days they churn, that critter, unless they ketch him an’ tie him up the night before, ’ll be under the barn all day, an’ he’s jest blowed off steam enough to run a dog churn a hull forenoon.”
Whether or not the episode of the dog had diverted Mr. Harum’s mind from his previous topic, he did not resume it until John ventured to remind him of it, with “You were saying something about the surprise for your wife.”
“That’s so,” said David. “Yes, wa’al, when I went home that night I stopped into a mil’nery store, an’ after I’d stood ’round a minute, a girl come up an’ ast me if she c’d show me anythin’.
“ ‘I want to buy a bunnit,’ I says, an’ she kind o’ laughed. ‘No,’ I says, ‘it ain’t fer me, it’s fer a lady,’ I says; an’ then we both laughed.
“ ‘What sort of a bunnit do you want?’ she says.
“ ‘Wa’al, I dunno,’ I says, ‘this is the fust time I ever done anythin’ in the bunnit line.’ So she went over to a glass case an’ took one out an’ held it up, turnin’ it ’round on her hand.
“ ‘Wa’al,’ I says, ‘I guess it’s putty enough fur ’s it goes, but the’ don’t seem to be much of anythin’ *to* it. Hain’t you got somethin’ a little bit bigger an’﻿—’
“ ‘Showier?’ she says. ‘How is this?’ she says, doin’ the same trick with another.
“ ‘Wa’al,’ I says, ‘that looks more like it, but I had an idee that the A1, trible-extry fine article had more traps on’t, an’ most any one might have on either one o’ them you’ve showed me an’ not attrac’ no attention at all. You needn’t mind expense,’ I says.
“ ‘Oh, very well,’ she says, ‘I guess I know what you want,’ an’ goes over to another case an’ fetches out another bunnit twice as big as either the others, an’ with more notions on’t than you c’d shake a stick at﻿—flowers, an’ gard’n stuff, an’ fruit, an’ glass beads, an’ feathers, an’ all that, till you couldn’t see what they was fixed on to. She took holt on’t with both hands, the girl did, an’ put it onto her head, an’ kind o’ smiled an’ turned ’round slow so ’t I c’d git a gen’ral view on’t.

===Chinese===

大卫切齿地骂，回头看那条挨他咒的东西还在朝远去的车一抽一抽地叫。「俺出五块的票子，换结结实实抽它一鞭。俺叫它喊一声『钢笔水』，喊*一*声！怎么有人肯养那么一条倒霉、没用、招灾惹祸的畜生，俺想不通。俺敢打赌，他们家搅黄油那天，那家伙——除非头天夜里把他逮住拴起来——一整天准钻在牲口棚底下不出来；他这股撒掉的气，够狗力黄油机转一整个前晌。」
狗这一出究竟有没有把哈勒姆先生的心思从原来的题目上岔开，反正他不接着讲了，直到约翰试着一提：「您刚说到给您太太的意外惊喜。」
「正是，」大卫说。「是，这个嘛，那天晚上俺回家，顺脚进了一家女帽店；俺在店里杵了一会儿，一个姑娘走过来问俺要看什么。
「『俺要买顶帽子，』俺说；她哧哧笑了。『不是，』俺说，『不是俺戴，是给一位太太，』俺说；这一下俺俩都乐了。
「『您要什么样的帽子？』她说。
「『这个嘛，俺说不上，』俺说，『俺头一回在帽子上做买卖。』她就去一个玻璃柜里取出一顶，托在手上，转着圈给俺瞧。
「『这个嘛，』俺说，『就它自个儿说，够俊的了，可瞧着不像有多少*货*。有没有稍大点儿的，还有——』
「『更扎眼的？』她说，『这顶怎么样？』说着拿另一顶照样来了一遍。
「『这个嘛，』俺说，『有点儿那个意思了。可俺原先的印象，头等三绝的好货，上头的零碎儿该更多；你给俺看的这两顶，随便哪顶谁戴上街都招不来一眼。你别管价钱，』俺说。
「『哦，好办，』她说，『俺知道您要什么了。』她走到另一口柜子跟前，又取出一顶，比先前哪顶都大一倍，上头的名堂多得你拿棍子都数不过来——花呀，园子里的出产呀，果子呀，玻璃珠子呀，羽毛呀，这一路，到底缝在什么上头都瞧不出来了。那姑娘两手捧着它，戴到自己头上，微微一笑，慢慢转了一圈，好叫俺看个四面八方。

===Original===

“ ‘Style all right?’ I says.
“ ‘The very best of its kind,’ she says.
“ ‘How ’bout the *kind*?’ I says.
“ ‘The very best of its style,’ she says.”
John laughed outright. David looked at him for a moment with a doubtful grin.
“She *was* a slick one, wa’n’t she?” he said. “What a hoss trader she would ’a’ made. I didn’t ketch on at the time, but I rec’lected afterward. Wa’al,” he resumed, after this brief digression, “ ‘how much is it?’ I says.
“ ‘Fifteen dollars,’ she says.
“ ‘What?’ I says. ‘Scat my —﻿—﻿—! I c’d buy head rigging enough to last me ten years fer that.’
“ ‘We couldn’t sell it for less,’ she says.
“ ‘S’posin’ the lady ’t I’m buyin’ it fer don’t jest like it,’ I says, ‘can you alter it or swap somethin’ else for it?’
“ ‘Cert’nly, within a reasonable time,’ she says.
“ ‘Wa’al, all right,’ I says, ‘do her up.’ An’ so she wrapped the thing ’round with soft paper an’ put it in a box, an’ I paid for’t an’ moseyed along up home, feelin’ that ev’ry man, woman, an’ child had their eyes on my parcel, but thinkin’ how tickled my wife would be.”

===Chinese===

「『样子行吗？』俺说。
「『这一款里的头一份。』她说。
「『那*款*呢？』俺说。
「『这一样式里的头一份。』她说。」
约翰放声大笑。大卫拿将信将疑的咧嘴望了他一会儿。
「她可真是只老油子，是不是？」他说，「她要干贩马这一行，得是多大一把好手。俺当时没转过筋，过后才回过味来。这个嘛，」这一小段跑题之后他接着讲，「『多少钱？』俺说。
「『十五块。』她说。
「『什么？』俺说。『哎呀俺的——！这点钱够俺买十年的头上行头。』
「『少了俺们不卖。』她说。
「『俺买它的那位太太要是不中意呢，』俺说，『能改吗？或者换点儿别的？』
「『自然能，限期之内。』她说。
「『这个嘛，行了，』俺说，『给她包上。』她拿软纸把那玩意儿一层层裹好，装进盒子；俺付了钱，慢悠悠蹭回家去，只觉着满街的男人女人小孩，眼睛全钉在俺这包东西上，心里想的却是俺媳妇见了该多欢喜。」
